## THUS SPAKE ZARATHUSTRA.

## FIRST PART. ZARATHUSTRA’S DISCOURSES.

## ZARATHUSTRA’S PROLOGUE.

1.

When Zarathustra was thirty years old, he left his home and the lake of his home and went into the mountains. There he enjoyed his own spirit and his solitude, and for ten years he did not tire of them.

But at last his heart changed. One morning he rose with the rosy dawn, stepped out before the sun, and spoke to it:

You great star! What would your happiness be if you had no one to shine for?

For ten years you have climbed up here to my cave. You would have grown tired of your light and of the journey, if it had not been for me, my eagle, and my serpent.

But we waited for you every morning. We took your overflow from you, and we blessed you for it.

Look! I am weary of my wisdom, like a bee that has gathered too much honey. I need hands reaching out to take it.

I want to give it away and share it out, until the wise have once again become joyful in their folly, and the poor have become happy in their riches.

For that, I must descend into the depths. I must do as you do in the evening, when you sink behind the sea and bring light even to the underworld, you overflowing star!

Like you, I must *go down*, as it is called by the people to whom I shall descend.

So bless me, you calm eye, which can look even on the greatest happiness without envy!

Bless the cup that is about to overflow, so that the water may flow golden out of it and carry the reflection of your bliss everywhere!

Look! This cup is about to empty itself again, and Zarathustra is about to become a man again.

So began Zarathustra’s going-down.

2.

Zarathustra went down the mountain alone, and no one met him. But when he entered the forest, an old man suddenly stood before him. The old man had left his holy hut to look for roots. And the old man spoke to Zarathustra:

“This wanderer is no stranger to me. Many years ago he passed this way. He was called Zarathustra, but he has changed.

Back then you carried your ashes up into the mountains. Will you now carry your fire down into the valleys? Are you not afraid of the fate of those who set fires?

Yes, I recognize Zarathustra. His eye is clear, and no disgust lurks around his mouth. Doesn’t he walk like a dancer?

Zarathustra has changed. Zarathustra has become a child. Zarathustra is awake. What will you do in the land of the sleepers?

You lived in solitude as if in the sea, and the sea carried you. Alas, will you now go ashore? Alas, will you once again drag your own body along?”

Zarathustra answered: “I love mankind.”

“Why,” said the saint, “did I go into the forest and the desert? Was it not because I loved people far too much?

Now I love God. People I do not love. Man is too imperfect a thing for me. Love of man would destroy me.”

Zarathustra answered: “Who said anything about love? I am bringing people gifts.”

“Give them nothing,” said the saint. “Instead, take part of their load and carry it along with them. That will please them most, as long as it pleases you too!

But if you want to give them something, give them no more than charity, and make them beg for it as well!”

“No,” replied Zarathustra, “I give no charity. I am not poor enough for that.”

The saint laughed at Zarathustra and said: “Then make sure they accept your treasures! They distrust hermits, and they do not believe that we come bearing gifts.

Our footsteps ring too hollow through their streets. When they lie in bed at night and hear someone walking outside long before sunrise, they ask themselves: ‘Where is that thief going?’ They ask the same about us.

Do not go to people. Stay in the forest! Go to the animals instead! Why not be like me, a bear among bears, a bird among birds?”

“And what does the saint do in the forest?” asked Zarathustra.

The saint answered: “I make up hymns and sing them. And as I make them, I laugh and weep and hum. That is how I praise God.

With singing, weeping, laughing, and humming, I praise the God who is my God. But what do you bring us as a gift?”

When Zarathustra heard these words, he bowed to the saint and said: “What could I have to give you? Let me hurry away instead, before I take something from you!” And so they parted, the old man and Zarathustra, laughing like two schoolboys.

But when Zarathustra was alone, he said to his heart: “Could it be possible? This old saint in the forest has not yet heard that *God is dead*!”

3.

When Zarathustra came to the nearest town, at the edge of the forest, he found many people gathered in the market square. It had been announced that a tightrope walker would give a performance. And Zarathustra spoke to the people:

*I teach you the Superman.* Man is something that must be overcome. What have you done to overcome him?

Every kind of being so far has created something beyond itself. Do you want to be the ebb of that great tide? Would you rather go back to the beast than overcome man?

What is the ape to man? A joke, or a painful embarrassment. And man shall be exactly the same to the Superman: a joke, or a painful embarrassment.

You have made your way from worm to man, and much in you is still worm. Once you were apes, and even now man is more of an ape than any ape.

Even the wisest among you is only a discord, a cross between plant and ghost. But do I tell you to become ghosts or plants?

Look, I teach you the Superman!

The Superman is the meaning of the earth. Let your will say: the Superman *shall be* the meaning of the earth!

I beg you, my brothers, *remain true to the earth*. Do not believe those who speak to you of hopes beyond the earth! They are poisoners, whether they know it or not.

They despise life. They are dying and poisoned themselves, and the earth is tired of them. So let them go!

Once, blasphemy against God was the greatest blasphemy. But God died, and those blasphemers died with him. Now the most dreadful sin is to blaspheme the earth, and to value the innards of the unknowable more highly than the meaning of the earth!

Once, the soul looked down on the body with contempt, and that contempt was considered the highest thing. The soul wanted the body thin, ghastly, and starved. In that way it hoped to escape from the body and from the earth.

Oh, but that soul was itself thin, ghastly, and starved, and cruelty was its pleasure!

But you too, my brothers, tell me: what does your body say about your soul? Isn’t your soul poverty and filth and miserable self-satisfaction?

Truly, man is a polluted stream. One must be a sea to take in a polluted stream without becoming unclean.

Look, I teach you the Superman. He is that sea. In him your great contempt can sink and disappear.

What is the greatest thing you can experience? It is the hour of great contempt. It is the hour in which even your happiness disgusts you, and so do your reason and your virtue.

It is the hour when you say: “What good is my happiness? It is poverty and filth and miserable self-satisfaction. Yet my happiness ought to justify existence itself!”

It is the hour when you say: “What good is my reason? Does it hunger for knowledge the way a lion hungers for food? It is poverty and filth and miserable self-satisfaction!”

It is the hour when you say: “What good is my virtue? So far it has not made me passionate. How tired I am of my good and my evil! It is all poverty and filth and miserable self-satisfaction!”

It is the hour when you say: “What good is my justice? I do not see that I am fire and fuel. But the just are fire and fuel!”

It is the hour when you say: “What good is my pity? Isn’t pity the cross on which the one who loves mankind is nailed? But my pity is no crucifixion.”

Have you ever spoken like this? Have you ever cried out like this? Ah, if only I had heard you cry out like this!

It is not your sin that cries out to heaven. It is your self-satisfaction. Your very stinginess in sinning cries out to heaven!

Where is the lightning to lick you with its tongue? Where is the frenzy you ought to be inoculated with?

Look, I teach you the Superman. He is that lightning. He is that frenzy!

When Zarathustra had finished speaking, someone in the crowd called out: “We’ve heard enough about the tightrope walker. Now let’s see him!” And all the people laughed at Zarathustra. But the tightrope walker, who thought the words were meant for him, began his performance.

4.

Zarathustra, however, looked at the people and wondered. Then he spoke:

Man is a rope stretched between the animal and the Superman, a rope over an abyss.

It is a dangerous crossing, a dangerous journey, a dangerous looking-back, a dangerous trembling and stopping.

What is great in man is that he is a bridge and not a goal. What can be loved in man is that he is a *going-over* and a *going-down*.

I love those who do not know how to live except by going down, for they are the ones who go over.

I love the great despisers, because they are the great worshippers, arrows of longing for the far shore.

I love those who do not first look for a reason beyond the stars to go down and be sacrificed. Instead they sacrifice themselves to the earth, so that the earth may one day belong to the Superman.

I love the one who lives in order to know, and who seeks knowledge so that the Superman may one day live. In this way he seeks his own going-down.

I love the one who works and invents in order to build a house for the Superman, and to prepare earth, animal, and plant for him. In this way he seeks his own going-down.

I love the one who loves his virtue, for virtue is the will to go down, and an arrow of longing.

I love the one who keeps no drop of spirit for himself, but wants to be entirely the spirit of his virtue. In this way he walks as spirit over the bridge.

I love the one who makes his virtue his inclination and his destiny. For the sake of his virtue, he is willing either to live on or to live no longer.

I love the one who does not want too many virtues. One virtue is more of a virtue than two, because it is a stronger knot for one’s destiny to hang on.

I love the one whose soul spends itself freely, who wants no thanks and gives nothing back. He always gives, and does not want to keep anything for himself.

I love the one who is ashamed when the dice fall in his favor, and who then asks: “Am I a cheat?” For he is willing to perish.

I love the one who scatters golden words ahead of his deeds, and always does more than he promises. For he seeks his own going-down.

I love the one who justifies those who will come in the future and redeems those who lived in the past. For he is willing to perish through those of the present.

I love the one who punishes his God because he loves his God. For he must perish through the anger of his God.

I love the one whose soul is deep even when it is wounded, and who can perish over some small thing. In this way he goes gladly over the bridge.

I love the one whose soul is so overfull that he forgets himself, and all things are within him. In this way all things become his going-down.

I love the one who has a free spirit and a free heart. His head is then only the entrails of his heart, but his heart drives him to his going-down.

I love all those who are like heavy drops falling one by one from the dark cloud that hangs over mankind. They announce that the lightning is coming, and they perish as its heralds.

Look, I am a herald of the lightning, a heavy drop from the cloud. But the lightning is called *Superman*.

5.

When Zarathustra had spoken these words, he looked at the people again and fell silent. “There they stand,” he said to his heart. “There they laugh. They do not understand me. I am not the mouth for these ears.

Must one first smash their ears so that they learn to hear with their eyes? Must one rattle like kettledrums and preachers of repentance? Or do they only believe someone who stammers?

They have something they are proud of. What do they call this thing that makes them proud? They call it culture. It sets them apart from the goatherds.

That is why they dislike hearing the word ‘contempt’ applied to themselves. So I will appeal to their pride instead.

I will speak to them of the most contemptible thing of all: and that is *the last man*!”

And so Zarathustra spoke to the people:

It is time for man to set his goal. It is time for man to plant the seed of his highest hope.

His soil is still rich enough for it. But one day that soil will be poor and worn out, and no tall tree will be able to grow in it anymore.

Alas! The time is coming when man will no longer shoot the arrow of his longing beyond man, and the string of his bow will have forgotten how to hum!

I tell you: one must still have chaos within oneself to give birth to a dancing star. I tell you: you still have chaos within you.

Alas! The time is coming when man will no longer give birth to any star. Alas! The time is coming of the most contemptible man, who can no longer feel contempt for himself.

Look! I show you *the last man*.

“What is love? What is creation? What is longing? What is a star?” So asks the last man, and blinks.

The earth has then become small, and on it hops the last man, who makes everything small. His kind is as impossible to wipe out as the ground-flea. The last man lives longest.

"We have discovered happiness," say the last men, and they blink.

They have left the regions where life is hard, because they need warmth. People still love their neighbor and rub up against him, because they need warmth.

They consider falling ill and being distrustful sinful, so they walk carefully. Only a fool still stumbles over stones or over people!

A little poison now and then: that makes for pleasant dreams. And a lot of poison at the end, for a pleasant death.

People still work, because work is a pastime. But they take care that the pastime never hurts them.

No one becomes poor or rich anymore; both are too much of a burden. Who still wants to rule? Who still wants to obey? Both are too much of a burden.

No shepherd, and one herd! Everyone wants the same thing; everyone is equal. Whoever feels differently goes into the madhouse of his own free will.

"In the old days, the whole world was mad," say the most refined among them, and they blink.

They are clever and know everything that has ever happened, so there is no end to their mockery. People still quarrel, but they soon make up. Otherwise it upsets their stomachs.

They have their little pleasures for the day and their little pleasures for the night, but they have a high regard for health.

"We have discovered happiness," say the last men, and they blink.—

Here ended Zarathustra's first discourse, which is also called "The Prologue," because at this point the shouting and merriment of the crowd interrupted him.

"Give us this last man, O Zarathustra," they called out. "Make us into these last men! Then we will make you a present of the Superman!" And all the people cheered and smacked their lips. Zarathustra, however, grew sad, and said to his heart:

"They do not understand me. I am not the mouth for these ears.

"Perhaps I have lived in the mountains too long. I have listened too much to the brooks and the trees, and now I speak to these people as I would to goatherds.

"My soul is calm and clear, like the mountains in the morning. But they think I am cold, a mocker who tells terrible jokes.

"And now they look at me and laugh, and while they laugh, they hate me too. There is ice in their laughter."

6.

Then something happened that silenced every mouth and fixed every eye. In the meantime, the rope-dancer had begun his performance. He had come out through a little door and was walking along the rope, which was stretched between two towers so that it hung above the market-place and the people.

When he was exactly halfway across, the little door opened again. A gaudily dressed fellow, like a clown, sprang out and went quickly after the first one.

"Keep going, limp-foot!" cried his frightful voice. "Keep going, lazy-bones, intruder, pasty-face, or I'll tickle you with my heel! What are you doing here between the towers? The tower is the place for you. You should be locked up. You are blocking the way for someone better than you!"

With every word he came closer and closer to the first one. When he was only a step behind, the frightful thing happened that silenced every mouth and fixed every eye. He let out a yell like a devil and jumped over the man who stood in his way.

When the rope-dancer saw his rival triumph like this, he lost both his head and his footing on the rope. He threw away his pole and plunged downward faster than it, a whirl of arms and legs, into the depth below.

The market-place and the people were like the sea when a storm comes on. They all scattered in disorder, especially where the body was about to fall.

Zarathustra, however, stood still, and the body fell right beside him, badly injured and broken, but not yet dead. After a while the shattered man came to, and he saw Zarathustra kneeling beside him.

"What are you doing there?" he said at last. "I knew long ago that the devil would trip me up. Now he is dragging me to hell. Will you stop him?"

"On my honor, my friend," answered Zarathustra, "none of what you speak of exists. There is no devil and no hell. Your soul will be dead even sooner than your body. So fear nothing anymore!"

The man looked up distrustfully. "If you are telling the truth," he said, "then I lose nothing when I lose my life. I am not much more than an animal that has been taught to dance by beatings and meager food."

"Not at all," said Zarathustra. "You made danger your calling, and there is nothing contemptible in that. Now you are dying by your calling. For that reason I will bury you with my own hands."

When Zarathustra had said this, the dying man made no further reply. But he moved his hand as if he were reaching for Zarathustra's hand to thank him.

7.

Meanwhile evening came on, and the market-place was wrapped in gloom. Then the people went away, for even curiosity and terror grow tired. Zarathustra, however, still sat on the ground beside the dead man, lost in thought, and so he forgot the time.

At last night fell, and a cold wind blew on the lonely man. Then Zarathustra rose and said to his heart:

Truly, Zarathustra has made a fine catch of fish today! It is not a man he has caught, but a corpse.

Human life is somber, and still without meaning. A clown can decide its fate.

I want to teach people the meaning of their existence. That meaning is the Superman, the lightning out of the dark cloud that is man.

But I am still far from them, and my meaning does not speak to their minds. To people I am still something between a fool and a corpse.

The night is dark, and dark are the ways of Zarathustra. Come, my cold and stiff companion! I will carry you to the place where I will bury you with my own hands.

8.

When Zarathustra had said this to his heart, he lifted the corpse onto his shoulders and set out on his way. But before he had gone a hundred steps, a man crept up to him and whispered in his ear. And look! The one who spoke was the clown from the tower.

"Leave this town, O Zarathustra," he said. "There are too many here who hate you. The good and just hate you, and call you their enemy and despiser. The believers in the true faith hate you, and call you a danger to the masses.

"It was your good luck that they laughed at you, and truly, you spoke like a clown. It was your good luck to take up with the dead dog. By humbling yourself like this, you saved your life today.

"But leave this town, or tomorrow I will jump over you, a living man over a dead one." When he had said this, the clown vanished. Zarathustra, however, went on through the dark streets.

At the town gate the grave-diggers met him. They shone their torch in his face, recognized Zarathustra, and mocked him harshly.

"Zarathustra is carrying off the dead dog! How fine that Zarathustra has become a grave-digger! Our hands are too clean for that roast. Is Zarathustra going to steal the devil's dinner? Well then, enjoy the meal! If only the devil isn't a better thief than Zarathustra! He'll steal them both, he'll eat them both!" And they laughed among themselves and put their heads together.

Zarathustra made no answer, but went on his way. When he had walked for two hours, past forests and swamps, he had heard too much of the hungry howling of the wolves, and he grew hungry himself. So he stopped at a lonely house where a light was burning.

"Hunger attacks me like a robber," said Zarathustra. "In the forests and swamps my hunger attacks me, and late at night.

"My hunger has strange moods. Often it comes to me only after a meal, and today it did not come all day. Where has it been?"

With that, Zarathustra knocked at the door of the house. An old man appeared, carrying a light, and asked: "Who comes to me and my bad sleep?"

"A living man and a dead one," said Zarathustra. "Give me something to eat and drink. I forgot to during the day. 'Whoever feeds the hungry refreshes his own soul,' says wisdom."

The old man went away, but came back immediately and offered Zarathustra bread and wine. "This is a bad country for the hungry," he said. "That is why I live here. Animals and people come to me, the hermit. But tell your companion to eat and drink too. He is more tired than you."

Zarathustra answered: "My companion is dead. I will hardly be able to persuade him to eat."

"That is no concern of mine," said the old man sullenly. "Whoever knocks at my door must take what I offer him. Eat, and farewell!"—

After that Zarathustra walked on for another two hours, trusting the path and the light of the stars. He was an experienced night-walker, and he liked to look into the face of everything that sleeps.

When morning dawned, however, Zarathustra found himself in a thick forest, and the path could no longer be seen. He then placed the dead man in a hollow tree above his head, because he wanted to protect him from the wolves. He lay down on the ground and the moss, and immediately fell asleep, tired in body but tranquil in soul.

9.

Zarathustra slept a long time. Not only the rosy dawn passed over his head, but the whole morning as well. At last, however, his eyes opened. In amazement he gazed into the forest and the stillness; in amazement he gazed into himself.

Then he rose quickly, like a sailor who suddenly sees land, and he shouted for joy, because he saw a new truth. And he said to his heart:

A light has dawned on me. I need companions, living ones, not dead companions and corpses that I carry with me wherever I go.

I need living companions, who will follow me because they want to follow themselves, and who will go where I want to go.

A light has dawned on me. Zarathustra is not to speak to the people, but to companions! Zarathustra must not be the herdsman and sheepdog of the herd!

I have come to lure many away from the herd. The people and the herd must be angry with me. The herdsmen will call Zarathustra a robber.

I say herdsmen, but they call themselves the good and just. I say herdsmen, but they call themselves the believers in the true faith.

Look at the good and just! Whom do they hate most? The one who breaks their tablets of values, the breaker, the lawbreaker. Yet he is the creator.

Look at the believers of every faith! Whom do they hate most? The one who breaks their tablets of values, the breaker, the lawbreaker. Yet he is the creator.

The creator seeks companions, not corpses, and not herds or believers either. The creator seeks fellow creators, those who carve new values on new tablets.

The creator seeks companions and fellow harvesters, for with him everything is ripe for the harvest. But he lacks the hundred sickles, so he plucks the ears of grain by hand and is frustrated.

The creator seeks companions who know how to sharpen their sickles. They will be called destroyers, and despisers of good and evil. But they are the harvesters and the rejoicers.

Zarathustra seeks fellow creators. Zarathustra seeks fellow harvesters and fellow rejoicers. What has he to do with herds and herdsmen and corpses!

And you, my first companion, rest in peace! I have buried you well in your hollow tree. I have hidden you well from the wolves.

But I am leaving you; the time has come. Between one rosy dawn and the next, a new truth came to me.

I am not to be a herdsman. I am not to be a grave-digger. I will no longer speak to the people. For the last time I have spoken to the dead.

I will keep company with the creators, the harvesters, and the rejoicers. I will show them the rainbow and all the steps up to the Superman.

I will sing my song to those who live alone and to those who live in pairs. And for anyone who still has ears for the unheard, I will make the heart heavy with my happiness.

I am heading for my goal; I follow my own course. I will leap over the lingering and the slow. So let my going forward be their down-going!

10.

Zarathustra had said this to his heart when the sun stood at noon. Then he looked up questioningly, because he heard the sharp cry of a bird above him.

And look! An eagle swept through the air in wide circles, and a serpent hung from it, not like prey but like a friend, for it kept itself coiled around the eagle's neck.

"They are my animals," said Zarathustra, and his heart rejoiced.

"The proudest animal under the sun, and the wisest animal under the sun: they have come out to scout.

"They want to know whether Zarathustra is still alive. Truly, am I still alive?

"I have found it more dangerous among people than among animals. Zarathustra walks dangerous paths. Let my animals lead me!"

When Zarathustra had said this, he remembered the words of the saint in the forest. Then he sighed and said to his heart:

"If only I were wiser! If only I were wise from the depths of my heart, like my serpent!

"But I am asking for the impossible. So I ask my pride always to go along with my wisdom!

"And if my wisdom should someday abandon me—alas, it loves to fly away!—then may my pride fly away with my folly!"

So began Zarathustra's down-going.

## ZARATHUSTRA’S DISCOURSES.

## I. THE THREE METAMORPHOSES.

Three transformations of the spirit I name for you: how the spirit becomes a camel, the camel becomes a lion, and the lion at last becomes a child.

There are many heavy things for the spirit, for the strong, load-bearing spirit in which reverence lives. Its strength longs for what is heavy, and for what is heaviest of all.

What is heavy? So asks the load-bearing spirit. Then it kneels down like the camel and wants to be well loaded.

What is the heaviest thing, you heroes? asks the load-bearing spirit, so that I may take it upon myself and rejoice in my strength.

Is it this: to humble yourself in order to wound your pride? To put your foolishness on display in order to mock your own wisdom?

Or is it this: to abandon your cause at the very moment it celebrates its triumph? To climb high mountains in order to tempt the tempter?

Or is it this: to live on the acorns and grass of knowledge, and for the sake of truth to let your soul go hungry?

Or is it this: to be sick and send away those who come to comfort you, and to make friends of the deaf, who never hear what you ask?

Or is it this: to wade into foul water when it is the water of truth, and not to shrink from the cold frogs and hot toads in it?

Or is it this: to love those who despise us, and to offer your hand to the ghost when it comes to frighten us?

The load-bearing spirit takes all these heaviest things upon itself. And just as the camel, once loaded, hurries into the desert, so the spirit hurries into its own desert.

But in the loneliest desert the second transformation takes place. Here the spirit becomes a lion. It wants to seize freedom for itself and to be master in its own desert.

Here it seeks out its last master. It will become the enemy of that master and of its last God. It will fight the great dragon for victory.

What is the great dragon that the spirit no longer wishes to call master and God? The great dragon's name is "Thou shalt." But the spirit of the lion says, "I will."

"Thou shalt" lies in its path, sparkling with gold, a beast covered in scales. And on every scale, in gold, glitters "Thou shalt!"

The values of a thousand years glitter on those scales. And so the mightiest of all dragons speaks: "All the values of things glitter on me.

"All values have already been created, and I stand for every value that has been created. Truly, there shall be no more 'I will.'" So speaks the dragon.

My brothers, why does the spirit need the lion? Why is the beast of burden not enough, the one that renounces and reveres?

To create new values is something even the lion cannot yet do. But to win for itself the freedom for new creating is within the lion's power.

To win freedom for itself, and to say a sacred No even to duty: for that, my brothers, the lion is needed.

To claim the right to new values is the most fearsome claim a load-bearing, reverent spirit can make. Truly, to such a spirit it feels like seizing prey, the work of a beast of prey.

Once it loved "Thou shalt" as its holiest thing. Now it is forced to find illusion and caprice even in the holiest things, so that it can tear its freedom away from what it loved. The lion is needed for this seizure.

But tell me, my brothers, what can the child do that even the lion could not? Why must the preying lion still become a child?

The child is innocence and forgetting, a new beginning, a game, a wheel that rolls by itself, a first motion, a sacred Yes.

Yes, my brothers, the game of creating requires a sacred Yes to life. Now the spirit wills its own will. The one who was cast out of the world now wins his own world.

Three transformations of the spirit I have named for you: how the spirit became a camel, the camel a lion, and the lion at last a child.

Thus spoke Zarathustra. And at that time he was staying in the town called The Pied Cow.

## II. THE ACADEMIC CHAIRS OF VIRTUE.

People recommended to Zarathustra a wise man who was said to speak well about sleep and virtue. He was greatly honored and well paid for it, and all the young men sat before his lecturer's chair.

Zarathustra went to him and sat among the young men before his chair. And this is what the wise man said:

Respect and modesty in the presence of sleep! That comes first! And keep away from everyone who sleeps badly and lies awake at night!

Even the thief is modest in the presence of sleep: he always creeps softly through the night. But the night watchman is immodest; he carries his horn without any shame.

Sleeping is no small art. To manage it, you must stay awake all day.

Ten times a day you must overcome yourself. That produces a healthy weariness and works on the soul like poppy, the old sleeping draught.

Ten times you must make peace with yourself again. Overcoming yourself leaves bitterness behind, and those who have not made peace sleep badly.

Ten truths you must find during the day. Otherwise you will go looking for truth at night, and your soul will have stayed hungry.

Ten times a day you must laugh and be cheerful. Otherwise your stomach, that father of misery, will disturb you in the night.

Few people know it, but you need all the virtues in order to sleep well. Shall I bear false witness? Shall I commit adultery?

Shall I covet my neighbor's maidservant? None of that would go well with good sleep.

And even if you have all the virtues, one thing more is needed: you must send the virtues themselves to sleep at the right time.

Otherwise they will quarrel with one another, those good little women! And they will quarrel over you, you unhappy one!

Peace with God and with your neighbor: that is what good sleep requires. And peace also with your neighbor's devil! Otherwise it will haunt you in the night.

Honor and obedience to the government, even to a crooked government! That is what good sleep requires. Is it my fault if power likes to walk on crooked legs?

Whoever leads his sheep to the greenest pasture will always be the best shepherd for me. That is what agrees with good sleep.

I do not want many honors or great treasures; they only stir up bile. But it is hard to sleep well without a good name and a little treasure.

I would rather have a small company than a bad one. But they must come and go at the right time. That is what agrees with good sleep.

The poor in spirit please me too: they encourage sleep. Blessed are they, especially if you always give in to them.

So passes the day for the virtuous. When night comes, I take good care not to call for sleep. Sleep does not like to be called, sleep, the master of the virtues!

Instead I think over what I have done and thought during the day. Chewing it over, as patient as a cow, I ask myself: What were your ten overcomings?

And what were the ten reconciliations, the ten truths, and the ten laughs that my heart enjoyed?

As I ponder this, rocked by forty thoughts, it suddenly overtakes me: sleep, the uncalled, the master of the virtues.

Sleep taps on my eye, and my eye grows heavy. Sleep touches my mouth, and my mouth stays open.

Truly, it comes to me on soft soles, the dearest of thieves, and steals my thoughts away. Then I stand there stupid, like this lecturer's chair.

But I do not stand much longer. I am already lying down.

When Zarathustra heard the wise man speak like this, he laughed in his heart, for a light had dawned on him. And he said to his heart:

This wise man with his forty thoughts seems a fool to me. But I believe he knows very well how to sleep.

Lucky even is anyone who lives near this wise man! Sleep like his is contagious, contagious even through a thick wall.

There is a kind of magic even in his lecturer's chair. And it was not for nothing that the young men sat before this preacher of virtue.

His wisdom is to stay awake in order to sleep well. And truly, if life had no meaning and I had to choose some nonsense, this would be the most desirable nonsense for me too.

Now I understand what people used to seek above all else when they went looking for teachers of virtue. They were looking for good sleep, and for virtues like poppy heads to bring it on!

For all those celebrated sages in their academic chairs, wisdom meant sleep without dreams. They knew of no higher meaning in life.

Even today, of course, there are still some like this preacher of virtue, and not always as honest as he is. But their time is over. They will not stand much longer. They are already lying down.

Blessed are those drowsy ones, for they shall soon nod off.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## III. BACKWORLDSMEN.

Once, I, Zarathustra, also cast my imagination beyond humanity, like all the backworldsmen, those who dream of a world behind this one. At that time the world seemed to me the work of a suffering and tormented God.

At that time the world seemed to me a God's dream and a God's poem. It seemed colored mist before the eyes of a divinely dissatisfied being.

Good and evil, joy and sorrow, I and you: all these seemed to me colored mist before the eyes of a creator. The creator wanted to look away from himself, and so he created the world.

For the sufferer, it is an intoxicating joy to look away from his suffering and forget himself. Intoxicating joy and self-forgetting: that is what the world once seemed to me.

This world, eternally imperfect, the image of an eternal contradiction and an imperfect image at that, an intoxicating joy to its imperfect creator: that is how the world once seemed to me.

So once I too cast my imagination beyond humanity, like all backworldsmen. Beyond humanity? Really?

Ah, my brothers, the God I created was human work and human madness, like all gods!

He was a man, and only a poor fragment of a man and of a self. That ghost came to me out of my own ashes and my own fire. And truly, it did not come to me from the beyond!

What happened, my brothers? I overcame myself, the sufferer. I carried my own ashes up the mountain and made a brighter flame for myself. And look! Then the ghost withdrew from me!

Now that I am recovering, it would be suffering and torment for me to believe in such ghosts. It would be suffering for me now, and humiliation. This is what I say to the backworldsmen.

It was suffering and helplessness that created all backworlds, together with that brief madness of happiness that only the greatest sufferer experiences.

Weariness created all gods and backworlds: a weariness that wants to reach the ultimate in a single leap, a fatal leap. It is a poor, ignorant weariness that no longer even wants to will.

Believe me, my brothers! It was the body that despaired of the body. With the fingers of a deluded spirit, it groped at the final walls.

Believe me, my brothers! It was the body that despaired of the earth. It heard the belly of existence speaking to it.

And then it tried to break through the final walls with its head, and not only with its head, into "the other world."

But that "other world" is well hidden from human beings. It is a dehumanized, inhuman world, a heavenly nothing. And the belly of existence does not speak to human beings except as a human being.

Truly, it is difficult to prove any being and hard to make it speak. Tell me, brothers, is not the strangest of all things the best proven?

Yes, this self, with all its contradiction and confusion, speaks most honestly of its own being. This self creates, wills, and assigns value; it is the measure and value of things.

And this most honest form of existence, the self, speaks of the body. It still requires the body, even when it daydreams and raves and flutters about with broken wings.

The self keeps learning to speak more honestly. And the more it learns, the more titles and honors it finds for the body and the earth.

My self taught me a new pride, and I teach it to others: no longer to bury your head in the sand of heavenly things, but to carry it freely, an earthly head that gives meaning to the earth!

I teach people a new will: to choose the path that humanity has followed blindly, and to affirm it, and no longer to sneak off it like the sick and dying!

It was the sick and dying who despised the body and the earth. They invented the heavenly world and the redeeming drops of blood. But even those sweet, sad poisons they borrowed from the body and the earth!

They wanted to escape their misery, and the stars were too far away. So they sighed: "If only there were heavenly paths by which we could slip into another existence, and into happiness!" Then they invented their side paths and their drinks of blood!

These ungrateful ones imagined they had been carried away beyond the reach of their bodies and of this earth. But to what did they owe the convulsion and rapture of being carried away? To their bodies and to this earth.

Zarathustra is gentle with the sickly. Truly, he is not angered by their ways of consoling themselves or by their ingratitude. May they recover and overcome, and create higher bodies for themselves!

Nor is Zarathustra angry with someone recovering who looks tenderly on his old delusions, and at midnight creeps around the grave of his God. But sickness and a sick body remain even in his tears.

There have always been many sickly people among those who dream and pine for God. They fiercely hate those who see clearly, and they hate the newest of the virtues, which is honesty.

They always look back toward the dark ages. Then, of course, delusion and faith were something different. Then the raving of reason was considered likeness to God, and doubt was a sin.

I know those godlike people all too well. They insist on being believed, and on doubt being a sin. I also know all too well what they themselves believe in most.

Truly, it is not backworlds and redeeming drops of blood. They too believe most of all in the body, and for them their own body is the thing-in-itself.

But the body is a sickly thing to them, and they would gladly get out of their own skin. That is why they listen to the preachers of death, and why they themselves preach backworlds.

Listen instead, my brothers, to the voice of the healthy body. It is a more upright and purer voice.

The healthy body, perfect and squarely built, speaks more uprightly and more purely. And it speaks of the meaning of the earth.—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## IV. THE DESPISERS OF THE BODY.

I will speak my word to the despisers of the body. I do not want them to learn differently or to teach differently. I only want them to say farewell to their own bodies, and so fall silent.

"I am body and soul," says the child. And why should one not speak like children?

But the one who has awakened, the one who knows, says: "I am body entirely, and nothing besides. Soul is only the name for something in the body."

The body is a great intelligence, a multiplicity with a single sense, a war and a peace, a flock and a shepherd.

Your little intelligence, my brother, which you call "spirit," is also an instrument of your body. It is a little instrument and plaything of your great intelligence.

"Ego," you say, and you are proud of that word. But the greater thing, which you refuse to believe in, is your body with its great intelligence. It does not say "ego"; it acts as one.

What the senses feel and what the spirit perceives never has its end in itself. Yet sense and spirit would like to persuade you that they are the end of all things. That is how vain they are.

Sense and spirit are instruments and playthings. Behind them there is still the Self. The Self searches with the eyes of the senses, and it also listens with the ears of the spirit.

The Self is always listening and searching. It compares, masters, conquers, and destroys. It rules, and it is also the ruler of the ego.

Behind your thoughts and feelings, my brother, stands a mighty ruler, an unknown sage. It is called the Self. It lives in your body; it is your body.

There is more intelligence in your body than in your best wisdom. And who, then, knows why your body needs precisely your best wisdom?

Your Self laughs at your ego and its proud leaps. "What are these leaps and flights of thought to me?" it says to itself. "A detour toward my purpose. I am the string by which the ego is led, and the one who prompts its ideas."

The Self says to the ego: "Feel pain!" Then the ego suffers, and thinks about how to put an end to its suffering. That is exactly the purpose it IS MEANT to think for.

The Self says to the ego: "Feel pleasure!" Then the ego rejoices, and thinks about how it might rejoice more often. That, too, is exactly the purpose it IS MEANT to think for.

I will speak a word to the despisers of the body. Their contempt comes from their esteem. What was it that created esteeming and despising, worth and will?

The creating Self created esteeming and despising for itself. It created joy and sorrow for itself. The creating body created spirit for itself, as a hand for its will.

Even in your folly and your contempt, each of you serves your Self, you despisers of the body. I tell you: your Self itself wants to die, and is turning away from life.

Your Self can no longer do what it wants most of all: to create beyond itself. That is what it wants most; that is its whole passion.

But now it is too late for that. And so your Self wants to perish, you despisers of the body.

Your Self wants to perish, and that is why you have become despisers of the body. For you can no longer create beyond yourselves.

And that is why you are now angry at life and at the earth. There is an unconscious envy in the sidelong glance of your contempt.

I do not go your way, you despisers of the body! You are no bridges for me to the Superman!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## V. JOYS AND PASSIONS.

My brother, when you have a virtue, and it is your own virtue, you share it with no one.

Of course, you would like to call it by name and pet it. You would like to tug its ears and play with it.

And look! Then you share its name with the people, and with your virtue you have become one of the people and the herd!

It would be better for you to say: "What torments and delights my soul, and is also the hunger in my belly, is unspeakable and nameless."

Let your virtue be too lofty for the familiarity of names. And if you must speak of it, do not be ashamed to stammer about it.

So speak and stammer: "That is MY good. That is what I love. It pleases me entirely this way, and only in this way do I want the good.

"I do not want it as the law of a God. I do not want it as a human law or a human necessity. It is not meant to be a signpost for me toward worlds above the earth and toward paradises.

"It is an earthly virtue that I love. There is little prudence in it, and the least of everyday wisdom.

"But that bird built its nest beside me. That is why I love and cherish it. Now it sits beside me on its golden eggs."

This is how you should stammer and praise your virtue.

Once you had passions and called them evil. But now you have only your virtues, and they grew out of your passions.

You planted your highest goal in the heart of those passions. Then they became your virtues and your joys.

And even if you came from the race of the hot-tempered, or of the lustful, or of the fanatical, or of the vengeful,

in the end all your passions became virtues, and all your devils became angels.

Once you had wild dogs in your cellar. But in the end they turned into birds and lovely singers.

Out of your poisons you brewed a healing balm for yourself. You milked your cow, affliction, and now you drink the sweet milk of her udder.

And nothing evil grows in you any longer, unless it is the evil that grows out of the conflict among your virtues.

My brother, if you are fortunate, you will have one virtue and no more. Then you will cross the bridge more easily.

It is a distinction to have many virtues, but a hard fate. Many have gone into the wilderness and killed themselves, because they were weary of being the battle and the battlefield of virtues.

My brother, are war and battle evil? Yet this evil is necessary. The envy, the distrust, and the slander among the virtues are necessary.

Look how each of your virtues craves the highest place. Each wants your whole spirit to be ITS herald. Each wants your whole strength, in wrath, in hatred, and in love.

Every virtue is jealous of the others, and jealousy is a dreadful thing. Even virtues can perish through jealousy.

Whoever is surrounded by the flame of jealousy finally turns the poisoned sting against himself, like the scorpion.

Ah, my brother, have you never seen a virtue slander and stab itself?

Man is something that must be surpassed. And therefore you shall love your virtues, for you will perish by them.—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## VI. THE PALE CRIMINAL.

You judges and sacrificers, you do not mean to kill until the animal has bowed its head? Look! The pale criminal has bowed his head. Out of his eye speaks the great contempt.

"My ego is something to be surpassed. My ego is, to me, the great contempt for man." This is what speaks out of that eye.

When he judged himself, that was his highest moment. Do not let the exalted one sink back into his lowly state!

For someone who suffers from himself in this way, there is no salvation except a quick death.

Your killing, you judges, should be pity and not revenge. And as you kill, see to it that you yourselves justify life!

It is not enough to be reconciled with the one you kill. Let your sorrow be love for the Superman. In that way you will justify your own continuing to live!

You should say "enemy," but not "villain." You should say "invalid," but not "wretch." You should say "fool," but not "sinner."

And you, red judge: if you said aloud everything you have already done in thought, everyone would cry out, "Away with this filth and this poisonous snake!"

But the thought is one thing, the deed is another, and the image of the deed is another still. The wheel of cause and effect does not roll between them.

An image made this pale man pale. He was equal to his deed when he did it. But once it was done, he could not bear the image of it.

From then on he always saw himself as the doer of one deed. I call this madness: in him the exception turned itself into the rule.

A hen held with its beak to a chalk line will stare at it as if spellbound. In the same way, the blow he struck cast a spell on his weak reason. I call this madness AFTER the deed.

Listen, you judges! There is another madness as well, and it comes BEFORE the deed. Ah, you have not gone deep enough into this soul!

The red judge says: "Why did this criminal commit murder? He meant to rob." But I tell you, his soul wanted blood, not loot. He thirsted for the happiness of the knife!

But his weak reason did not understand this madness, and it talked him round. "What does blood matter?" it said. "Don't you at least want to take some loot while you are at it? Or take revenge?"

And he listened to his weak reason. Its words lay on him like lead, and so he robbed when he murdered. He did not want to be ashamed of his madness.

And now the lead of his guilt lies on him again, and once again his weak reason is so numb, so paralyzed, and so dull.

If only he could shake his head, his burden would roll off. But who will shake that head?

What is this man? A heap of diseases that reach out into the world through the spirit, where they want to catch their prey.

What is this man? A tangle of wild snakes that are seldom at peace with one another. So they go out separately and look for prey in the world.

Look at that poor body! What it suffered and craved, the poor soul interpreted to itself. It took it to be a lust for murder and a greed for the happiness of the knife.

Whoever falls sick now is overtaken by the evil that counts as evil now: he seeks to cause pain with whatever causes him pain. But there have been other ages, and another evil and another good.

Once, doubt was evil, and so was the will to be a Self. Then the sick man became a heretic or a sorcerer. As heretic or sorcerer he suffered, and he sought to make others suffer.

But this will not enter your ears. It hurts your good people, you tell me. But what do your good people matter to me!

Much about your good people disgusts me, and truly, it is not their evil. I wish they had a madness through which they would perish, like this pale criminal!

Truly, I wish their madness were called truth, or loyalty, or justice! But they have their virtue in order to live long, and in miserable self-satisfaction.

I am a railing beside the torrent: whoever can grasp me, let him grasp me! But I am not your crutch.—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## VII. READING AND WRITING.

Of all that is written, I love only what someone has written with his own blood. Write with blood, and you will find that blood is spirit.

It is not easy to understand someone else's blood. I hate those who read out of idleness.

Whoever knows the reader does nothing more for the reader. Another century of readers, and spirit itself will stink.

Allowing everyone to learn to read ruins, in the long run, not only writing but thinking as well.

Once spirit was God. Then it became man. And now it is even becoming the mob.

Whoever writes in blood and in proverbs does not want to be read, but to be learned by heart.

In the mountains the shortest way is from peak to peak, but for that route you must have long legs. Proverbs should be peaks, and those they are spoken to should be great and tall.

The air thin and pure, danger near, and the spirit full of a joyful malice: these things go well together.

I want goblins around me, for I am brave. The courage that scares away ghosts creates goblins for itself, because it wants to laugh.

I no longer feel as you do. This very cloud that I see beneath me, this blackness and heaviness that I laugh at, is your thundercloud.

You look up when you long to be lifted high. I look down, because I have been lifted high.

Which of you can laugh and be lifted high at the same time?

Whoever climbs the highest mountains laughs at all tragic plays and all tragic realities.

Brave, unconcerned, scornful, forceful: that is how wisdom wants us. She is a woman, and she always loves only a warrior.

You tell me, "Life is hard to bear." But why else would you have your pride in the morning and your resignation in the evening?

Life is hard to bear, but do not act so delicate! We are all fine, sturdy pack-asses, male and female alike.

What do we have in common with the rosebud, which trembles because a drop of dew has settled on it?

It is true that we love life. But we love it not because we are used to living, but because we are used to loving.

There is always some madness in love. But there is also always some reason in madness.

And to me as well, who love life, it seems that butterflies and soap bubbles, and whatever among us is like them, enjoy the most happiness.

To see these light, foolish, pretty, lively little spirits flutter about: that moves Zarathustra to tears and to songs.

I would believe only in a God who knew how to dance.

And when I saw my devil, I found him serious, thorough, deep, and solemn. He was the spirit of gravity, and through him all things fall.

Not with anger but with laughter do we kill. Come, let us kill the spirit of gravity!

I learned to walk; since then I have let myself run. I learned to fly; since then I need no one to push me before I can move from where I stand.

Now I am light, now I fly; now I see myself beneath myself. Now a God dances within me.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## VIII. THE TREE ON THE HILL.

Zarathustra had noticed that a certain young man was avoiding him. One evening he was walking alone over the hills around the town called "The Pied Cow." There he found the young man sitting against a tree, gazing wearily into the valley.

Zarathustra took hold of the tree beside which the young man sat, and spoke:

"If I wanted to shake this tree with my hands, I could not do it.

But the wind, which we cannot see, torments it and bends it wherever it pleases. We are bent and tormented most painfully by invisible hands."

At this the young man stood up, disconcerted, and said: "I hear Zarathustra, and I was just thinking of him!" Zarathustra answered:

"Why does that frighten you? But it is the same with human beings as with this tree.

The more a person tries to rise toward the heights and the light, the more strongly his roots strive earthward, downward, into the dark and the deep, into evil."

"Yes, into evil!" cried the young man. "How is it possible that you have discovered my soul?"

Zarathustra smiled and said: "Some souls will never be discovered unless one first invents them."

"Yes, into evil!" the young man cried again.

"You have spoken the truth, Zarathustra. Ever since I began trying to climb to the heights, I no longer trust myself, and no one trusts me anymore. How does that happen?

I change too quickly: my today refutes my yesterday. When I climb, I often skip steps, and none of the steps forgives me for it.

When I am up high, I always find myself alone. No one speaks to me; the frost of solitude makes me shiver. What am I looking for on the heights?

My contempt and my longing grow together. The higher I climb, the more I despise the one who climbs. What is he looking for on the heights?

How ashamed I am of my climbing and stumbling! How I mock my own violent panting! How I hate the one who flies! How tired I am on the heights!"

Here the young man fell silent. Zarathustra looked at the tree beside which they stood, and spoke:

"This tree stands alone here in the hills. It has grown up high above people and animals.

And if it wanted to speak, it would find no one who could understand it, so high has it grown.

Now it waits and waits. What is it waiting for? It lives too close to the seat of the clouds. Perhaps it is waiting for the first lightning?"

When Zarathustra had said this, the young man cried out with violent gestures: "Yes, Zarathustra, you speak the truth. I longed for my own destruction when I wanted to be on the heights, and you are the lightning I was waiting for!

Look, what have I been since you appeared among us? It is my envy of you that has destroyed me!"

So spoke the young man, and he wept bitterly. But Zarathustra put his arm around him and led him away.

When they had walked together for a while, Zarathustra began to speak:

It tears at my heart. Your eyes tell me all your danger more clearly than your words can.

You are not yet free; you are still *seeking* freedom. Your seeking has made you sleepless and overly wakeful.

You want to be on the open heights; your soul thirsts for the stars. But your bad impulses thirst for freedom too.

Your wild dogs want to be let loose. They bark with joy in their cellar when your spirit tries to open every prison door.

To me you still seem a prisoner who is plotting his own freedom. Ah, the souls of such prisoners grow sharp, but also deceitful and wicked.

Even one whose spirit has been set free must still purify himself. Much of the prison and its mold still remains in him. His eye has yet to become pure.

Yes, I know your danger. But by my love and hope I implore you: do not throw away your love and hope!

You still feel yourself to be noble, and others still feel that you are noble, even though they resent you and cast evil looks at you. Know this: a noble person stands in everyone's way.

A noble person stands in the way of the good as well. Even when they call him a good man, they mean to push him aside by it.

The noble man wants to create something new, and a new virtue. The good man wants the old, and wants the old preserved.

But the noble man's danger is not that he will become a good man. His danger is that he will become a blusterer, a scoffer, or a destroyer.

Ah, I have known noble people who lost their highest hope. And then they belittled all high hopes.

Then they lived shamelessly for passing pleasures, and hardly had any aim beyond the day.

"Spirit is also sensual pleasure," they said. Then the wings of their spirit broke. Now it crawls about and defiles whatever it gnaws.

Once they thought of becoming heroes; now they are pleasure-seekers. To them the hero is a trouble and a terror.

But by my love and hope I implore you: do not throw away the hero in your soul! Keep your highest hope holy!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## IX. THE PREACHERS OF DEATH.

There are preachers of death, and the earth is full of people to whom turning away from life must be preached.

The earth is full of the superfluous; life is spoiled by the many-too-many. May they be lured out of this life by "eternal life"!

The preachers of death are called "the yellow ones," or "the black ones." But I will show them to you in other colors as well.

There are the terrible ones, who carry a beast of prey within themselves and have no choice except lusts or tearing themselves apart. And even their lusts are a way of tearing themselves apart.

They have not yet become human, those terrible ones. Let them preach turning away from life, and pass away themselves!

There are those whose spirit is wasting with consumption. Hardly are they born before they begin to die, and they long for doctrines of weariness and renunciation.

They would gladly be dead, and we should approve of their wish! Let us take care not to wake these dead, and not to damage these living coffins!

They meet a sick person, or an old man, or a corpse, and at once they say: "Life is refuted!"

But only they are refuted, they and their eye, which sees only one side of existence.

Wrapped in thick melancholy, and eager for the small accidents that bring death: so they wait, and clench their teeth.

Or else they grab at sweets, and mock their own childishness while doing it. They cling to their straw of life, and mock themselves for still clinging to it.

Their wisdom says: "Whoever stays alive is a fool, and so far we are such fools! And that is the most foolish thing in life!"

"Life is only suffering," say others, and they are not lying. Then see to it that *you* stop! See to it that the life which is only suffering comes to an end!

And let this be the teaching of your virtue: "You shall kill yourself! You shall steal away from yourself!"

"Lust is sin," say some who preach death. "Let us go apart and have no children!"

"Giving birth is a burden," say others. "Why keep giving birth? One bears only the unfortunate!" They too are preachers of death.

"Pity is necessary," says a third group. "Take what I have! Take what I am! Then life will bind me all the less!"

If they were thoroughly pitying, they would make their neighbors sick of life. To be wicked: that would be their true goodness.

But they want to be rid of life. What do they care if they bind others still tighter with their chains and gifts!

And you too, for whom life is hard labor and restlessness: are you not very tired of life? Are you not very ripe for the sermon of death?

All of you who love hard labor and whatever is fast, new, and strange: you put up with yourselves badly. Your industriousness is flight, and a will to forget yourselves.

If you believed more in life, you would throw yourselves less into the passing moment. But you do not have enough in you for waiting, not even for idleness!

Everywhere the voices of those who preach death resound, and the earth is full of those to whom death must be preached.

Or "eternal life." It is all the same to me, as long as they pass away quickly!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## X. WAR AND WARRIORS.

We do not want to be spared by our best enemies, nor by those we love from the bottom of our hearts. So let me tell you the truth!

My brothers in war! I love you from the bottom of my heart. I am, and always was, your equal. And I am also your best enemy. So let me tell you the truth!

I know the hatred and envy in your hearts. You are not great enough to be unacquainted with hatred and envy. Then be great enough not to be ashamed of them!

And if you cannot be saints of knowledge, then I beg you, at least be its warriors. Warriors are the companions and forerunners of such sainthood.

I see many soldiers; if only I could see many warriors! What they wear is called a "uniform." May what they hide beneath it not be uniform too!

You shall be those whose eyes are always looking for an enemy, for *your* enemy. And in some of you there is hatred at first sight.

You shall seek your enemy and wage your war, and for the sake of your thoughts! And if your thoughts are defeated, your honesty shall still shout in triumph over it!

You shall love peace as a means to new wars, and the short peace more than the long.

I do not advise you to work, but to fight. I do not advise you to make peace, but to win. Let your work be a fight, and your peace a victory!

One can be silent and sit peacefully only when one has bow and arrow; otherwise one chatters and quarrels. Let your peace be a victory!

You say it is the good cause that makes even war holy? I tell you: it is the good war that makes every cause holy.

War and courage have done more great things than charity. Not your sympathy but your bravery has so far saved the victims.

"What is good?" you ask. To be brave is good. Let the little girls say: "Being good is whatever is pretty and touching at the same time."

They call you heartless, but your heart is true, and I love the bashfulness of your goodwill. You are ashamed of your flood tide, and others are ashamed of their ebb.

Are you ugly? Well then, my brothers, wrap the sublime around you, the cloak of the ugly!

And when your soul grows great, it grows arrogant, and in your loftiness there is wickedness. I know you.

In wickedness the arrogant person and the weakling meet. But they misunderstand each other. I know you.

You shall have only enemies to be hated, not enemies to be despised. You must be proud of your enemies; then your enemies' successes are your successes too.

Resistance: that is the slave's mark of distinction. Let your distinction be obedience! Let even your commanding be an obeying!

To the good warrior, "you shall" sounds more pleasant than "I will." And everything you hold dear, you shall first have commanded to you.

Let your love of life be love of your highest hope, and let your highest hope be the highest thought of life!

But your highest thought you shall have commanded to you by me, and it is this: man is something that is to be surpassed.

So live your life of obedience and war! What does long life matter? What warrior wants to be spared?

I do not spare you; I love you from the bottom of my heart, my brothers in war!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XI. THE NEW IDOL.

Somewhere there are still peoples and herds, but not among us, my brothers. Here there are states.

A state? What is that? Well, open your ears to me now, for now I will speak my word about the death of peoples.

The state is the name of the coldest of all cold monsters. It lies coldly, too, and this lie crawls out of its mouth: "I, the state, am the people."

That is a lie! It was creators who created peoples and hung a faith and a love over them. In this way they served life.

It is destroyers who set traps for the many and call it the state. They hang a sword and a hundred cravings over them.

Where there is still a people, the state is not understood. It is hated as the evil eye, and as a sin against laws and customs.

I give you this sign: every people speaks its own language of good and evil, which its neighbor does not understand. It has invented this language for itself in its laws and customs.

But the state lies in every language of good and evil. Whatever it says, it lies; and whatever it has, it has stolen.

Everything in it is false. It bites with stolen teeth, this biter. Even its bowels are false.

Confusion of the language of good and evil: I give you this as the sign of the state. Truly, this sign points to the will to death! Truly, it beckons to the preachers of death!

Far too many are born: the state was invented for the superfluous!

Just see how it lures them to itself, the many-too-many! How it swallows them, and chews them, and chews them again!

"On earth there is nothing greater than I: I am the ordering finger of God." So the monster roars. And it is not only the long-eared and the short-sighted who fall to their knees!

Ah, even into your ears, you great souls, it whispers its gloomy lies! Ah, it seeks out the rich hearts that gladly spend themselves!

Yes, it seeks you out too, you conquerors of the old God! You grew weary of the struggle, and now your weariness serves the new idol!

The new idol would like to set up heroes and honorable people around itself! It gladly basks in the sunshine of good consciences, the cold monster!

It will give YOU everything, if YOU worship it, this new idol. In this way it buys the luster of your virtue and the glance of your proud eyes.

Through you it means to lure the many-too-many! Yes, a hellish trick has been devised here: a horse of death, jingling with the trappings of divine honors!

Yes, a dying for the many has been devised here, and it glorifies itself as life. Truly, it is a heartfelt service to all preachers of death!

I call it the state, where all drink poison, the good and the bad alike. It is the state, where all lose themselves, the good and the bad alike. It is the state, where the slow suicide of everyone is called "life."

Just look at these superfluous people! They steal the works of inventors and the treasures of the wise. They call their theft "culture," and everything turns into sickness and trouble for them!

Just look at these superfluous people! They are always sick. They vomit up their bile and call it a newspaper. They devour one another and cannot even digest themselves.

Just look at these superfluous people! They acquire wealth and become poorer by it. They seek power, and above all the lever of power, which is plenty of money. These powerless ones!

See them climb, these nimble apes! They clamber over one another, and so they scuffle their way down into the mud and the abyss.

They all strive toward the throne. That is their madness, as if happiness sat on the throne! Often filth sits on the throne, and often the throne sits on filth.

They all seem to me madmen, climbing apes, and far too eager. Their idol, the cold monster, smells foul to me. They all smell foul to me, these idol-worshippers.

My brothers, will you suffocate in the fumes of their mouths and appetites? Better to break the windows and leap out into the open air!

Get away from the foul smell! Withdraw from the idol-worship of the superfluous!

Get away from the foul smell! Withdraw from the steam of these human sacrifices!

The earth still stands open for great souls. Many places are still empty for those who live alone or in pairs. Around those places drifts the scent of quiet seas.

A free life still stands open for great souls. Truly, whoever possesses little is that much less possessed. Blessed be modest poverty!

Where the state ends, only there begins the human being who is not superfluous. There begins the song of the necessary ones, the single and irreplaceable melody.

Where the state ENDS, look there, my brothers! Do you not see it, the rainbow and the bridges of the Superman?

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XII. THE FLIES IN THE MARKET-PLACE.

Flee, my friend, into your solitude! I see you deafened by the noise of the great men and stung all over by the stings of the little ones.

Forest and rock know very well how to be silent with you. Be once more like the tree you love, the broad-branched one. Silent and attentive, it leans out over the sea.

Where solitude ends, the market-place begins. And where the market-place begins, there also begins the noise of the great actors and the buzzing of the poisonous flies.

In the world, even the best things are worth nothing without someone to present them. The people call these presenters great men.

The people understand little of what is great, that is, of the creative force. But they have a taste for everyone who presents and acts out great things.

The world revolves around the inventors of new values. It revolves around them invisibly. But the people and the glory revolve around the actors. That is the way of things.

The actor has spirit, but little conscience of the spirit. He always believes in whatever lets him make others believe most strongly. He believes in HIMSELF!

Tomorrow he has a new belief, and the day after, a newer one still. Like the people, he has quick perceptions and changeable moods.

To him, overturning something means proving it. Driving people mad means convincing them. And he counts blood as the best of all arguments.

A truth that slips only into sensitive ears he calls a lie and a trifle. Truly, he believes only in gods that make a great noise in the world!

The market-place is full of clattering clowns, and the people boast of their great men! For them, these are the masters of the hour.

But the hour presses them, so they press you. They want a Yes or a No from you as well. Alas, would you set your chair between For and Against?

Do not envy those absolute, impatient ones, you lover of truth! Truth has never yet clung to the arm of someone who deals in absolutes.

Because of those abrupt people, go back to your place of safety. Only in the market-place is a person ambushed with "Yes?" or "No?"

All deep wells are slow in what they come to know. They must wait a long time before they learn WHAT has fallen into their depths.

Everything great happens away from the market-place and from fame. The inventors of new values have always lived away from the market-place and from fame.

Flee, my friend, into your solitude. I see you stung all over by the poisonous flies. Flee to where a rough, strong wind blows!

Flee into your solitude! You have lived too close to the small and the pitiful. Flee from their invisible revenge! Toward you they feel nothing but revenge.

Raise your arm against them no longer! They are countless, and it is not your fate to be a flyswatter.

The small and pitiful are countless. Many a proud building has been ruined by raindrops and weeds.

You are not stone, but the many drops have already hollowed you out. You will yet crack and burst from the many drops.

I see you exhausted by poisonous flies. I see you bleeding and torn in a hundred places. And your pride will not even scold them.

They want your blood in all innocence. Their bloodless souls crave blood, and so they sting in all innocence.

But you, deep one, suffer too deeply even from small wounds. Before you had healed, the same poisonous worm was crawling over your hand.

You are too proud to kill these sweet-toothed creatures. But take care that it does not become your fate to bear all their poisonous injustice!

They also buzz around you with their praise. Their praise is pushiness. They want to be close to your skin and your blood.

They flatter you as one flatters a god or a devil. They whimper before you as before a god or a devil. What does it amount to? They are flatterers and whimperers, and nothing more.

Often they also show themselves to you as charming. But that has always been the cleverness of cowards. Yes, cowards are clever!

With their narrow souls they think about you a great deal, and you are always under suspicion. Whatever is thought about a great deal eventually comes to be thought suspicious.

They punish you for all your virtues. In their hearts they forgive you only for your mistakes.

Because you are gentle and upright, you say, "They are not to blame for their small existence." But their narrow souls think, "All great existence is to blame."

Even when you are gentle with them, they still feel despised by you. They repay your kindness with secret malice.

Your silent pride always goes against their taste. They are delighted if you are ever humble enough to be frivolous.

Whatever we recognize in a person, we also inflame in that person. So be on your guard against the small ones!

In your presence they feel small, and their baseness smolders and glows against you in invisible revenge.

Did you not see how often they fell silent when you approached them? Did you not see how their strength left them like the smoke of a dying fire?

Yes, my friend, you are the bad conscience of your neighbors, because they are unworthy of you. That is why they hate you and would like to suck your blood.

Your neighbors will always be poisonous flies. What is great in you must itself make them more poisonous, and always more fly-like.

Flee, my friend, into your solitude, to where a rough, strong wind blows. It is not your fate to be a flyswatter.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XIII. CHASTITY.

I love the forest. It is bad to live in cities, because too many there are full of lust.

Is it not better to fall into the hands of a murderer than into the dreams of a lustful woman?

And just look at these men. Their eyes say it: they know nothing better on earth than to lie with a woman.

There is filth at the bottom of their souls. And alas, it is worse if their filth still has spirit in it!

If only you were perfect, at least as animals! But innocence belongs to animals.

Do I advise you to kill your instincts? I advise you to be innocent in your instincts.

Do I advise you to be chaste? For some, chastity is a virtue, but for many it is almost a vice.

These people practice restraint, certainly. But a dog's lust looks enviously out of everything they do.

This creature follows them, with all its discord, even into the heights of their virtue and into their cold spirit.

And how sweetly a dog's lust can beg for a scrap of spirit when it is denied a scrap of flesh!

You love tragedies and everything that breaks the heart? But I do not trust your dog's lust.

Your eyes are too cruel, and you look at sufferers with lust. Has your lust not simply disguised itself and taken the name of sympathy?

I also give you this parable. Not a few who meant to drive out their devil went into the swine themselves in the process.

Those who find chastity difficult should be advised against it. Otherwise it may become the road to hell, that is, to filth and lust of the soul.

Am I speaking of filthy things? That is not the worst thing I could do.

The person who seeks knowledge is reluctant to wade into a truth's waters not when the truth is filthy, but when it is shallow.

Truly, there are people who are chaste by nature. They are gentler of heart, and they laugh better and more often than you.

They laugh at chastity too, and ask, "What is chastity?

Is chastity not foolishness? But this foolishness came to us; we did not go to it.

We offered that guest shelter and our hearts. Now it lives with us. Let it stay as long as it likes!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XIV. THE FRIEND.

"One is always one too many around me," thinks the hermit. "Always one times one: in the long run, that makes two!"

I and me are always too deeply engaged in conversation. How could it be endured if there were no friend?

For the hermit, the friend is always the third person. The third person is the cork that keeps the conversation of the two from sinking into the depths.

Ah, there are too many depths for all hermits. That is why they long so much for a friend, and for a friend's height.

Our faith in others reveals where we would like to have faith in ourselves. Our longing for a friend gives us away.

Often with our love we only want to leap over envy. And often we attack and make enemies in order to hide that we can be wounded.

"At least be my enemy!" So speaks true reverence, which does not dare to ask for friendship.

If you want a friend, you must also be willing to wage war for him. And to wage war, you must be CAPABLE of being an enemy.

You should still honor the enemy in your friend. Can you go close to your friend without going over to his side?

In your friend you should have your best enemy. Your heart should be closest to him when you resist him.

Would you wear no clothes in front of your friend? Is it in your friend's honor that you show yourself to him exactly as you are? But for that he wishes you to the devil!

Whoever hides nothing of himself is shocking. That is how much reason you have to fear nakedness! Yes, if you were gods, then you could be ashamed of clothing!

You cannot dress yourself finely enough for your friend. For you should be to him an arrow and a longing for the Superman.

Have you ever seen your friend asleep, to learn what he looks like? What is your friend's face, usually? It is your own face, in a rough and imperfect mirror.

Have you ever seen your friend asleep? Were you not dismayed to see your friend looking like that? O my friend, man is something that must be surpassed.

A friend should be a master of guessing and of keeping silent. You must not want to see everything. Your dream should reveal to you what your friend does while awake.

Let your pity be a guessing: first find out whether your friend wants pity. Perhaps what he loves in you is the unmoved eye and the look of eternity.

Let your pity for your friend be hidden under a hard shell, one you would break a tooth on. In that way it will have delicacy and sweetness.

Are you pure air and solitude and bread and medicine to your friend? Many cannot loosen their own chains and yet free their friends.

Are you a slave? Then you cannot be a friend. Are you a tyrant? Then you cannot have friends.
