For far too long a slave and a tyrant have been hidden in woman. For that reason woman is not yet capable of friendship. She knows only love.

In a woman's love there is injustice and blindness toward everything she does not love. Even in a woman's conscious love there is always surprise, lightning, and night alongside the light.

Woman is not yet capable of friendship: women are still cats and birds. Or, at best, cows.

Woman is not yet capable of friendship. But tell me, you men: which of you is capable of friendship?

Oh, your poverty, you men, and the meanness of your souls! As much as you give to your friend, I will give even to my enemy, and I will not have become any poorer by it.

There is comradeship: let there be friendship!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XV. THE THOUSAND AND ONE GOALS.

Zarathustra saw many lands and many peoples, and so he discovered the good and bad of many peoples. Zarathustra found no greater power on earth than good and bad.

No people could live without first valuing. But if a people wants to preserve itself, it must not value things as its neighbor does.

Much that one people considered good, another regarded with scorn and contempt: this is what I found. Much that I found called bad in one place was adorned with purple honors in another.

One neighbor never understood the other. Each one's soul always marveled at the neighbor's delusion and wickedness.

A table of excellences hangs over every people. Look: it is the table of their triumphs. Look: it is the voice of their Will to Power.

Whatever they find hard, they consider praiseworthy. What is both indispensable and hard they call good. And what brings relief in the direst distress, the rarest and hardest thing of all, they exalt as holy.

Whatever makes them rule, conquer, and shine, to the dismay and envy of their neighbors, they regard as the highest and foremost thing. It becomes the test and the meaning of everything else.

Truly, my brother, if you only knew a people's need, its land, its sky, and its neighbor, you could guess the law of its self-overcomings. You could guess why it climbs that particular ladder toward its hope.

"You shall always be first and stand out above others. Your jealous soul shall love no one except a friend." That made the soul of a Greek thrill, and by following it he went his way to greatness.

"To speak the truth and to be skillful with bow and arrow." This seemed both dear and hard to the people from whom my name comes, the name that is both dear and hard to me.

"To honor father and mother, and to do their will from the root of the soul." Another people hung this table of overcoming over themselves, and through it they became powerful and lasting.

"To keep faith, and for the sake of faithfulness to risk honor and blood, even in evil and dangerous causes." By teaching itself this, another people mastered itself. And by mastering itself, it became pregnant and heavy with great hopes.

Truly, human beings gave themselves all their good and bad. Truly, they did not take it, they did not find it, and it did not come to them as a voice from heaven.

Humans assigned values to things only in order to preserve themselves. They created only the meaning of things, a human meaning! That is why the human being calls himself "man," that is, the one who values.

Valuing is creating: hear this, you creators! Valuation itself is the treasure and jewel of all valued things.

Only through valuation is there value. Without valuation, the nut of existence would be hollow. Hear this, you creators!

A change of values means a change in the creators. Whoever must be a creator always destroys.

The first creators were peoples, and only later individuals. Truly, the individual himself is still the most recent creation.

Peoples once hung tables of the good over themselves. Love that wanted to rule and love that wanted to obey created such tables for themselves.

Delight in the herd is older than delight in the ego. As long as the good conscience belongs to the herd, only the bad conscience says: ego.

Truly, the crafty ego, the loveless one that seeks its own advantage in the advantage of the many, is not the origin of the herd but its ruin.

It was always lovers and creators who created good and bad. The fire of love glows in the names of all the virtues, and the fire of anger as well.

Zarathustra saw many lands and many peoples. He found no greater power on earth than the creations of the lovers: "good" and "bad" they are called.

Truly, this power of praising and blaming is a monster. Tell me, my brothers, who will master it for me? Who will put a fetter on the thousand necks of this beast?

There have been a thousand goals so far, because there have been a thousand peoples. Only the fetter for the thousand necks is still missing; the one goal is missing. Humanity does not yet have a goal.

But tell me, my brothers: if humanity still lacks a goal, is it not also still lacking humanity itself?

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XVI. NEIGHBOUR-LOVE.

You crowd around your neighbor, and you have fine words for doing so. But I tell you: your love of your neighbor is your bad love of yourselves.

You flee from yourselves to your neighbor, and you would like to make a virtue of it. But I see through your "selflessness."

The You is older than the I. The You has been declared holy, but the I has not yet been. So people press close to their neighbor.

Do I advise you to love your neighbor? I would rather advise you to flee your neighbor and to love the most distant!

Higher than love of your neighbor is love of the most distant and of those still to come. Higher still than love of human beings is love of things and of phantoms.

The phantom that runs ahead of you, my brother, is more beautiful than you. Why don't you give it your flesh and your bones? But you are afraid, and you run to your neighbor.

You cannot bear your own company, and you do not love yourselves enough. So you try to seduce your neighbor into loving you, and you would like to gild yourselves with his mistake.

I wish you could not bear any of those near you, or their neighbors either. Then you would have to create your friend and his overflowing heart out of yourselves.

You call in a witness when you want to speak well of yourselves. And once you have misled him into thinking well of you, you think well of yourselves too.

It is not only the person who speaks against what he knows who lies. The person who speaks against what he does not know lies even more. This is how you speak about yourselves in company, and you deceive your neighbor along with yourselves.

So says the fool: "Associating with people spoils one's character, especially when one has none."

One person goes to his neighbor because he is looking for himself, another because he wants to lose himself. Your bad love of yourselves turns solitude into a prison for you.

The most distant are the ones who pay for your love of those near you. And whenever even five of you are together, a sixth must always die.

Nor do I love your festivals. I found too many actors there, and even the spectators often behaved like actors.

I do not teach you the neighbor, but the friend. Let the friend be the festival of the earth for you, and a foretaste of the Superman.

I teach you the friend and his overflowing heart. But you must know how to be a sponge if you want to be loved by overflowing hearts.

I teach you the friend in whom the world stands complete, a capsule holding the good. I teach the creating friend, who always has a complete world to give.

And just as the world unrolled itself for him, it rolls itself up again for him in rings: as the growth of good through evil, as the growth of purpose out of chance.

Let the future and the most distant be the motive of your today. In your friend you shall love the Superman as your motive.

My brothers, I do not advise you to love your neighbor. I advise you to love the most distant!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XVII. THE WAY OF THE CREATING ONE.

Do you want to go into solitude, my brother? Do you want to seek the way to yourself? Wait a little longer and listen to me.

"Whoever seeks may easily get lost himself. All isolation is wrong": so says the herd. And you belonged to the herd for a long time.

The voice of the herd will still echo in you. And when you say, "I no longer share a conscience with you," it will be a lament and a pain.

Notice that this same conscience produced that very pain. The last glow of that conscience still shines on your distress.

But do you want to go the way of your distress, which is the way to yourself? Then show me your right and your strength to do so!

Are you a new strength and a new right? A first movement? A wheel that rolls by itself? Can you also force stars to revolve around you?

Alas, there is so much craving for the heights! There are so many convulsions of ambition! Show me that you are not one of the cravers and the ambitious!

Alas, there are so many great thoughts that do no more than a bellows does: they puff things up and leave them emptier than before.

You call yourself free? I want to hear your ruling thought, not that you have escaped from a yoke.

Are you someone who has the RIGHT to escape a yoke? Many people threw away their last worth when they threw away their servitude.

Free from what? What does that matter to Zarathustra! But your eye should show me clearly: free FOR WHAT?

Can you give yourself your own bad and your own good, and hang your will over yourself as a law? Can you be your own judge, and the avenger of your own law?

It is terrible to be alone with the judge and avenger of one's own law. This is how a star is flung out into empty space and into the icy breath of solitude.

Today you still suffer from the many, you individual. Today you still have your courage undiminished, and your hopes.

But one day solitude will weary you. One day your pride will bend and your courage will falter. One day you will cry: "I am alone!"

One day you will no longer see your height, and you will see your lowliness all too closely. Your very sublimity will frighten you like a ghost. One day you will cry: "Everything is false!"

There are feelings that seek to kill the solitary one. If they do not succeed, then they themselves must die! But are you capable of that, of being a murderer?

Have you ever known, my brother, the word "disdain"? And the anguish of your justice in being just to those who disdain you?

You force many people to think differently about you, and they hold that heavily against you. You came close to them and yet walked past, and for that they will never forgive you.

You go beyond them. But the higher you climb, the smaller the eye of envy sees you. And the one who flies is hated most of all.

"How could you be just to me!" you must say. "I choose your injustice as the share allotted to me."

They throw injustice and filth at the solitary one. But, my brother, if you want to be a star, you must shine for them no less because of it!

And beware of the good and the just! They would gladly crucify those who invent their own virtue. They hate the solitary.

Beware also of holy simplicity! Everything that is not simple seems unholy to it. It, too, would gladly play with fire: the fire of bundled firewood heaped around the stake where condemned people were burned alive.

And beware also of the attacks of your own love! The solitary person too readily reaches out his hand to anyone he meets.

To many people you should not give your hand, but only your paw. And I want your paw to have claws as well.

But the worst enemy you can meet will always be yourself. You lie in wait for yourself in caves and forests.

Solitary one, you are going the way to yourself! And your way leads past yourself and your seven devils!

You will be a heretic to yourself, and a sorcerer, and a soothsayer, and a fool, and a doubter, and a reprobate, and a villain.

You must be ready to burn yourself in your own flame. How could you become new if you have not first become ashes!

Solitary one, you are going the way of the creator: you will create a god for yourself out of your seven devils!

Solitary one, you are going the way of the lover: you love yourself, and for that reason you despise yourself, as only lovers despise.

The lover wants to create because he despises! What does anyone know of love who has not had to despise precisely what he loved!

Go into your solitude with your love and with your creating, my brother. Only later will justice come limping after you.

Go into your solitude with my tears, my brother. I love the one who wants to create beyond himself, and so perishes.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XVIII. OLD AND YOUNG WOMEN.

"Why do you creep along so furtively in the twilight, Zarathustra? And what are you hiding so carefully under your cloak?

Is it a treasure someone has given you? Or a child born to you? Or are you going on a thief's errand yourself, you friend of the wicked?"

Truly, my brother, said Zarathustra, it is a treasure that has been given to me: it is a little truth that I am carrying.

But it is unruly, like a young child. If I do not hold its mouth shut, it screams too loudly.

As I went on my way alone today, at the hour when the sun goes down, an old woman met me, and she spoke to my soul like this:

"Zarathustra has spoken a great deal to us women too, but he has never spoken to us about woman."

And I answered her: "About woman, one should speak only to men."

"Talk to me about woman too," she said. "I am old enough to forget it right away."

So I obliged the old woman and spoke to her like this:

Everything about woman is a riddle, and everything about woman has one solution. It is called pregnancy.

For woman, man is a means; the purpose is always the child. But what is woman for man?

The true man wants two different things: danger and play. That is why he wants woman, as the most dangerous plaything.

Man should be trained for war, and woman for the recreation of the warrior. Everything else is folly.

The warrior does not like fruit that is too sweet. That is why he likes woman: even the sweetest woman is bitter.

Woman understands children better than man does, but man is more childish than woman.

In the true man a child is hidden, and it wants to play. Come, then, you women, and discover the child in man!

Let woman be a plaything, pure and fine like a precious stone, lit by the virtues of a world that has not yet come.

Let the beam of a star shine in your love! Let your hope say: "May I give birth to the Superman!"

Let there be courage in your love! With your love you should go after the one who makes you afraid.

Let your honor lie in your love! Woman understands little about honor otherwise. But let this be your honor: always to love more than you are loved, and never to come second.

Let man fear woman when she loves. Then she makes every sacrifice, and counts everything else as worthless.

Let man fear woman when she hates. For in his innermost soul man is merely wicked, but woman is mean.

Whom does woman hate most? This is what the iron said to the magnet: "I hate you most, because you attract me but are too weak to pull me to you."

The happiness of man is "I will." The happiness of woman is "He will."

"Look, now the world has become perfect!" So every woman thinks when she obeys with all her love.

Woman must obey, and find a depth for her surface. Woman's soul is surface, a restless, stormy film on shallow water.

Man's soul, however, is deep. Its current rushes through underground caverns. Woman senses its force but does not understand it.

Then the old woman answered me: "Zarathustra has said many fine things, especially for those who are young enough for them.

"It is strange! Zarathustra knows little about women, and yet he is right about them! Does this happen because with women nothing is impossible?

"And now, as thanks, accept a little truth! I am old enough for it!

"Wrap it up and hold its mouth shut, or it will scream too loudly, this little truth."

"Give me your little truth, woman!" I said. And the old woman spoke thus:

"You are going to women? Do not forget your whip!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XIX. THE BITE OF THE ADDER.

One day, because of the heat, Zarathustra had fallen asleep under a fig tree with his arms over his face. An adder came and bit him in the neck, so that Zarathustra cried out in pain.

When he had taken his arm from his face, he looked at the snake. The snake recognized Zarathustra's eyes, writhed awkwardly, and tried to get away.

"Not at all," said Zarathustra. "You have not yet received my thanks! You woke me in time, and my journey is still long."

"Your journey is short," said the adder sadly. "My poison is fatal."

Zarathustra smiled. "When did a dragon ever die of a snake's poison?" he said. "But take your poison back! You are not rich enough to give it to me." Then the adder fell on his neck again and licked his wound.

When Zarathustra once told this story to his disciples, they asked him: "And what, O Zarathustra, is the moral of your story?" Zarathustra answered them thus:

The good and the just call me the destroyer of morality. My story is immoral.

When you have an enemy, do not repay him good for evil, for that would shame him. Instead, prove that he has done you some good.

It is better to be angry than to shame anyone! And when you are cursed, I do not like it when you want to bless in return. Rather, curse a little too!

If a great injustice befalls you, then quickly commit five small ones besides. It is hideous to see someone on whom injustice weighs alone.

Did you ever know this? Shared injustice is half justice. And whoever can bear it should take the injustice on himself!

A small revenge is more humane than no revenge at all. And unless the punishment is also a right and an honor for the wrongdoer, I do not like your punishing.

It is nobler to admit you are wrong than to insist on being right, especially when you are right. Only, one must be rich enough to do so.

I do not like your cold justice. From your judges' eyes the executioner and his cold steel are always looking out.

Tell me: where do we find justice that is love with seeing eyes?

Then invent for me the love that bears not only all punishment but also all guilt!

Then invent for me the justice that acquits everyone except the judge!

Would you hear this as well? For someone who seeks to be just from the heart, even a lie becomes an act of love for humanity.

But how could I be just from the heart? How can I give everyone what is his own? Let this be enough for me: I give everyone what is mine.

Finally, my brothers, beware of wronging any hermit. How could a hermit forget! How could he repay!

A hermit is like a deep well. It is easy to throw a stone in. But if it sinks to the bottom, tell me, who will bring it out again?

Beware of injuring the hermit! But if you have done so, well then, kill him too!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XX. CHILD AND MARRIAGE.

I have a question for you alone, my brother. I cast this question into your soul like a sounding lead, the weight dropped on a line to measure depth, so that I may know how deep your soul is.

You are young, and you want a child and marriage. But I ask you: are you a man who has the RIGHT to want a child?

Are you the victorious one, the self-conqueror, the ruler of your passions, the master of your virtues? This is what I ask you.

Or does the animal speak in your wish, and need? Or loneliness? Or discord within you?

I want your victory and your freedom to long for a child. You should build living monuments to your victory and your liberation.

You should build beyond yourself. But first you must be built yourself, square and true in body and soul.

You should propagate yourself not only forward but upward! May the garden of marriage help you toward that end!

You should create a higher body, a first movement, a self-propelled wheel. You should create a creator.

Marriage is what I call the will of two people to create the one who is more than those who created it. Marriage is what I call the reverence each holds for the other as people who share such a will.

Let this be the meaning and the truth of your marriage. But what the many-too-many call marriage, those superfluous ones, ah, what shall I call that?

Ah, the poverty of soul in the pair! Ah, the filth of soul in the pair! Ah, the pitiful self-satisfaction in the pair!

They call all this marriage, and they say their marriages are made in heaven.

Well, I do not like it, that heaven of the superfluous! No, I do not like them, those animals tangled in heavenly nets!

Keep far from me, too, the God who limps over to bless what he did not join together!

Do not laugh at such marriages! What child has not had reason to weep over its parents?

This man seemed worthy and ripe for the meaning of the earth. But when I saw his wife, the earth seemed to me a home for lunatics.

Yes, I wish the earth would shake with convulsions when a saint and a goose mate with each other.

This one set out in search of truth like a hero, and in the end won himself a small, dressed-up lie. He calls it his marriage.

That one was reserved with people and chose his company carefully. But at one stroke he spoiled his company forever. He calls it his marriage.

Another looked for a maidservant with the virtues of an angel. But all at once he became a woman's maidservant, and now he would need to become an angel too.

I have found all buyers careful, and all of them have shrewd eyes. But even the shrewdest of them buys his wife sight unseen, like goods in a sack.

You call many brief follies love. And your marriage puts an end to many brief follies with one long stupidity.

Your love for woman, and woman's love for man: ah, if only it were compassion for suffering and veiled gods! But usually two animals simply land on each other.

Yet even your best love is only a rapturous image and a painful burning. It is a torch meant to light your way to higher paths.

Someday you shall love beyond yourselves! So first LEARN to love. That is why you had to drink the bitter cup of your love.

There is bitterness in the cup of even the best love. In this way it makes you long for the Superman. In this way it makes you thirst, you creator!

Thirst in the creator, an arrow and a longing for the Superman: tell me, my brother, is this your will to marriage?

Such a will and such a marriage I call holy.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXI. VOLUNTARY DEATH.

Many die too late, and some die too early. Still, the teaching sounds strange: "Die at the right time!"

Die at the right time: this is what Zarathustra teaches.

Of course, if someone never lives at the right time, how could he ever die at the right time? Better that he were never born! That is my advice to the superfluous.

But even the superfluous make a great fuss about their deaths, and even the hollowest nut wants to be cracked.

Everyone treats dying as a great matter, but death is not yet a festival. People have not yet learned how to consecrate the finest festivals.

I show you the consummating death, the death that completes a life and becomes a spur and a promise to the living.

The one who completes his life dies his death victoriously, surrounded by people who hope and make promises.

This is how one should learn to die. And there should be no festival at which such a dying man does not consecrate the vows of the living!

To die like this is best. The next best is to die in battle and spend a great soul.

But your grinning death, which creeps up like a thief and yet arrives as master, is as hateful to the fighter as it is to the victor.

I praise to you my own death, the voluntary death, which comes to me because I want it.

And when will I want it? Whoever has a goal and an heir wants death at the right time for the sake of the goal and the heir.

Out of reverence for the goal and the heir, he will hang no more withered wreaths in the sanctuary of life.

Truly, I will not be like the rope-makers. They draw out their cord longer and longer, and in doing so they keep walking backward.

Many also grow too old for their truths and their triumphs. A toothless mouth no longer has the right to every truth.

Whoever wants fame must take leave of honor in good time, and practice the difficult art of leaving at the right time.

One must stop letting oneself be feasted on when one tastes best. Those who want to be loved for a long time know this.

There are sour apples, of course, whose fate is to wait until the last day of autumn. They become ripe, yellow, and shriveled all at once.

In some, the heart grows old first; in others, the spirit. Some are gray-haired in youth, but those who become young late stay young for a long time.

For many people life is a failure; a poisonous worm gnaws at their heart. Then let them see to it that their dying is all the more a success.

Many never become sweet; they rot while it is still summer. It is cowardice that keeps them clinging to their branches.

Far too many live, and they hang far too long on their branches. If only a storm would come and shake all this rot and worm-eaten fruit from the tree!

If only preachers of QUICK death would come! They would be the proper storms to shake the trees of life! But I hear only slow death preached, and patience with everything "earthly."

Ah! You preach patience with what is earthly? It is the earthly that has too much patience with you, you blasphemers!

Truly, that Hebrew whom the preachers of slow death honor died too early. And for many it has been a disaster that he died too early.

He had known only tears and the melancholy of the Hebrews, together with the hatred of the good and just. Then the Hebrew Jesus was seized by the longing for death.

If only he had stayed in the wilderness, far from the good and just! Then perhaps he would have learned to live and to love the earth, and to laugh as well!

Believe it, my brothers! He died too early. He himself would have renounced his teaching had he lived to my age! He was noble enough to renounce it!

But he was still unripe. The young man loves unripely, and he also hates humanity and the earth unripely. His soul and the wings of his spirit are still cramped and clumsy.

But in the grown man there is more of the child than in the youth, and less melancholy. He understands life and death better.

Free for death and free in death, a holy no-sayer when the time for yes has passed: this is how he understands death and life.

That your dying may not be a reproach against humanity and the earth, my friends: this I ask of the honey of your souls.

In your dying, your spirit and your virtue should still glow around the earth like the afterglow of evening. Otherwise your dying has turned out badly.

This is how I myself want to die: so that you, my friends, may love the earth more for my sake. And I will become earth again, to find rest in her who bore me.

Truly, Zarathustra had a goal; he threw his ball. Now you, my friends, are the heirs of my goal. To you I throw the golden ball.

More than anything, my friends, I want to see you throw the golden ball! And so I linger a little longer on the earth. Forgive me for it!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXII. THE BESTOWING VIRTUE.

1.

Zarathustra took leave of the town his heart was attached to, a town called "The Pied Cow." Many people who called themselves his disciples followed him and kept him company. So they came to a crossroads. There Zarathustra told them that he now wanted to go on alone, for he liked walking alone.

As he was leaving, his disciples gave him a staff. On its golden handle, a serpent coiled around the sun. Zarathustra was delighted with the staff and leaned on it. Then he spoke to his disciples:

Tell me, how did gold come to have the highest value? Because it is rare and useless, gleaming and soft in its shine. It always gives itself away.

Gold came to have the highest value only as an image of the highest virtue. The glance of the giver gleams like gold. The shine of gold makes peace between moon and sun.

The highest virtue is rare and useless. It gleams, and its shine is soft. The highest virtue is a bestowing virtue, a virtue that gives.

Truly, I read you well, my disciples: like me, you strive for the bestowing virtue. What could you have in common with cats and wolves?

You thirst to become sacrifices and gifts yourselves. That is why you thirst to gather every kind of wealth into your souls.

Your soul strives endlessly for treasures and jewels, because your virtue is endless in its desire to give.

You compel all things to flow toward you and into you, so that they will flow back out of your fountain as the gifts of your love.

Truly, such bestowing love must take possession of all values. But I call this selfishness healthy and holy.

There is another selfishness, a far too poor and hungry kind, which always wants to steal. It is the selfishness of the sick, a sickly selfishness.

It looks at everything that shines with a thief's eye. It sizes up anyone who has abundance with the craving of hunger. And it always prowls around the tables of those who give.

Sickness speaks in such craving, and an invisible degeneration. The thieving craving of this selfishness speaks of a sick body.

Tell me, my brother: what do we consider bad, and worst of all? Is it not **degeneration**? And we always suspect degeneration wherever the giving soul is missing.

Our course leads upward, from one kind to a higher kind beyond it. But we are horrified by the degenerating attitude that says, "Everything for me."

Our sense soars upward. In this it is an image of our body, an image of elevation. The names of the virtues are such images of elevation.

So the body moves through history, always becoming and fighting. And the spirit: what is it to the body? It is the herald of the body's battles and victories, its companion and echo.

All the names of good and evil are images. They do not speak plainly; they only hint. Anyone who seeks knowledge from them is a fool!

Pay attention, my brothers, to every hour when your spirit wants to speak in images. That is where your virtue begins.

Then your body is elevated and lifted up. Its delight enraptures the spirit, so that the spirit becomes a creator, a valuer, a lover, and a benefactor of all things.

When your heart overflows, broad and full like a river, a blessing and a danger to those who live in the lowlands: that is where your virtue begins.

When you are raised above praise and blame, and your will wants to command all things, as the will of one who loves: that is where your virtue begins.

When you despise comfortable things and the effeminate bed, and cannot lie down far enough from the effeminate: that is where your virtue begins.

When you will a single will, and when this turning of every need becomes your necessity: that is where your virtue begins.

Truly, it is a new good and evil! Truly, a new deep murmuring, and the voice of a new spring!

This new virtue is power. It is a ruling thought, and around it a subtle soul: a golden sun, with the serpent of knowledge coiled around it.

2.

Here Zarathustra paused for a while and looked lovingly at his disciples. Then he went on speaking, and his voice had changed:

Stay true to the earth, my brothers, with the power of your virtue! Let your giving love and your knowledge serve the meaning of the earth. This I beg and urge you.

Do not let it fly away from earthly things and beat its wings against eternal walls! Ah, there has always been so much virtue that flew away!

Like me, lead the virtue that flew away back to the earth. Yes, lead it back to the body and to life, so that it may give the earth its meaning, a human meaning!

Until now, spirit and virtue have flown off and blundered a hundred times. Alas, all this delusion and blundering still lives in our bodies. There it has become body and will.

Until now, spirit and virtue have tried and failed a hundred times. Yes, man has been an experiment. Alas, much ignorance and error has become flesh in us!

Not only the reason of thousands of years breaks out in us, but also their madness. It is dangerous to be an heir.

We still fight step by step with the giant Chance. Until now, nonsense, the lack of sense, has ruled over all humankind.

Let your spirit and your virtue serve the sense of the earth, my brothers. Let the value of everything be set anew by you! That is why you must be fighters. That is why you must be creators!

The body purifies itself through knowing. By experimenting with knowledge, it raises itself up. For those who seek knowledge, all instincts become holy. For those who are elevated, the soul becomes joyful.

Physician, heal yourself: then you will heal your patient too. Let his best cure be to see with his own eyes someone who makes himself whole.

There are a thousand paths that no one has yet walked, a thousand kinds of health and hidden islands of life. Man and man's world are still unexhausted and undiscovered.

Wake up and listen, you lonely ones! Winds blow from the future on secret wings, and good news is announced to sharp ears.

You lonely ones of today, you who have withdrawn: one day you will be a people. Out of you who have chosen yourselves, a chosen people will arise, and out of it, the Superman.

Truly, the earth will yet become a place of healing! Already a new scent surrounds it, a scent that brings salvation, and a new hope!

3.

When Zarathustra had spoken these words, he paused like someone who has not yet said his last word. For a long time he weighed the staff doubtfully in his hand. At last he spoke, and his voice had changed:

Now I go alone, my disciples! You too go away now, and alone! This is what I want.

Truly, I advise you: leave me, and guard yourselves against Zarathustra! Better still, be ashamed of him! Perhaps he has deceived you.

The person who seeks knowledge must be able not only to love his enemies but also to hate his friends.

You repay a teacher poorly if you remain only a pupil. And why will you not pluck at my wreath?

You revere me. But what if your reverence collapses one day? Take care that a statue does not crush you!

You say you believe in Zarathustra? But what does Zarathustra matter? You are my believers, but what do any believers matter?

You had not yet looked for yourselves when you found me. All believers do this. That is why all belief counts for so little.

Now I tell you to lose me and find yourselves. Only when you have all denied me will I return to you.

Truly, my brothers, I will then look for my lost ones with different eyes. I will love you then with a different love.

And once again you will have become my friends, and children of a single hope. Then I will be with you for the third time, to celebrate the great noon with you.

The great noon is the moment when man stands in the middle of his path between animal and Superman. He celebrates his way toward evening as his highest hope, because it is the way to a new morning.

Then the one who goes under will bless himself for being one who goes over. And the sun of his knowledge will stand at noon.

"ALL GODS ARE DEAD: NOW WE WANT THE SUPERMAN TO LIVE." Let this be our final will at the great noon!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## THUS SPAKE ZARATHUSTRA. SECOND PART.

> "—and only when you have all denied me will I return to you.
>
> Truly, my brothers, I will then look for my lost ones with different eyes. I will love you then with a different love." —ZARATHUSTRA, I., "The Bestowing Virtue."

## XXIII. THE CHILD WITH THE MIRROR.

After this, Zarathustra went back into the mountains, to the solitude of his cave, and withdrew from people. He waited like a sower who has scattered his seed.

But his soul grew impatient and filled with longing for those he loved, because he still had much to give them. For this is the hardest thing of all: to close the open hand out of love, and to stay modest as a giver.

So months and years passed for the lonely one. Meanwhile his wisdom grew, and its very abundance caused him pain.

One morning, however, he woke before the rosy dawn. After lying on his bed thinking for a long time, he finally spoke to his heart:

Why was I so startled in my dream that I woke up? Did a child not come to me, carrying a mirror?

"O Zarathustra," the child said to me, "look at yourself in the mirror!"

But when I looked into the mirror, I cried out, and my heart pounded. I did not see myself there, but a devil's grimace and sneer.

Truly, I understand the dream's meaning and warning all too well: my **doctrine** is in danger. Weeds want to be called wheat!

My enemies have grown powerful and have distorted the image of my teaching, so that my dearest ones must blush at the gifts I gave them.

My friends are lost. The hour has come for me to look for my lost ones!

With these words Zarathustra sprang up. He was not like someone in anguish seeking relief, but like a seer and a singer seized by the spirit. His eagle and serpent gazed at him in amazement, for a coming bliss spread across his face like the rosy dawn.

What has happened to me, my animals? said Zarathustra. Am I not transformed? Has bliss not come to me like a whirlwind?

My happiness is foolish, and it will say foolish things. It is still too young, so be patient with it!

I am wounded by my happiness. Let all who suffer be my physicians!

I can go down to my friends again, and to my enemies too! Zarathustra can speak and give again, and show his best love to those he loves!

My impatient love overflows in streams, down toward sunrise and sunset. Out of silent mountains and storms of pain, my soul rushes into the valleys.

Too long have I longed and gazed into the distance. Too long has solitude owned me. So I have forgotten how to keep silent.

I have become pure speech, the roar of a brook falling from high rocks. I will hurl my speech down into the valleys.

And let the stream of my love rush into channels no one travels! How could a stream not find its way to the sea in the end?

It is true that there is a lake in me, secluded and sufficient to itself. But the stream of my love carries it along, down to the sea!

I walk new paths, and a new way of speaking comes to me. Like all creators, I have grown tired of the old tongues. My spirit will no longer walk on worn-out soles.

All speech runs too slowly for me. Into your chariot, storm, I leap! And I will whip even you with my spite!

Like a shout and a cheer, I will cross wide seas until I find the Happy Isles where my friends are staying,

and my enemies among them! How I love everyone now to whom I may simply speak! Even my enemies are part of my bliss.

And when I want to mount my wildest horse, my spear always helps me up best. It is my foot's ever-ready servant:

the spear I hurl at my enemies! How grateful I am to my enemies that at last I may hurl it!

The tension in my cloud has been too great. Between the laughter of lightning bolts, I will throw showers of hail into the depths.

Then my chest will heave violently. Violently it will blow its storm over the mountains, and so it will find relief.

Truly, my happiness and my freedom come like a storm! But my enemies will think that **the Evil One** is raging over their heads.

Yes, you too, my friends, will be frightened by my wild wisdom. Perhaps you will run from it, along with my enemies.

Ah, if only I knew how to lure you back with shepherds' flutes! Ah, if only my lioness wisdom would learn to roar gently! And we have already learned so much together!

My wild wisdom became pregnant on the lonely mountains. On the rough stones she gave birth to the youngest of her young.

Now she runs foolishly through the dry wilderness, searching and searching for soft grass—my old, wild wisdom!

On the soft grass of your hearts, my friends!—on your love, she would gladly lay down her dearest one!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXIV. IN THE HAPPY ISLES.

The figs are falling from the trees. They are good and sweet, and as they fall, their red skins split open. I am a north wind to ripe figs.

Like figs, these teachings fall to you, my friends. Now drink their juice and take in their sweet flesh! It is autumn all around, with a clear sky, and afternoon.

Look at the fullness all around us! And out of the midst of this overabundance, it is a delight to gaze out at distant seas.

Once, when people looked out at distant seas, they said "God." But now I have taught you to say "Superman."

God is a conjecture. But I do not want your conjecturing to reach any further than your creating will.

Could you *create* a god? Then, I beg you, be silent about all gods! But you could well create the Superman.

Perhaps not you yourselves, my brothers! But you could transform yourselves into fathers and ancestors of the Superman. Let that be your best creating!—

God is a conjecture. But I would like your conjecturing kept within the limits of what can be conceived.

Could you *conceive* a god? Then let the will to truth mean this to you: that everything be transformed into what humans can conceive, what humans can see, what humans can feel! You must follow your own perceptions all the way to the end!

And what you have called the world must first be created by you. It must itself become your reason, your image, your will, your love! And truly, this is for your happiness, you who seek knowledge!

And how would you endure life without that hope, you who seek knowledge? You could not have been born into the inconceivable, nor into the irrational.

But to open my heart to you completely, my friends: *if* there were gods, how could I bear not to be a god! *Therefore* there are no gods.

Yes, I drew that conclusion. But now it draws me.—

God is a conjecture. But who could drink all the bitterness of this conjecture without dying? Shall the creator be robbed of his faith, and the eagle of its flights into eagle heights?

God is a thought that makes everything straight crooked, and makes everything that stands reel. What? Would time be gone, and everything perishable be nothing but a lie?

To think this brings dizziness and vertigo to human limbs, and even nausea to the stomach. Truly, I call it the reeling sickness, to conjecture such a thing.

I call it evil and hostile to humanity: all this teaching about the One, the Full, the Unmoved, the Self-Sufficient, the Imperishable!

Everything imperishable—that is only a metaphor, and the poets lie too much.—

But the best metaphors should speak of time and becoming. They should be a praise and a justification of everything perishable!

Creating—that is the great redemption from suffering, and what makes life lighter. But for the creator to come into being, suffering itself is needed, and much transformation.

Yes, there must be much bitter dying in your life, you creators! In this way you are the advocates and justifiers of everything perishable.

For the creator to be himself the newborn child, he must also be willing to be the one who gives birth, and to endure the pains of giving birth.

Truly, I have made my way through a hundred souls, through a hundred cradles and birth pangs. I have said many farewells. I know the heartbreaking final hours.

But my creating will, my fate, wills it so. Or, to tell you more honestly: precisely such a fate is what my will wills.

All *feeling* in me suffers and lies in prison. But my *willing* always comes to me as my liberator and comforter.

Willing liberates: that is the true teaching about will and freedom—so Zarathustra teaches you.

No longer willing, no longer valuing, no longer creating! Ah, may that great weakness always stay far from me!

Even in knowing, I feel only my will's delight in begetting and becoming. And if there is innocence in my knowledge, it is because there is a will to beget in it.

This will lured me away from God and gods. What would there be to create if there were—gods!

But my ardent creative will drives me again and again toward human beings, the way the hammer is driven toward the stone.

Ah, you humans, an image sleeps for me in the stone, the image of my visions! Ah, that it must sleep in the hardest, ugliest stone!

Now my hammer rages mercilessly against its prison. Fragments fly from the stone: what is that to me?

I will finish it. For a shadow came to me—the stillest and lightest of all things once came to me!

The beauty of the Superman came to me as a shadow. Ah, my brothers! What do the gods matter to me now!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXV. THE PITIFUL.

My friends, a mocking remark has been made about your friend: "Look at Zarathustra! Doesn't he walk among us as if among animals?"

But it is better put this way: "The one who knows walks among human beings *as* among animals."

To the one who knows, man himself is the animal with red cheeks.

How did that happen to him? Isn't it because he has had to feel ashamed too often?

O my friends! This is what the one who knows says: shame, shame, shame—that is the history of man!

And for that reason the noble person commands himself not to put others to shame. In the presence of all who suffer, he commands himself to be modest.

Truly, I do not like them, the merciful ones whose happiness lies in their pity. They are far too lacking in modesty.

If I must feel pity, I don't like to be called pitying. And if I do feel it, I prefer to feel it from a distance.

I also prefer to cover my head and flee before I am recognized. And I tell you to do the same, my friends!

May my destiny always lead untroubled people like you across my path, people with whom I *may* share hope, a meal, and honey!

Truly, I have done this and that for the afflicted. But I always seemed to do something better when I had learned to enjoy myself better.

Since humanity came into being, man has enjoyed himself too little. That alone, my brothers, is our original sin!

And when we learn to enjoy ourselves better, we best unlearn how to hurt others and how to devise ways of hurting them.

That is why I wash the hand that has helped the sufferer. That is why I also wipe my soul clean.

For when I saw the sufferer suffering, I was ashamed on account of his shame. And when I helped him, I wounded his pride badly.

Great obligations do not make people grateful, but vengeful. And when a small kindness is not forgotten, it becomes a gnawing worm.

"Be reluctant to accept! Show distinction in how you accept!"—that is my advice to those who have nothing to give.

I, however, am a giver. I give gladly, as a friend to friends. But strangers and the poor may pick the fruit from my tree for themselves; that causes less shame.

Beggars, though, should be done away with entirely! Truly, it is annoying to give to them, and it is annoying not to give to them.

And the same goes for sinners and bad consciences! Believe me, my friends: the sting of conscience teaches one to sting.

The worst things, however, are petty thoughts. Truly, it is better to have done evil than to have thought pettily!

Of course, you say: "Taking pleasure in petty evils spares one many a great evil deed." But here one should not want to be sparing.

The evil deed is like a boil. It itches, irritates, and breaks out—it speaks honestly.

"Look, I am disease," says the evil deed: that is its honesty.

But the petty thought is like an infection. It creeps and hides and wants to be nowhere—until the whole body is rotten and withered by the petty infection.

But to the one who is possessed by a devil, I would whisper this word in his ear: "Better for you to raise your devil to its full size! Even for you there is still a path to greatness!"—

Ah, my brothers! We know a little too much about everyone! And many a person becomes transparent to us, yet we still cannot see through him at all.

It is hard to live among people, because keeping silent is so hard.

And it is not the person who offends us whom we treat most unfairly, but the person who does not concern us at all.

But if you have a suffering friend, be a resting place for his suffering—yet a hard bed, a camp bed. That is how you will serve him best.

And if a friend wrongs you, say: "I forgive you what you did to me. But that you did it to *yourself*—how could I forgive that!"

So speaks all great love: it rises above even forgiveness and pity.

One should keep a firm hold on one's heart. For once one lets it go, how quickly one's head runs off as well!

Ah, where in the world have there been greater follies than among the pitying? And what in the world has caused more suffering than the follies of the pitying?

Woe to all who love without having a height that stands above their pity!

Thus the devil once spoke to me: "Even God has his hell: it is his love for man."

And recently I heard him say these words: "God is dead. God died of his pity for man."—

So be warned against pity: *from there* a heavy cloud is still coming over humanity! Truly, I know how to read the signs of the weather!

But mark this word too: all great love is above all its pity. For it seeks—to create what it loves!

"I offer myself to my love, *and my neighbor as myself*"—that is how all creators speak.

But all creators are hard.—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXVI. THE PRIESTS.

One day Zarathustra made a sign to his disciples and spoke these words to them:

"Here are priests. Although they are my enemies, pass them quietly and with sleeping swords!

Even among them there are heroes. Many of them have suffered too much—and so they want to make others suffer.

They are bad enemies: nothing is more vengeful than their meekness. And whoever touches them easily soils himself.

But my blood is related to theirs. And even so, I want to see my blood honored in theirs."—

And when they had passed by, a pain came over Zarathustra. But he had not struggled with the pain for long before he began to speak this way:

My heart goes out to those priests. They also offend my taste; but that matters least of all to me, since I am among people.

But I suffer and have suffered with them. To me they are prisoners and branded men. The one they call Savior put them in chains—

In chains of false values and empty words! Oh, if only someone would save them from their Savior!

Once, when the sea was tossing them about, they thought they had landed on an island. But look, it was a sleeping monster!

False values and empty words: these are the worst monsters for mortals. The fate within them sleeps and waits a long time.

But at last it comes, wakes up, and devours and swallows whatever has built shelters upon it.

Oh, just look at the shelters these priests have built for themselves! They call their sweet-smelling caves churches!

Oh, that falsified light, that stale, musty air! Where the soul is not allowed to fly up to its height!

But their faith commands: "On your knees, up the stairs, you sinners!"

Truly, I would rather see someone shameless than the twisted eyes of their shame and devotion!

Who built themselves such caves and stairs of penance? Was it not those who wanted to hide, and who were ashamed under the clear sky?

And only when the clear sky looks in again through ruined roofs, and down onto grass and red poppies on ruined walls, will I turn my heart once more to the dwellings of this God.

They called God whatever opposed and tormented them. And truly, there was much heroic spirit in their worship!

And they knew no way to love their God except by nailing human beings to the cross!

They meant to live as corpses, and they dressed their corpses in black. Even in their speech I still taste the foul flavor of burial vaults.

And whoever lives near them lives near black ponds, where the toad sings its song with sweet solemnity.

They would have to sing better songs for me to believe in their Savior. His disciples would have to look more redeemed to me!

I would like to see them naked, for beauty alone should preach repentance. But whom would that disguised misery convince!

Truly, their saviors themselves did not come from freedom and from freedom's seventh heaven! Truly, they themselves never walked on the carpets of knowledge!

The spirit of those saviors was made of gaps. But into every gap they had put their illusion, their stopgap, which they called God.

Their spirit drowned in their pity. And whenever they swelled and overflowed with pity, a great folly always floated to the surface.

Eagerly and with shouting, they drove their flock over their footbridge, as if there were only one footbridge to the future! Truly, those shepherds too still belonged to the flock!

Those shepherds had small spirits and roomy souls. But, my brothers, what small territories even the roomiest souls have been until now!

They wrote signs in blood along the way they went, and their folly taught that truth is proved by blood.

But blood is the very worst witness to truth. Blood poisons the purest teaching and turns it into delusion and hatred of the heart.

And when someone walks through fire for his teaching—what does that prove! Truly, it is more when one's own teaching comes out of one's own burning!

Sultry heart and cold head: where these two meet, the blusterer arises, the "Savior."

Truly, there have been greater ones, and higher-born ones, than those the people call Saviors, those rapturous blusterers! And you must be saved by ones still greater than any of the Saviors, my brothers, if you want to find the way to freedom!

There has never yet been a Superman. I have seen both of them naked, the greatest human being and the smallest:—

They are still all too similar to each other. Truly, even the greatest I found—all too human!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXVII. THE VIRTUOUS.

To senses that are lazy and half-asleep, one must speak with thunder and heavenly fireworks.

But beauty's voice speaks gently: it reaches only the most awakened souls.

Today my shield trembled gently and laughed at me; it was beauty's holy laughter and thrill. At you, you virtuous ones, my beauty laughed today. And its voice came to me like this: "They want—to be paid on top of it!"

You want to be paid on top of it, you virtuous ones! You want a reward for virtue, heaven in exchange for earth, and eternity in exchange for your today?

And now you reproach me for teaching that there is no one who hands out rewards, no paymaster? And truly, I do not even teach that virtue is its own reward.

Ah, this is my sorrow: reward and punishment have been slipped into the very foundation of things. And now they have been slipped even into the foundation of your souls, you virtuous ones!

But like a boar's snout, my word will root up the foundation of your souls; you will call me a plowshare.

All the secrets of your heart will be brought into the light. And when you lie in the sun, rooted up and broken, your falsehood will also be separated from your truth.

For this is your truth: you are TOO PURE for the filth of these words: vengeance, punishment, recompense, retribution.

You love your virtue as a mother loves her child; but who ever heard of a mother wanting to be paid for her love?

Your virtue is your dearest Self. The ring's thirst is in you: every ring struggles and turns in order to reach itself again.

And every work of your virtue is like a star that goes out. Its light is always still on its way, still traveling—and when will it ever stop traveling?

So the light of your virtue is still on its way, even when its work is done. Even if the work is forgotten and dead, its ray of light still lives and travels.

That your virtue is your Self, and not something outward, a skin or a cloak: that is the truth from the foundation of your souls, you virtuous ones!—

But to be sure, there are those for whom virtue means writhing under the whip: and you have listened too much to their crying!

And there are others who call it virtue when their vices grow lazy. Once their hatred and jealousy let their limbs relax, their "justice" comes alive and rubs its sleepy eyes.

And there are others who are dragged downward: their devils drag them. But the deeper they sink, the more fiercely their eyes glow, and the more ardent their longing for their God.

Ah, their crying too has reached your ears, you virtuous ones: "What I am NOT—that, that is God to me, and virtue!"

And there are others who move along heavily and creakingly, like carts hauling stones downhill. They talk a great deal about dignity and virtue—the brake that holds them back, they call virtue!

And there are others who are like eight-day clocks once they are wound up: they tick, and they want people to call the ticking—virtue.

Truly, these amuse me. Wherever I find such clocks, I will wind them up with my mockery, and they will even whirr from it!

And others are proud of their scrap of righteousness, and for its sake they do violence to everything, so that the world is drowned in their unrighteousness.

Ah, how awkwardly the word "virtue" comes out of their mouths! And when they say, "I am just," it always sounds like, "I am just—revenged!"

With their virtues they want to scratch out their enemies' eyes; and they raise themselves up only in order to bring others down.

And again there are those who sit in their swamp and speak like this from among the bulrushes: "Virtue—that means sitting quietly in the swamp.

We bite no one, and we get out of the way of anyone who wants to bite; and on every matter we hold the opinion we are given."

And again there are those who love poses, and think virtue is a kind of pose. Their knees are always worshiping, and their hands are hymns of praise to virtue, but their hearts know nothing of it.

And again there are those who think it virtuous to say, "Virtue is necessary"; but in the end all they believe is that policemen are necessary.

And many a person who cannot see the loftiness in people calls it virtue to see their baseness far too well. In this way he calls his evil eye virtue.—

And some want to be edified and lifted up, and call that virtue; and others want to be thrown down—and call that virtue too.

And so almost everyone thinks they have a share in virtue; at the very least, everyone claims to be an authority on "good" and "evil."

But Zarathustra did not come to say to all these liars and fools: "What do YOU know of virtue! What COULD you know of virtue!"—

He came so that you, my friends, might grow weary of the old words you have learned from the fools and liars. He came so that you might grow weary of the words "reward," "retribution," "punishment," "righteous vengeance."—

He came so that you might grow weary of saying: "An action is good because it is unselfish."

Ah, my friends! That YOUR very Self be in your action, as the mother is in the child: let that be YOUR formula of virtue!

Truly, I have taken from you a hundred formulas and your virtue's favorite toys; and now you scold me, as children scold.

They were playing by the sea. Then a wave came and swept their toys into the deep: and now they cry.

But the same wave will bring them new toys, and spread new speckled shells before them!

So they will be comforted; and like them, my friends, you too shall have your comfort—and new speckled shells!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXVIII. THE RABBLE.

Life is a well of delight; but wherever the rabble drink too, all fountains are poisoned.

I am well disposed toward everything clean; but I hate to see the grinning mouths and the thirst of the unclean.

They cast their eyes down into the fountain: and now their hateful smile glances up at me out of the fountain.

They have poisoned the holy water with their lust. And when they called their filthy dreams delight, they poisoned the words too.

The flame grows indignant when they bring their damp hearts to the fire; the spirit itself bubbles and smokes when the rabble come near the fire.

In their hands the fruit turns sickly-sweet and overripe. Their gaze makes the fruit tree unsteady and withered at the top.

And many who have turned away from life have only turned away from the rabble: they hated sharing fountain, flame, and fruit with them.

And many who went into the wilderness and suffered thirst among beasts of prey simply did not want to sit at the cistern with filthy camel drivers.

And many who came as destroyers, like a hailstorm over all the grainfields, only wanted to plant a foot in the rabble's jaws and so stop up their throat.

And the mouthful that has choked me most is not the knowledge that life itself requires enmity and death and crosses of torture:—

But I once asked a question, and almost suffocated on it: What? Is the rabble also NECESSARY for life?

Are poisoned fountains necessary, and stinking fires, and filthy dreams, and maggots in the bread of life?

Not my hatred but my loathing gnawed hungrily at my life! Ah, I often grew weary of the spirit when I found that even the rabble had spirit.

And I turned my back on the rulers when I saw what they now call ruling: haggling and bargaining for power—with the rabble!

I lived among peoples who spoke a foreign tongue, with my ears stopped, so that the language of their haggling and their bargaining for power might stay foreign to me.

And holding my nose, I walked gloomily through all yesterdays and todays. Truly, all yesterdays and todays smell bad of the scribbling rabble!

Like a cripple who has gone deaf, blind, and mute—so I lived for a long time, so as not to live with the power-rabble, the scribbler-rabble, and the pleasure-rabble.

My spirit climbed stairs with effort and caution; small handouts of delight were its refreshment; life crept along on a staff with the blind man.

What has happened to me? How did I free myself from loathing? Who made my eyes young again? How did I fly to the height where no rabble sit at the wells anymore?

Did my loathing itself give me wings and the power to divine hidden springs? Truly, I had to fly to the loftiest height to find the well of delight again!

Oh, I have found it, my brothers! Here on the loftiest height the well of delight bubbles up for me! And there is a life at whose waters none of the rabble drink with me!

You flow almost too violently for me, fountain of delight! And often you empty the cup again in your eagerness to fill it!

And yet I must learn to approach you more modestly: my heart still flows toward you far too violently:—

My heart, on which my summer burns, my short, hot, melancholy, overhappy summer: how my summer heart longs for your coolness!

Gone, the lingering distress of my spring! Gone, the spite of my snowflakes in June! I have become summer entirely, and summer noon!

A summer on the loftiest height, with cold springs and blissful stillness: oh, come, my friends, so that the stillness may become more blissful still!

For this is OUR height and our home: we live too high and too steep here for all the unclean and their thirst.

Just cast your pure eyes into the well of my delight, my friends! How could that make it muddy? It will laugh back at you with ITS purity.

We build our nest in the tree of the future; eagles shall bring food in their beaks to us solitary ones!

Truly, it is no food that the impure could share! They would think they were devouring fire, and burn their mouths!

Truly, we keep no dwellings ready here for the impure! Our happiness would be an ice cave to their bodies, and to their spirits!

And like strong winds we will live above them, neighbors of the eagles, neighbors of the snow, neighbors of the sun: that is how strong winds live.

And like a wind I will one day blow among them, and with my spirit take away their spirit's breath: so wills my future.

Truly, Zarathustra is a strong wind to all low places. And he gives this advice to his enemies, and to everything that spits and spews: "Take care not to spit AGAINST the wind!"—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXIX. THE TARANTULAS.

Look, this is the tarantula's den! Do you want to see the tarantula itself? Here hangs its web: touch it, so that it trembles.

Here comes the tarantula, willingly enough: Welcome, tarantula! Black on your back is your triangle, your emblem; and I also know what is in your soul.

Revenge is in your soul: wherever you bite, a black scab grows. With revenge, your poison makes the soul dizzy!

So I speak to you in a parable, you who make the soul dizzy, you preachers of EQUALITY! To me you are tarantulas, secretly vengeful ones!

But I will soon drag your hiding places into the light: that is why I laugh in your face with my laughter of the heights.

That is why I tear at your web: so that your rage lures you out of your den of lies, and your revenge leaps out from behind your word "justice."

For THAT HUMANITY BE REDEEMED FROM REVENGE: that, for me, is the bridge to the highest hope, and a rainbow after long storms.

But the tarantulas want it otherwise. "Let justice mean precisely this: that the world fills with the storms of our vengeance"—so they say to one another.

"We will take vengeance and heap insult on all who are not like us"—so the tarantula-hearts vow.

"And 'Will to Equality'—that itself shall from now on be the name for virtue; and against everything that has power we will raise an outcry!"

You preachers of equality, it is the tyrant-madness of impotence that cries out in you for "equality." This is how your most secret tyrant-longings disguise themselves in words of virtue!

Wounded conceit and suppressed envy, perhaps your fathers' conceit and envy: in you they break out as the flame and frenzy of vengeance.

What the father hid comes out in the son; and I have often found the father's secret revealed in the son.

They look like inspired people, but it is not the heart that inspires them—it is vengeance. And when they become subtle and cold, it is not spirit but envy that makes them so.

Their jealousy leads them onto the paths of thinkers too. And this is the mark of their jealousy: they always go too far, until at last their exhaustion has to lie down and sleep on the snow.

Vengeance sounds in all their complaints, malice in all their praises; and being a judge seems to them bliss.

But this is my advice to you, my friends: mistrust all in whom the urge to punish is powerful!

They are people of bad breed and descent; the hangman and the bloodhound peer out of their faces.

Mistrust all who talk a great deal about their justice! Truly, it is not only honey that their souls lack.

And when they call themselves "the good and just," do not forget that the only thing they lack to be Pharisees is—power!

My friends, I will not be mixed up and confused with others.

There are those who preach my teaching about life and are at the same time preachers of equality, and tarantulas.

These poison-spiders speak in favor of life, even though they sit in their den, withdrawn from life. They do so because they want to cause injury by it.

They want to injure those who hold power now, for among those people the preaching of death is still most at home.

If things were otherwise, the tarantulas would teach otherwise. And they themselves were once the best slanderers of the world and burners of heretics.

I will not be mixed up and confused with these preachers of equality. For this is what justice says TO ME: "Men are not equal."

Nor shall they become equal! What would my love of the Superman be, if I said otherwise?

On a thousand bridges and piers they shall crowd toward the future, and there shall always be more war and inequality among them. My great love makes me say this!

In their hostilities they shall invent images and phantoms. And with those images and phantoms they shall yet fight the supreme fight against one another!

Good and evil, rich and poor, high and low, and all the names of values: these shall be weapons, and ringing signs that life must surpass itself again and again!

Life itself wants to build itself upward with columns and stairs. It wants to gaze into far distances and out toward blissful beauties. THAT is why it needs height!

And because it needs height, it needs steps, and difference between steps and between climbers! Life strives to rise, and in rising to surpass itself.

Just look, my friends! Here, where the tarantula's den is, the ruins of an ancient temple rise up. Look at it with enlightened eyes!

Truly, whoever once raised his thoughts aloft here in stone knew the secret of life as well as the wisest do!

He teaches us here, in the plainest parable, that there is struggle and inequality even in beauty, and war for power and supremacy.

How divinely vault and arch contrast with each other here in their struggle! How they strive against each other with light and shadow, these divinely striving ones!

So let us also be enemies, steadfast and beautiful, my friends! Divinely we will strive AGAINST one another!

Alas! The tarantula, my old enemy, has bitten me too! Divinely steadfast and beautiful, it has bitten me on the finger!

"There must be punishment, and justice," it thinks. "He shall not sing songs in honor of enmity here for nothing!"

Yes, it has taken its revenge! And alas, now it will make my soul dizzy with revenge too!

But so that I do NOT become dizzy, tie me fast to this pillar, my friends! I would rather be a pillar-saint, one of those hermits who lived atop a column, than a whirl of vengeance!

Truly, Zarathustra is no cyclone or whirlwind. And if he is a dancer, he is certainly not a tarantula-dancer!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXX. THE FAMOUS WISE ONES.

You have served the people and the people's superstition, NOT the truth, all you famous wise ones! And that is exactly why the people revered you.

That is also why they tolerated your unbelief: to the people it was a joke and a harmless side path. In the same way a master gives his slaves free rein and even enjoys their insolence.

But the one the people hate, as dogs hate the wolf, is the free spirit. He is the enemy of chains, the one who does not worship, the dweller in the woods.

The people have always called it their "sense of right" to hunt him out of his lair. They still set their sharpest-toothed dogs on him.

"For the truth is there, where the people are! Woe, woe to those who seek!" This cry has echoed through all time.

You wanted to justify your people in their reverence, and you called that "Will to Truth," you famous wise ones!

And your heart has always told itself: "I came from the people, and from them the voice of God also came to me."

As advocates of the people, you have always been stubborn and cunning, like the donkey.

And many a powerful man who wanted to get along well with the people has harnessed a donkey in front of his horses: a famous wise man.

And now, you famous wise ones, I would have you finally throw off the lion's skin entirely!

Throw off the skin of the beast of prey, the spotted skin, and the tangled mane of the investigator, the searcher, and the conqueror!

Ah! Before I could learn to believe in your "conscientiousness," you would first have to break your will to revere.

I call conscientious the one who goes into God-forsaken wildernesses and has broken his reverent heart.

In the yellow sand, burned by the sun, he no doubt peers thirstily at the islands rich in springs, where life rests under shady trees.

But his thirst does not persuade him to become like those comfortable ones. For where there are oases, there are also idols.

Hungry, fierce, lonely, God-forsaken: that is how the lion-will wants itself to be.

Free from the happiness of slaves, released from gods and worship, fearless and fear-inspiring, great and lonely: such is the will of the conscientious.

The conscientious, the free spirits, have always lived in the wilderness as its lords. But in the cities live the well-fed, famous wise ones: the beasts of burden.

For they always pull, like donkeys, the PEOPLE'S carts!

Not that I reproach them for this. But they remain servants and harnessed animals, even when they glitter in golden harness.

And they have often been good servants, worth their wages. For virtue says: "If you must be a servant, find the one to whom your service is most useful!

Your master's spirit and virtue shall grow because you are his servant. That way you yourself will grow along with his spirit and virtue!"

And truly, you famous wise ones, you servants of the people! You yourselves have grown with the people's spirit and virtue, and the people have grown through you! I say this to your honor!

But even with your virtues you remain the people to me: the people with dim eyes, the people who do not know what SPIRIT is!

Spirit is life that cuts into life itself. Through its own torment it increases its own knowledge. Did you know that before?

And the spirit's happiness is this: to be anointed and consecrated with tears as a sacrificial victim. Did you know that before?

And the blindness of the blind man, his seeking and groping, shall yet bear witness to the power of the sun he gazed into. Did you know that before?

And the one who understands shall learn to BUILD with mountains! It is a small thing for the spirit to move mountains. Did you know that before?

You know only the sparks of the spirit. But you do not see the anvil that it is, or the cruelty of its hammer!

Truly, you do not know the spirit's pride! But you could endure the spirit's humility even less, if it ever wanted to speak!

And you have never been able to throw your spirit into a pit of snow. You are not hot enough for that! So you also do not know the delight of its coldness.

In every way, though, you treat the spirit too familiarly. Out of wisdom you have often made a poorhouse and a hospital for bad poets.

You are not eagles, so you have never known the happiness of the spirit's terror. And whoever is not a bird should not camp above abysses.

You seem lukewarm to me. But all deep knowledge flows coldly. The innermost springs of the spirit are ice-cold: a refreshment for hot hands and for those who act.

There you stand, respectable and stiff, with straight backs, you famous wise ones! No strong wind or will drives you.

Have you never seen a sail crossing the sea, rounded and swollen, trembling with the force of the wind?

Like that sail, trembling with the force of the spirit, my wisdom crosses the sea: my wild wisdom!

But you servants of the people, you famous wise ones: how COULD you go with me!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXXI. THE NIGHT-SONG.

It is night: now all leaping fountains speak more loudly. And my soul too is a leaping fountain.

It is night: only now do all the songs of lovers awaken. And my soul too is the song of a lover.

Something unsatisfied, unsatisfiable, is in me, and it longs to be voiced. A craving for love is in me, and it speaks the language of love itself.

I am light: ah, if only I were night! But this is my loneliness, that I am surrounded by light!

Ah, if only I were dark and of the night! How I would suck at the breasts of light!

And I would bless you yourselves, you twinkling little stars and glowworms up above! I would rejoice in the gifts of your light.

But I live in my own light. I drink back into myself the flames that break out of me.

I do not know the happiness of the one who receives. And I have often dreamed that stealing must be more blessed than receiving.

This is my poverty: that my hand never stops giving. This is my envy: that I see waiting eyes and the brightened nights of longing.

Oh, the misery of all who give! Oh, the eclipse of my sun! Oh, the craving to crave! Oh, the ravenous hunger in fullness!

They take from me, but do I yet touch their soul? There is a gap between giving and receiving, and the smallest gap is the last to be bridged.

A hunger grows out of my beauty. I would like to hurt those I give light to; I would like to rob those I have given gifts to. That is how I hunger for wickedness.

I would withdraw my hand when another hand is already reaching for it. I would hesitate like the waterfall that hesitates even as it plunges. That is how I hunger for wickedness!

Such revenge my abundance dreams up. Such mischief wells up out of my loneliness.

My happiness in giving died in the giving. My virtue grew weary of itself through its own abundance!

Whoever always gives is in danger of losing his sense of shame. Whoever always hands things out finds that hand and heart grow callused from the very handing out.

My eye no longer overflows at the shame of those who beg. My hand has grown too hard to feel the trembling of hands I have filled.

Where have the tears of my eye gone, and the softness of my heart? Oh, the loneliness of all who give! Oh, the silence of all who shine!

Many suns circle in empty space. To everything dark they speak with their light, but to me they are silent.

Oh, this is the hostility of light toward whatever shines: pitilessly it goes its way.

Unfair in its innermost heart to whatever shines, cold toward other suns: that is how every sun travels.

Like a storm the suns fly along their courses: that is their traveling. They follow their relentless will: that is their coldness.

Oh, it is only you, you dark ones, you creatures of night, who draw warmth from the shining ones! Oh, only you drink milk and refreshment from the udders of light!

Ah, there is ice around me, and my hand burns from the iciness! Ah, there is thirst in me, and it pants for your thirst!

It is night: alas, that I must be light! And thirst for what belongs to night! And loneliness!

It is night: now my longing bursts out of me like a fountain. I long to speak.

It is night: now all leaping fountains speak more loudly. And my soul too is a leaping fountain.

It is night: now all the songs of lovers awaken. And my soul too is the song of a lover.

Thus sang Zarathustra.

## XXXII. THE DANCE-SONG.

One evening Zarathustra and his disciples were walking through the forest. While looking for a spring, he came upon a green meadow, peacefully surrounded by trees and bushes, where girls were dancing together.

As soon as the girls recognized Zarathustra, they stopped dancing. But Zarathustra came up to them with a friendly expression and spoke these words:

Don't stop dancing, you lovely girls! No spoilsport with an evil eye has come to you, no enemy of girls.

I am God's advocate before the devil, and the devil is the spirit of gravity. How could I, you light-footed ones, be hostile to divine dances? Or to girls' feet with fine ankles?

True, I am a forest and a night of dark trees. But whoever is not afraid of my darkness will find banks full of roses under my cypresses.

And he may even find the little god whom girls love best. He lies quietly beside the spring, with his eyes closed.

Truly, he fell asleep in broad daylight, the lazybones! Did he perhaps chase butterflies too much?

Don't scold me, you beautiful dancers, if I discipline the little god a bit! He will certainly cry and weep. But he is laughable even when he weeps!

And with tears in his eyes he shall ask you for a dance, and I myself will sing a song for his dance:

A dance-song and a satire on the spirit of gravity, my highest and most powerful devil, who is said to be "lord of the world."

And this is the song that Zarathustra sang while Cupid and the girls danced together:

Not long ago I gazed into your eye, O Life! And there I seemed to sink into the unfathomable.

But you pulled me out with a golden fishing hook. You laughed mockingly when I called you unfathomable.

"That is how all fish talk," you said. "Whatever THEY cannot fathom is unfathomable.

But I am only changeable, and wild, and in every way a woman, and not a virtuous one,
