even though you men call me 'the profound one,' or 'the faithful one,' 'the eternal one,' 'the mysterious one.'

But you men always give us your own virtues. Alas, you virtuous ones!"

So she laughed, the unbelievable one. But I never believe her or her laughter when she speaks ill of herself.

And when I spoke face to face with my wild Wisdom, she said to me angrily: "You will, you crave, you love: that is the only reason you PRAISE Life!"

Then I almost answered her angrily and told the angry one the truth. There is no angrier way to answer than to "tell the truth" to one's own Wisdom.

For this is how things stand among the three of us. In my heart I love only Life, and truly, I love her most when I hate her!

But I am fond of Wisdom, often too fond, because she reminds me so strongly of Life!

She has Life's eyes, her laugh, and even her little golden fishing rod. Is it my fault that the two are so alike?

And once, when Life asked me, "Who is she, then, this Wisdom?" I answered eagerly: "Ah, yes! Wisdom!

People thirst for her and are never satisfied. They look at her through veils; they grasp at her through nets.

Is she beautiful? How should I know! But even the oldest carp still take her bait.

She is changeable and willful. I have often seen her bite her lip and drag the comb against the grain of her hair.

Perhaps she is wicked and false, and a woman through and through. But it is exactly when she speaks ill of herself that she is most seductive."

When I said this to Life, she laughed maliciously and closed her eyes. "Whom are you talking about?" she said. "Perhaps about me?

And even if you were right, is it proper to say *that* to my face, like this? But now, please, talk about your Wisdom too!"

Ah, and now you have opened your eyes again, O beloved Life! And once again I seemed to sink into the unfathomable.

Thus sang Zarathustra. But when the dance was over and the young women had gone, he grew sad.

"The sun set long ago," he said at last. "The meadow is damp, and a coolness is coming from the forest.

Some unknown presence is around me, gazing thoughtfully. What! You are still alive, Zarathustra?

Why? For what? By what means? Where to? Where? How? Isn't it foolish to go on living?

Ah, my friends, it is the evening that questions me like this. Forgive me my sadness!

Evening has come. Forgive me that evening has come!"

Thus sang Zarathustra.

## XXXIII. THE GRAVE-SONG.

"There is the island of graves, the silent island. The graves of my youth are there too. I will carry an evergreen wreath of life there."

With this resolve in my heart, I sailed across the sea.

Oh, you sights and scenes of my youth! Oh, all you glimpses of love, you divine, fleeting glimpses! How could you die so soon for me? Today I think of you as my dead.

From you, my dearest dead, a sweet fragrance comes to me, one that opens and melts the heart. Truly, it shakes and opens the heart of the lonely seafarer.

I am still the richest and most enviable of all, I, the loneliest one! For I *have possessed* you, and you possess me still. Tell me: who has ever had such rosy apples fall to him from the tree as I have?

I am still the heir of your love and its inheritance, blooming in your memory with many-colored, wild-growing virtues, O my dearest ones!

Ah, we were made to stay close to one another, you kind and strange marvels. You did not come to me and my longing like timid birds. No, you came as trusting ones to one who trusted!

Yes, you were made for faithfulness, as I am, and for tender eternities. Yet now I must name you by your faithlessness, you divine glances and fleeting glimpses. I have not yet learned any other name for you.

Truly, you died too early for me, you fugitives. Yet you did not flee from me, nor did I flee from you. We are innocent toward each other in our faithlessness.

To kill *me*, they strangled you, you singing birds of my hopes! Yes, it was at you, my dearest ones, that malice always shot its arrows, in order to hit my heart!

And they hit it! Because you were always dearest to me, my possession and what possessed me, *for that very reason* you had to die young, and far too early!

They shot the arrow at my most vulnerable point: at you, whose skin is like down, or more like a smile that dies at a single glance!

But I will say this to my enemies: What is any murder compared with what you have done to me!

You did me a worse wrong than any murder. You took from me what can never be recovered. This is what I say to you, my enemies!

Did you not kill the visions and dearest marvels of my youth? You took my playmates from me, those blessed spirits! To their memory I lay down this wreath and this curse.

This curse upon you, my enemies! Did you not cut my eternity short, as a note dies away on a cold night? It came to me barely for the twinkle of divine eyes, a fleeting glimpse!

Once, in a happy hour, my purity said: "Everything shall be divine to me."

Then you haunted me with foul phantoms. Ah, where has that happy hour fled now?

"All days shall be holy to me," the wisdom of my youth once said. Truly, that was the language of a joyful wisdom!

But then you, my enemies, stole my nights and sold them to sleepless torment. Ah, where has that joyful wisdom fled now?

Once I longed for happy omens. Then you led a monstrous owl across my path, an evil sign. Ah, where did my tender longing flee then?

Once I vowed to renounce all disgust. Then you turned those nearest and dearest to me into festering sores. Ah, where did my noblest vow flee then?

Once I walked blessed paths like a blind man. Then you threw filth in the blind man's way, and now he is disgusted by the old footpath.

And when I accomplished my hardest task and celebrated the triumph of my victories, you made those who loved me cry out that it was then that I hurt them most.

Truly, it was always your doing. You made my best honey bitter to me, and the labor of my best bees.

To my charity you always sent the most impudent beggars. Around my compassion you always crowded the incurably shameless. In this way you wounded the faith of my virtue.

And when I offered up what was holiest to me as a sacrifice, your "piety" immediately set its fatter offerings beside it, so that my holiest thing suffocated in the fumes of your fat.

And once I wanted to dance as I had never danced before. I wanted to dance beyond all the heavens. Then you seduced my favorite minstrel.

And he struck up a dreadful, gloomy tune. Alas, he blared in my ear like a mournful horn!

Murderous minstrel, instrument of evil, most innocent instrument! I was already standing ready for the best dance of all, and then you killed my rapture with your notes!

Only in dance do I know how to speak the parable of the highest things. And now my greatest parable has remained unspoken in my limbs!

My highest hope has remained unspoken and unfulfilled! And all the visions and consolations of my youth have died for me!

How did I ever bear it? How did I survive and overcome such wounds? How did my soul rise again out of those tombs?

Yes, there is something in me that cannot be wounded or buried, something that could split rocks apart. It is called *my will*. It goes its way silently, unchanged through the years.

It will walk its path on my feet, my old Will. Its nature is hard-hearted and invulnerable.

I am invulnerable only in my heel. You live there always, and you stay true to yourself, you most patient one! You have always burst every shackle of the tomb!

In you still lives everything in my youth that went unfulfilled. You sit here, as life and youth, full of hope on the yellow ruins of graves.

Yes, you are still the destroyer of all graves for me. Hail to you, my Will! And only where there are graves are there resurrections.

Thus sang Zarathustra.

## XXXIV. SELF-SURPASSING.

"Will to Truth": is that what you call the thing that drives you and sets you burning, you wisest ones?

I call your will a will to make all being thinkable!

You want to *make* all being thinkable, because you doubt, with good reason, whether it is already thinkable.

But it must adapt and bend itself to you! That is what your will wants. Being must become smooth and subject to the spirit, as its mirror and reflection.

That is your whole will, you wisest ones: a Will to Power. And it remains so even when you speak of good and evil, and of valuations.

You still want to create a world before which you can kneel. That is your ultimate hope and ecstasy.

The ignorant, of course, the people, are like a river on which a boat floats along. And in the boat sit the valuations, solemn and disguised.

You have set your will and your valuations on the river of becoming. What the people believe to be good and evil reveals to me an old Will to Power.

It was you, you wisest ones, who put such passengers in this boat and gave them pomp and proud names: you and your ruling Will!

Now the river carries your boat onward. It *must* carry it. It matters little if the rough wave foams and angrily resists its keel!

The river is not your danger, you wisest ones, nor the end of your good and evil. Your danger is that Will itself, the Will to Power: the inexhaustible, procreating will of life.

But so that you may understand my gospel of good and evil, I will tell you my gospel of life and of the nature of all living things.

I followed the living thing. I walked the broadest and the narrowest paths to learn its nature.

With a hundred-faced mirror I caught its glance when its mouth was closed, so that its eye might speak to me. And its eye spoke to me.

But wherever I found living things, I also heard the language of obedience. All living things are obeying things.

The second thing I heard was this: whatever cannot obey itself is commanded. That is the nature of living things.

The third thing I heard was this: commanding is harder than obeying. This is not only because the one who commands bears the burden of all who obey, and because this burden can easily crush him.

All commanding seemed to me an experiment and a risk. Whenever a living thing commands, it puts itself at risk.

Yes, even when it commands itself, it must pay for its commanding. It must become the judge, the avenger, and the victim of its own law.

How does this happen? I asked myself. What persuades the living thing to obey, and to command, and even to be obedient while commanding?

Listen now to my word, you wisest ones! Test it seriously, and see whether I have crept into the heart of life itself, and into the roots of its heart!

Wherever I found a living thing, I found Will to Power. Even in the will of the servant I found the will to be master.

That the weaker should serve the stronger: his own will persuades him of this, because he wants to be master over someone weaker still. That one delight he will not give up.

And just as the lesser surrenders to the greater so that he may have delight and power over the least of all, so even the greatest surrenders himself and stakes his life for the sake of power.

This is the surrender of the greatest: to run risk and danger, and to play dice with death.

And where there is sacrifice and service and loving glances, there too is the will to be master. The weaker then slips by hidden paths into the fortress, into the very heart of the mightier one, and there steals power.

And Life herself told me this secret. "Behold," she said, "I am *that which must always surpass itself*.

Of course, you call it the will to procreate, or the drive toward a goal, toward what is higher, more distant, more manifold. But all of that is one and the same secret.

I would rather perish than renounce this one thing. And truly, wherever there is perishing and the falling of leaves, see, there Life sacrifices itself for power!

I must be struggle and becoming and purpose and conflicting purposes. Ah, whoever guesses my will also guesses on what *crooked* paths it must walk!

Whatever I create, and however much I love it, I must soon turn against it and against my love. That is what my will wills.

And even you, seeker of knowledge, are only a path and footstep of my will. Truly, my Will to Power walks even on the feet of your Will to Truth!

He certainly missed the truth who shot the formula 'will to existence' at it. That will does not exist!

For what does not exist cannot will. But what already exists, how could it still strive for existence?

Only where there is life is there also will. But it is not will to life. It is, so I teach you, Will to Power!

The living being values many things more highly than life itself. But out of that very valuing speaks the Will to Power!"

So Life once taught me. And with this, you wisest ones, I solve for you the riddle of your hearts.

Truly, I tell you: a good and evil that would last forever does not exist! Of its own accord it must surpass itself again and again.

With your values and formulas of good and evil you exercise power, you who assign values. That is your hidden love, and the sparkling, trembling, and overflowing of your souls.

But a stronger power grows out of your values, and a new self-surpassing. It breaks both egg and eggshell.

And whoever must be a creator in good and evil must first, truly, be a destroyer, and smash values to pieces.

Thus the greatest evil belongs to the greatest good. But that is the creative good.

Let us *speak* of this, you wisest ones, even if speaking is bad. Silence is worse. All suppressed truths become poisonous.

And let everything break apart that can be broken by our truths! Many a house is still to be built!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXXV. THE SUBLIME ONES.

Calm is the floor of my sea. Who would guess that it hides playful monsters?

My depths are unmoved, yet they sparkle with swimming riddles and laughter.

Today I saw a sublime one, a solemn one, a penitent of the spirit. Oh, how my soul laughed at his ugliness!

He stood with his chest thrust out, like someone holding in a breath. That is how he stood, the sublime one, and in silence.

He was hung all over with ugly truths, the spoils of his hunting, and rich in torn clothing. Many thorns hung on him too, but I saw no rose.

He had not yet learned laughter and beauty. This hunter came back gloomy from the forest of knowledge.

He came home from his fight with wild beasts. Yet a wild beast still gazes out of his seriousness, an unconquered wild beast!

He always stands like a tiger about to spring. But I do not like these tense souls. My taste is unkind toward all these self-absorbed ones.

And you tell me, friends, that there is no arguing about taste? But all of life is an argument about taste and tasting!

Taste is at once the weight, the scales, and the one who weighs. And pity any living thing that tries to live without arguing over weight, scales, and weigher!

If this sublime one should grow tired of his sublimity, only then will his beauty begin. Only then will I taste him and find him to my liking.

And only when he turns away from himself will he leap over his own shadow, and indeed, leap into *his* sun.

He sat far too long in the shade. The cheeks of this penitent of the spirit grew pale, and he nearly starved on his expectations.

Contempt is still in his eye, and disgust hides in his mouth. True, he is resting now, but he has not yet rested in the sunshine.

He should do as the ox does. His happiness should smell of the earth, and not of contempt for the earth.

I would like to see him as a white ox, snorting and lowing as he walks ahead of the plow. And his lowing should also praise everything earthly!

His face is still dark, and the shadow of his hand dances across it. The meaning in his eyes is still overshadowed.

His deed itself is still the shadow on him: his doing darkens the doer. He has not yet overcome his deed.

True, I love the ox's shoulders on him. But now I also want to see the eye of the angel.

He must also unlearn his hero's will. He should be lifted up, and not merely sublime. The very air of the heavens should raise him, the one without will!

He has tamed monsters and solved riddles. But he should also redeem his monsters and riddles, and transform them into heavenly children.

His knowledge has not yet learned to smile and to be free of jealousy. His rushing passion has not yet grown calm in beauty.

Indeed, his longing should not stop and vanish in being satisfied, but in beauty! Grace belongs to the generosity of the great-souled.

With his arm resting across his head: that is how the hero should rest. And that is how he should also overcome his rest.

But for the hero, of all people, *beauty* is the hardest thing. No passionate will can reach beauty.

A little more, a little less: here that is a great deal. Here it is the most of all.

To stand with relaxed muscles and with the will unharnessed: that is the hardest thing for all of you sublime ones!

When power becomes gracious and comes down into the visible world, I call that descent beauty.

And from no one do I want beauty as much as from you, you powerful one. Let your goodness be your final conquest of yourself.

I believe you capable of every evil. That is why I ask the good of you.

Truly, I have often laughed at the weaklings who think they are good because their paws are crippled!

You should strive for the virtue of the pillar. The higher it rises, the more beautiful and graceful it becomes, but inside it grows harder and better able to bear weight.

Yes, you sublime one, one day you too will be beautiful, and you will hold up the mirror to your own beauty.

Then your soul will shiver with divine desires, and there will be worship even in your vanity!

For this is the secret of the soul: only when the hero has abandoned it does the superhero approach it, in dreams.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXXVI. THE LAND OF CULTURE.

I flew too far into the future, and horror seized me.

And when I looked around, I saw that time was my only contemporary.

Then I flew back, homeward, and faster and faster. So I came to you, you people of today, and into the land of culture.

For the first time I brought an eye that could see you, and a good will toward you. Truly, I came with longing in my heart.

But how did it go for me? Frightened as I was, I still had to laugh! My eye had never seen anything so many-colored!

I laughed and laughed, while my foot was still trembling, and my heart too. "This must be the home of all the paint pots," I said.

There you sat, to my astonishment, you people of today, with fifty patches of paint on your faces and limbs!

And with fifty mirrors around you, flattering your play of colors and repeating it back!

Truly, you could wear no better masks than your own faces, you people of today! Who could possibly *recognize* you?

You are written all over with the signs of the past, and those signs are themselves scribbled over with new signs. In this way you have hidden yourselves well from anyone trying to decipher you!

Even someone who can examine the inner organs would hardly believe you have any inside you! You seem to be baked out of paints and glued-together scraps.

All ages and peoples look out, many-colored, from behind your veils. All customs and beliefs speak, many-colored, through your gestures.

Anyone who stripped you of your veils and wrappings, your paints and gestures, would have just enough left over to scare the crows.

Truly, I myself am the frightened crow that once saw you naked and without paint. I flew away when the skeleton made eyes at me.

I would rather be a day laborer in the underworld among the shades of the dead! Even the dwellers of the underworld are fatter and fuller than you!

This, yes, this is bitterness to my gut: that I can bear you neither naked nor clothed, you people of today!

Everything strange in the future, everything that makes stray birds shiver, is truly more homelike and familiar than your "reality."

For this is what you say: "We are completely real, without faith or superstition." That is how you preen yourselves, alas, even without feathers!

And indeed, how *could* you believe, you many-colored ones? You are paintings of everything that has ever been believed!

You are walking refutations of belief itself, and a dislocation of all thought. *Untrustworthy ones*: that is what I call you, you real ones!

All the ages chatter against one another in your minds. And the dreams and chatter of all those ages were more real than your waking state!

You are barren, and *that* is why you lack belief. But whoever had to create always had prophetic dreams and signs in the stars, and believed in believing!

You are half-open doors where gravediggers wait. And this is *your* reality: "Everything deserves to perish."

Oh, how you stand before me, you barren ones, and how thin your ribs are! Many of you have surely noticed it yourselves.

Many a one has said: "Surely some god secretly stole something from me while I slept? Truly, enough to make himself a girl out of it!

"Astonishing is the poverty of my ribs!" So many a person of today has spoken.

Yes, I find you laughable, you people of today! Especially when you marvel at yourselves!

And woe to me if I could not laugh at your marveling, and had to swallow everything repulsive on your plates!

As it is, I will take you lightly, since I have heavy things to carry. What does it matter if beetles and May bugs land on my load as well?

Truly, my load will not grow heavier because of it! And my great weariness will not come from you, you people of today.

Ah, where shall I climb now with my longing? From every mountain I look out for fatherlands and motherlands.

But I have found a home nowhere. I am restless in every city, and leaving at every gate.

The people of today, to whom my heart recently drove me, are strangers to me and a mockery. And I am exiled from fatherlands and motherlands.

So I love only my *children's land*, the undiscovered land in the farthest sea. I tell my sails to search and search for it.

To my children I will make amends for being the child of my fathers, and to all the future for *this* present!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXXVII. IMMACULATE PERCEPTION.

When the moon rose yesterday evening, I imagined it was about to give birth to a sun, so broad and swollen did it lie on the horizon.

But its pregnancy was a lie. I would sooner believe in the man in the moon than in the woman.

Though there is little of a man in him either, that timid night-prowler. Truly, he creeps over the rooftops with a bad conscience.

For he is greedy and jealous, the monk in the moon. He covets the earth and all the joys of lovers.

No, I do not like him, that tomcat on the rooftops! I find hateful all those who slink around half-closed windows!

He creeps along piously and silently on carpets of stars. But I do not like light-stepping human feet on which not even a spur jingles.

Every honest person's step speaks. But the cat steals along over the ground. See, the moon comes along like a cat, and dishonestly.

I tell this parable to you sentimental hypocrites, to you "pure knowers"! I call you the greedy ones!

You too love the earth and earthly things. I have seen through you! But there is shame in your love, and a bad conscience. You are like the moon!

Your mind has been persuaded to despise earthly things, but not your gut. And your gut is the strongest thing in you!

Now your mind is ashamed to serve your gut, and it takes side roads and lying roads to escape its own shame.

"For me the highest thing would be," your lying mind says to itself, "to gaze at life without desire, and not like a dog with its tongue hanging out.

"To be happy in gazing, with a dead will, free of the grip and greed of selfishness, cold and ash-gray all over, but with drunken moon-eyes!

"That would be the dearest thing to me," so the seduced one seduces himself, "to love the earth as the moon loves it, and to feel its beauty with the eye alone.

"And this I call *immaculate* perception of all things: to want nothing from them except to be allowed to lie before them like a mirror with a hundred facets."

Oh, you sentimental hypocrites, you greedy ones! You lack innocence in your desire, and that is why you now slander desire itself!

Truly, you do not love the earth as creators, as begetters, or as those who rejoice!

Where is innocence? Where there is a will to beget. And whoever seeks to create beyond himself has, for me, the purest will.

Where is beauty? Where I *must will* with my whole will, where I will love and perish, so that an image does not remain merely an image.

Loving and perishing have rhymed since eternity. The will to love means being ready for death as well. So I speak to you cowards!

But now your unmanned ogling claims to be "contemplation"! And whatever can be examined with cowardly eyes is to be baptized "beautiful"! Oh, you defilers of noble names!

But this shall be your curse, you immaculate ones, you pure knowers: you shall never give birth, even though you lie broad and swollen on the horizon!

Truly, you fill your mouths with noble words. And we are supposed to believe that your hearts overflow, you swindlers?

But *my* words are poor, despised, stammering words. I gladly pick up what falls from the table at your feasts.

Yet with them I can still tell the truth to hypocrites! Yes, my fish bones, shells, and prickly leaves shall tickle the noses of hypocrites!

There is always bad air around you and your feasts. Your lustful thoughts, your lies, and your secrets hang right there in the air!

Only dare to believe in yourselves, in yourselves and in your inner parts! Whoever does not believe in himself always lies.

You "pure ones" have hung a god's mask in front of you. And your loathsome coiling snake has crawled into that god's mask.

Truly, you deceive, you "contemplative ones"! Even Zarathustra was once fooled by your godlike surface. He did not guess at the coiled serpent stuffed inside it.

I once thought I saw a god's soul playing in your games, you pure knowers! Once I dreamed of no better arts than yours!

Distance hid from me the serpents' filth and stench, and hid the lizard's cunning prowling lustfully nearby.

But I came *near* to you, and then day came to me. And now it is coming to you. The moon's love affair is over!

Look there! It stands caught and pale before the rosy dawn!

For she is already coming, the glowing one. *Her* love for the earth is coming! All the sun's love is innocence and creative desire!

Look how impatiently she comes over the sea! Do you not feel the thirst and the hot breath of her love?

She wants to suck at the sea and drink its depths up to her height. Now the desire of the sea rises with its thousand breasts.

It wants to be kissed and drunk by the sun's thirst. It wants to become vapor, and height, and a path of light, and light itself!

Truly, I love life as the sun does, and all deep seas.

And this is what knowledge means TO ME: everything that is deep shall rise up—to my height!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXXVIII. SCHOLARS.

While I lay asleep, a sheep ate at the ivy wreath on my head. As it ate, it said: "Zarathustra is no longer a scholar."

It said this and walked away, clumsy and proud. A child told me about it.

I like to lie here where the children play, beside the crumbling wall, among thistles and red poppies.

To the children I am still a scholar, and to the thistles and red poppies too. They are innocent, even in their wickedness.

But to the sheep I am no longer a scholar. That is what my fate wills, and I bless it for that!

For this is the truth: I have left the house of the scholars, and I slammed the door behind me as well.

My soul sat hungry at their table for too long. I never picked up their knack for investigating, which they practice the way others have a knack for cracking nuts.

I love freedom, and the air over fresh soil. I would rather sleep on ox hides than on their honors and dignities.

I am too hot, too scorched by my own thought, and it often nearly takes my breath away. Then I have to go out into the open air, away from all dusty rooms.

But they sit cool in the cool shade. In everything they want to be mere spectators, and they avoid sitting where the sun burns on the steps.

They are like people who stand in the street and gape at passersby. In the same way, they wait and gape at thoughts that others have thought.

If you take hold of them, they give off dust like flour sacks, without meaning to. But who would guess that their dust came from grain, and from the yellow delight of the summer fields?

When they present themselves as wise, their petty sayings and truths chill me. Their wisdom often has a smell as if it came from a swamp. Truly, I have even heard the frog croak in it!

They are clever, and they have nimble fingers. What can MY simplicity claim beside their many skills? Their fingers understand all threading, knitting, and weaving. That is how they make the stockings of the spirit!

They are good clockworks. Just be careful to wind them up properly! Then they tell the time without error, and make a modest noise as they do it.

They work like millstones and like pestles. Just throw them seed grain! They know well how to grind grain fine and make white dust out of it.

They keep a sharp eye on one another and do not trust each other much. Inventive in small tricks, they lie in wait for anyone whose knowledge walks on lame feet. They wait like spiders.

I always saw them prepare their poison carefully, and they always wore glass gloves on their fingers while doing it.

They also know how to play with loaded dice. I found them playing so eagerly that they sweated over it.

We are strangers to each other, and their virtues are even more distasteful to me than their lies and their loaded dice.

When I lived with them, I lived above them. That is why they came to dislike me.

They want to hear nothing of anyone walking over their heads. So they put wood and earth and rubbish between me and their heads.

In this way they muffled the sound of my steps. So far, the most learned have heard me least of all.

They put all of mankind's faults and weaknesses between themselves and me. In their houses they call this a "false ceiling."

But I still walk with my thoughts ABOVE their heads. Even if I walked on my own mistakes, I would still be above them and their heads.

For human beings are NOT equal: so speaks justice. And what I will, THEY may not will!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XXXIX. POETS.

"Since I have come to know the body better," Zarathustra said to one of his disciples, "the spirit has been spirit to me only in a symbolic sense. And everything 'imperishable' is also only a simile."

"I have heard you say that once before," the disciple answered, "and then you added: 'But the poets lie too much.' Why did you say that the poets lie too much?"

"Why?" said Zarathustra. "You ask why? I am not one of those who may be asked for their Why.

Is my experience only from yesterday? It was long ago that I experienced the reasons for my opinions.

Wouldn't I have to be a barrel of memory if I wanted to carry my reasons around with me as well?

It is already too much for me just to keep hold of my opinions, and many a bird flies away.

And sometimes I also find a stray creature in my dovecote. It is foreign to me, and it trembles when I lay my hand on it.

But what did Zarathustra once say to you? That the poets lie too much? But Zarathustra is also a poet.

Do you believe that he spoke the truth there? Why do you believe it?"

The disciple answered: "I believe in Zarathustra." But Zarathustra shook his head and smiled.

Belief does not make me holy, he said, and least of all belief in myself.

But suppose someone did say, in all seriousness, that the poets lie too much. He was right: WE do lie too much.

We also know too little, and we are poor learners. So we are forced to lie.

And which of us poets has not doctored his wine? Many a poisonous concoction has been brewed in our cellars, and many an unspeakable thing has been done there.

And because we know little, we take heartfelt pleasure in the poor in spirit, especially when they are young women!

We even crave the things that old women tell one another in the evening. We call this the eternal feminine in us.

We act as if there were a special secret path to knowledge, one that GETS BLOCKED for anyone who actually learns something. That is why we believe in the common people and their "wisdom."

All poets believe this: that anyone who pricks up his ears while lying in the grass or on lonely slopes learns something about the things between heaven and earth.

And when tender feelings come over them, the poets always think that nature herself is in love with them,

and that she creeps up to their ear to whisper secrets and loving flatteries into it. They preen and pride themselves on this before all mortals!

Ah, there are so many things between heaven and earth that only the poets have dreamed of!

And especially ABOVE the heavens: for all gods are poets' symbols, poets' sophistries!

Truly, we are always drawn upward, which means to the realm of the clouds. On the clouds we set our gaudy puppets, and then we call them gods and Supermen.

Aren't they just light enough for those seats, all these gods and Supermen?

Ah, how weary I am of everything inadequate that is insisted upon as real! Ah, how weary I am of the poets!

When Zarathustra spoke this way, his disciple resented it but said nothing. Zarathustra was silent too. His gaze turned inward, as if it were looking into the far distance. At last he sighed and drew a breath.

I belong to today and to the past, he then said. But there is something in me that belongs to tomorrow, and the day after, and the time still to come.

I grew weary of the poets, the old ones and the new ones. They all seem superficial to me, shallow seas.

They did not think far enough into the depths. That is why their feeling did not reach the bottom.

A little sensual pleasure and a little boredom: so far, that has been the best of their reflection.

All the jingling and jangling of their harps seems to me like ghostly breathing and ghostly flitting. What have they ever known of the passion in musical tones?

They are also not pure enough for me. They all muddy their water so that it will seem deep.

They would like to prove themselves reconcilers this way. But to me they are go-betweens and mixers, half-and-half, and impure!

Ah, I did cast my net into their sea, hoping to catch good fish. But I always pulled up the head of some ancient god.

So the sea gave a stone to the hungry man. And the poets themselves may well have come from the sea.

Certainly, one finds pearls in them, and that makes them all the more like hard-shelled shellfish. And instead of a soul, I have often found salty slime in them.

They have also learned vanity from the sea. Is the sea not the peacock of peacocks?

It spreads out its tail even before the ugliest of all buffaloes. It never tires of its lace fan of silver and silk.

The buffalo glances at it with scorn. Its soul is close to the sand, closer still to the thicket, but closest of all to the swamp.

What do beauty and sea and peacock splendor mean to it! I speak this parable to the poets.

Truly, their spirit itself is the peacock of peacocks, and a sea of vanity!

The poet's spirit wants spectators, even if they are only buffaloes!

But I grew weary of this spirit, and I see the time coming when it will grow weary of itself.

Indeed, I have already seen the poets changed, with their gaze turned toward themselves.

I have seen penitents of the spirit appear. They grew out of the poets.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XL. GREAT EVENTS.

There is an island in the sea, not far from Zarathustra's Happy Isles, where a volcano is always smoking. The people, and especially the old women among them, say that this island stands like a rock in front of the gate of the underworld. They also say that the narrow path leading down to this gate runs through the volcano itself.

Around the time Zarathustra was staying on the Happy Isles, a ship anchored at the island with the smoking mountain, and the crew went ashore to shoot rabbits. Around noon, when the captain and his men had gathered again, they suddenly saw a man coming toward them through the air. A voice said clearly: "It is time! It is the highest time!"

The figure flew past quickly, like a shadow, toward the volcano. But when it was closest to them, they recognized with the greatest astonishment that it was Zarathustra. All of them except the captain had seen him before, and they loved him the way the people love: with love and awe in equal measure.

"Look!" said the old helmsman. "There goes Zarathustra to hell!"

Around the same time these sailors landed on the fire island, a rumor spread that Zarathustra had disappeared. When his friends were asked about it, they said he had boarded a ship at night without saying where he was going.

This caused some uneasiness. After three days, the story of the ship's crew was added to it, and then all the people said that the devil had taken Zarathustra.

His disciples laughed at this talk, of course. One of them even said: "I would sooner believe that Zarathustra has taken the devil." But deep in their hearts they were all full of anxiety and longing. So their joy was great when Zarathustra appeared among them on the fifth day.

And this is Zarathustra's account of his conversation with the fire-dog:

The earth, he said, has a skin, and this skin has diseases. One of these diseases, for example, is called "man."

Another of these diseases is called "the fire-dog." People have greatly deceived themselves about HIM, and have let themselves be deceived.

I crossed the sea to get to the bottom of this mystery. And I have seen the truth naked, truly! Barefoot up to the neck.

Now I know the truth about the fire-dog. I also know the truth about all the eruptive, subversive devils that not only old women fear.

"Come up out of your depth, fire-dog!" I cried. "Confess how deep that depth really is! Where does the stuff you snort up come from?

You drink heavily from the sea: your bitter eloquence gives that away! For a dog of the depths, you really take too much of your nourishment from the surface!

At most, I take you for the earth's ventriloquist. And whenever I have heard subversive, eruptive devils speak, I have found them just like you: bitter, lying, and shallow.

You all know how to roar and darken everything with ashes! You are the best of braggarts, and you have learned well enough the art of making dregs boil.

Wherever you are, there must always be dregs close at hand, and a great deal that is spongy, hollow, and compressed. All of it wants freedom.

You all roar 'Freedom' most eagerly. But I have unlearned my belief in 'great events' whenever there is a great deal of roaring and smoke around them.

And believe me, friend Hullabaloo! The greatest events are not our loudest hours, but our stillest ones.

The world does not revolve around the inventors of new noise. It revolves around the inventors of new values, and it revolves INAUDIBLY.

Just admit it! Little had ever happened once your noise and smoke had cleared. So what if a city turned into a mummy, and a statue lay in the mud!

And I also say this to those who topple statues: it is surely the greatest folly to throw salt into the sea, and statues into the mud.

The statue lay in the mud of your contempt. But it is precisely its law that out of contempt, its life and living beauty grow back!

It now rises again with more godlike features, seductive in its suffering. And truly, it will yet thank you for overthrowing it, you subverters!

But this is my advice to kings and churches, and to everything that is weak with age or with virtue: let yourselves be overthrown! Then you may come to life again, and virtue may come to you!—”

So I spoke before the fire-dog. Then he sullenly interrupted me and asked: “Church? What is that?”

“Church?” I answered. “That is a kind of state, and in fact the most deceitful kind. But keep quiet, you hypocritical dog! Surely you know your own species best!

Like you, the state is a hypocritical dog. Like you, it likes to speak with smoke and roaring, to make people believe, just as you do, that it speaks from the heart of things.

For the state wants, by every possible means, to be the most important creature on earth. And people believe that it is.”

When I had said this, the fire-dog behaved as if he were mad with envy. “What!” he cried. “The most important creature on earth? And people believe it?”

So much steam and so many terrible voices came out of his throat that I thought he would choke with rage and envy.

At last he grew calmer, and his panting subsided. As soon as he was quiet, however, I said with a laugh:

“You are angry, fire-dog. So I am right about you!

And so that I may keep on being right, listen to the story of another fire-dog. This one really does speak out of the heart of the earth.

His breath gives off gold and golden rain, for that is what his heart desires. What are ashes and smoke and hot dregs to him!

Laughter drifts from him like a many-colored cloud. He has no taste for your gargling and spewing and the cramps in your bowels!

But the gold and the laughter he takes out of the heart of the earth. For, so that you may know it: THE HEART OF THE EARTH IS MADE OF GOLD.”

When the fire-dog heard this, he could no longer bear to listen to me. Ashamed, he drew in his tail, said “bow-wow!” in a cowed voice, and crept down into his cave.—

So Zarathustra told it. But his disciples hardly listened to him, so eager were they to tell him about the sailors, the rabbits, and the flying man.

“What am I to make of this?” said Zarathustra. “Am I really a ghost?

But perhaps it was my shadow. You have surely heard something about the Wanderer and his Shadow?

One thing is certain, however: I must keep a tighter hold on it. Otherwise it will ruin my reputation.”

And once more Zarathustra shook his head and wondered. “What am I to make of this?” he said again.

“Why did the ghost cry, ‘It is time! It is high time!’

High time for what?”—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XLI. THE SOOTHSAYER.

“—And I saw a great sadness come over mankind. The best grew weary of their works.

A doctrine appeared, and a faith ran alongside it: ‘All is empty, all is the same, all has been before!’

And from every hill came the echo: ‘All is empty, all is the same, all has been before!’

Yes, we have brought in our harvest. But why have all our fruits turned rotten and brown? What fell from the evil moon last night?

All our labor was in vain. Our wine has turned to poison. The evil eye has scorched our fields and our hearts yellow.

We have all grown dry. If fire falls on us, we crumble into dust like ashes. Yes, we have made even fire weary.

All our springs have dried up, and even the sea has pulled back. All the ground tries to open up, but the depths refuse to swallow us!

‘Alas! Where is there still a sea deep enough to drown in?’ So our complaint sounds, out across the shallow swamps.

Truly, we have grown too weary even to die. Now we stay awake and go on living—in tombs.”

Zarathustra heard a soothsayer speak in this way. The prophecy touched his heart and changed him. He went about sorrowful and weary, and he became like the people the soothsayer had described.—

Truly, he said to his disciples, a little while longer, and the long twilight will come. Alas, how shall I keep my light alive through it!

May it not be smothered in this sorrow! It is meant to be a light for more distant worlds, and for the most distant nights as well!

So Zarathustra went about with a grieving heart. For three days he took no food or drink. He found no rest, and he lost the power of speech. At last he fell into a deep sleep.

His disciples sat around him through long night-watches. They waited anxiously to see whether he would wake, speak again, and recover from his affliction.

And this is the speech Zarathustra gave when he awoke. His voice, however, reached his disciples as if from far away:

Listen, I beg you, to the dream I dreamed, my friends, and help me work out its meaning!

This dream is still a riddle to me. Its meaning is hidden inside it, caged, and does not yet fly above it on free wings.

I dreamed that I had renounced all life. I had become a night-watchman and a guardian of graves, high up in the lonely mountain fortress of Death.

There I guarded his coffins. The musty vaults stood full of these trophies of his victory. Out of glass coffins, conquered life gazed back at me.

I breathed the smell of dust-covered eternities. My soul lay sultry and dust-covered. And who could have aired out his soul in such a place!

The brightness of midnight was always around me. Loneliness crouched beside her. And the third was a death-rattle stillness, the worst of my female companions.

I carried keys, the rustiest of all keys, and I knew how to open the most creaking of all gates with them.

When the two halves of the gate swung open, the sound ran through the long corridors like a bitterly angry croaking. This bird cried out ungraciously; it did not want to be woken.

But it was even more frightening, and more suffocating to the heart, when everything fell silent and still again, and I sat alone in that malicious silence.

So time passed for me and slipped away, if there was still such a thing as time. What do I know about it! But at last something happened that woke me.

Three times blows struck the gate like thunder. Three times the vaults echoed and howled back. Then I went to the gate.

Alpa! I cried. Who is carrying his ashes up the mountain? Alpa! Alpa! Who is carrying his ashes up the mountain?

I turned the key, pulled at the gate, and strained with all my strength. But it had not yet opened even a finger’s breadth.

Then a roaring wind tore its two halves apart. Whistling, whizzing, and piercing, it hurled a black coffin toward me.

And in the roaring and whistling and whizzing, the coffin burst open and spat out a thousand peals of laughter.

A thousand grotesque figures of children, angels, owls, fools, and butterflies as big as children laughed at me, mocked me, and roared at me.

I was horribly terrified. It knocked me to the ground. And I cried out in horror as I had never cried before.

But my own cry woke me, and I came to myself.—

So Zarathustra told his dream, and then he fell silent, for he did not yet know its interpretation. But the disciple whom he loved most rose quickly, took Zarathustra’s hand, and said:

“Your life itself interprets this dream for us, O Zarathustra!

Are you not yourself the shrill, whistling wind that bursts open the gates of the fortress of Death?

Are you not yourself the coffin full of many-colored mischief and angel-like caricatures of life?

Truly, Zarathustra comes into every tomb like a thousand peals of children’s laughter. He laughs at all those night-watchmen and grave-guardians, and at anyone else who rattles sinister keys.

You will frighten them with your laughter and knock them to the ground. Their fainting and recovering will prove your power over them.

And when the long twilight comes, and the deathly weariness, even then you will not vanish from our sky, you champion of life!

You have shown us new stars and new glories of the night. Truly, you have spread laughter itself over us like a many-colored canopy.

From now on, children’s laughter will always flow out of coffins. From now on, a strong wind will always come and triumph over all deathly weariness. You yourself are the pledge and the prophet of this!

Truly, it was THEM YOU DREAMED, your enemies. That was your most painful dream.

But just as you woke from them and came to yourself, so they will wake from themselves—and come to you!”

So the disciple spoke. Then all the others crowded around Zarathustra, took him by the hands, and tried to persuade him to leave his bed and his sadness and come back to them.

Zarathustra, however, sat upright on his couch with a distant look. He looked at his disciples like someone returning from a long stay abroad, and studied their faces. But he still did not recognize them.

When they lifted him and set him on his feet, however, his eyes suddenly changed. He understood everything that had happened. He stroked his beard and said in a strong voice:

“Well! There is a time for this. But see to it, my disciples, that we have a good meal, and without delay! That is how I mean to make up for bad dreams!

The soothsayer, however, shall eat and drink at my side. And truly, I will yet show him a sea in which he can drown himself!”—

Thus spoke Zarathustra. Then he gazed for a long time into the face of the disciple who had interpreted the dream, and shook his head.—

## XLII. REDEMPTION.

One day, as Zarathustra was crossing the great bridge, the cripples and beggars surrounded him, and a hunchback spoke to him:

“Look, Zarathustra! Even the people learn from you and come to believe in your teaching. But for them to believe in you completely, one thing is still needed: first of all, you must convince us cripples!

Here you have a fine selection, and truly, an opportunity with more than one forelock to seize it by! You can heal the blind and make the lame run.

And from the man who carries too much behind him, you could surely take away a little as well. That, I think, would be the right way to make the cripples believe in Zarathustra!”

But Zarathustra answered the one who had spoken: If you take the hump from the hunchback, you take away his spirit. So the people teach.

And if you give the blind man eyes, he sees too many bad things on earth, so that he curses whoever healed him.

But whoever makes the lame man run does him the greatest harm. For as soon as he can run, his vices run away with him. So the people teach about cripples.

And why should Zarathustra not also learn from the people, when the people learn from Zarathustra?

But since I have been among men, it seems the smallest thing to me that one person lacks an eye, another an ear, and a third a leg, and that others have lost their tongue, or their nose, or their head.

I see, and have seen, worse things. Some are so hideous that I would not want to speak of all of them, yet I cannot keep silent about some of them.

I mean men who lack everything, except that they have too much of one thing. They are nothing more than a big eye, or a big mouth, or a big belly, or something else that is big. I call such men reversed cripples.

When I came out of my solitude and crossed this bridge for the first time, I could not trust my eyes. I looked again and again, and at last I said: “That is an ear! An ear as big as a man!”

I looked still more closely. And something really did move beneath the ear, something pitifully small and poor and thin. In truth, this enormous ear sat on a small, thin stalk—and the stalk was a man!

Anyone who held a magnifying glass to his eyes could even make out a small, envious face, and a bloated little soul dangling from the stalk.

The people told me, however, that the big ear was not only a man, but a great man, a genius. But I never believed the people when they talked about great men. And I hold to my belief that it was a reversed cripple, who had too little of everything and too much of one thing.

When Zarathustra had spoken in this way to the hunchback, and to those whose mouthpiece and advocate the hunchback was, he turned to his disciples in deep dejection and said:

Truly, my friends, I walk among men as if among the fragments and limbs of human beings!

This is what is terrible to my eye: I find man broken into pieces and scattered about, as if on a battlefield or in a slaughterhouse.

And when my eye flees from the present to the past, it always finds the same thing: fragments and limbs and dreadful accidents—but no men!

The present and the past on earth—ah, my friends—that is MY most unbearable burden. And I would not know how to live if I were not also a seer of what is to come.

A seer, a man of purpose, a creator, a future in himself, and a bridge to the future—and alas, also something like a cripple standing on this bridge: all this is Zarathustra.

And you, too, have often asked yourselves: “Who is Zarathustra to us? What shall we call him?” And like me, you answered your questions with more questions.

Is he a promiser? Or a fulfiller? A conqueror? Or an heir? A harvest? Or a plowshare? A physician? Or one who has been healed?

Is he a poet? Or a truthful man? A liberator? Or a subjugator? A good man? Or an evil one?

I walk among men as among the fragments of the future: that future which I envision.

And all my creating and striving is to compose and gather into one whole what is fragment, riddle, and dreadful accident.

And how could I bear to be a man, if man were not also the composer, the reader of riddles, and the redeemer of chance!

To redeem what is past, and to transform every "It was" into "That is how I wanted it!": that alone is what I call redemption!

Will is the name of the liberator and bringer of joy. That is what I have taught you, my friends! But now learn this as well: the will itself is still a prisoner.

Willing liberates. But what do we call the thing that still keeps the liberator in chains?

"It was": that is the name of the will's teeth-gnashing and its loneliest affliction. Powerless against what has already been done, the will is an angry spectator of everything past.

The will cannot will backward. That it cannot break time and time's desire is the will's loneliest affliction.

Willing liberates. What, then, does willing itself devise to free itself from its affliction and to mock its prison?

Ah, every prisoner becomes a fool! And the imprisoned will also frees itself foolishly.

That time does not run backward is the will's resentment. "That which was" is the name of the stone it cannot roll away.

And so, out of resentment and bad temper, it rolls stones about. It takes revenge on whatever does not feel rage and bad temper as it does.

So the will, the liberator, became a torturer. It takes revenge on everything capable of suffering, because it cannot go backward.

This, yes, this alone is **revenge** itself: the will's aversion to time and its "It was."

Truly, a great folly lives in our will. And it became a curse to all humanity that this folly acquired spirit!

**The spirit of revenge**: my friends, that has so far been humanity's best reflection. And wherever there was suffering, people claimed there must also be punishment.

"Punishment" is what revenge calls itself. With a lying word it pretends to have a good conscience.

And the one who wills suffers too, because he cannot will backward. So willing itself, and all of life, was declared to be punishment!

Then cloud after cloud rolled over the spirit, until at last madness preached: "Everything passes away; therefore everything deserves to pass away!"

"And this itself is justice, the law of time: that time must devour its own children." So madness preached.

"Things are ordered morally, according to justice and punishment. Oh, where is there deliverance from the flow of things and from the 'existence' of punishment?" So madness preached.

"Can there be deliverance when there is eternal justice? Alas, the stone 'It was' cannot be rolled away. So all punishments must be eternal too!" So madness preached.

"No deed can be annihilated. How could punishment undo it! This, this is what is eternal in the 'existence' of punishment: that existence itself must be deed and guilt, eternally repeated!

"Unless the will should at last redeem itself, and willing become not-willing—" But you know this fable-song of madness, my brothers!

I led you away from those fable-songs when I taught you: "The will is a creator."

Every "It was" is a fragment, a riddle, a dreadful accident, until the creating will says to it: "But that is how I wanted it."

Until the creating will says to it: "But that is how I will it! That is how I shall will it!"

But has it ever spoken this way? And when does this happen? Has the will been unharnessed from its own folly?

Has the will become its own redeemer and bringer of joy? Has it unlearned the spirit of revenge and all its teeth-gnashing?

And who has taught it reconciliation with time, and something higher than any reconciliation?

The will that is the will to power must will something higher than any reconciliation. But how does that happen? Who has taught it to will backward as well?

But at this point in his speech, Zarathustra suddenly stopped, and he looked like someone in the greatest alarm. With terror in his eyes he gazed at his disciples. His glances pierced their thoughts, and the thoughts behind their thoughts, like arrows. But after a short while he laughed again and said, calmed:

"It is hard to live among people, because silence is so hard, especially for a chatterbox."

So spoke Zarathustra. The hunchback, however, had been listening to the conversation, and he had covered his face the whole time. But when he heard Zarathustra laugh, he looked up with curiosity and said slowly:

"But why does Zarathustra speak differently to us than to his disciples?"

Zarathustra answered: "What is surprising about that! With hunchbacks one may well speak in a hunchbacked way!"

"Very well," said the hunchback, "and with pupils one may well tell tales out of school.

"But why does Zarathustra speak differently to his pupils than to himself?"

## XLIII. MANLY PRUDENCE.

It is not the height that is terrifying, but the slope!

On the slope the gaze plunges **downward** while the hand reaches **upward**. There the heart grows dizzy from its double will.

Ah, friends, can you also guess my heart's double will?

This, this is **my** slope and my danger: my gaze plunges toward the summit, while my hand would gladly clutch at the depths and lean on them!

My will clings to man. I bind myself to man with chains, because I am pulled upward toward the Superman, and that is where my other will is drawn.

**That is why** I live blindly among people, as if I did not know them: so that my hand does not entirely lose its faith in something firm.

"I do not know you humans." This darkness, and this consolation, often surround me.

I sit at the gateway where every rogue passes, and I ask: Who wants to deceive me?

This is my first manly prudence: I allow myself to be deceived, so that I need not be on guard against deceivers.

Ah, if I were on guard against people, how could man be an anchor for my ball! I would be pulled upward and away too easily!

This is the providence that rules my fate: that I must be without foresight.

Whoever does not want to waste away among people must learn to drink from every glass. Whoever wants to stay clean among people must know how to wash even with dirty water.

And so I often said to myself for comfort: "Courage! Cheer up, old heart! A misfortune has failed to strike you. Enjoy that as your good fortune!"

This, however, is my second manly prudence: I am more patient with the **vain** than with the proud.

Is not wounded vanity the mother of all tragedies? But where pride is wounded, something better than pride grows.

For life to be good to watch, its game must be played well. And for that it needs good actors.

I have found all the vain to be good actors. They perform, and they want people to enjoy watching them. All their spirit lies in this wish.

They put themselves on stage; they invent themselves. Near them I like to watch life, for it cures my melancholy.

So I am patient with the vain, because they are the physicians of my melancholy, and they keep me attached to man as to a play.

And besides, who grasps the full depth of the vain man's modesty! I am well disposed toward him, and I sympathize with him, because of his modesty.

He wants to learn his belief in himself from you. He feeds on your glances; he eats praise out of your hands.

He even believes your lies when you lie flatteringly about him, for deep down his heart sighs: "What am I?"

And if true virtue is the kind that is unaware of itself, well, the vain man is unaware of his modesty!

This, however, is my third manly prudence: your timidity does not spoil my taste for the **wicked**.

I am happy to see the marvels that the warm sun hatches: tigers and palms and rattlesnakes.

Among humans, too, there is a beautiful brood of the warm sun, and there is much that is marvelous in the wicked.

Indeed, just as your wisest did not seem so very wise to me, I also found that human wickedness falls short of its reputation.

And often I asked, shaking my head: Why do you still bother to rattle, you rattlesnakes?

Truly, there is still a future even for evil! And the hottest south is still undiscovered by man.

How many things are now called the worst wickedness that are only twelve feet wide and three months long! But some day greater dragons will come into the world.

For the Superman must not lack his dragon, the superdragon worthy of him. For that, much warm sun must still glow on damp virgin forests!

Your wildcats must first evolve into tigers, and your poison toads into crocodiles, for the good hunter shall have a good hunt!

And truly, you good and just! There is much in you to laugh at, and especially your fear of what has until now been called "the devil"!

Your souls are so foreign to what is great that the Superman would be **frightful** to you in his goodness!

And you wise and knowing ones, you would flee from the sun-glow of the wisdom in which the Superman joyfully bathes his nakedness!

You highest men I have come to know! This is my doubt about you, and my secret laughter: I suspect you would call my Superman a devil!

Ah, I grew tired of these highest and best ones. From their "height" I longed to go up, out, and away to the Superman!

A horror came over me when I saw these best ones naked. Then wings grew on me, to soar away into distant futures.

Into more distant futures, into hotter souths than any artist ever dreamed of: there, where gods are ashamed of all clothing!

But I want to see **you** disguised, you neighbors and fellow men, well dressed and vain and dignified, as "the good and just."

And I myself will sit among you disguised, so that I may **mistake** both you and myself. For that is my last manly prudence.

So spoke Zarathustra.

## XLIV. THE STILLEST HOUR.

What has happened to me, my friends? You see me troubled, driven out, obedient against my will, ready to go. Alas, ready to go away from **you**!

Yes, Zarathustra must once more return to his solitude. But this time the bear goes back to his cave without joy!

What has happened to me? Who orders this? Ah, my angry mistress wants it so; she spoke to me. Have I ever told you her name?

Yesterday, toward evening, **my stillest hour** spoke to me. That is the name of my terrible mistress.

And this is how it happened. I must tell you everything, so that your hearts do not harden against the one who is suddenly leaving!

Do you know the terror of someone who is falling asleep?

He is terrified down to his toes, because the ground gives way beneath him and the dream begins.

I tell you this as a parable. Yesterday, at the stillest hour, the ground gave way beneath me: the dream began.

The hour hand moved on, and the clock of my life drew breath. Never had I heard such stillness around me, so that my heart was terrified.

Then something spoke to me without a voice: "**You know it, Zarathustra?**"

I cried out in terror at this whisper, and the blood drained from my face. But I said nothing.

Then once more something spoke to me without a voice: "You know it, Zarathustra, but you do not speak it!"

At last I answered, like someone defiant: "Yes, I know it, but I will not speak it!"

Then something spoke to me again without a voice: "You **will** not, Zarathustra? Is this true? Do not hide behind your defiance!"

And I wept and trembled like a child, and said: "Ah, I would like to, but how can I do it! Spare me only this! It is beyond my strength!"

Then something spoke to me again without a voice: "What does it matter about you, Zarathustra! Speak your word, and succumb!"

And I answered: "Ah, is it **my** word? Who am I? I am waiting for someone worthier. I am not worthy even to succumb to it."

Then something spoke to me again without a voice: "What does it matter about you? You are not yet humble enough for me. Humility has the toughest skin."

And I answered: "What has the skin of my humility not endured! I live at the foot of my height. How high my summits are, no one has yet told me. But I know my valleys well."

Then something spoke to me again without a voice: "O Zarathustra, whoever has to move mountains also moves valleys and lowlands."

And I answered: "So far my word has not moved mountains, and what I have spoken has not reached people. I did go to people, but I have not yet reached them."

Then something spoke to me again without a voice: "What do you know about **that**! The dew falls on the grass when the night is most silent."

And I answered: "They mocked me when I found my own path and walked it, and my feet certainly trembled then.

"And this is what they said to me: You lost the path before, and now you are forgetting how to walk as well!"

Then something spoke to me again without a voice: "What does their mockery matter! You are one who has unlearned how to obey. Now you shall command!

"Do you not know who is most needed by everyone? The one who commands great things.

"To carry out great things is hard. But the harder task is to command great things.

"This is your most unforgivable stubbornness: you have the power, and you will not rule."

And I answered: "I lack the lion's voice for all commanding."

Then something spoke to me again, as a whisper: "It is the stillest words that bring the storm. Thoughts that come on doves' feet guide the world.

"O Zarathustra, you shall go as a shadow of what is to come. That is how you will command, and in commanding you will lead the way."

And I answered: "I am ashamed."

Then once again something spoke to me without a voice: "You must still become a child, and be without shame.

"The pride of youth is still on you. You became young late. But whoever wants to become a child must overcome even his youth."—

And I thought about it for a long while, and trembled. But at last I said what I had said at first: "I will not."

Then laughter broke out all around me. Ah, how that laughter tore at my insides and cut into my heart!

And for the last time it was said to me: "O Zarathustra, your fruits are ripe, but you are not ripe for your fruits!

"So you must go back into solitude, for you must still grow mellow."—

And again there was laughter, and then it fled. After that it grew still around me, as if with a double stillness. But I lay on the ground, and sweat poured from my limbs.

—Now you have heard everything, and why I must return to my solitude. I have kept nothing hidden from you, my friends.

But you have also heard this from me: WHO is still the most reserved of men—and means to remain so!

Ah, my friends! I would have something more to say to you! I would have something more to give you! Why do I not give it? Am I a miser, then?—

But when Zarathustra had spoken these words, the force of his pain and the sense that he would soon be parted from his friends overwhelmed him, so that he wept aloud. No one knew how to comfort him. In the night, however, he went away alone and left his friends.

## THIRD PART.

"You look up when you long to be exalted, and I look down because I am exalted.

"Who among you can laugh and be exalted at the same time?

"Whoever climbs the highest mountains laughs at all tragic plays and tragic realities."—ZARATHUSTRA, I., "Reading and Writing."

## XLV. THE WANDERER.

Then, around midnight, Zarathustra set off across the ridge of the island, so that he would reach the other coast early in the morning, because he meant to set sail from there. There was a good anchorage on that coast, where foreign ships also liked to anchor. These ships took on many people who wanted to cross over from the Happy Isles.

As Zarathustra climbed the mountain, he thought along the way about his many solitary wanderings since his youth, and about how many mountains and ridges and summits he had already climbed.

I am a wanderer and a mountain-climber, he said to his heart. I do not love the plains, and it seems I cannot sit still for long.

And whatever may still come to me as fate and experience, there will be wandering in it, and mountain-climbing. In the end, one experiences only oneself.

The time is past when mere accidents could happen to me. What COULD still fall to my lot that is not already my own?

It only returns; it comes home to me at last—my own Self, and those parts of it that have long been abroad, scattered among things and accidents.

And I know one thing more: I now stand before my last summit, and before what has been saved for me the longest. Ah, I must climb my hardest path! Ah, I have begun my loneliest wandering!

But whoever is of my nature does not avoid such an hour: the hour that tells him, Only now are you going the way to your greatness! Summit and abyss—these are now joined together!

You are going the way to your greatness: what was until now your last danger has now become your last refuge!

You are going the way to your greatness: now your best courage must be that there is no longer any path behind you!

You are going the way to your greatness: here no one shall sneak after you! Your own foot has erased the path behind you, and above it is written: Impossibility.

And if from now on all ladders fail you, then you must learn to climb on your own head. How else could you climb upward?

On your own head, and beyond your own heart! Now what is gentlest in you must become the hardest.

Whoever has always indulged himself greatly grows sick at last from his own indulgence. Praise be to what makes us hardy! I do not praise the land where butter and honey—flow!

To learn TO LOOK AWAY FROM oneself is necessary in order to see MANY THINGS. Every mountain-climber needs this hardiness.

But whoever, in seeking knowledge, presses his eyes too close upon things—how can he ever see more of anything than its foreground!

But you, O Zarathustra, want to see the ground of everything, and its background. So you must climb even above yourself—up, upward, until even your stars lie BENEATH you!

Yes! To look down on myself, and even on my stars: only that would I call my SUMMIT. That is what has remained for me as my LAST summit!—

So Zarathustra spoke to himself as he climbed, comforting his heart with harsh maxims, for his heart was sore as never before. And when he reached the top of the ridge, there lay the other sea, spread out before him. He stood still and was silent for a long time. The night at this height was cold, and clear and starry.

I recognize my destiny, he said at last, sadly. Very well! I am ready. Now my last loneliness has begun.

Ah, this dark, sad sea below me! Ah, this dark, sullen night! Ah, fate and sea! To you I must now GO DOWN!

I stand before my highest mountain, and before my longest wandering. Therefore I must first go deeper down than I ever climbed:

—Deeper down into pain than I ever climbed, even into its darkest flood! So my fate wills it. Very well! I am ready.

Where do the highest mountains come from? I once asked. Then I learned that they come out of the sea.

That testimony is written in their stones, and on the walls of their summits. Out of the deepest the highest must rise to its height.—

So Zarathustra spoke on the cold mountain ridge. But when he came near the sea, and at last stood alone among the cliffs, he had grown weary from his journey, and more full of longing than ever before.

Everything is still asleep, he said; even the sea is asleep. Its eye gazes at me drowsily and strangely.

But it breathes warmly—I feel it. And I feel too that it is dreaming. It tosses dreamily on hard pillows.

Listen! Listen! How it groans with evil memories! Or with evil expectations?

Ah, I am sad along with you, you dark monster, and angry with myself even for your sake.

Ah, that my hand is not strong enough! How gladly I would free you from evil dreams!—

And as Zarathustra spoke this way, he laughed at himself with melancholy and bitterness. What, Zarathustra! he said. Will you even sing comfort to the sea?

Ah, you lovable fool, Zarathustra, you too-trusting one! But you have always been this way: you have always approached everything terrible with confidence.

You would pet every monster. A whiff of warm breath, a little soft tuft of fur on its paw—and at once you were ready to love it and coax it near.

LOVE is the danger of the loneliest one: love for anything, IF ONLY IT IS ALIVE! Laughable, truly, are my folly and my modesty in love!—

So spoke Zarathustra, and laughed a second time. But then he thought of the friends he had left behind. As if he had wronged them with his thoughts, he reproached himself for those thoughts. And at once the one who had laughed began to weep: with anger and longing, Zarathustra wept bitterly.

## XLVI. THE VISION AND THE ENIGMA.

1.

When word spread among the sailors that Zarathustra was on board—for a man from the Happy Isles had come aboard with him—there was great curiosity and expectation. But Zarathustra kept silent for two days. Sadness made him cold and deaf, so that he answered neither glances nor questions.

On the evening of the second day, however, he opened his ears again, though he still kept silent. There were many strange and dangerous things to be heard on that ship, which came from far away and was going still farther. And Zarathustra was fond of all who make distant voyages and do not like to live without danger.

And look! As he listened, his own tongue was at last loosened, and the ice around his heart broke. Then he began to speak:

To you, the bold seekers and adventurers, and whoever has set out with cunning sails on terrible seas—

To you who are drunk on riddles, who delight in twilight, whose souls are lured by flutes into every treacherous abyss—

—For you do not like to grope along a thread with a cowardly hand; and where you can GUESS, you hate to CALCULATE—

To you alone I tell the riddle that I SAW—the vision of the loneliest one.—

Recently I walked gloomily through a corpse-colored twilight—gloomy and stern, with my lips pressed tight. More than one sun had set for me.

A path that climbed boldly among boulders, an evil, lonely path that no herb or shrub cheered any longer, a mountain path, crunched under the boldness of my foot.

Marching silently over the scornful clinking of pebbles, trampling the stone that made it slip: so my foot forced its way upward.

Upward—in spite of the spirit that pulled it downward, toward the abyss: the spirit of gravity, my devil and archenemy.

Upward—though it sat on me, half dwarf, half mole; paralyzed and paralyzing; dripping lead into my ear, and thoughts like drops of lead into my brain.

"O Zarathustra," it whispered mockingly, syllable by syllable, "you stone of wisdom! You threw yourself high, but every stone that is thrown must—fall!

"O Zarathustra, you stone of wisdom, you sling-stone, you star-destroyer! You threw yourself so high—but every stone that is thrown—must fall!

"Condemned by yourself, and to your own stoning: O Zarathustra, far indeed you threw your stone—but it will fall back upon YOURSELF!"

Then the dwarf fell silent, and the silence lasted long. But the silence weighed on me; and to be two together like this is truly lonelier than being alone!

I climbed, I climbed, I dreamed, I thought—but everything weighed on me. I was like a sick man worn out by cruel torment, whom a worse dream wakes again out of his first sleep.—

But there is something in me that I call courage. Until now it has slain every despondency for me. This courage at last told me to stand still and say: "Dwarf! You! Or I!"—

For courage is the best slayer—courage that ATTACKS: for in every attack there is the sound of triumph.

Man, however, is the most courageous animal: by courage he has overcome every animal. With the sound of triumph he has overcome every pain; but human pain is the sorest pain.

Courage also slays dizziness at the edge of abysses: and where does man not stand at abysses! Is not seeing itself—seeing abysses?

Courage is the best slayer: courage also slays fellow-suffering, or pity. But fellow-suffering is the deepest abyss: as deeply as man looks into life, so deeply does he also look into suffering.

But courage is the best slayer, courage that attacks: it slays even death itself, for it says: "WAS THAT life? Very well! Once more!"

In such a saying there is much sound of triumph. Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.—

2.

"Stop, dwarf!" I said. "Either I—or you! But I am the stronger of the two: you do not know my abysmal thought! IT—you could not endure!"

Then something happened that made me lighter: the dwarf, that prying sprite, jumped down from my shoulder. It squatted on a stone in front of me. And just where we had stopped, there was a gateway.

"Look at this gateway, dwarf!" I went on. "It has two faces. Two roads meet here, and no one has yet followed either to its end.

"This long lane backward: it goes on for an eternity. And that long lane forward—that is another eternity.

"They contradict each other, these roads; they run head-on into each other—and it is here, at this gateway, that they come together. The name of the gateway is written above it: 'This Moment.'

"But if someone were to follow them farther—and ever farther and farther on—do you think, dwarf, that these roads would contradict each other forever?"—

"Everything straight lies," the dwarf murmured contemptuously. "All truth is crooked; time itself is a circle."

"You spirit of gravity!" I said angrily. "Do not make it too easy for yourself! Or I will leave you squatting where you squat, Lamefoot—and I carried you HIGH!"

"Look," I went on, "at This Moment! From this gateway, This Moment, a long eternal lane runs BACKWARD: behind us lies an eternity.

"Must not whatever of all things CAN run its course have already run along that lane? Must not whatever of all things CAN happen have already happened, been done, and passed by?

"And if everything has already existed, what do you think, dwarf, of This Moment? Must not this gateway too—have already existed?

"And are not all things knotted together so tightly that This Moment draws all coming things after it? THEREFORE—itself as well?

"For whatever of all things can run its course must also run it once more, in this long lane OUTWARD!—

"And this slow spider creeping in the moonlight, and this moonlight itself, and you and I in this gateway, whispering together, whispering of eternal things—must we not all have existed before?

"—And must we not return and run in that other lane out ahead of us, that long, eerie lane—must we not eternally return?"—

So I spoke, more and more softly, for I was afraid of my own thoughts and of the thoughts behind them. Then suddenly I heard a dog HOWL nearby.

Had I ever heard a dog howl like this? My thoughts ran back. Yes! When I was a child, in my most distant childhood:

—Then I heard a dog howl like this. And I saw it too, its hair bristling, its head raised, trembling in the stillest midnight, when even dogs believe in ghosts:
