—so that I felt pity for it. For just then the full moon, silent as death, was passing over the house. Just then it stood still, a glowing globe, resting on the flat roof as if it were trespassing on someone's property.

That was what had terrified the dog, for dogs believe in thieves and ghosts. And when I heard such howling again, I felt pity for it once more.

Where was the dwarf now? And the gateway? And the spider? And all the whispering? Had I been dreaming? Had I woken up? Suddenly I stood alone among rugged rocks, desolate in the most desolate moonlight.

*But there lay a man!* And there was the dog, leaping, bristling, whining. Now it saw me coming. Then it howled again, then it *cried out*. Had I ever heard a dog cry for help like that?

And truly, I had never seen anything like what I saw then. I saw a young shepherd writhing, choking, shuddering, his face contorted, with a heavy black snake hanging out of his mouth.

Had I ever seen so much disgust and pale horror on a single face? Perhaps he had fallen asleep? Then the snake had crawled into his throat, and there it had bitten down and held fast.

My hand pulled at the snake, and pulled, but in vain. I could not pull the snake out of his throat. Then something cried out of me: "Bite! Bite!

"Bite its head off! Bite!" So it cried out of me. My horror, my hatred, my disgust, my pity, everything good and bad in me cried out with a single voice.

You daring ones around me! You venturers and adventurers, and all of you who have set out with cunning sails on unexplored seas! You who delight in riddles!

Solve for me the riddle that I saw then. Interpret for me the vision of the loneliest one!

For it was a vision and a foreseeing. *What* did I see then, in the form of a parable? And *who* is it that must come someday?

*Who* is the shepherd into whose throat the snake crawled like this? *Who* is the man into whose throat everything heaviest and blackest will crawl like this?

But the shepherd bit, as my cry had urged him. He bit with a strong bite! He spat the snake's head far away, and sprang to his feet.

No longer a shepherd, no longer a man, but a transformed being, surrounded by light, and *laughing*! Never on earth has a man laughed as *he* laughed!

O my brothers, I heard a laughter that was no human laughter. And now a thirst gnaws at me, a longing that is never quieted.

My longing for that laughter gnaws at me. Oh, how can I still bear to live! And how could I bear to die now!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XLVII. INVOLUNTARY BLISS.

With such riddles and bitterness in his heart, Zarathustra sailed across the sea. But when he was four days' journey from the Happy Isles and from his friends, he had overcome all his pain. Triumphantly, and with a firm step, he accepted his fate once more. And then Zarathustra spoke like this to his rejoicing conscience:

I am alone again, and I want to be: alone with the pure sky and the open sea. And once again the afternoon surrounds me.

It was in an afternoon that I first found my friends. It was in an afternoon, too, that I found them a second time: at the hour when all light grows quieter.

For whatever happiness is still on its way between heaven and earth now seeks a radiant soul to lodge in. All light has now grown quieter *with happiness*.

O afternoon of my life! Once my happiness also came down into the valley to seek lodging, and there it found those open, hospitable souls.

O afternoon of my life! What did I not give up in order to have one thing: this living garden of my thoughts, and this dawn of my highest hope!

Once the creator went looking for companions, for children of *his* hope. And it turned out that he could not find them unless he first created them himself.

So I am in the midst of my work, going to my children and returning from them. For the sake of his children, Zarathustra must perfect himself.

For in our hearts we love only our child and our work. And where there is great love of oneself, it is a sign of pregnancy. So I have found it.

My children are still green in their first spring. They stand close to one another and are shaken together by the winds. They are the trees of my garden and of my best soil.

And truly, wherever such trees stand side by side, there *are* Happy Isles!

But one day I will dig them up and plant each one by itself, alone, so that it may learn loneliness, defiance, and prudence.

Then it shall stand by the sea, gnarled and crooked and with a supple hardness, a living lighthouse of unconquerable life.

There, where the storms rush down into the sea and the mountain's snout drinks the water, each shall one day keep his watches by day and by night, for *his* testing and recognition.

Each shall be recognized and tested, to see whether he is of my kind and lineage. Is he master of a long will? Is he silent even when he speaks? Does he give in such a way that he *takes* in giving?

Such a one may one day become my companion, a fellow creator and fellow celebrant with Zarathustra. He will be someone who writes my will on my tablets, for the fuller perfection of all things.

And for his sake, and for the sake of those like him, I must perfect *myself*. That is why I now avoid my happiness and offer myself to every misfortune, for *my* final testing and recognition.

And truly, it was time for me to leave. The wanderer's shadow, the longest boredom, and the stillest hour have all said to me: "It is high time!"

The word blew in to me through the keyhole and said, "Come!" The door sprang quietly open for me and said, "Go!"

But I lay chained by my love for my children. Desire had set this trap for me, the desire for love, so that I would become my children's prey and lose myself in them.

For me, to desire now means to have lost myself. *I possess you, my children!* In this possessing, everything shall be certainty and nothing shall be desire.

But the sun of my love lay brooding on me, and Zarathustra stewed in his own juice. Then shadows and doubts flew past me.

I longed for frost and winter. "Oh, if only frost and winter would make me crack and crunch again!" I sighed. Then an icy mist rose out of me.

My past burst open its tomb. Many pains that had been buried alive woke up. They had only been sleeping, hidden in burial shrouds.

So everything called to me in signs: "It is time!" But I did not hear, until at last my abyss stirred and my thought bit me.

Ah, abysmal thought, you who are *my* thought! When will I find the strength to hear you burrowing and no longer tremble?

My heart pounds up into my throat when I hear you burrowing! Even your silence nearly strangles me, you abysmal silent one!

I have never yet dared to call you *up*. It has been enough that I have carried you around with me! I have not yet been strong enough for my final lion's recklessness and playfulness.

Your weight has always been formidable enough for me. But one day I will find the strength and the lion's voice to call you up!

When I have overcome myself in that, I will overcome myself in what is greater still. And a *victory* shall be the seal of my perfection!

Meanwhile I sail on uncertain seas. Chance flatters me, smooth-tongued chance. I look forward and backward, and still I see no end.

The hour of my final struggle has not yet come to me. Or is it perhaps coming to me right now? Truly, sea and life gaze at me from all around with treacherous beauty.

O afternoon of my life! O happiness before evening! O harbor on the high seas! O peace in uncertainty! How I distrust you all!

Truly, I distrust your treacherous beauty! I am like the lover who distrusts smiles that are too smooth.

As the jealous lover pushes his dearest one away from him, tender even in his harshness, so I push this blissful hour away from me.

Away with you, blissful hour! With you an involuntary bliss has come to me! I stand here ready for my harshest pain. You have come at the wrong time!

Away with you, blissful hour! Go and find shelter over there, with my children! Hurry! Bless them with *my* happiness before evening falls!

Look, evening is already approaching. The sun is sinking. Away, my happiness!

Thus spoke Zarathustra. And he waited for his misfortune all night long, but he waited in vain. The night stayed clear and calm, and happiness itself came closer and closer to him.

Toward morning, however, Zarathustra laughed to his heart and said mockingly: "Happiness runs after me. That is because I do not run after women. And happiness is a woman."

## XLVIII. BEFORE SUNRISE.

O sky above me, you pure, you deep sky! You abyss of light! As I gaze at you, I tremble with divine longings.

To hurl myself up to your height: that is *my* depth! To hide myself in your purity: that is *my* innocence!

The god veils his beauty; in the same way you hide your stars. You do not speak; in *this* way you proclaim your wisdom to me.

Today you have risen silently for me over the raging sea. Your love and your modesty are a revelation to my raging soul.

You came to me beautiful, veiled in your beauty. You spoke to me without words, your wisdom plain to see.

Oh, how could I fail to sense all the modesty of your soul! You came to me *before* the sun, to me, the loneliest one.

We have been friends from the beginning. Grief, dread, and depth are ours in common. Even the sun is ours in common.

We do not speak to each other, because we know too much. We keep silent with each other, and smile our knowledge to each other.

Are you not the light of my fire? Do you not have the sister-soul of my insight?

Together we learned everything. Together we learned to climb beyond ourselves to ourselves, and to smile without a cloud.

We learned to smile down without a cloud, from bright eyes and from miles away, while below us compulsion and purpose and guilt steam like rain.

And when I wandered alone, *what* was my soul hungry for at night and on winding paths? And when I climbed mountains, *whom* did I ever seek there, if not you?

All my wandering and mountain-climbing was only a necessity, a makeshift for someone clumsy. My whole will wants only to *fly*, to fly into *you*!

And what have I hated more than passing clouds, and everything that stains you? I have even hated my own hatred, because it stained you!

I loathe the passing clouds, those stealthy cats of prey. They take from you and me what we share: the vast, unbounded Yes and Amen.

We loathe these go-betweens and mixers, the passing clouds. They are half-and-half creatures that have learned neither to bless nor to curse from the heart.

I would rather sit in a barrel under a closed-over sky. I would rather sit in the abyss with no sky at all, than see you, bright sky, stained with passing clouds!

And often I have longed to pin them down with the jagged golden wires of lightning, so that I could beat the drum on their kettle-bellies, like the thunder.

I would be an angry drummer, because they rob me of your Yes and Amen, you sky above me, you pure, bright sky, you abyss of light! And because they rob you of *my* Yes and Amen.

For I would rather have noise and thunder and blasts of storm than this discreet, doubting, catlike calm. And among people, too, I hate most of all the soft-footed ones, the half-and-half ones, the doubting, hesitating passing clouds.

"Whoever cannot bless shall *learn* to curse!" This clear teaching fell to me from the clear sky. This star stands in my sky even on dark nights.

But I am one who blesses and says Yes, as long as you are around me, you pure, bright sky, you abyss of light! Then I carry my blessing Yes into every abyss.

I have become one who blesses and says Yes. That is why I struggled for so long and was a fighter: so that one day I would have my hands free for blessing.

And this is my blessing: to stand over each thing as its own sky, its round roof, its azure bell and eternal security. And blessed is the one who blesses in this way!

For all things are baptized at the font of eternity, beyond good and evil. Good and evil themselves are only fleeting shadows, damp sorrows, and passing clouds.

Truly, it is a blessing and not a blasphemy when I teach: "Over all things stands the sky of chance, the sky of innocence, the sky of accident, the sky of exuberance."

"By accident": that is the oldest nobility in the world. I gave it back to all things. I set them free from their slavery under purpose.

I placed this freedom and heavenly serenity like an azure bell over all things when I taught that no "eternal Will" wills over them and through them.

In place of that Will I put this exuberance and this folly, when I taught: "In everything, one thing is impossible: rationality!"

A *little* reason, to be sure, a seed of wisdom scattered from star to star: this yeast is mixed into all things. For the sake of folly, wisdom is mixed into all things!

A little wisdom is indeed possible. But this blessed certainty I have found in all things: they prefer to dance on the feet of chance.

O sky above me, you pure, you lofty sky! This is what your purity now means to me: that there is no eternal reason-spider and no reason-cobweb.

It means that you are a dance floor for divine chances, that you are a table of the gods for divine dice and dice-players!

But you blush? Have I spoken things that cannot be spoken? Have I insulted you when I meant to bless you?

Or is it the shame of being two of us that makes you blush? Do you tell me to go and be silent because now *day* is coming?

The world is deep, deeper than the day has ever understood. Not everything may be said in the presence of day. But day is coming, so let us part!

O heaven above me, you modest one, you glowing one! O my happiness before sunrise! The day is coming, so let us part!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## XLIX. THE BEDWARFING VIRTUE.

1.

When Zarathustra was back on the mainland, he did not go straight to his mountains and his cave. Instead he wandered a great deal, asked many questions, and found out this and that. He joked about himself: "Look, a river that flows back to its source in many windings!"

He wanted to learn what had happened *among people* while he was away: whether they had become greater or smaller. Once, when he saw a row of new houses, he marveled and said:

"What do these houses mean? Truly, no great soul put them up as an image of itself!

Did some silly child take them out of its toy box? If only another child would put them back in the box!

And these rooms and chambers: can *people* go in and out of them? They seem made for silk dolls, or for dainty eaters who perhaps let others eat with them."

Zarathustra stood still and thought. At last he said sorrowfully: "*Everything* has become smaller here!

Everywhere I see lower doorways. Someone of *my* kind can still go through them, but he has to stoop!

Oh, when will I be home again, where I no longer have to stoop, no longer have to stoop *before the small*!" And Zarathustra sighed and gazed into the distance.

That same day, however, he gave his speech on the virtue that makes people small.

2.

I walk among this people and keep my eyes open. They do not forgive me for not envying their virtues.

They snap at me because I tell them that small people need small virtues, and because I find it hard to accept that small people are *necessary*!

Here I am still like a rooster in a strange farmyard, where even the hens peck at me. But I am not unfriendly to the hens because of that.

I am polite to them, as I am to all small annoyances. Being prickly toward small things seems to me wisdom fit for hedgehogs.

They all talk about me when they sit around their fire in the evening. They talk about me, but no one thinks about me!

This is the new stillness I have experienced: their noise around me spreads a cloak over my thoughts.

They shout to one another: "What is this gloomy cloud going to do to us? Let us make sure it does not bring a plague on us!"

Recently a woman grabbed her child, who was coming toward me. "Take the children away!" she cried. "Eyes like those scorch children's souls."

They cough when I speak. They think coughing is an objection to strong winds. They have no idea how boisterous my happiness is!

"We don't have time for Zarathustra yet," they object. But what does a time matter that "has no time" for Zarathustra?

And even if they praised me completely, how could I go to sleep on *their* praise? Their praise is a belt of thorns to me: it scratches me even when I take it off.

I learned this among them too: the one who praises acts as if he were giving something back, but in truth he wants to be given more!

Ask my foot whether their tunes of praise and enticement please it! Truly, to such a beat and tick-tock it wants neither to dance nor to stand still.

They would like to lure and praise me into small virtues. They would like to talk my foot into the tick-tock of small happiness.

I walk among this people and keep my eyes open. They have become *smaller*, and they keep becoming smaller. *The reason is their doctrine of happiness and virtue.*

They are moderate even in virtue, because they want comfort. And only moderate virtue is compatible with comfort.

Of course, they too learn in their way to stride along and stride forward. I call that their *hobbling*. In doing it, they get in the way of everyone who is in a hurry.

Many of them move forward while looking backward with stiff necks. Those are the ones I like to run into.

Foot and eye should not lie, nor contradict each other. But there is a great deal of lying among small people.

Some of them *will*, but most of them are *willed*. Some of them are genuine, but most of them are bad actors.

Among them are actors who do not know it, and actors who do not intend it. The genuine ones are always rare, especially the genuine actors.

There is little of man here, and so their women make themselves mannish. For only someone who is man enough will *save the woman* in woman.

And the worst hypocrisy I found among them is this: even those who command pretend to have the virtues of those who serve.

"I serve, you serve, we serve": so even the rulers' hypocrisy chants here. And alas, if the first lord is *only* the first servant!

Even their hypocrisy caught my eyes' curiosity. I understood well all their fly-happiness, their buzzing around sunny windowpanes.

So much kindness, so much weakness I see. So much justice and pity, so much weakness.

They are round, fair, and considerate toward one another, just as grains of sand are round, fair, and considerate toward grains of sand.

To embrace a small happiness modestly: that they call "submission"! And at the same time they are already glancing modestly toward a new small happiness.

Deep down they want one thing above all: that no one hurt them. So they anticipate everyone's wishes and do good to everyone.

That, however, is *cowardice*, even if it is called "virtue."

And when these small people happen to speak harshly, I hear only their hoarseness in it. Every draft of air makes them hoarse.

They are clever indeed; their virtues have clever fingers. But they lack fists. Their fingers do not know how to curl themselves into fists.

For them, virtue is whatever makes people modest and tame. With it they have turned the wolf into a dog, and turned man himself into man's best domestic animal.

"We set our chair in the *middle*," their smirk tells me, "as far from dying gladiators as from contented pigs."

That, however, is *mediocrity*, even if it is called moderation.

3.

I walk among this people and let fall many words. But they know neither how to take them nor how to keep them.

They wonder why I did not come to denounce lust and vice. But truly, I did not come to warn against pickpockets either!

They wonder why I am not ready to encourage and sharpen their cleverness. As if they did not already have enough know-it-alls, whose voices grate on my ear like slate pencils!

And when I call out, "Curse all the cowardly devils in you that want to whimper, fold their hands, and worship!" they shout, "Zarathustra is godless."

Their teachers of submission shout this most of all. But it is precisely into their ears that I love to cry: "Yes! I *am* Zarathustra, the godless!"

Those teachers of submission! Wherever there is something puny, sickly, or scabby, they crawl like lice. Only my disgust keeps me from cracking them.

Well, then! This is my sermon for *their* ears: I am Zarathustra the godless, who says, "Who is more godless than I, so that I may enjoy his teaching?"

I am Zarathustra the godless: where do I find my equal? All those are my equals who give themselves their own will and throw off all submission.

I am Zarathustra the godless! I cook every chance in *my* pot. Only when it is thoroughly cooked do I welcome it as *my* food.

And truly, many a chance came to me commandingly. But my *will* spoke to it even more commandingly, and then it lay begging on its knees,

begging to find a home and a heart with me, and saying flatteringly: "See, O Zarathustra, how only a friend comes to a friend!"

But why do I talk, when no one has *my* ears? So I will shout it out to all the winds:

You keep getting smaller, you small people! You are crumbling away, you comfortable ones! You will yet perish—

—from your many small virtues, your many small omissions, and your many small submissions!

Too soft, too yielding: that is your soil! But for a tree to become *great*, it seeks to wrap hard roots around hard rocks!

Even what you leave undone helps weave the web of the whole human future. Even your nothing is a cobweb, and a spider that lives on the blood of the future.

And when you take, you small virtuous ones, it is like stealing. But even among rogues, *honor* says: "One should steal only when one cannot rob."

"It gives itself": that is also a doctrine of submission. But I tell you, you comfortable ones: *it takes for itself*, and it will take more and more from you!

Ah, if only you would give up all *half*-willing, and choose idleness as decisively as you choose action!

Ah, if only you understood my word: "Always do what you will, but first be people who *can will*.

Always love your neighbor as yourselves, but first be people who *love themselves*—

—who love with great love, who love with great contempt!" So speaks Zarathustra the godless.

But why do I talk, when no one has *my* ears? It is still an hour too early for me here.

I am my own forerunner among this people, my own rooster crowing in dark lanes.

But *their* hour is coming! And mine is coming too! Hour by hour they become smaller, poorer, less fruitful. Poor herbs! Poor earth!

And *soon* they will stand before me like dry grass and prairie, truly tired of themselves, and thirsting for *fire* more than for water!

O blessed hour of the lightning! O mystery before noontide! Someday I will turn them into running fires, heralds with tongues of flame.

With tongues of flame they will one day proclaim: It is coming, it is near, *the great noontide*!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## L. ON THE OLIVE-MOUNT.

Winter, a bad guest, sits with me at home. My hands are blue from his friendly handshakes.

I honor him, that bad guest, but I am glad to leave him alone. I like to run away from him, and if you run *well*, you escape him!

With warm feet and warm thoughts I run to where the wind is still: to the sunny corner of my olive mountain.

There I laugh at my stern guest and am still fond of him, because he clears my house of flies and quiets many little noises.

He will not put up with even one gnat buzzing, let alone two. He also makes the lanes lonely, so that even the moonlight is afraid there at night.

He is a hard guest, but I honor him. I do not worship the pot-bellied fire-idol as the softlings do.

Better a little teeth-chattering than idol worship! That is what my nature wants. And I have a special grudge against all fervent, steaming, steamy fire-idols.

Whoever I love, I love better in winter than in summer. I mock my enemies better and more heartily now, when winter sits in my house.

Heartily, truly, even when I *crawl* into bed: there my hidden happiness still laughs and plays; even my deceptive dream laughs.

I, a crawler? Never in my life did I crawl before the powerful. And if I ever lied, I lied out of love. That is why I am glad even in my winter bed.

A poor bed warms me more than a rich one, for I am jealous of my poverty. And in winter she is most faithful to me.

I begin every day with a piece of mischief: I mock the winter with a cold bath. My stern housemate grumbles at that.

I also like to tickle him with a wax candle, so that he will finally let the sky emerge from its ash-grey twilight.

For I am especially mischievous in the morning, at the early hour when the bucket rattles at the well and horses neigh warmly in grey lanes.

Then I wait impatiently for the clear sky finally to dawn for me: the snow-bearded winter sky, the hoary one, the white-haired one—

—the winter sky, the silent winter sky, which often stifles even its own sun!

Did I learn the long, clear silence from it? Or did it learn it from me? Or did each of us invent it alone?

All good things have a thousand origins. All good, roguish things leap into existence out of joy: how could they do that only once?

The long silence is also a good roguish thing, and so is looking out, like the winter sky, from a clear, round-eyed face—

—and, like it, stifling one's own sun and one's unbending solar will. Truly, I have learned this art and this winter-roguishness *well*!

My favorite mischief and art is this: my silence has learned not to give itself away by being silent.

Clattering with words and dice, I outwit the solemn attendants. All those stern watchers will fail to catch my will and purpose.

I devised the long, clear silence so that no one could see down into my depths and into my ultimate will.

I found many a clever man who veiled his face and made his water muddy, so that no one could see through it and beneath it.

But it was precisely to him that the cleverer distrusters and nutcrackers came. It was precisely from him that they fished out his best-hidden fish!

But the clear, the honest, the transparent: these are, for me, the wisest of the silent. In them the depth is so *profound* that even the clearest water does not give it away.

You snow-bearded, silent winter sky, you round-eyed white-head above me! Oh, you heavenly image of my soul and its playfulness!

And *must* I not hide myself like someone who has swallowed gold, so that no one rips my soul open?

*Must* I not walk on stilts, so that all those envious and hurtful people around me will *overlook* my long legs?

Those dingy, stove-warmed, worn-out, green-tinged, ill-natured souls: how *could* their envy bear my happiness!

So I show them only the ice and winter of my peaks. I do *not* show them that every warm belt of the sun wraps around my mountain!

They hear only the whistling of my winter storms. They do *not* know that I also travel over warm seas, like longing, heavy, hot south winds.

They also pity me for my accidents and chances. But *my* word says: "Let chance come to me: it is as innocent as a little child!"

How *could* they bear my happiness, if I did not wrap it in accidents and winter hardships, in bearskin caps and cloaks of snowflakes?

If I did not myself take pity on their *pity*, the pity of those envious and hurtful people?

If I did not sigh in front of them, and let my teeth chatter with cold, and patiently *let* myself be bundled up in their pity?

This is the wise mischief and goodwill of my soul: it *does not hide* its winters and icy storms. Nor does it hide its chilblains.

For one person, solitude is the escape of the sick. For another, it is the escape *from* the sick.

Let them *hear* me shivering and sighing with winter cold, all those poor squint-eyed rascals around me! With this sighing and shivering, I escape from their overheated rooms.

Let them feel sorry for me and sigh with me over my chilblains. "He will *freeze to death* yet on the ice of knowledge!" they mourn.

Meanwhile I run here and there with warm feet on my olive mount. In the sunny corner of my olive mount I sing, and I mock all pity.

Thus sang Zarathustra.

## LI. ON PASSING-BY.

So, wandering slowly through many peoples and many different cities, Zarathustra made his way back by roundabout roads toward his mountains and his cave. And along the way, without expecting it, he came to the gate of the *great city*.

There a foaming fool sprang toward him with outstretched hands and blocked his path. It was the same fool whom the people called "Zarathustra's ape." He had picked up something of Zarathustra's phrasing and tone of speech, and perhaps he also liked to borrow from the store of his wisdom. And the fool spoke to Zarathustra like this:

O Zarathustra, here is the great city. Here you have nothing to look for and everything to lose.

Why would you wade through this mud? Have pity on your feet! Spit on the city gate instead, and turn back!

This is hell for a hermit's thoughts. Here great thoughts are boiled alive and boiled down small.

Here every great feeling decays. Here only bony, rattling little sensations are allowed to rattle!

Can you not already smell the slaughterhouses and cheap kitchens of the spirit? Is this city not steaming with the fumes of slaughtered spirit?

Do you not see the souls hanging like limp, dirty rags? And they make newspapers out of these rags too!

Do you not hear how the spirit has become a game of words here? It vomits up disgusting verbal swill! And they make newspapers out of this verbal swill too.

They hound one another, and do not know where they are going! They stir each other up, and do not know why! They clink their imitation gold, and they jingle their real gold.

They are cold, and look for warmth in distilled liquor. They are overheated, and look for coolness in frozen spirits. They are all sick and sore from public opinion.

Every lust and every vice is at home here. But there are also virtuous people here, and much virtue that is fit for official appointment, and much that has been appointed.

There is much office-seeking virtue with clerks' fingers, and with tough backsides made for sitting and waiting. It is blessed with little stars of honor on its chest, and with padded daughters who have no hips.

There is also much piety here, and much faithful licking and smearing of spittle, before the God of Hosts.

The star of honor drips down "from on high," and so does the gracious spittle. And every chest that has no star longs for what is on high.

The moon has its court, and the court has its mooncalves. But the begging people, and all the begging virtues that hope for appointment, pray to everything that comes from the court.

"I serve, you serve, we serve": so all the office-seeking virtue prays to the prince, hoping that the star it has earned will finally be pinned to its narrow chest!

But the moon still circles around everything earthly. In the same way, the prince circles around what is most earthly of all: the shopkeeper's gold.

The God of the armies of war is not the god of the gold bar. The prince proposes, but the shopkeeper disposes!

By everything bright and strong and good in you, O Zarathustra! Spit on this city of shopkeepers and turn back!

Here all blood flows rotten, lukewarm, and frothy through every vein. Spit on the great city, which is the great slum where all the scum froths together!

Spit on the city of cramped souls and narrow chests, of sharp eyes and sticky fingers,

on the city of the pushy, the shameless, the demagogues of the pen and the demagogues of the tongue, the overheated ambitious ones,

where everything crippled, ill-famed, lustful, distrustful, overripe, sickly-yellow, and seditious festers together, pernicious.

Spit on the great city and turn back!

At this point Zarathustra interrupted the foaming fool and shut his mouth.

"Stop this at once!" Zarathustra cried. "Your speech and your kind have disgusted me for a long time!

"Why did you live so long by the swamp that you had to become a frog and a toad yourself?

"Does not tainted, frothy swamp blood flow in your own veins, now that you have learned to croak and rail like this?

"Why did you not go into the forest? Or why did you not till the soil? Is the sea not full of green islands?

"I despise your contempt. And when you warned me, why did you not warn yourself?

"My contempt and my warning bird shall take flight out of love alone, but not out of the swamp!

"They call you my ape, you foaming fool. But I call you my grunting pig. With your grunting you spoil even my praise of folly.

"What first made you grunt? Nobody *flattered* you enough. So you sat yourself down beside this filth, so that you would have reason to grunt a great deal,

"so that you would have reason for much *revenge*! For all your foaming is revenge, you vain fool. I have seen through you.

"But your fool's word harms *me*, even when you are right! And even if Zarathustra's word *were* a hundred times justified, you would always *do* wrong with my word!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra. Then he looked at the great city, sighed, and was silent for a long time. At last he spoke:

"I loathe this great city too, and not only this fool. In both, there is nothing to make better and nothing to make worse.

"Woe to this great city! I wish I could already see the pillar of fire in which it will be consumed!

"For such pillars of fire must come before the great noontide. But that has its own time and its own fate.

"But I give you this rule as I leave, you fool: Where one can no longer love, one should *pass by*!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he passed by the fool and the great city.

## LII. THE APOSTATES.

1.

Ah, is everything on this meadow already withered and gray that only lately stood green and many-colored? And how much honey of hope I once carried from here back to my beehives!

Those young hearts have all grown old already. No, not even old! Only tired, ordinary, comfortable. They say it openly: "We have become pious again."

Not long ago I saw them run out in the early morning with brave steps. But the feet of their knowledge grew tired, and now they even slander their morning courage!

Many of them once lifted their legs like dancers, and the laughter of my wisdom winked at them. Then they had second thoughts. Just now I saw them bent over, crawling to the cross.

They used to flutter around light and freedom like gnats and young poets. A little older, a little colder, and already they are mystery-mongers, mumblers, and milksops.

Did their hearts perhaps lose courage because solitude had swallowed me like a whale? Did their ears perhaps listen, long and longingly, for me *in vain*, for my trumpet notes and my herald's calls?

Ah! There are always only a few whose hearts have lasting courage and abundance, and in such people the spirit also stays patient. The rest, however, are *cowardly*.

The rest: these are always the great majority, the commonplace, the superfluous, the far too many. They are all cowardly!

Whoever is of my kind will meet the experiences of my kind along the way. His first companions must be corpses and jesters.

His second companions, however, will call themselves his *believers*. They will be a living crowd, with much love, much foolishness, and much beardless reverence.

Whoever among human beings is of my kind should not bind his heart to these believers. Whoever knows the fickle, faint-hearted human species should not trust in these springtimes and many-colored meadows!

If they *could* act otherwise, then they would also *will* otherwise. The half-and-half spoil every whole. Leaves wither: what is there to mourn about that?

Let them go and fall away, O Zarathustra, and do not mourn! Better still, blow among them with rustling winds.

Blow among those leaves, O Zarathustra, so that everything *withered* will run away from you all the faster!

2.

"We have become pious again": so these apostates confess. And some of them are still too timid even to confess it.

I look them in the eye. I say it to their faces and to the blush on their cheeks: You are the ones who *pray* again!

But it is a disgrace to pray! Not for everyone, but for you, and for me, and for anyone who keeps his conscience in his head. For *you* it is a disgrace to pray!

You know it well. The faint-hearted devil in you would like to fold its arms, put its hands in its lap, and take things easier. This faint-hearted devil persuades you that "there *is* a God!"

But *by this* you belong to the light-fearing kind, to those whom the light never lets rest. Now you must push your head deeper every day into darkness and fog!

And you have chosen your hour well, for right now the night birds are flying out again. The hour has come for all light-fearing people: the evening hour, the hour of rest, when they do not "rest."

I hear it and I smell it: their hour for hunting and processions has come. Not a wild hunt, but a tame, lame, sniffling hunt of soft-steppers and soft-prayers,

a hunt for impressionable simpletons. All the mousetraps for the heart have been set again! And whenever I lift a curtain, a moth rushes out.

Was it perhaps crouching there with another moth? For everywhere I smell small hidden communities. Wherever there are little closets, there are new devotees inside, and the stale air of devotees.

They sit beside one another for long evenings and say: "Let us become like little children again and say 'dear God!'" Their mouths and stomachs have been ruined by the pious confectioners.

Or they spend long evenings watching a crafty, lurking cross-spider, the kind marked with a cross. It preaches prudence to the spiders themselves and teaches that "under crosses, it is good to spin webs!"

Or they sit all day at swamps with fishing rods, and for that reason think themselves *deep*. But whoever fishes where there are no fish, I would not even call shallow!

Or they learn to play the harp in a godly, cheerful style with a hymn-writer, who would like to harp his way into the hearts of young girls, because he has grown tired of old girls and their praise.

Or they learn to shudder with a learned half-madman, who waits in darkened rooms for spirits to come to him. And then the spirit runs away entirely!

Or they listen to an old wandering piper of howls and growls, who has learned the sadness of sounds from the sad winds. Now he pipes like the wind, and preaches sadness in sad tunes.

And some of them have even become night watchmen. They now know how to blow horns, and they go around at night waking up old things that fell asleep long ago.

Last night by the garden wall I heard five sentences about old things. They came from just such old, sorrowful, dried-up night watchmen.

"For a father, he does not care enough for his children. Human fathers do it better!"

"He is too old! He no longer cares for his children at all," answered the other night watchman.

"*Does* he even have children? No one can prove it unless he proves it himself! I have long wished that he would prove it thoroughly, just once."

"Prove? As if *he* had ever proved anything! Proving is hard for him. He sets great store by people *believing* him."

"Yes! Yes! Belief makes him blessed, belief in him. That is how old people are! It is the same with us!"

So the two old night watchmen and scarers of light spoke to each other, and then they tooted sorrowfully on their horns. That is what happened last night by the garden wall.

But my heart twisted with laughter and almost burst. It did not know where to go, and it sank down into my diaphragm.

It will be the death of me yet, choking with laughter when I see drunken asses and hear night watchmen doubting God like this.

Has the time for all such doubts not *long* since passed? Who can still wake up such old, sleeping, light-shunning things nowadays?

The old gods came to an end long ago. And truly, they had a good and joyful end!

They did not "gloom" themselves to death. People make that up! On the contrary, they once *laughed* themselves to death!

That happened when the most ungodly saying came from a god himself, the saying: "There is only one God! You shall have no other gods before me!"

An old grim-bearded god, a jealous one, forgot himself in this way.

And all the gods laughed then, and rocked on their thrones, and cried: "Is this not exactly what divinity is, that there are gods, but no God?"

Whoever has an ear, let him hear.

Thus Zarathustra spoke in the city he loved, which is called "The Pied Cow." From there he had only two more days to travel before he would reach his cave and his animals again. His soul rejoiced without pause because his homecoming was so near.

## LIII. THE RETURN HOME.

O solitude! My *home*, solitude! I have lived too long and too wildly in wild, distant places to come back to you without tears!

Now shake your finger at me as mothers do. Now smile at me as mothers smile. Now simply say: "Who was it that once rushed away from me like a whirlwind?

"Who was it that called out on leaving: 'I have sat too long with solitude, and there I have forgotten how to be silent!' *That* you have surely learned by now?

"O Zarathustra, I know everything. I know that among the many you were *more abandoned*, you unique one, than you ever were with me!

"Abandonment is one thing, and solitude is another. *That* you have now learned! You have also learned that among people you will always be wild and strange.

"You will be wild and strange even when they love you, because above all they want to be *treated gently*!

"Here, though, you are at home and at ease with yourself. Here you can say everything and pour out all your motives. Nothing here is ashamed of hidden, hardened feelings.

"Here all things come fondly to your words and flatter you, because they want to ride on your back. Here you ride on every image toward every truth.

"Here you may speak honestly and openly to all things. And in truth, it sounds like praise in their ears when someone speaks to all things *directly*!

"Abandonment is another matter. Do you remember, O Zarathustra? Your bird screamed overhead while you stood in the forest beside a corpse, undecided, not knowing where to go.

"Then you said: 'Let my animals lead me! I have found it more dangerous among people than among animals.' *That* was abandonment!

"And do you remember, O Zarathustra? You sat on your island like a well of wine among empty buckets, giving and granting, handing out and sharing among the thirsty.

"In the end you alone sat thirsty among the drunken, and cried out every night: 'Is taking not more blessed than giving? And is stealing not more blessed still than taking?' *That* was abandonment!

"And do you remember, O Zarathustra? Your stillest hour came and drove you out of yourself, whispering wickedly: 'Speak and be broken!'

"It made you sick of all your waiting and silence, and it disheartened your humble courage. *That* was abandonment!"

O solitude! My home, solitude! How tenderly and blissfully your voice speaks to me!

We do not question each other, and we do not complain to each other. We walk together openly through open doors.

With you, everything is open and clear, and even the hours run here on lighter feet. In the dark, time weighs more heavily on a person than in the light.

Here all the words of being, and all the cabinets where words are kept, fly open to me. Here all being wants to become words, and all becoming wants to learn from me how to speak.

Down there, though, all talking is useless! There the best wisdom is to forget and pass by. *That* I have now learned!

Whoever wants to understand everything in people must handle everything. But my hands are too clean for that.

I do not even like breathing in their breath. Alas, that I lived so long among their noise and their bad breath!

O blessed stillness around me! O pure scents around me! How this stillness draws pure breath from a deep chest! How it listens, this blessed stillness!

But down there everything speaks, and everything is misheard. If you announce your wisdom with bells, the shopkeepers in the marketplace will drown it out by jingling their pennies!

Among them everything talks, and no one knows how to understand anymore. Everything falls into the water, and nothing falls into deep wells anymore.

Among them everything talks, and nothing succeeds or comes to completion anymore. Everything cackles, but who will still sit quietly on the nest and hatch the eggs?

Among them everything talks, and everything is talked to death. What yesterday was too hard even for time and its teeth hangs today, chewed and gnawed to pieces, from the mouths of the people of today.

Among them everything talks, and everything is betrayed. What was once called the secret and secrecy of deep souls now belongs to the street criers and other butterflies.

O human hubbub, you strange and wonderful thing! You noise in dark streets! Now you are behind me again. My greatest danger lies behind me!

My greatest danger always lay in sparing and pitying others, and all human hubbub wants to be spared and tolerated.

I always lived among people with truths held back, with a fool's hand and a fooled heart, and rich in the small lies of pity.

I sat among them in disguise, ready to misjudge *myself* so that I could bear *them*. I gladly told myself: "You fool, you do not know people!"

You forget what people are when you live among them. There is too much foreground in everyone. What can eyes that see far and long for far things do *there*?

Fool that I was, when they misjudged me I spared them more than I spared myself. I was used to being hard on myself, and often I even took revenge on myself for being so lenient with them.

Stung all over by poisonous flies, and hollowed like a stone by many drops of malice, I sat among them. And still I told myself: "Everything petty is innocent of its pettiness!"

I found the most poisonous flies to be those who call themselves "the good." They sting in all innocence, and they lie in all innocence. How *could* they be just toward me!

Whoever lives among the good is taught by pity to lie. Pity makes the air stifling for every free soul, for the stupidity of the good has no bottom.

Down there I learned to hide myself and my riches, because I found everyone poor in spirit. That was the lie of my pity: that I knew in everyone,

that I saw and smelled in everyone, how much spirit was *enough* for him and how much was *too much*!

Their stiff wise men I called wise, not stiff. That is how I learned to slur over words.

The gravediggers dig up diseases for themselves. Bad fumes rest under old rubbish. One should not stir up the swamp. One should live on mountains.

With blissful nostrils I breathe mountain freedom again. My nose is finally free of the smell of all human hubbub!

Tickled by sharp breezes as if by sparkling wine, my soul *sneezes*. It sneezes and cries out to itself in congratulation: "Health to you!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LIV. THE THREE EVIL THINGS.

1.

In my dream, my last morning dream, I stood today on a headland beyond the world. I held a pair of scales, and I *weighed* the world.

Alas, the rosy dawn came too early for me. She glowed me awake, the jealous one! She is always jealous of the glow of my morning dreams.

My dream found the world measurable by anyone who has time, weighable by a good weigher, reachable by strong wings, and guessable by divine nutcrackers.

My dream is a bold sailor, half ship and half hurricane, silent as a butterfly, impatient as a falcon. How did it have the patience and leisure today to weigh the world?

Perhaps my wisdom spoke to it in secret: my laughing, wide-awake daytime wisdom, which mocks all "infinite worlds." For it says: "Where there is force, *number* becomes master, because number has more force."

How confidently my dream looked upon this finite world! It looked neither with a taste for novelty nor with a taste for the old, neither timidly nor pleadingly.

The world offered itself to me as if a big round apple were placed in my hand, a ripe golden apple with cool, soft, velvety skin.

The world stood on my headland as if a tree were nodding to me: a broad-branched, strong-willed tree, bent to form a backrest and a footstool for weary travelers.

The world presented itself to me today as if delicate hands were carrying a casket toward me, a casket left open for the delight of modest, admiring eyes.

It was not enough of a riddle to frighten human love away, and not enough of a solution to put human wisdom to sleep. Today the world was a humanly good thing to me, though such bad things are said of it!

How I thank my morning dream for letting me weigh the world at today's dawn! It came to me as a humanly good thing, this dream and comforter of the heart!

So that I may do the same by day, imitating and copying the best of it, I will now put the three worst things on the scales and weigh them well, in a human way.

Whoever taught people to bless also taught them to curse. What are the three most thoroughly cursed things in the world? These I will put on the scales.

*Sensual pleasure*, *lust for power*, and *selfishness*. Until now these three have been cursed most thoroughly and have had the worst and falsest reputation. These three I will weigh well, in a human way.

Well, then! Here is my headland, and there is the sea. *It* rolls toward me, shaggy and fawning, the old, faithful, hundred-headed dog-monster that I love!

Well, then! Here I will hold the scales over the surging sea. I also choose a witness to watch: you, the hermit tree, you strong-scented, broad-arched tree that I love!

By what bridge does the present cross to the hereafter? What compels the high to stoop down to the low? And what commands even the highest to keep growing upward?

Now the scales hang balanced and still. I have thrown three heavy questions into one pan, and the other pan carries three heavy answers.

2.

Sensual pleasure: to all body-despisers in hair shirts, a thorn and a stake. All backworldsmen, the believers in a world behind this one, curse it as "the world," because it mocks and fools all mistaken teachers who draw false conclusions.

Sensual pleasure: to the rabble, the slow fire on which it is burned. To all worm-eaten wood and all stinking rags, the furnace already heated and waiting.

Sensual pleasure: to free hearts, something innocent and free, the garden happiness of the earth, the overflowing thanks of the whole future to the present.

Sensual pleasure: a sweet poison only to the withered. To the lion-willed, however, it is the great restorative, the wine of wines, reverently saved.

Sensual pleasure: the great symbol of happiness, pointing to a higher happiness and the highest hope. For marriage is promised to many, and more than marriage,

promised to many who are stranger to each other than man and woman. And who has fully understood *how* strange man and woman are to each other!

Sensual pleasure: but I want hedges around my thoughts, and even around my words, so that swine and libertines do not break into my gardens!

Lust for power: the glowing whip of the hardest of the hard-hearted, the cruel torture saved for the cruelest themselves, the dark flame of living pyres.

Lust for power: the wicked gadfly set upon the vainest peoples, the scorner of all uncertain virtue, riding on every horse and every pride.

Lust for power: the earthquake that breaks and breaks open everything rotten and hollow. It is the rolling, rumbling, punishing destroyer of whitewashed tombs, the flashing question mark beside answers given too soon.

Lust for power: under its gaze people creep and crouch and toil, and sink lower than the snake and the swine, until at last a great contempt cries out of them.

Lust for power: the terrible teacher of great contempt, which tells cities and empires to their face: "Away with you!" It does so until a voice cries out of them: "Away with *me*!"

Lust for power: yet it also climbs enticingly up to the pure and the solitary, up to heights content with themselves. It glows there like a love that paints purple joys enticingly across earthly skies.

Lust for power: but who would call it a *lust* when the height longs to stoop down for power! Truly, there is nothing sick or diseased in such longing and descending!

It longs that the solitary height may not stay solitary and self-sufficient forever, that the mountains may come down to the valleys, and the winds of the heights to the plains.

Oh, who could find the right name, an honoring name, for such longing! "The bestowing virtue": that is what Zarathustra once called the unnamable.

And then it also happened, and truly it happened for the first time, that his word blessed *selfishness*: the wholesome, healthy selfishness that springs from a powerful soul.

It springs from the powerful soul to which the lofty body belongs: the beautiful, triumphant, refreshing body around which everything becomes a mirror.

This is the supple, persuasive body, the dancer, whose symbol and essence is the self-enjoying soul. The self-enjoyment of such bodies and souls calls itself "virtue."

Such self-enjoyment shelters itself with its words of good and bad as if with sacred groves. With the names of its happiness it drives away everything contemptible.

It drives away everything cowardly. It says: "Bad: *that* is cowardly!" The ever-anxious, the sighing, the complaining, and those who snatch up the smallest advantage all seem contemptible to it.

It also despises all bittersweet wisdom. For there is indeed a wisdom that blooms in the dark, a nightshade wisdom, which always sighs: "All is vain!"

It regards timid distrust as base, and also everyone who wants oaths instead of looks and handshakes. It regards all overly distrustful wisdom the same way, because that is the manner of cowardly souls.

It regards as baser still the fawning, doglike one who rolls onto his back at once, the submissive one. There is also a wisdom that is submissive, doglike, pious, and fawning.

It finds hateful and loathsome the one who will never defend himself. This is the one who swallows poisonous spit and hostile looks: the all-too-patient one, the one who endures everything, the one satisfied with everything. For that is the manner of slaves.

Whether they grovel before gods and divine kicks, or before people and stupid human opinions, this blessed selfishness spits on *all* kinds of slaves!

It calls everything *bad* that is broken in spirit and meanly servile: forced, blinking eyes, crushed hearts, and the false, submissive manner that kisses with broad, cowardly lips.

And it calls *false wisdom* all the wit that slaves and the gray-haired and weary put on, and above all the cunning, falsely clever, curiously clever foolishness of priests!

But the falsely wise, all the priests, the world-weary, and those whose souls are of a womanish and servile nature: oh, how their game has always abused selfishness!

And precisely *that* was supposed to be virtue, and was to be called virtue: abusing selfishness! And "selfless" is what all those world-weary cowards and cross-spiders wished to be, and with good reason!

But now the day is coming for all of them: the change, the sword of judgment, *the great noontide*. Then many things will be revealed!

And whoever proclaims the *ego* wholesome and holy, and selfishness blessed, truly, he is a prophet who speaks what he knows: "*Behold, it is coming, it is near, the great noontide!*"

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LV. THE SPIRIT OF GRAVITY.

1.

My mouth is of the people. I talk too coarsely and too heartily for Angora rabbits. And my words sound stranger still to all ink-fish and pen-foxes.

My hand is a fool's hand. Woe to all tables and walls, and to anything else that has room for a fool's sketches and a fool's scrawls!

My foot is a horse's hoof. With it I trample and trot over sticks and stones, up and down the fields, and I am bedevilled with delight in every fast race.

My stomach: surely it is an eagle's stomach? For it prefers lamb's flesh. Certainly it is a bird's stomach.

I am nourished on innocent things, and on few of them, ready and impatient to fly, to fly away. That is my nature now. Why shouldn't there be something of the bird's nature in it!

Above all, my hostility to the spirit of gravity is the bird's nature. Truly, I am deadly hostile to it, supremely hostile, hostile from the very beginning! Oh, where has my hostility not flown, and flown astray!

I could sing a song about it, and I *will* sing it, even if I am alone in an empty house and must sing it to my own ears.

There are other singers, of course. Only a full house makes their voices soft, their hands eloquent, their eyes expressive, and their hearts wakeful. I am not like them.

2.

Whoever one day teaches people to fly will have moved all the boundary stones. The boundary stones themselves will fly into the air for him. He will christen the earth anew, as "the light one."

The ostrich runs faster than the fastest horse, but it also thrusts its head heavily into the heavy earth. So it is with the man who cannot yet fly.

Earth and life seem heavy to him, and that is just what the spirit of gravity *wants*! But whoever would become light, and be a bird, must love himself. That is what I teach.

He must not, of course, love himself with the love of the sick and infected, for in them even self-love stinks!

One must learn to love oneself with a wholesome and healthy love. That is what I teach. Then one can bear to be with oneself and need not go wandering about.

Such wandering about calls itself "brotherly love." With these words the best lying and pretending have been done until now, especially by those who have been a burden to everyone.

And truly, learning to love oneself is no commandment for today and tomorrow. Of all arts, it is the finest, the subtlest, the last, and the most patient.

For everything a person possesses is well hidden from him, and of all treasure pits, one's own is the last to be dug up. That is how the spirit of gravity arranges it.

Almost in the cradle we are handed heavy words and values: "good" and "evil." That is what this dowry calls itself. For its sake we are forgiven for being alive.

And that is why people suffer the little children to come to them: to forbid them, early on, to love themselves. That is how the spirit of gravity arranges it.

And we loyally carry what we are given, on hard shoulders, over rugged mountains! And when we sweat, people tell us: "Yes, life is hard to bear!"

But only man himself is hard to bear! The reason is that he carries too many foreign things on his shoulders. Like the camel, he kneels down and lets himself be well loaded.

This is especially true of the strong, load-bearing man in whom reverence dwells. He loads too many *foreign* heavy words and values onto himself, and then life seems to him a desert!

And truly, many things that are *our own* are hard to bear too! Many inner things in a person are like the oyster: repulsive, slippery, and hard to grasp.

So an elegant shell, elegantly adorned, must plead on their behalf. But this art too must be learned: to *have* a shell, a fine appearance, and a wise blindness!

Then again, many things in a person mislead us, because many a shell is poor and pitiable, and too much shell. Much hidden goodness and power is never suspected. The choicest delicacies find no one to taste them!

Women know this, the choicest of them: a little fatter, a little leaner. Oh, how much fate lies in so little!

Man is hard to discover, and hardest of all for himself to discover. The spirit often lies about the soul. That is how the spirit of gravity arranges it.

But he has discovered himself who says: "This is *my* good and evil." With that he has silenced the mole and the dwarf, who say: "Good for all, evil for all."

Truly, I do not like those either who call everything good and call this world the best of all. Those I call the all-satisfied.

All-satisfiedness, which knows how to taste everything, is not the best taste! I honor the stubborn, choosy tongues and stomachs that have learned to say "I" and "Yes" and "No."

But to chew and digest everything is the true nature of swine! Always to say Ye-a: only the ass has learned that, and those like it!

Deep yellow and hot red: that is what *my* taste wants. It mixes blood into every color. But whoever whitewashes his house reveals to me a whitewashed soul.

Some fall in love with mummies, others with phantoms. Both alike are hostile to all flesh and blood. Oh, how both offend my taste! For I love blood.

And I will not live and stay where everyone spits and spews. That is *my* taste now. I would rather live among thieves and perjurers. No one carries gold in his mouth.

Still more offensive to me are all lickspittles. The most offensive human animal I found, I christened "parasite": it would not love, and yet it wanted to live off love.

I call unhappy all those who have only one choice: to become either evil beasts or evil beast-tamers. Among such people I would not set up my tabernacle.

I also call unhappy those who must always *wait*. They offend my taste: all the toll-collectors and traders, and kings, and the other keepers of lands and keepers of shops.

Truly, I learned waiting too, and thoroughly, but only waiting for *myself*. Above all, I learned standing and walking and running and leaping and climbing and dancing.

This is my teaching: whoever wants to fly one day must first learn standing and walking and running and climbing and dancing. One does not fly straight into flying!

With rope ladders I learned to reach many a window. With nimble legs I climbed high masts. To sit on the high masts of knowledge seemed to me no small bliss:

to flicker like small flames on high masts. It is a small light, certainly, but a great comfort to castaway sailors and the shipwrecked!

I arrived at my truth by many ways and windings. It was not by one ladder that I climbed to the height from which my eye roams into my distance.

And I asked the way only unwillingly. That always went against my taste! I preferred to question and test the ways themselves.

All my traveling has been a testing and a questioning. And truly, one must also *learn* to answer such questioning! That, however, is my taste:

neither a good taste nor a bad one, but *my* taste, which I no longer hide and of which I am no longer ashamed.

"This is now *my* way. Where is yours?" That is how I answered those who asked me for "the way." For *the* way does not exist!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LVI. OLD AND NEW TABLES.

1.

Here I sit and wait, with old broken tables around me, and new half-written tables too. When will my hour come?

It is the hour of my descent, of my going down, for once more I will go to men.

I am waiting for that hour now. First the signs must come to me that it is *my* hour: the laughing lion with the flock of doves.

Meanwhile I talk to myself, as one who has time. No one tells me anything new, so I tell myself my own story.

2.

When I came to men, I found them resting on an old conceit. All of them thought they had long known what was good and bad for man.

All talk about virtue seemed to them an old, tiresome business. Whoever wanted to sleep well spoke of "good" and "bad" before going to bed.

I disturbed this sleepiness when I taught that *no one yet knows* what is good and bad, unless it is the one who creates!

He is the one who creates man's goal and gives the earth its meaning and its future. He alone *makes* it so *that* anything is good or bad.

I told them to overturn their old academic chairs, and wherever else that old conceit had sat. I told them to laugh at their great moralists, their saints, their poets, and their Saviors.

I told them to laugh at their gloomy sages, and at whoever had sat on the tree of life like a black scarecrow, giving warnings.

I sat down on their great highway of graves, even beside the carrion and the vultures. And I laughed at all their past and its mellow, decaying glory.

Truly, like preachers of repentance and like fools, I cried wrath and shame on all their greatness and their smallness. Oh, that their best is so very small! Oh, that their worst is so very small! So I laughed.

So my wise longing, born in the mountains, cried and laughed in me. It was a wild wisdom, truly: my great longing with rustling wings.

Often it carried me off and up and away, in the middle of my laughter. Then I flew, quivering like an arrow, in sun-drunk rapture:

out into distant futures that no dream has yet seen, into warmer souths than any sculptor ever imagined, where gods, dancing, are ashamed of all clothing.

(I speak in parables, and limp and stammer like the poets. And truly, I am ashamed that I still have to be a poet!)

There all becoming seemed to me the dancing of gods and the wanton play of gods, and the world seemed set loose and unbridled, fleeing back to itself:

as many gods eternally fleeing themselves and seeking one another again, as many gods blessedly contradicting themselves, communing again, and becoming brothers once more.

There all time seemed to me a blessed mockery of moments. There necessity was freedom itself, playing happily with the goad of freedom.

There I also found again my old devil and archenemy, the spirit of gravity, and everything it created: constraint, law, necessity and consequence and purpose and will and good and evil.

For must there not be something that is danced *over*, danced beyond? For the sake of the nimble, the nimblest, must there not be moles and clumsy dwarfs?

3.

It was there, too, that I picked up the word "Superman" from the path, along with the teaching that man is something that must be surpassed.

I learned that man is a bridge and not a goal. He rejoices in his noontides and evenings as paths toward new rosy dawns.

There too I found the Zarathustra-word of the great noontide, and whatever else I have hung up over men like purple evening afterglows.

Truly, I also made them see new stars, along with new nights. And over cloud and day and night I spread out laughter like a brightly colored canopy.

I taught them all *my* creating and striving: to compose into one and gather together what in man is fragment and riddle and dreadful chance.

As composer, as reader of riddles, and as redeemer of chance, I taught them to create the future, and to redeem by creating all that *has been*.

I taught them to redeem man's past and to transform every "It was," until the will says: "But I willed it so! So shall I will it!"

This I called redemption. This alone I taught them to call redemption.

Now I await *my* redemption, so that I may go to them for the last time.

For once more I will go to men. *Among* them my sun shall set. In dying I will give them my richest gift!

I learned this from the sun when it goes down, the overflowing one. Then it pours gold into the sea out of its inexhaustible riches,

so that even the poorest fisherman rows with *golden* oars! I once saw this, and I did not tire of weeping as I watched it.

Like the sun, Zarathustra too will go down. Now he sits here and waits, with old broken tables around him, and new tables too, half-written.

4.

Behold, here is a new table. But where are my brothers who will carry it with me down to the valley and into hearts of flesh?

This is what my great love for the most distant ones demands: *Do not be considerate of your neighbor!* Man is something that must be surpassed.

There are many different ways and means of surpassing. See to it *yourself*! But only a buffoon thinks: "Man can also be *leaped over*."

Surpass yourself even in your neighbor. And a right that you can seize, you should not allow to be given to you!

What you do, no one can do back to you. Behold, there is no repayment.

Whoever cannot command himself shall obey. And many can command themselves, but still badly lack obedience to themselves!

5.

This is what the noble type of soul wants: to have nothing *for free*, least of all life.

Whoever belongs to the rabble wants to live for free. But we others, to whom life has given itself, are always considering *what* we can best give *in return*!

And truly, it is a noble saying: "What life promises *us*, that promise *we* will keep to life!"

One should not want to enjoy where one does not contribute to the enjoyment. And one should not *want* to enjoy at all!

For enjoyment and innocence are the most bashful things. Neither likes to be sought. One should *have* them, but one should rather *seek* guilt and pain!

6.

O my brothers, whoever is a firstborn is always sacrificed. And now we are the firstborn!

We all bleed on secret sacrificial altars. We all burn and roast in honor of ancient idols.

Our best is still young, and that excites old palates. Our flesh is tender; our skin is only lambskin. How could we fail to tempt the old idol-priests!

The old idol-priest still lives *within ourselves*, and he roasts our best for his banquet. Ah, my brothers, how could the firstborn fail to be sacrifices!

But our kind wants it so. And I love those who do not wish to preserve themselves. Those who go under I love with my whole love, for they go beyond.

7.

To be truthful: few *can* be! And whoever can, will not! But the good are the least able of all to be truthful.

Oh, those good people! *Good people never speak the truth.* For the spirit, being good in this way is a sickness.

They give way, these good people; they submit. Their heart repeats what it is told, and their soul obeys. But whoever obeys *does not listen to himself!*

Everything the good call evil must come together for a single truth to be born. O my brothers, are you evil enough for *this* truth?

The daring venture, the long distrust, the cruel No, the weariness, the cutting into living flesh: how seldom do *these* come together! Yet it is from such seed that truth is produced!

Until now, all *knowledge* has grown up *alongside* the bad conscience! Break, break the old tables, you who seek knowledge!

8.

When the water has planks across it, when walkways and railings span the stream, no one believes the person who says, "Everything is in flux."

Even the simpletons contradict him. "What?" say the simpletons. "Everything in flux? But the planks and railings are still *above* the stream!

"*Above* the stream everything is solid: all the values of things, the bridges and supports, all 'good' and 'evil.' These are all *solid*!"

But then comes the hard winter, the tamer of streams, and even the cleverest learn distrust. And truly, then it is not only the simpletons who say: "Shouldn't everything *stand still*?"

"At bottom, everything stands still." That is a fitting winter doctrine. It is good cheer for a barren season, a great comfort for those who sleep through the winter and those who lounge by the fire.

"At bottom, everything stands still." But the thawing wind preaches the *opposite*!

The thawing wind is a bull, but not a plowing bull. It is a furious bull, a destroyer, that breaks the ice with angry horns! And the ice in turn *breaks the walkways!*

O my brothers, isn't everything *now in flux*? Haven't all the railings and walkways fallen into the water? Who would still *cling* to "good" and "evil"?

"Woe to us! Hail to us! The thawing wind is blowing!" Preach this, my brothers, through every street!

9.

There is an old illusion, and it is called good and evil. Until now, this illusion has revolved around soothsayers and astrologers.

Once people *believed* in soothsayers and astrologers, and *therefore* they believed: "Everything is fate. You shall, because you must!"

Then people came to distrust all soothsayers and astrologers, and *therefore* they believed: "Everything is freedom. You can, because you will!"

O my brothers, about the stars and the future there has so far been only illusion, not knowledge. And *therefore* about good and evil there has so far been only illusion, not knowledge!

10.

"You shall not rob! You shall not kill!" Such commandments were once called holy. Before them people bent the knee and bowed the head, and took off their shoes.

But I ask you: where in the world have there ever been better robbers and killers than such holy commandments?

Is there not robbing and killing in all life itself? And when such commandments were called holy, was not *truth* itself thereby killed?

Or was it a sermon of death that called holy whatever contradicted life and turned people away from it? O my brothers, break, break the old tables for me!

11.

This is my pity for all the past: I see that it has been abandoned,

abandoned to the favor, the spirit, and the madness of every generation that comes along and reinterprets everything that has been as a bridge to itself!

A great despot might arise, a cunning prodigy, who through his approval and disapproval could force and bend the whole past until it became his bridge, his forerunner, his herald, and his cock's crow.

But this is the other danger, and my other pity: the thoughts of a man of the mob reach back only to his grandfather. And with his grandfather, time comes to an end.

So all the past is abandoned. For it might one day happen that the mob became master and drowned all time in shallow waters.

Therefore, O my brothers, a *new nobility* is needed. It must oppose all rule by the mob and by despots alike, and write the word "noble" anew on new tables.

For many noble ones are needed, and many kinds of noble ones, *to make a new nobility!* Or, as I once said in a parable: "This is exactly what divinity is: that there are gods, but no God!"

12.

O my brothers, I consecrate you and point you toward a new nobility. You shall become begetters and cultivators and sowers of the future,

and truly, not a nobility you could buy like traders with traders' gold. Whatever has its price is worth little.

From now on, let your honor lie not in where you come from but in where you are going! Your will, and your feet that seek to go beyond you: let these be your new honor!

Truly, your honor is not that you have served a prince (what do princes count for now!), nor that you have become a bulwark for what already stands, so that it may stand more firmly.

Nor is it that your family has grown courtly at court, and that you have learned, brightly colored like the flamingo, to stand for long hours in shallow pools.

(For the *ability to stand* is a merit in courtiers, and all courtiers believe that the bliss after death includes *permission to sit*!)

Nor is it even that a spirit called Holy led your forefathers into promised lands, which I do not praise. For where the worst of all trees grew, the cross, in that land there is nothing to praise!

And truly, wherever this "Holy Spirit" led its knights on such campaigns, it was always goats and geese, cranks and muddleheads, who ran *at the front*!

O my brothers, your nobility shall not gaze backward but *outward*! You shall be exiles from all fatherlands and forefather-lands!

You shall love your *children's land*. Let this love be your new nobility: the undiscovered land in the farthest seas! For that land I bid your sails search and search!

You shall *make amends* to your children for being the children of your fathers. *That* is how you shall redeem all the past! This new table I place over you!

13.

"Why live? Everything is vain! To live is to thresh straw. To live is to burn oneself and still not get warm."

Such ancient babble still passes for "wisdom." And *because* it is old and smells musty, it is honored all the more. Even mold confers nobility.

Children might speak this way: they *shun* the fire because it has burned them! There is much childishness in the old books of wisdom.

And if someone forever "threshes straw," why should he be allowed to rail against threshing? Such a fool ought to be muzzled!

Such people sit down at the table and bring nothing with them, not even a good appetite. And then they complain: "Everything is vain!"

But to eat and drink well, my brothers, is truly no vain art! Break, break for me the tables of those who are never joyful!

14.

"To the pure, all things are pure," the people say. But I say to you: to swine, all things become swinish!

That is why the dreamers and the bowed-heads, whose hearts are bowed down too, preach: "The world itself is a filthy monster."

For these are all unclean spirits, especially those who have no peace or rest unless they see the world *from behind*: the backworldsmen!

To *them* I say it to their faces, though it may sound unpleasant: the world is like a human being in that it has a backside. *That much* is true!

There is much filth in the world: *that much* is true! But that does not make the world itself a filthy monster!

There is wisdom in the fact that much in the world smells bad. Disgust itself creates wings, and powers that can divine hidden springs!

Even in the best there is still something to loathe, and even the best is still something that must be surpassed!

O my brothers, there is much wisdom in the fact that there is much filth in the world!

15.

I heard pious backworldsmen speak sayings like these to their consciences. And truly, they spoke without malice or deceit, although nothing in the world is more deceitful or more wicked.

"Let the world be as it is! Do not lift a finger against it!"

"Let anyone who wants to choke and stab and flay and fleece the people do so. Do not lift a finger against it! In that way they will learn to renounce the world."

"And your own reason you shall stifle and choke yourself, for it is a reason of this world. In that way you yourself will learn to renounce the world."

Shatter, shatter, O my brothers, those old tables of the pious! Tear to tatters the maxims of the slanderers of the world!

16.

"Whoever learns much unlearns all violent desires." That is what people now whisper to each other in every dark alley.

"Wisdom makes weary; nothing is worthwhile; you shall not desire!" I found this new table hanging even in the public marketplaces.

Break for me, O my brothers, break this *new* table too! The world-weary hung it up, along with the preachers of death and the jailer. For look: it is also a sermon for slavery.

They learned badly, and not the best things, and everything too early and too fast. They *ate* badly, and that is how they ruined their stomachs.

For their spirit is a ruined stomach, and *it* counsels death! For truly, my brothers, the spirit *is* a stomach!

Life is a well of delight. But for anyone through whom the ruined stomach speaks, that father of gloom, every fountain is poisoned.

To know: that is *delight* to the lion-willed! But whoever has grown weary is himself merely "willed"; all the waves play with him.

And this is always the way of weak people: they lose themselves along the way. And at last their weariness asks: "Why did we ever set out on the way? It is all the same!"

To *them* it sounds pleasant to hear preached: "Nothing is worthwhile! You shall not will!" But that is a sermon for slavery.

O my brothers, Zarathustra comes as a fresh, blustering wind to all who are weary of the way. He will yet make many noses sneeze!

My free breath blows even through walls, and into prisons and imprisoned spirits!

Willing liberates, for willing is creating: so I teach. And you shall learn *only* in order to create!

And you shall *learn* learning itself only from me, learning well! Whoever has ears, let him hear!

17.
