There stands the boat. Over there it goes, perhaps into vast nothingness. But who wants to step into this "Perhaps"?

None of you wants to step into the death-boat! How then can you be *world-weary*!

World-weary! And you have not even withdrawn from the earth! I have always found you eager for the earth, still in love with your own weariness of it!

Your lip does not droop for nothing: a small earthly wish still sits on it! And in your eye, doesn't a little cloud of unforgotten earthly bliss still float?

There are many good inventions on earth, some useful, some pleasant. For their sake the earth is to be loved.

And some of these inventions are so good that they are like a woman's breasts: useful and pleasant at the same time.

But you world-weary ones! You idlers of the earth! You should be beaten with the whip! With the whip you should be given lively limbs again.

For if you are not invalids, or worn-out creatures of whom the earth is weary, then you are sly sluggards, or dainty, sneaking, pleasure-loving cats. And if you will not *run* happily again, then you shall pass away!

One should not try to be a physician to the incurable: so Zarathustra teaches. So pass away!

But it takes more *courage* to make an end than to make a new verse. All physicians and poets know that well.

18.

O my brothers, there are tables created by weariness, and tables created by laziness, rotten laziness. Although they speak alike, they want to be heard differently.

Look at this exhausted man! He is only a hand's breadth from his goal, but out of weariness he has stubbornly lain down in the dust, this brave one!

Out of weariness he yawns at the path, at the earth, at the goal, and at himself. He will not take one more step, this brave one!

Now the sun burns down on him, and the dogs lick at his sweat. But he lies there in his stubbornness and prefers to waste away,

to waste away a hand's breadth from his goal! Truly, you would have to drag him into his heaven by the hair of his head, this hero!

Better still to let him lie where he has lain down, so that sleep, the comforter, may come to him with cooling, pattering rain.

Let him lie until he wakes of his own accord, until of his own accord he rejects all weariness and everything weariness has taught through him!

Only, my brothers, be sure to chase the dogs away from him, the idle sneaks, and all the swarming vermin,

all the swarming vermin of the "cultured," who feast on the sweat of every hero!

19.

I draw circles around me, and holy boundaries. Fewer and fewer climb with me up higher and higher mountains. I build a mountain range out of ever holier mountains.

But wherever you would climb with me, O my brothers, take care that no *parasite* climbs with you!

A parasite: that is a reptile, a creeping, cringing reptile that tries to fatten itself on your sick and sore places.

And *this* is its art: it senses where climbing souls are weary. In your trouble and dejection, in your sensitive modesty, it builds its loathsome nest.

Where the strong are weak, where the noble are all too gentle, there it builds its loathsome nest. The parasite lives where the great have small sore places.

What is the highest of all kinds of being, and what is the lowest? The parasite is the lowest kind. But whoever belongs to the highest kind feeds the most parasites.

For the soul that has the longest ladder, and can go deepest down: how could it fail to carry the most parasites?—

—The most comprehensive soul, which can run and stray and roam farthest within itself; the most necessary soul, which out of sheer joy flings itself into chance:—

—The soul in Being, which plunges into Becoming; the soul that already possesses, yet still SEEKS to reach desire and longing:—

—The soul fleeing from itself, which catches up with itself in the widest circle; the wisest soul, to which folly speaks most sweetly:—

—The most self-loving soul, in which all things have their current and countercurrent, their ebb and their flow:—oh, how could THE LOFTIEST SOUL fail to have the worst parasites?

20.

O my brothers, am I cruel, then? But I say: What is falling, one should also push!

Everything of today is falling and decaying. Who would want to preserve it? But I—I want to push it as well!

Do you know the delight of rolling stones down into steep depths?—Those people of today: just watch how they roll down into my depths!

I am a prelude to better players, my brothers! An example! ACT according to my example!

And anyone you do not teach to fly, teach him, I beg you—TO FALL FASTER!—

21.

I love the brave. But it is not enough to be a swordsman. One must also know WHOM to use one's swordsmanship against!

Often it is the greater bravery to hold back and pass by, so that one saves oneself for a worthier enemy!

You should have only enemies worth hating, never enemies worth despising. You must be proud of your enemies. I have taught this already.

Save yourselves, my brothers, for the worthier enemy. That is why you must pass many people by,—

—Especially many of the rabble, who fill your ears with noise about the people and about peoples.

Keep your eyes clear of their For and Against! There is much right there, and much wrong. Whoever looks on grows angry.

Looking in and lashing out are the same thing there. So go off into the forests and lay your sword to sleep!

Go YOUR ways! And let the people and the peoples go theirs. They are gloomy ways, truly, on which not a single glimmer of hope shines any longer!

Let the trader rule there, where all that still glitters is traders' gold. The time of kings is over. What now calls itself "the people" is unworthy of kings.

See how these peoples now behave just like traders. They pick up the smallest advantage from every kind of rubbish!

They lay traps for one another and coax things out of one another, and they call that "good neighbourliness." O blessed, distant age when a people said to itself: "I will be—MASTER over peoples!"

For, my brothers, the best shall rule, and the best also WANT to rule! And wherever the teaching says otherwise, there—the best is LACKING.

22.

If THEY had bread for nothing—alas! What would THEY have left to clamour for? Earning their keep is what really keeps them occupied. And they shall have it hard!

They are beasts of prey. In their "working" there is still plundering, and in their "earning" there is still cheating! That is why they shall have it hard!

In this way they will become better beasts of prey: subtler, cleverer, MORE HUMAN. For man is the best beast of prey.

Man has already robbed all the animals of their virtues. That is why, of all animals, man has had the hardest time.

Only the birds are still beyond him. And if man should ever learn to fly, alas! TO WHAT HEIGHT would his greed fly!

23.

This is how I would have man and woman: the one fit for war, the other fit for motherhood, but both fit for dancing, with head and legs alike.

And let the day be counted lost on which we have not danced at least once. And let every truth be counted false that did not bring at least one laugh with it!

24.

Your arranging of marriages: see that it does not turn out to be a bad ARRANGEMENT! You have arranged too hastily, and so marriage-breaking FOLLOWS from it!

Yet marriage-breaking is better than marriage-bending and marriage-lying! A woman once said to me: "Yes, I broke the marriage. But first the marriage broke—me!"

I have always found the badly matched to be the most vengeful. They make everyone suffer for the fact that they no longer walk alone.

That is why I want honest people to say to each other: "We love each other. Let us SEE TO IT that we keep our love alive! Or will our promise turn out to be a blunder?"

—"Give us a fixed term and a small marriage, so that we can see whether we are fit for the great marriage! It is a great thing always to be two."

This is my advice to all honest people. And what would my love for the Superman, and for all that is to come, be worth if I advised or spoke otherwise!

Not only to reproduce yourselves onward, but UPWARD—may the garden of marriage help you to that, my brothers!

25.

Whoever has grown wise about the old origins will at last go looking for the springs of the future and for new origins.

My brothers, it will not be long before NEW PEOPLES arise and new springs rush down into new depths.

For the earthquake chokes many wells and causes much suffering from thirst. But it also brings hidden powers and secrets to light.

The earthquake uncovers new springs. When old peoples are shaken by earthquakes, new springs burst forth.

And whoever cries out, "Look, here is a well for many who are thirsty, one heart for many who long, one will for many instruments"—around him gathers a PEOPLE, that is, many who are attempting.

Who can command, and who must obey—THAT is what is being attempted there! Ah, with what long seeking, guessing, failing, learning, and trying again!

Human society is an attempt—so I teach—a long seeking. But what it seeks is the ruler!

—An attempt, my brothers! And NOT a "contract"! Destroy, I beg you, destroy that word of the soft-hearted and the half-and-half!

26.

O my brothers! Where does the greatest danger to the whole human future lie? Does it not lie with the good and just?—

—With those who say and feel in their hearts: "We already know what is good and just, and we possess it too. Woe to those who are still searching for it!"

Whatever harm the wicked may do, the harm the good do is the most harmful harm!

And whatever harm the slanderers of the world may do, the harm the good do is the most harmful harm!

O my brothers, someone once looked into the hearts of the good and just and said: "They are the Pharisees." But people did not understand him.

The good and just themselves were not free to understand him. Their spirit was imprisoned in their good conscience. The stupidity of the good is unfathomably clever.

But this is the truth: the good MUST be Pharisees. They have no choice!

The good MUST crucify anyone who devises his own virtue! That IS the truth!

But the second person who discovered their country—the country, heart, and soil of the good and just—was the one who asked: "Whom do they hate most?"

They hate the CREATOR most: the one who breaks the tables and the old values, the breaker. Him they call the lawbreaker.

For the good CANNOT create. They are always the beginning of the end:—

—They crucify whoever writes new values on new tables. They sacrifice the future TO THEMSELVES. They crucify the whole human future!

The good have always been the beginning of the end.—

27.

O my brothers, have you understood this word too? And what I once said about the "last man"?—

Where does the greatest danger to the whole human future lie? Does it not lie with the good and just?

BREAK, BREAK, I BEG YOU, THE GOOD AND JUST!—O my brothers, have you understood this word too?

28.

You run from me? You are frightened? You tremble at this word?

O my brothers, only when I told you to break the good, and the tables of the good, did I launch man onto his high seas.

Only now does the great terror come upon him: the great outlook, the great sickness, the great nausea, the great seasickness.

The good taught you false shores and false securities. You were born and raised in the lies of the good. The good have twisted and distorted everything down to the root.

But whoever discovered the country called "man" also discovered the country called "man's future." Now you shall be my sailors, brave and patient!

Stand upright in good time, my brothers. Learn to keep yourselves upright! The sea is storming, and many are trying to pull themselves up by holding on to you.

The sea is storming: everything is at sea. Well then! Take heart, you old sailor-hearts!

What is a fatherland to us? Our helm strives toward the place where our CHILDREN'S LAND lies! Our great longing storms in that direction, stormier than the sea!—

29.

"Why so hard?" the charcoal once said to the diamond. "Aren't we close relatives?"

Why so soft? O my brothers, that is what I ask you. Are you not—my brothers?

Why so soft, so submissive and yielding? Why is there so much denial and self-denial in your hearts? Why is there so little fate in your eyes?

And if you will not be fates, unyielding ones, how can you one day—conquer with me?

And if your hardness will not flash and cut and chop things to pieces, how can you one day—create with me?

For creators are hard. And it must seem bliss to you to press your hand upon millennia as if upon wax,—

—Bliss to write upon the will of millennia as if upon bronze: harder than bronze, nobler than bronze. Only the noblest is completely hard.

This new table, O my brothers, I set up over you: BECOME HARD!—

30.

O my Will! You turning-point of every need, MY necessity! Keep me from all small victories!

You destiny of my soul, which I call fate! You In-me! Over-me! Keep me and save me for one great fate!

And your last greatness, my Will, save it for your last task, so that you may be unyielding IN your victory! Ah, who has not been overcome by his own victory?

Ah, whose eye has not grown dim in that drunken twilight? Ah, whose foot has not stumbled in victory and forgotten how to stand?—

—So that one day I may be ready and ripe in the great noontide: ready and ripe like glowing ore, like a cloud heavy with lightning, like a swelling udder full of milk:—

—Ready for myself and for my most hidden Will: a bow eager for its arrow, an arrow eager for its star:—

—A star, ready and ripe in its noontide, glowing, pierced, blessed by annihilating sun-arrows:—

—A sun itself, and an unyielding sun-will, ready for annihilation in victory!

O Will, you turning-point of every need, MY necessity! Save me for one great victory!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LVII. THE CONVALESCENT.

1.

One morning, not long after his return to his cave, Zarathustra sprang up from his bed like a madman. He cried out in a terrible voice and acted as if someone else were still lying on the bed who refused to get up.

His voice rang out so loudly that his animals came to him in fright. From all the nearby caves and hiding places, every creature slipped away, flying, fluttering, crawling, or leaping, each according to its kind of foot or wing. But Zarathustra spoke these words:

Up, abysmal thought, out of my depths! I am your rooster and your dawn, you oversleeping reptile. Up! Up! My voice will soon crow you awake!

Loosen the chains on your ears and listen! For I want to hear you! Up! Up! Here is thunder enough to make even the graves listen!

And rub the sleep and all the dimness and blindness out of your eyes! Hear me with your eyes as well: my voice is a medicine even for those born blind.

And once you are awake, you shall stay awake forever. It is not MY custom to wake great-grandmothers from their sleep only to tell them—go back to sleep!

You stir, you stretch, you wheeze? Up! Up! You shall not wheeze, you shall speak to me! Zarathustra is calling you, Zarathustra the godless!

I, Zarathustra, the advocate of life, the advocate of suffering, the advocate of the circle: I call you, my most abysmal thought!

Joy to me! You are coming. I hear you! My abyss SPEAKS. I have turned my lowest depth out into the light!

Joy to me! Come here! Give me your hand—ha! let go! aha!—Disgust, disgust, disgust—woe is me!

2.

But hardly had Zarathustra spoken these words when he fell down as if dead, and he stayed that way for a long time. When he came to himself again, he was pale and trembling, and he stayed lying down. For a long time he would neither eat nor drink.

This condition lasted seven days. His animals did not leave him day or night, except that the eagle flew off to fetch food. Whatever it fetched and gathered, it laid on Zarathustra's bed.

So at last Zarathustra lay among yellow and red berries, grapes, rosy apples, sweet-smelling herbs, and pine cones. And at his feet lay two lambs, which the eagle had carried off from their shepherds only with great difficulty.

At last, after seven days, Zarathustra sat up on his bed, took a rosy apple in his hand, smelled it, and found the smell pleasant. Then his animals thought the time had come to speak to him.

"O Zarathustra," they said, "you have now lain like this for seven days with heavy eyes. Will you not get back on your feet?

Step out of your cave: the world is waiting for you like a garden. The wind is playing with heavy fragrances that are looking for you, and all the streams would like to run after you.

All things long for you, because you have stayed alone for seven days. Step out of your cave! All things want to be your physicians!

Did some new knowledge come to you, perhaps, a bitter and painful knowledge? You lay there like rising dough. Your soul rose and swelled beyond all its bounds.—"

—O my animals, answered Zarathustra, go on talking like this and let me listen! It refreshes me so much to hear you talk. Where there is talk, the world lies before me like a garden.

How lovely it is that there are words and sounds! Are not words and sounds rainbows, illusory bridges between things that are eternally separate?

Each soul has a world of its own. For each soul, every other soul is a back-world, a world hidden behind its own.

Appearance deceives most delightfully between things that are most alike. The smallest gap is the hardest to bridge.

For me, how could there be anything outside of me? There is no outside! But we forget this when we hear sounds. How delightful it is that we forget!

Were not names and sounds given to things so that human beings might refresh themselves with them? Speaking is a beautiful folly. With it, man dances over everything.

How lovely all speech is, and all the falsehoods of sound! With sounds our love dances on many-colored rainbows.—

—"O Zarathustra," his animals then said, "to those who think as we do, all things dance by themselves. They come and offer their hand, and laugh, and run away—and come back.

"Everything goes, everything comes back; the wheel of existence rolls on eternally. Everything dies, everything blossoms again; the year of existence runs on eternally.

"Everything breaks, everything is joined together anew; the same house of existence builds itself eternally. All things part, all things greet one another again; the ring of existence remains eternally true to itself.

"Existence begins in every moment. Around every 'Here' rolls the sphere of 'There.' The center is everywhere. The path of eternity is crooked."—

—You jokers, you barrel organs cranking out the same tune! answered Zarathustra, and smiled again. How well you know what had to be accomplished in those seven days:—

—And how that monster crept into my throat and choked me! But I bit off its head and spat it away from me.

And you—you have already made a song for the lyre out of it? Yet here I lie, still exhausted from that biting and spitting, still sick from my own deliverance.

*And you watched all of it?* O my animals, are you cruel too? Did you enjoy watching my great pain, the way people do? For man is the cruelest animal.

Until now, man has been happiest on earth at tragedies, bullfights, and crucifixions. And when he invented his hell, that was his heaven on earth.

When the great man cries out, the little man immediately comes running, his tongue hanging out of his mouth with craving. He, however, calls it his "pity."

The little man, especially the poet—how passionately he accuses life in words! Listen to him, but do not fail to hear the delight that lies in all accusation!

Life overcomes such accusers with a single glance. "You love me?" says the insolent one. "Wait a little. I have no time for you yet."

Man is the cruelest animal toward himself. In all who call themselves "sinners," "bearers of the cross," and "penitents," do not overlook the sensual pleasure in their laments and accusations!

And I myself—do I want to be man's accuser in saying this? Ah, my animals, this is the only thing I have learned so far: the most evil in man is necessary for his best,—

—All that is most evil is his best *power*, and the hardest stone for the highest creator. Man must become better *and* more evil:—

I was not tied to *this* torture stake, the knowledge that man is evil. Instead, I cried out as no one has ever cried out:

"Ah, that his most evil is so very small! Ah, that his best is so very small!"

The great disgust at man—*it* choked me. It had crept into my throat. And so had what the soothsayer foretold: "All is the same, nothing is worthwhile, knowledge chokes."

A long twilight limped along ahead of me, a sadness weary to death and drunk to death, which spoke with a yawning mouth.

"He returns eternally, the man you are weary of, the small man"—so my sadness yawned. It dragged its foot and could not fall asleep.

The human earth became a cavern to me. Its breast caved in. Everything living became human dust and bones and moldering past to me.

My sighing sat on all human graves and could no longer rise. My sighing and questioning croaked and choked, and gnawed and nagged, day and night:

—"Ah, man returns eternally! The small man returns eternally!"

I had once seen both of them naked, the greatest man and the smallest man. They were all too like one another—all too human, even the greatest man!

All too small, even the greatest man!—that was my disgust at man! And the eternal return of even the smallest man!—that was my disgust at all existence!

Ah, disgust! Disgust! Disgust!—Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he sighed and shuddered, for he remembered his sickness. Then his animals kept him from speaking further.

"Do not speak further, you convalescent!" his animals answered. "Instead, go out where the world waits for you like a garden.

"Go out to the roses, the bees, and the flocks of doves! Above all, go to the songbirds, so that you may learn *singing* from them!

"For singing is for the convalescent; the healthy may talk. And when the healthy also want songs, they want different songs from those of the convalescent."

—"You jokers, you barrel organs, do be quiet!" answered Zarathustra, smiling at his animals. "How well you know what consolation I devised for myself in those seven days!

"That I must sing once more—*that* is the consolation I devised for myself, and *this* is my convalescence. Will you make another song for the lyre out of that too?"

—"Do not talk further," his animals answered once more. "Rather, you convalescent, first make yourself a lyre, a new lyre!

"For look, O Zarathustra! Your new songs need new lyres.

"Sing and overflow, O Zarathustra. Heal your soul with new songs, so that you may bear your great fate, which has never yet been anyone's fate!

"For your animals know well, O Zarathustra, who you are and must become. Behold, *you are the teacher of the eternal return*—that is now *your* fate!

"You must be the first to teach this teaching. How could this great fate not also be your greatest danger and sickness!

"Behold, we know what you teach: that all things return eternally, and we ourselves with them, and that we have already existed countless times, and all things with us.

"You teach that there is a great year of Becoming, a monster of a great year. Like an hourglass, it must always be turned over again, so that it may run down and run out again:—

"—So that all these years are like one another, in the greatest things and in the smallest. And so we ourselves, in every great year, are like ourselves, in the greatest things and in the smallest.

"And if you were to die now, O Zarathustra, behold, we also know how you would then speak to yourself. But your animals beg you not to die yet!

"You would speak without trembling, rather buoyant with bliss, for a great weight and burden would be lifted from you, you most patient one!—

"'Now I die and disappear,' you would say, 'and in a moment I am nothing. Souls are as mortal as bodies.

"'But the knot of causes in which I am entangled returns—and it will create me again! I myself belong among the causes of the eternal return.

"'I come again with this sun, with this earth, with this eagle, with this serpent—*not* to a new life, or a better life, or a similar life:

"'—I come again eternally to this identical, selfsame life, in its greatest things and its smallest, to teach once more the eternal return of all things,—

"'—To speak once more the word of the great noontide of earth and man, to proclaim the Superman to man once more.

"'I have spoken my word. I break apart on my word. So my eternal fate wills it—as the one who proclaims it, I perish!

"'The hour has now come for the down-goer to bless himself. Thus *ends* Zarathustra's down-going.'"—

When the animals had said these words, they fell silent and waited for Zarathustra to say something to them. But Zarathustra did not notice that they were silent.

Instead, he lay quietly with his eyes closed, like someone asleep, although he was not sleeping, for he was communing with his soul. When the serpent and the eagle found him silent in this way, they respected the great stillness around him and prudently withdrew.

## LVIII. THE GREAT LONGING.

O my soul, I taught you to say "today" just as you say "once upon a time" and "formerly," and to dance your round over every Here and There and Over-there.

O my soul, I freed you from every corner and nook. I brushed the dust and spiders and twilight off you.

O my soul, I washed the petty shame and the narrow, nook-dwelling virtue from you, and persuaded you to stand naked before the eyes of the sun.

With the storm called "spirit" I blew across your surging sea. I blew every cloud away from it. I even strangled the strangler called "sin."

O my soul, I gave you the right to say No like the storm, and to say Yes as the open sky says Yes. You remain as calm as the light, and now you walk through storms that deny.

O my soul, I gave you back your freedom over what is created and what is uncreated. And who knows the sensual delight of the future as you know it?

O my soul, I taught you a contempt that does not come gnawing like a worm: the great, loving contempt, which loves most where it despises most.

O my soul, I taught you to persuade so well that you persuade even the very grounds to come to you, like the sun, which persuades even the sea to rise to its height.

O my soul, I took from you all obeying, all bending of the knee, all paying of homage. I myself gave you the names "Change of need" and "Fate."

O my soul, I gave you new names and brightly colored playthings. I called you "Fate," and "the Circuit of circuits," and "the Umbilical cord of time," and "the Azure bell."

O my soul, I gave your soil all wisdom to drink: all new wines, and also all the immemorially old, strong wines of wisdom.

O my soul, I poured every sun upon you, and every night, every silence, and every longing. Then you grew up for me like a vine.

O my soul, now you stand there, exuberant and heavy, a vine with swelling udders and full clusters of brown-golden grapes:—

—Filled and weighed down by your happiness, waiting out of superabundance, and yet ashamed of your waiting.

O my soul, there is no soul anywhere that could be more loving, more embracing, or more wide-ranging! Where could future and past lie closer together than in you?

O my soul, I have given you everything, and all my hands have become empty because of you. And now! Now you say to me, smiling and full of melancholy: "Which of us owes thanks?—

"—Does the giver not owe thanks because the receiver received? Is giving not a need? Is receiving not—pity?"—

O my soul, I understand the smile of your melancholy. Your overabundance itself now stretches out longing hands!

Your fullness looks out over raging seas, and searches, and waits. The longing of overfullness looks out from the smiling sky of your eyes!

And truly, O my soul! Who could see your smile and not melt into tears? The angels themselves melt into tears at the overflowing kindness of your smile.

It is your kindness and overflowing kindness that will not complain and weep. And yet, O my soul, your smile longs for tears, and your trembling mouth longs for sobs.

"Is not all weeping complaining? And all complaining accusing?" So you say to yourself. And that is why, O my soul, you would rather smile than pour out your grief—

—Rather than pour out in gushing tears all your grief over your fullness, and over the vine's craving for the grape-gatherer and the vintage knife!

But if you will not weep, if you will not weep out your purple melancholy, then you will have to *sing*, O my soul!—Look, I who foretell this to you am smiling myself:

—You will have to sing with a passionate song, until all the seas fall calm to listen to your longing,—

—Until over calm, longing seas the boat glides, the golden marvel, around whose gold all good, bad, and marvelous things leap and play:—

—Many large and small animals too, and everything that has light, marvelous feet, so that it can run on violet-blue paths,—

—Toward the golden marvel, the boat that comes of its own accord, and toward its master. He is the grape-gatherer who waits with the diamond vintage knife,—

—Your great deliverer, O my soul, the nameless one—for whom only future songs will find names! And truly, your breath already carries the fragrance of future songs,—

—Already you glow and dream, already you drink thirstily at all the deep, echoing wells of consolation, already your melancholy rests in the bliss of future songs!—

O my soul, now I have given you everything, even my last possession, and all my hands have become empty because of you. *That I told you to sing*—behold, that was the last thing I had to give!

That I told you to sing—say now, say: *which* of us now owes thanks?—But better still: sing to me, sing, O my soul! And let me thank you!—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LIX. THE SECOND DANCE-SONG.

1.

"I looked into your eyes not long ago, O Life. I saw gold gleaming in your night-eyes—my heart stood still with delight:

—I saw a golden boat gleaming on darkened waters, a sinking, drinking, winking-again, golden rocking boat!

At my dance-mad foot you cast a glance, a laughing, questioning, melting, tossed glance:

Only twice did you shake your rattle with your little hands—and already my feet were swinging in a dancing frenzy.—

My heels reared up high, my toes listened—they wanted to know you. Does a dancer not have his ear—in his toes!

I sprang toward you: then you fled back from my bound, and your fleeing, flying hair swept toward me all around!

I sprang away from you and from your snaky tresses: then you stood there half-turned, your eyes full of caresses.

With sidelong glances you teach me crooked ways; on crooked ways my feet learn—cunning fancies!

I fear you near, I love you far; your flight lures me, your seeking holds me fast:—I suffer, but for you, what would I not gladly bear!

For you, whose coldness sets me on fire, whose hatred leads me astray, whose flight binds me in chains, whose mockery—pleads with me:

—Who would not hate you, you great binder, entangler, temptress, seeker, finder! Who would not love you, you innocent, impatient, wind-swift, child-eyed sinner!

Where are you pulling me now, you paragon and tomboy? And now you flee and make a fool of me; you sweet romp, how you tease!

I dance after you, I follow even your faintest traces, alone. Where are you? Give me your hand! Or just one finger!

Here are caves and thickets: we will lose our way!—Stop! Stand still! Don't you see the owls and bats fluttering in a swarm?

You bat! You owl! Would you play me false? Where are we? You learned this barking and howling from the dogs.

You snap at me sweetly with little white teeth; your wicked eyes dart out at me from under your curly little mane!

This is a dance over stick and stone: I am the hunter—will you be my hound, or the chamois I chase?

Now beside me! Quickly, wickedly leaping! Now up! And over!—Alas! I leapt too far and fell myself!

Oh, look at me lying here, you arrogant one, begging for mercy! I would gladly walk with you—in some lovelier place!

—On the paths of love, through quiet, trim, many-colored bushes! Or there along the lake, where goldfish dance and swim!

Are you tired now? Up there are sheep and streaks of sunset: isn't it sweet to sleep while the shepherd plays his pipe?

Are you so very tired? I will carry you there; just let your arm drop! And if you are thirsty—I would have something, but your mouth would not care to drink it!—

—Oh, that cursed, nimble, supple snake, that lurking witch! Where have you gone? But on my face I feel two spots from your hand, red blotches that itch!

Truly, I am tired of always being your sheepish shepherd. You witch, if until now I have sung to you, now YOU shall—cry out to me!

To the rhythm of my whip you shall dance and cry out! Did I forget my whip?—Not I!"—

2.

Then Life answered me, keeping her delicate ears covered as she spoke:

"O Zarathustra! Don't crack your whip so terribly! Surely you know that noise kills thought—and just now such delicate thoughts were coming to me.

We are both genuine good-for-nothings and do-no-harms. Beyond good and evil we found our island and our green meadow—we two alone! So we must be friendly to each other!

And even if we do not love each other from the bottom of our hearts—must we bear a grudge against each other because our love is not perfect?

You know that I am friendly to you, and often too friendly. The reason is that I am jealous of your Wisdom. Ah, that mad old fool, Wisdom!

If your Wisdom ever ran away from you, ah! then my love would quickly run away from you too."—

Then Life looked thoughtfully behind her and around her, and said softly: "O Zarathustra, you are not faithful enough to me!

You do not love me nearly as much as you say; I know you are thinking of leaving me soon.

There is an old, heavy, heavy booming clock: at night its booming carries up to your cave:—

—When you hear this clock strike the hours at midnight, then between one and twelve you think about it—

—You think, O Zarathustra, I know it—of leaving me soon!"—

"Yes," I answered hesitantly, "but you know this too"—And I whispered something into her ear, in among her tangled, yellow, foolish locks.

"You KNOW that, O Zarathustra? No one knows that—"

And we gazed at each other, and looked at the green meadow over which the cool evening was just passing, and we wept together.—But at that moment Life was dearer to me than all my Wisdom had ever been.—

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

3.

One!

O man! Take heed!

Two!

What does deep midnight's voice declare?

Three!

"I slept my sleep—

Four!

"From deepest dream I've woken, and I plead:—

Five!

"The world is deep,

Six!

"And deeper than the day could read.

Seven!

"Deep is its woe—

Eight!

"Joy—deeper still than grief can be:

Nine!

"Woe says: Pass away! Go!

Ten!

"But all joy wants eternity—

Eleven!

"Wants deep, profound eternity!"

Twelve!

## LX. THE SEVEN SEALS.

(OR THE YES AND AMEN SONG.)

1.

If I am a seer, full of that prophetic spirit which wanders along high mountain ridges between two seas,—

Which wanders between past and future like a heavy cloud—hostile to sultry lowlands, and to everything that is weary and can neither die nor live:

Ready for lightning in its dark breast, and for the redeeming flash of light; charged with lightning bolts that say Yes! that laugh Yes! ready for prophetic flashes of lightning:—

—Blessed, however, is the one who is so charged! And truly, whoever is one day to kindle the light of the future must hang long on the mountain like a heavy storm!—

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

2.

If ever my anger has burst open graves, moved boundary stones, or rolled old shattered tablets into steep depths:

If ever my scorn has scattered rotten words to the winds, and if I have come like a broom to the cross-spiders, and like a cleansing wind to old charnel houses:

If ever I have sat rejoicing where old gods lie buried, blessing the world, loving the world, beside the monuments of the old slanderers of the world:—

—For I love even churches and the graves of gods, if only heaven looks with pure eyes through their broken roofs; I gladly sit like grass and red poppies on ruined churches—

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

3.

If ever a breath of the creative breath has come to me, a breath of that heavenly necessity which compels even chance to dance in star-dances:

If ever I have laughed with the laughter of the creative lightning, which the long thunder of the deed follows, grumbling but obedient:

If ever I have played dice with the gods at the divine table of the earth, so that the earth shook and split open and snorted out streams of fire:—

—For the earth is a divine table, trembling with new creative words and the dice-throws of the gods:

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

4.

If ever I have drunk a full draught from that foaming bowl of spices and blends in which all things are well mixed:

If ever my hand has mixed the farthest with the nearest, fire with spirit, joy with sorrow, and the harshest with the kindest:

If I myself am a grain of that redeeming salt which makes everything in the bowl mix well:—

—For there is a salt that unites good with evil; and even the most evil thing is worthy, as spice and as the final foaming over:—

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

5.

If I love the sea and everything sea-like, and love it most when it angrily contradicts me:

If that delight in exploring is in me which drives sails toward the undiscovered, if the seafarer's delight is part of my delight:

If ever my rejoicing has cried out: "The shore has vanished—now the last chain has fallen from me—

The boundless roars around me, far away space and time glitter for me—well then! Take heart, old heart!"—

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

6.

If my virtue is a dancer's virtue, and if I have often leapt with both feet into golden-emerald rapture:

If my wickedness is a laughing wickedness, at home among banks of roses and hedges of lilies:

—For in laughter all evil is present, but it is made holy and absolved by its own bliss:—

And if it is my Alpha and Omega that everything heavy shall become light, every body a dancer, and every spirit a bird: and truly, that is my Alpha and Omega!—

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

7.

If ever I have spread a calm sky above me and flown into my own sky on my own wings:

If I have swum playfully in deep, luminous distances, and if the bird-wisdom of my freedom has come to me:—

—And this is how bird-wisdom speaks:—"Look, there is no above and no below! Throw yourself about—outward, backward, you light one! Sing! Speak no more!

—Are not all words made for the heavy? Do not all words lie to those who are light? Sing! Speak no more!"—

Oh, how could I not burn with longing for Eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings—the ring of the return?

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I would want to have children, unless it is this woman whom I love: for I love you, O Eternity!

FOR I LOVE YOU, O ETERNITY!

## FOURTH AND LAST PART.

Ah, where in the world have there been greater follies than among the pitying? And what in the world has caused more suffering than the follies of the pitying?

Woe to all who love without having a height that rises above their pity!

Thus the devil once spoke to me: "Even God has his hell: it is his love for man."

And recently I heard him say these words: "God is dead: God died of his pity for man."—ZARATHUSTRA, II., "The Pitiful."

## LXI. THE HONEY SACRIFICE.

—And again moons and years passed over Zarathustra's soul, and he paid no attention to them; but his hair turned white.

One day he sat on a stone in front of his cave and gazed calmly into the distance—from there one looks out over the sea and far beyond winding abysses. Then his animals walked thoughtfully around him, and at last they placed themselves in front of him.

"O Zarathustra," they said, "are you perhaps looking out for your happiness?"—"What does my happiness matter!" he answered. "I stopped striving for happiness long ago. I strive for my work."

—"O Zarathustra," the animals said again, "you say that like someone who has more than enough good things. Aren't you lying in a sky-blue lake of happiness?"

—"You jesters," Zarathustra answered, and smiled, "how well you chose that comparison! But you also know that my happiness is heavy, not like a flowing wave of water. It presses on me and will not let me go. It is like molten pitch."—

Then his animals again walked thoughtfully around him and placed themselves in front of him once more. "O Zarathustra," they said, "is THAT the reason you yourself keep growing yellower and darker, even though your hair looks white and flaxen? Look, you are sitting in your pitch!"

—"What are you saying, my animals?" said Zarathustra, laughing. "Truly, I spoke slanderously when I spoke of pitch. What is happening to me happens to all fruits that ripen. It is the HONEY in my veins that makes my blood thicker, and my soul stiller too."

—"So it will be, O Zarathustra," answered his animals, pressing close to him. "But won't you climb a high mountain today? The air is pure, and today one can see more of the world than ever."

—"Yes, my animals," he answered, "your advice is admirable and suits my heart: today I will climb a high mountain! But see that honey is ready at hand up there—yellow, white, good, ice-cool, golden honey in the comb. For know that up there I will make the honey sacrifice."—

When Zarathustra reached the summit, however, he sent home the animals that had come with him, and found that he was now alone. Then he laughed from the bottom of his heart, looked around him, and spoke thus:

My talk of sacrifices and honey sacrifices was merely a trick of speech, and truly, a useful foolishness! Up here I can speak more freely than in front of mountain caves and the pets of hermits.

What is there to sacrifice! I squander what is given to me, a squanderer with a thousand hands: how could I call that—sacrificing?

And when I asked for honey, I was only asking for bait: for sweet slime and syrup, which make even the mouths of growling bears and of strange, sulky, evil birds water:

—The best bait, the kind hunters and fishermen need. For if the world is like a gloomy forest full of animals, a pleasure ground for all wild hunters, it seems to me rather—and I prefer it so—a bottomless, rich sea;

—a sea full of fish and crabs of every color, a sea that even the gods might long for. They might be tempted to become fishermen there and cast their nets, so rich is the world in wonderful things, great and small!

This is especially true of the human world, the human sea. Toward THAT sea I now cast my golden fishing rod, and I say: Open up, you human abyss!

Open up, and throw me your fish and your shining crabs! With my best bait I will lure the strangest human fish to myself today!

I throw my happiness itself out far and wide, everywhere between east, noon, and west, to see whether many human fish will learn to tug and pull at my happiness.

Then, biting on my sharp hidden hooks, they will have to come up to MY height: the most motley creatures from the bottom of the abyss, drawn up by the wickedest of all fishers of men.

For THIS is what I am, at heart and from the beginning: someone who draws, draws in, draws upward, and raises up. I am a drawer, a trainer, a disciplinarian, who once, and not in vain, advised himself: "Become what you are!"

So people may now come UP to me. I am still waiting for the signs that it is time for my descent. I do not yet go down among people myself, as I eventually must.

That is why I wait here on high mountains, sly and scornful. I am not impatient, and I am not patient either. Rather, I am someone who has even unlearned patience, because he no longer "suffers."

My fate gives me time. Has it perhaps forgotten me? Or is it sitting behind a big stone, catching flies?

And truly, I feel kindly toward my eternal fate, because it does not hound and hurry me. It leaves me time for fun and mischief. That is why I have climbed this high mountain today to catch fish.

Has anyone ever caught fish on high mountains? Even if what I am seeking and doing up here is folly, it is better than going down below and growing solemn with waiting, turning green and yellow—

—becoming a posturing, snorting, angry man made sour by waiting, a holy storm howling down from the mountains, an impatient one who shouts down into the valleys: "Listen, or I will whip you with the scourge of God!"

Not that I hold a grudge against such angry people for that. They are good enough for a laugh. They must be impatient now, those big alarm drums, which will find a voice now or never!

But my fate and I do not speak to the Present, nor do we speak to the Never. For speaking we have patience, and time, and more than time. For one day it must come, and it may not pass by.

What must one day come and may not pass by? Our great Hazar: our great, distant human kingdom, the Zarathustra-kingdom of a thousand years—

How distant may this "distance" be? What does that matter to me? It is no less certain to me for that. I stand securely with both feet on this ground—

—on eternal ground, on hard primeval rock, on this highest, hardest, primeval mountain ridge. All the winds come to it as to the place where storms divide, asking: Where? and From where? and To where?

Laugh here, laugh, my hearty, healthy wickedness! Cast down your glittering, scornful laughter from the high mountains! With your glitter, lure the finest human fish for me!

And whatever belongs to ME in all the seas, whatever is in-and-for-me in all things: fish THAT out for me, bring THAT up to me. That is what I wait for, the wickedest of all fishermen.

Out, out, my fishing hook! In and down, you bait of my happiness! Drip your sweetest dew, you honey of my heart! Bite, my fishing hook, into the belly of all black sorrow!

Look out, look out, my eye! Oh, how many seas surround me, what dawning human futures! And above me, what rosy-red stillness! What cloudless silence!

## LXII. THE CRY OF DISTRESS.

The next day Zarathustra was sitting again on the stone in front of his cave. His animals were out roaming the world to bring home new food, and also new honey, for Zarathustra had spent and squandered the old honey down to the last drop.

As he sat there with a stick in his hand, tracing the shadow of his figure on the ground and reflecting (truly, not on himself and his shadow), he suddenly started and shrank back. He had seen another shadow beside his own.

He quickly looked around and stood up. There beside him stood the soothsayer, the same one he had once fed and given drink at his table. This was the prophet of the great weariness, who taught: "All is the same, nothing is worthwhile, the world is meaningless, knowledge strangles."

But his face had changed since then. When Zarathustra looked into his eyes, his heart was startled once again, so many evil omens and ashen-grey flashes of lightning passed across that face.

The soothsayer, who had noticed what was going on in Zarathustra's soul, wiped his face with his hand, as if to wipe away the impression. Zarathustra did the same. When both of them had silently collected and steadied themselves in this way, they shook hands as a sign that they wanted to recognize each other once more.

"Welcome," said Zarathustra, "you soothsayer of the great weariness. It shall not have been for nothing that you were once my table companion and guest. Eat and drink with me again today, and forgive a cheerful old man for sitting at the table with you!"

"A cheerful old man?" answered the soothsayer, shaking his head. "But whoever you are, or want to be, Zarathustra, you have been up here the longest time. Before long your boat will no longer rest on dry land!"

"Do I rest on dry land, then?" asked Zarathustra, laughing.

"The waves around your mountain," answered the soothsayer, "are rising and rising, the waves of great distress and misery. Soon they will lift your boat too and carry you away."

At that Zarathustra fell silent and wondered.

"Do you still hear nothing?" the soothsayer went on. "Isn't something rushing and roaring up out of the depths?"

Zarathustra fell silent again and listened. Then he heard a long, long cry, which the abysses tossed to one another and passed along. None of them wanted to keep it, so evil did it sound.

"You bearer of bad news," said Zarathustra at last, "that is a cry of distress, and the cry of a human being. Perhaps it comes out of a black sea. But what does human distress matter to me! My last sin, the one saved up for me: do you know what it is called?"

"PITY!" answered the soothsayer from an overflowing heart, raising both hands high. "Zarathustra, I have come to seduce you into your last sin!"

Hardly had he spoken when the cry sounded again, longer and more alarming than before, and much nearer. "Do you hear it? Do you hear it, Zarathustra?" called the soothsayer. "The cry concerns you. It is calling you: Come, come, come. It is time, high time!"

At that Zarathustra was silent, confused and shaken. At last he asked, like someone hesitating within himself: "And who is it that calls me there?"

"But surely you know," answered the soothsayer warmly. "Why are you hiding? It is THE HIGHER MAN who is crying out for you!"

"The higher man?" cried Zarathustra, horrified. "What does HE want? What does HE want? The higher man! What does he want here?" And his skin broke out in sweat.

The soothsayer paid no attention to Zarathustra's alarm, but kept listening and listening toward the depths below. When it had been quiet there for a long while, he looked back and saw Zarathustra standing there trembling.

"Zarathustra," he began in a sorrowful voice, "you are not standing there like someone whose happiness makes him dizzy. You will have to dance so that you don't fall over!

But even if you danced before me and leapt all your sideways leaps, no one could say to me: 'Look, here dances the last joyful man!'

Anyone who came up to this height looking for HIM would come in vain. He would find caves, certainly, and caves behind caves, hiding places for those who hide. But he would find no lucky mines, no treasure chambers, no new veins of the gold of happiness.

Happiness: how could anyone find happiness among such people, buried alive and solitary! Must I still go looking for the last happiness on the Happy Isles, far away among forgotten seas?

But all is the same, nothing is worthwhile, no searching helps, there are no Happy Isles anymore!"

So sighed the soothsayer. But at his last sigh Zarathustra became calm and confident again, like someone who has climbed out of a deep chasm into the light. "No! No! Three times no!" he exclaimed in a strong voice, stroking his beard. "THAT I know better! There are still Happy Isles! Keep quiet about THAT, you sighing sack of sorrow!

Stop splashing on THAT, you morning rain cloud! Am I not already standing here wet with your misery, drenched like a dog?

Now I'll shake myself and run away from you, so that I can get dry again. Don't be surprised at that! Do I seem rude to you? But here is MY court.

As for the higher man: very well! I will look for him at once in those forests. That is where his cry came FROM. Perhaps an evil beast has him hard pressed.

He is in MY domain, and here he shall come to no harm! And truly, there are many evil beasts around me."

With those words Zarathustra turned to leave. Then the soothsayer said: "Zarathustra, you are a rogue!

I know it well: you want to be rid of me! You would rather run into the forest and set traps for evil beasts!

But what good will it do you? In the evening you will have me again. I will sit in your own cave, patient and heavy as a block of wood, and wait for you!"

"So be it!" Zarathustra shouted back as he went away. "And whatever is mine in my cave belongs to you too, my guest!

But if you find any honey in there, go ahead and lick it up, you growling bear, and sweeten your soul! For this evening we both want to be in good spirits—

—in good spirits and glad that this day has come to an end! And you yourself shall dance to my songs, as my dancing bear.

You don't believe it? You shake your head? Well then! Cheer up, old bear! But I too am a soothsayer."

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LXIII. TALK WITH THE KINGS.

1.

Zarathustra had not been walking an hour in the mountains and forests when he suddenly saw a strange procession. Coming down the very path he was about to descend were two kings, decked out in crowns and purple sashes and as brightly colored as flamingos. They were driving a loaded donkey ahead of them.

"What do these kings want in my domain?" said Zarathustra to his heart in astonishment, and he quickly hid behind a thicket. But when the kings came near him, he said half aloud, like someone talking only to himself: "Strange! Strange! How does this fit together? I see two kings, and only one ass!"

At that the two kings stopped. They smiled, looked toward the place the voice had come from, and then looked into each other's faces. "We think such things among ourselves too," said the king on the right, "but we don't say them out loud."

The king on the left, however, shrugged and answered: "That may be a goatherd. Or a hermit who has lived too long among rocks and trees. For having no company at all also spoils good manners."

"Good manners?" the other king replied, angry and bitter. "Then what is it we are running away from? Isn't it 'good manners'? Our 'good society'?

Truly, it is better to live among hermits and goatherds than with our gilded, false, over-rouged mob, even if it calls itself 'good society.'

Even if it calls itself 'nobility.' There everything is false and rotten, above all the blood, thanks to old, evil diseases and even worse doctors.

The best and dearest to me today is still a sound peasant: coarse, cunning, stubborn, and enduring. That is the noblest type today.

Today the peasant is the best, and the peasant type should be master! But this is the kingdom of the mob, and I no longer let anything be imposed on me. The mob, though, means hodgepodge.

Mob-hodgepodge: in it everything is mixed with everything, saint and swindler, gentleman and Jew, and every beast out of Noah's ark.

Good manners! With us everything is false and rotten. No one knows how to revere anymore, and that is exactly what we are running away from. They are fawning, pushy dogs. They gild palm leaves.

This disgust chokes me: that we kings ourselves have become false, draped and disguised in the old faded pomp of our ancestors, showpieces for the stupidest, the craftiest, and anyone who trades in power these days.

We are NOT the first men, and yet we have to STAND IN for them. We have finally grown weary and sick of this pretense.

We have stepped out of the way of the rabble, of all those bawlers and scribbling blowflies, of the stench of traders, the fidgeting of ambition, the bad breath. Ugh, to live among the rabble—

—ugh, to pass for the first men among the rabble! Ah, disgust! Disgust! Disgust! What do we kings matter now!"

"Your old sickness is seizing you," said the king on the left at this point. "Your disgust is seizing you, my poor brother. But you know that someone is listening to us."

At once Zarathustra, who had been all ears and eyes during this talk, rose from his hiding place, walked toward the kings, and began:

"The one who listens to you, who gladly listens to you, is called Zarathustra.

I am Zarathustra, who once said: 'What do kings matter now!' Forgive me, but I was glad when you said to each other: 'What do we kings matter!'

But here is MY domain and jurisdiction. What could you be seeking in my domain? Perhaps, though, you have FOUND on your way what I am looking for: namely, the higher man."

When the kings heard this, they beat their breasts and said with one voice: "We have been recognized!

"With the sword of your words you cut through the thickest darkness of our hearts. You have discovered our distress. For look: we are on our way to find the higher man.

"We mean the man who is higher than we are, even though we are kings. We are bringing him this ass. For the highest man should also be the highest lord on earth.

"There is no worse misfortune in all of human destiny than when the powerful of the earth are not also the first among men. Then everything becomes false, distorted, and monstrous.

"And when the powerful are even the last of men, more beast than man, then the populace rises higher and higher in honor. At last the virtue of the populace itself declares: 'Look, I alone am virtue!'"

"What have I just heard?" answered Zarathustra. "What wisdom in kings! I am enchanted. Indeed, I already feel the urge to make a rhyme about it.

"It may turn out to be a rhyme not suited to every ear. But I unlearned long ago any consideration for long ears. Well then! Here it is!"

(At this point, however, the ass also found its voice. It said clearly and maliciously: Y-E-A.)

'Twas once—I think the first year of our blessed Lord—Drunk without wine, the Sibyl thus deplored: "How badly things go! Decline! Decline! The world has never sunk so low! Rome has turned harlot and brothel too, Rome's Caesar a beast, and God—has turned Jew!"

2.

The kings were delighted with Zarathustra's rhymes. The king on the right said: "O Zarathustra, how good it was that we set out to see you!

"Your enemies showed us your likeness in their mirror. There you wore the grimace of a devil, sneering, and we were afraid of you.

"But what good did that do? Again and again your sayings pierced our hearts and ears. At last we said: What does it matter how he looks!

"We must HEAR him, the one who teaches: 'You shall love peace as a means to new wars, and the short peace more than the long!'

"No one ever spoke such warlike words: 'What is good? To be brave is good. It is the good war that makes every cause holy.'

"O Zarathustra, the blood of our fathers stirred in our veins at such words. It was like the voice of spring speaking to old wine casks.

"When swords crossed one another like red-spotted serpents, our fathers grew fond of life. The sun of every peace seemed weak and lukewarm to them, and a long peace made them ashamed.

"How our fathers sighed when they saw swords hanging on the wall, brightly polished and dried up! Like those swords, they thirsted for war. For a sword thirsts to drink blood, and it sparkles with desire."

As the kings went on eagerly about the happiness of their fathers, Zarathustra felt no small desire to mock their eagerness. Clearly these were very peaceable kings standing before him, kings with old and refined faces. But he restrained himself.

"Well!" he said. "That way leads the path; up there lies Zarathustra's cave. And this day is to have a long evening! For now, however, a cry of distress calls me away from you in haste.

"It will honor my cave if kings choose to sit and wait in it. But you will certainly have to wait a long time!

"Well, what of it? Where does anyone learn to wait better these days than at court? And the only virtue that kings have left, isn't it called today: the ABILITY to wait?"

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LXIV. THE LEECH.

Zarathustra went on, deep in thought, further and lower down, through forests and past marshy hollows. And as happens to anyone who ponders hard matters, he stepped on a man without noticing.

At once a cry of pain spurted into his face, along with two curses and twenty foul insults. In his fright, Zarathustra raised his stick and struck the man he had stepped on. But immediately he regained his composure, and his heart laughed at the foolish thing he had just done.

"Forgive me," he said to the man he had stepped on, who had gotten up furious and then sat down again. "Forgive me, and first of all hear a parable.

"Imagine a wanderer on a lonely road, dreaming of distant things, who stumbles without noticing onto a sleeping dog lying in the sun.

"Both of them start up and snap at each other like deadly enemies, two creatures frightened to death. That is what happened to us.

"And yet! And yet how little was missing for them to caress each other, that dog and that lonely man! Are they not both lonely ones?"

"Whoever you are," said the man, still furious, "you tread too close to me with your parable, and not only with your foot!

"Look! Am I a dog, then?" With that, the seated man got up and pulled his bare arm out of the swamp. At first he had been lying stretched out on the ground, hidden and invisible, like someone lying in wait for swamp game.

"But what are you doing?" Zarathustra cried in alarm, for he saw a great deal of blood streaming over the bare arm. "What has hurt you? Has some evil beast bitten you, you poor man?"

The bleeding man laughed, still angry. "What does it matter to you?" he said, and was about to walk on. "Here I am at home, in my own province. Let anyone question me who likes. But I will hardly answer a blockhead."

"You are mistaken," said Zarathustra with sympathy, and held him fast. "You are mistaken. Here you are not at home but in my domain, and in it no one shall come to harm.

"Call me whatever you like. I am who I must be. I call myself Zarathustra.

"Well! Up there is the way to Zarathustra's cave. It is not far. Won't you come and tend your wounds at my home?

"Life has treated you badly, you poor man. First a beast bit you, and then a man stepped on you!"

When the man heard the name Zarathustra, however, he was transformed. "What is happening to me!" he exclaimed. "WHO occupies me more in this life than this one man, Zarathustra, and that one animal that lives on blood, the leech?

"For the leech's sake I lay here by this swamp like a fisherman. My outstretched arm had already been bitten ten times, and now an even finer leech bites at my blood: Zarathustra himself!

"O happiness! O miracle! Blessed be this day that lured me into the swamp! Blessed be the best and liveliest cupping glass alive today! Blessed be the great leech of conscience, Zarathustra!"

So spoke the man who had been stepped on. Zarathustra was pleased with his words and their refined, reverent style. "Who are you?" he asked, and gave him his hand. "There is much to clear up and explain between us. But already, it seems to me, a pure, clear day is dawning."

"I am THE CONSCIENTIOUS ONE OF THE SPIRIT," answered the man. "In matters of the spirit, it would be hard for anyone to be more rigorous, more narrow, and more severe than I am, except the one from whom I learned it, Zarathustra himself.

"Better to know nothing than to half-know many things! Better to be a fool on one's own account than a sage by other people's approval! I go to the foundation.

"What does it matter whether it is great or small? Whether it is called swamp or sky? A handbreadth of foundation is enough for me, if it really is foundation and solid ground!

"On a handbreadth of foundation, one can stand. In true, knowing knowledge, there is nothing great and nothing small."

"Then perhaps you are an expert on the leech?" asked Zarathustra. "And you investigate the leech down to its ultimate foundation, you conscientious one?"

"O Zarathustra," answered the man, "that would be something enormous. How could I presume to do that!

"What I am master and knower of is the BRAIN of the leech. That is MY world!

"And it is a world too! Forgive me for letting my pride speak here, but in this I have no equal. That is why I said: 'Here I am at home.'

"How long I have investigated this one thing, the brain of the leech, so that the slippery truth could no longer slip away from me here! This is MY domain!

"For its sake I cast everything else aside. For its sake everything else became indifferent to me. And right beside my knowledge lies my black ignorance.

"My conscience of the spirit demands that it be so: that I know one thing and know nothing else. I loathe everything half-spiritual, everything hazy, hovering, and dreamy.

"Where my honesty ends, there I am blind, and I want to be blind. But where I want to know, there I also want to be honest, which means severe, rigorous, narrow, cruel, and relentless.

"Because YOU once said, O Zarathustra, 'Spirit is life that itself cuts into life,' I was led and drawn to your teaching. And truly, with my own blood I have increased my own knowledge!"

"As the evidence shows," Zarathustra broke in. The blood was still flowing down the conscientious man's bare arm, for ten leeches had bitten into it.

"O you strange fellow, how much this very evidence teaches me, namely you yourself! And perhaps I should not pour all of it into your rigorous ear!

"Well then! We part here. But I would gladly find you again. Up there is the way to my cave. Tonight you shall be my welcome guest there!

"I would also gladly make amends to your body for Zarathustra's having stepped on you. I am thinking about that. But right now a cry of distress calls me away from you in haste."

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LXV. THE MAGICIAN.

1.

When Zarathustra had gone around a rock, he saw on the same path, not far below him, a man throwing his limbs about like a madman, who at last fell flat on his belly. "Stop!" Zarathustra said to his heart. "That must surely be the higher man. That dreadful cry of distress came from him. I will see whether I can help him."

But when he ran to the spot where the man lay on the ground, he found a trembling old man with fixed, staring eyes. Zarathustra tried hard to lift him and set him back on his feet, but it was all in vain.

The unfortunate man did not even seem to notice that anyone was beside him. Instead, he kept looking around with pathetic gestures, like someone abandoned and cut off from the whole world. At last, after much trembling, convulsing, and curling himself up, he began to lament like this:

Who warms me, who loves me still?  
Give me burning fingers!  
Give me heart-warming braziers!  
Lying prone, stretched out, trembling,  
Like one half dead and cold whose feet are warmed—  
And shaken, ah! by unknown fevers,  
Shivering under sharp, icy frost-arrows,  
Hunted by you, my thought!  
Unspeakable! Hidden! Terrifying!  
You hunter behind the cloud-banks!  
Now struck down by your lightning,  
You mocking eye that watches me in darkness:  
—So I lie,  
Bend myself, twist myself, convulsed  
With every eternal torment,  
And struck  
By you, cruelest hunter,  
You unknown—GOD...

Strike deeper!  
Strike once more!  
Pierce and tear this heart!  
What does this torture mean  
With blunt, jagged arrows?  
Why do you look down here,  
Never weary of human pain,  
With mischief-loving, godly lightning-glances?  
You will not kill,  
Only torture, torture?  
Why torture—ME,  
You mischief-loving, unknown God?—

Ha! Ha!  
You creep near  
In midnight's gloomy hour?...  
What do you want? Speak!  
You crowd me, press me—  
Ha! now far too closely!  
You hear me breathing,  
You overhear my heart,  
You ever-jealous one!  
—Jealous of what?

Away! Away!  
Why the ladder?  
Do you want to GET IN?  
To climb into my heart?  
To climb into my most secret  
Thoughts?  
Shameless one! Unknown one!—Thief!  
What do you seek by your stealing?  
What do you seek by your listening?  
What do you seek by your torturing?  
You torturer!  
You—hangman-God!  
Or shall I, as mastiffs do,  
Roll over before you?  
And cringing, enraptured, frantic,  
Wag my tail—in friendship!

No use!  
Goad me further!  
Cruelest goader!  
No dog—just your quarry am I,  
Cruelest hunter!  
Your proudest captive,  
You robber behind the cloud-banks...  
Speak at last!  
You lightning-veiled one! You unknown one! Speak!  
What do you want, highway ambusher, from—ME?  
What do you WANT, unknown—God?  
What?  
Ransom?  
How much ransom?  
Ask for much—so my pride advises!  
And be brief—so my other pride advises!

Ha! Ha!  
ME—you want? Me?  
—All of me?...

Ha! Ha!  
And you torture me, fool that you are,  
Torture my pride to death?  
Give me LOVE—who warms me still?  
Who loves me still?—  
Give me burning fingers,  
Give me heart-warming braziers,  
Give me, the loneliest one,  
Whom ice (ah! sevenfold frozen ice  
Makes thirst for enemies,  
Even for foes),  
Give, yield to me,  
Cruelest foe,  
—YOURSELF!—

Gone!  
He has surely fled,  
My last, my only companion,  
My greatest foe,  
My unknown—  
My hangman-God!...

—No!  
Come back!  
WITH all your great tortures!  
To me, the last of the lonely,  
Oh, come back!  
All my hot tears run in streams  
Their course to you!  
And all my final heart's fire—  
Glows up to YOU!  
Oh, come back,  
My unknown God! My PAIN!  
My final happiness!

2.

At this point Zarathustra could restrain himself no longer. He took his staff and struck the wailing man with all his might. "Stop this!" he shouted at him with angry laughter. "Stop it, you play-actor! You counterfeiter! You liar from the very heart! I know you well!

"I'll soon warm your legs for you, you evil magician. I know very well how to make things hot for the likes of you!"

"Stop," said the old man, springing up from the ground. "Strike me no more, O Zarathustra! I did it only for amusement!

That kind of thing belongs to my art. I wanted to put *you* to the test when I gave this performance. And truly, you saw right through me!

But you have also given me no small proof of yourself. You are *hard*, wise Zarathustra! You strike hard with your 'truths,' and your cudgel forces *this* truth out of me!"

"Don't flatter me," answered Zarathustra, still agitated and frowning. "You are an actor to the core! You are false. Why do you talk about truth?

You peacock of peacocks, you sea of vanity, *what* were you acting out in front of me, you evil magician? *Whom* was I supposed to believe in when you wailed like that?"

"*The penitent in spirit*," said the old man. "That is who I was playing. You yourself once coined that expression:

the poet and magician who finally turns his spirit against himself, the transformed one who freezes to death from his own bad science and bad conscience.

And just admit it, Zarathustra: it took you a long time to discover my trick and my lie! You *believed* in my distress when you held my head in both your hands.

I heard you lament, 'We have loved him too little, loved him too little!' My wickedness rejoiced inside me that I had deceived you so far."

"You may have deceived people subtler than I am," said Zarathustra sternly. "I do not guard myself against deceivers. I *have* to be without precautions. That is what my fate requires.

But you *must* deceive. That much I know about you! You must always speak with two meanings, three, four, and five! Even what you have just confessed is not nearly true enough, or false enough, for me!

You wretched counterfeiter, how could you do otherwise? Even if you stood naked before your doctor, you would whitewash your own illness.

That is how you whitewashed your lie in front of me when you said, 'I did it *only* for amusement!' There was also *seriousness* in it. You *are* something of a penitent in spirit!

I see through you well. You have become the enchanter of the whole world. But for yourself you have no lie or trick left. You are disenchanted with yourself!

You have harvested disgust as your one truth. None of your words is genuine anymore, but your mouth is: that is, the disgust that clings to your mouth."

"Who are you, anyway?" the old magician cried at this, his voice defiant. "Who dares speak this way to *me*, the greatest man now living?" A green flash shot from his eye at Zarathustra. But immediately afterward he changed, and said sadly:

"O Zarathustra, I am weary of it. I am disgusted with my arts. I am not *great*. Why should I pretend? But you know it well: I sought greatness!

I wanted to look like a great man, and I persuaded many. But the lie has been beyond my strength. I am collapsing under it.

O Zarathustra, everything in me is a lie. But that I am collapsing, this collapse of mine, is *genuine*!"

"It does you honor," said Zarathustra gloomily, looking down and to the side. "It does you honor that you sought greatness, but it also gives you away. You are not great.

You wretched old magician, *this* is the best and most honest thing I honor in you: that you have grown weary of yourself and have said it aloud, 'I am not great.'

*In this* I honor you as a penitent in spirit. Even if only for the blink of an eye, in that one moment you were genuine.

But tell me, what are you looking for here in *my* forests and among *my* rocks? And if you have put yourself in *my* way, what proof of me did you want?

In what way did you put *me* to the test?"

So spoke Zarathustra, and his eyes flashed. The old magician was silent for a while. Then he said: "Did I put you to the test? I am only searching.

O Zarathustra, I am searching for someone genuine, someone true, someone simple and unambiguous, a man of perfect honesty, a vessel of wisdom, a saint of knowledge, a great man!

Don't you know it, Zarathustra? *I am searching for Zarathustra.*"

A long silence fell between them. Zarathustra became so deeply absorbed in thought that he closed his eyes. Then, returning to the moment, he took the magician's hand and spoke with great courtesy and shrewdness:

"Well! The path leads up there. That is Zarathustra's cave. In it you may search for the one you hope to find.

And ask advice from my animals, my eagle and my serpent. They will help you search. My cave, after all, is large.

I myself, admittedly, have not yet seen a great man. Today even the sharpest eye cannot perceive what is great. This is the kingdom of the mob.

I have found many who stretched and puffed themselves up, while the people cried, 'Look, a great man!' But what good are all these bellows? In the end the wind comes out.

In the end the frog that has puffed itself up too long bursts, and out comes the wind. To prick a swollen one in the belly is what I call good sport. Listen to that, you boys!

Our today belongs to the mob. Who still *knows* what is great and what is small? Who could search for greatness here and succeed? Only a fool. Fools succeed at it.

You are looking for great men, you strange fool? Who *taught* you that? Is today the time for it? Oh, you bad searcher, why do you tempt me?"

So spoke Zarathustra, comforted in his heart, and he went on his way laughing.

## LXVI. OUT OF SERVICE.

Not long after Zarathustra had freed himself from the magician, he again saw someone sitting beside the path he was following. It was a tall man dressed in black, with a gaunt, pale face, and *this man* troubled him greatly.

"Alas," he said to his heart, "there sits affliction in disguise. He seems to me to be of the priestly type. What do *they* want in my domain?

What! I have barely escaped that magician, and now another sorcerer must cross my path:

some wizard of the laying-on of hands, some gloomy wonder-worker by the grace of God, some anointed slanderer of the world. May the devil take him!

But the devil is never where he belongs. He always comes too late, that cursed dwarf with the clubfoot!"

So Zarathustra cursed impatiently in his heart, and he considered how he might slip past the man in black without looking at him. But it turned out otherwise. At that very moment the seated man had already noticed him.

Like someone overtaken by unexpected good fortune, the man sprang to his feet and went straight toward Zarathustra.

"Whoever you are, traveler," he said, "help a man who has lost his way, a seeker, an old man who could easily come to harm here!

This world is strange and remote to me. I have heard wild beasts howling. And the one who could have protected me no longer exists.

I was looking for the pious man, a saint and hermit who lived alone in his forest and had not yet heard what all the world knows now."

"*What* does all the world know now?" asked Zarathustra. "Perhaps that the old God in whom all the world once believed no longer lives?"

"You have said it," answered the old man sorrowfully. "And I served that old God until his last hour.

But now I am out of service, without a master, and still not free. Nor am I cheerful anymore, not even for an hour, except in memories.

That is why I climbed into these mountains: so that I might at last hold a festival for myself once more, as befits an old pope and father of the church. For know this: I am the last pope! I wanted a festival of pious memories and divine worship.

But now he too is dead, the most pious of men, the saint in the forest who constantly praised his God with singing and mumbling.

When I found his hut, I no longer found him. I found two wolves inside, howling over his death, for all the animals loved him. Then I hurried away.

Had I come into these forests and mountains for nothing? Then my heart resolved that I should seek another, the most pious of all those who do not believe in God. My heart resolved that I should seek Zarathustra!"

So spoke the white-haired man, and he looked sharply at the one standing before him. Zarathustra, however, took the old pope's hand and gazed at it for a long time with admiration.

"Look, venerable one," he then said, "what a fine, long hand! That is the hand of someone who has always given blessings. But now it holds the one you are looking for: me, Zarathustra.

It is I, the godless Zarathustra, who says: 'Who is more godless than I, so that I may enjoy his teaching?'"

So spoke Zarathustra, and his gaze pierced the thoughts of the old pope, and the thoughts behind those thoughts. At last the old man began:

"The one who loved and possessed him most has now also lost him most.

Look, surely I myself am now the most godless of us both? But who could rejoice in that?"

After a deep silence, Zarathustra asked thoughtfully: "You served him to the end? Do you know *how* he died? Is it true what they say, that pity choked him?

That he saw *man* hanging on the cross and could not bear it? That his love for man became his hell, and in the end his death?"

The old pope did not answer. He looked away timidly, with a pained and gloomy expression.
