After long reflection, still looking the old man straight in the eye, Zarathustra said: "Let him go.

Let him go. He is gone. And although it does you honor that you speak only in praise of this dead one, you know as well as I *who* he was, and that he went by strange ways."

"Speaking just between the three eyes of us," said the old pope cheerfully, for he was blind in one eye, "in divine matters I am better informed than Zarathustra himself, and I have every right to be.

My love served him for many years. My will followed all his will. But a good servant knows everything, including many things that a master hides even from himself.

He was a hidden God, full of secrets. Truly, he came by his son only through secret ways. At the door of his faith stands adultery.

Whoever praises him as a God of love does not think highly enough of love itself. Did that God not also want to be a judge? But one who truly loves loves without regard to reward or repayment.

When he was young, that God from the East was harsh and vengeful. He built himself a hell for the delight of his favorites.

But at last he grew old and soft and mellow and full of pity, more like a grandfather than a father, but most of all like a tottering old grandmother.

There he sat, shriveled, in his corner by the fire, fretting over his weak legs, weary of the world, weary of willing. And one day he suffocated on his excessive pity."

"Old pope," Zarathustra broke in at this point, "did you see *that* with your own eyes? It may well have happened that way: that way, *and* otherwise as well. When gods die, they always die many kinds of death.

Well! In any case, one way or another, he is gone! He offended the taste of my ears and eyes. I would rather not say anything worse against him than that.

I love everything that looks bright and speaks honestly. But he, as you surely know, old priest, had something of your type in him, the priestly type. He was ambiguous.

He was also unclear. How he raged at us, this snorter of wrath, because we understood him badly! But why did he not speak more clearly?

And if the fault lay in our ears, why did he give us ears that heard him badly? If there was dirt in our ears, well, who put it there?

Too much went wrong for him, this potter who never fully learned his trade! But that he took revenge on his pots and creations because they turned out badly, that was a sin against *good taste*.

There is good taste in piety too. *That* taste finally said: 'Away with *such* a God! Better to have no God, better to shape one's destiny on one's own account, better to be a fool, better to be God oneself!'"

"What do I hear!" said the old pope, listening intently. "O Zarathustra, with such unbelief you are more pious than you think! Some god in you has converted you to your godlessness.

Is it not your piety itself that no longer lets you believe in a God? And your overgreat honesty will yet lead you even beyond good and evil!

Look at what has been reserved for you! You have eyes and hands and a mouth that were destined from eternity to bless. One does not bless with the hand alone.

Near you, though you claim to be the most godless one, I sense a wholesome and holy fragrance of long blessings. It makes me both glad and sorrowful.

Let me be your guest, O Zarathustra, for a single night! Nowhere on earth will I now feel better than with you!"

"Amen! So be it!" said Zarathustra, greatly astonished. "The path leads up there. That is Zarathustra's cave.

Truly, I would gladly take you there myself, venerable one, for I love all pious men. But just now a cry of distress calls me hastily away from you.

In my domain no one shall come to harm. My cave is a good harbor. And what I would like best is to set every sorrowful person back on firm ground and firm legs.

But who could lift *your* melancholy from your shoulders? For that I am too weak. Truly, we would have to wait a long time for someone to reawaken your God for you.

For that old God no longer lives. He is truly dead."

So spoke Zarathustra.

## LXVII. THE UGLIEST MAN.

—And once again Zarathustra's feet ran through mountains and forests, and his eyes searched and searched. But nowhere could they find the one they wanted to see: the deeply distressed sufferer who had cried out.

All along the way, however, he rejoiced in his heart and was full of gratitude. "What good things this day has given me," he said, "to make up for its bad beginning! What strange people I have found to talk with!

"I will chew on their words for a long while, as if they were good grain. My teeth will grind and crush them small, until they flow like milk into my soul!"—

But when the path curved around a rock once more, the landscape suddenly changed, and Zarathustra entered a realm of death. Black and red cliffs rose up here, with no grass, no tree, and no birdsong.

It was a valley that all animals avoided, even the beasts of prey. The only exception was a kind of ugly, thick, green serpent that came here to die when it grew old. For this reason the shepherds called the valley "Serpent-Death."

Zarathustra, however, sank into dark memories, for it seemed to him that he had stood in this valley once before. A great heaviness settled on his mind, so that he walked slowly, then more slowly still, and finally stood still.

When he opened his eyes, he saw something sitting by the wayside. It was shaped like a man, and yet hardly like a man: something that could not be described.

All at once a great shame came over Zarathustra for having looked at such a thing. Blushing to the very roots of his white hair, he turned his eyes away and lifted his foot to leave this ill-fated place.

Then the dead wilderness found a voice. A noise welled up from the ground, gurgling and rattling, the way water gurgles and rattles at night through clogged pipes. At last it turned into a human voice and human speech, and it sounded like this:

"Zarathustra! Zarathustra! Solve my riddle! Speak, speak! *What is the revenge on the witness?*

"I am luring you back: here is slippery ice! Take care, take care that your pride doesn't break its legs here!

"You think you are wise, proud Zarathustra! Then solve the riddle, you cracker of hard nuts—the riddle that I am! Tell me, then: who am I?"

—And when Zarathustra heard these words, what do you think happened in his soul? *Pity overcame him.* He collapsed all at once, like an oak that has long withstood many woodcutters and then falls heavily and suddenly, to the terror even of those who meant to fell it.

But he immediately got up from the ground again, and his face became stern.

"I know you well," he said, in a voice like bronze. "*You are the murderer of God!* Let me go.

"You could not *endure* the one who saw *you*—who always saw you through and through, you ugliest man. You took revenge on this witness!"

So spoke Zarathustra, and he was about to leave. But the indescribable creature grabbed a corner of his robe and began again to gurgle and grope for words. "Stay," he said at last—

—"Stay! Don't pass me by! I have guessed what axe it was that struck you to the ground. Hail to you, Zarathustra, that you are back on your feet!

"You have guessed, I know it well, how the man feels who killed him—the murderer of God. Stay! Sit down here beside me. It will not be for nothing.

"To whom would I go, if not to you? Stay, sit down! But don't look at me! Honor my ugliness in that way!

"They are persecuting me, and now *you* are my last refuge. *Not* with their hatred, *not* with their bailiffs. Oh, I would mock such persecution, and be proud and cheerful about it!

"Hasn't all success so far belonged to those who were thoroughly persecuted? And whoever persecutes thoroughly readily learns to *follow*—once he has been left behind! But it is their *pity*—

"—it is their pity that I am fleeing from, fleeing to you. Zarathustra, protect me, my last refuge, the only one who has figured me out.

"You have guessed how the man feels who killed *him*. Stay! And if you must go, you impatient one, don't take the road I came by. *That* road is bad.

"Are you angry with me because I have tortured language for too long? Because I have already been giving you advice? But know that it is I, the ugliest man,

"—who also has the largest, heaviest feet. Wherever I have walked, the road is bad. I trample every path into death and ruin.

"But I saw that you passed me by in silence, and that you blushed. That is how I knew you were Zarathustra.

"Anyone else would have tossed me his charity, his pity, in a look or a word. But I am not beggar enough for that, and you guessed as much.

"For that I am too *rich*: rich in what is great, terrible, ugliest, most unspeakable! Your shame, Zarathustra, *honored* me!

"With difficulty I escaped the crowd of the pitying, so that I might find the only one who now teaches that 'pity is intrusive'—you, Zarathustra!

"Whether it is the pity of a God or human pity, it offends against modesty. And an unwillingness to help may be nobler than the virtue that rushes in to do so.

"Yet *that*, namely pity, is what all petty people now call virtue itself. They have no reverence for great misfortune, great ugliness, great failure.

"I look beyond all of them, the way a dog looks over the backs of crowded flocks of sheep. They are petty, well-fleeced, well-meaning, gray people.

"Just as the heron looks scornfully at shallow pools, with its head bent back, so I look at the throng of little gray waves and wills and souls.

"For too long we have conceded that they are right, these petty people. So in the end we have given them power as well. And now they teach that 'good is only what petty people call good.'

"And 'truth' today is what was said by the preacher who sprang from among them, that strange saint and advocate of the petty people, who testified of himself: 'I—am the truth.'

"That immodest man has long made the petty people very puffed up. He taught no small error when he taught: 'I—am the truth.'

"Has an immodest man ever been answered more politely? But you, Zarathustra, passed him by and said: 'No! No! Three times no!'

"You warned against his error. You were the first to warn against pity—not for everyone, and not for no one, but for yourself and your kind.

"You are ashamed at the shame of the great sufferer. And truly, when you say, 'From pity comes a heavy cloud; take heed, you men!'—

"—when you teach, 'All creators are hard, all great love is beyond its pity'—Zarathustra, how well you seem to me to read the signs of the weather!

"But you yourself must also warn yourself against *your* pity! For many are on their way to you: many who are suffering, doubting, despairing, drowning, freezing—

"I warn you against myself as well. You have solved my best and worst riddle: myself, and what I have done. I know the axe that fells you.

"But *he* had to die. He looked with eyes that saw *everything*. He saw the depths and dregs of human beings, all their hidden disgrace and ugliness.

"His pity knew no modesty. He crept into my dirtiest corners. This most prying, overly intrusive, overly pitying one had to die.

"He always saw *me*. On such a witness I had to have revenge, or else not live myself.

"The God who saw everything, *and man as well*: that God had to die! Man cannot *endure* that such a witness should live."

So spoke the ugliest man. Zarathustra, however, got up and prepared to go on, for he felt frozen to the very depths of his body.

"You indescribable one," he said, "you warned me against your path. As thanks, I will recommend mine to you. Look, up there is Zarathustra's cave.

"My cave is large and deep and has many corners. There even the most hidden one can find his hiding place. And close beside it are a hundred nooks and crannies for creatures that creep, flutter, and hop.

"You outcast, who have cast yourself out: you do not want to live among people and their pity? Well then, do as I do! That way you will also learn from me. Only the one who acts learns.

"And above all, talk to my animals! The proudest animal and the wisest animal—they may well be the right counselors for both of us!"

So spoke Zarathustra, and he went on his way even more thoughtfully and slowly than before. He was asking himself many things and hardly knew how to answer them.

"How poor man really is," he thought in his heart, "how ugly, how wheezing, how full of hidden shame!

"They tell me that man loves himself. Ah, how great that self-love must be! How much contempt stands against it!

"Even this man loved himself as much as he despised himself. A great lover, I think he is, and a great despiser.

"I have never yet found anyone who despised himself more thoroughly. Even *that* is a kind of height. Alas, was *this* perhaps the higher man whose cry I heard?

"I love the great despisers. Man is something that must be overcome."—

## LXVIII. THE VOLUNTARY BEGGAR.

When Zarathustra had left the ugliest man, he felt chilled and lonely. So much coldness and loneliness came over his spirit that even his limbs grew colder.

But as he wandered on and on, uphill and down, sometimes past green meadows and sometimes over wild, stony beds where perhaps an impatient brook had once run, he suddenly grew warmer and more cheerful again.

"What has happened to me?" he asked himself. "Something warm and living is reviving me. It must be nearby.

"Already I am less alone. Companions and brothers I don't yet know are roaming around me, and their warm breath touches my soul."

He looked around and searched for whatever was comforting his loneliness. And there, standing together on a hilltop, were cows. It was their nearness and their smell that had warmed his heart.

The cows, however, seemed to be listening eagerly to someone speaking, and paid no attention to the man approaching. When Zarathustra came quite close, he clearly heard a human voice speaking in the middle of the herd. Apparently all of the cows had turned their heads toward the speaker.

Zarathustra ran up quickly and pushed the animals aside, for he feared that someone had been hurt here, and that the cows' pity would hardly be able to help. But he was mistaken.

For there sat a man on the ground who seemed to be persuading the animals not to be afraid of him. He was a peaceful man, a Preacher-on-the-Mount, out of whose eyes kindness itself preached. "What are you looking for here?" Zarathustra called out in astonishment.

"What am I looking for here?" he answered. "The same thing you are looking for, you troublemaker: happiness on earth.

"For that purpose I would like to learn from these cows. I tell you, I have already been talking to them for half the morning, and they were just about to give me their answer. Why are you disturbing them?

"Unless we turn and become like cows, we shall by no means enter the kingdom of heaven. For there is one thing we ought to learn from them: ruminating.

"And truly, if a man should gain the whole world and yet not learn this one thing, ruminating, what would it profit him? He would not be rid of his affliction,

"—his great affliction, which today is called *disgust*. Who today does not have his heart, his mouth, and his eyes full of disgust? You too! You too! But look at these cows!"

So spoke the Preacher-on-the-Mount. Then he turned his own gaze toward Zarathustra, for until now it had rested lovingly on the cows. But at that moment his expression changed. "Who is this I am talking to?" he cried out in fright, and sprang up from the ground.

"This is the man without disgust. This is Zarathustra himself, the one who overcame the great disgust. This is the eye, this is the mouth, this is the heart of Zarathustra himself."

As he spoke, he kissed the hands of the man he was speaking to, with tears overflowing from his eyes. He behaved exactly like someone to whom a precious gift, a jewel, has unexpectedly fallen from heaven. The cows, meanwhile, watched it all and wondered.

"Don't speak about me, you strange one, you kind one!" said Zarathustra, holding back the man's affection. "Tell me first about yourself! Aren't you the voluntary beggar who once threw away great riches,

"—who was ashamed of his riches and of the rich, and fled to the poorest to give them his abundance and his heart? But they would not accept him."

"But they would not accept me," said the voluntary beggar. "You know that well enough. So in the end I went to the animals and to these cows."

"Then you learned," Zarathustra interrupted, "how much harder it is to give well than to take well, and that giving well is an *art*: the final and subtlest master-art of kindness."

"Especially nowadays," answered the voluntary beggar. "That is, now that everything low has become rebellious, standoffish, and haughty in its manner—in the manner of the mob.

"For the hour has come, as you know well enough, for the great, wicked, long, slow uprising of the mob and the slaves. It keeps spreading and spreading!

"Now all charity and petty giving only provokes the lower classes. The over-rich had better be on their guard!

"Whoever gives today in drips, like bulging bottles with far too narrow necks: people are now glad to break the necks of such bottles.

"Greedy lust, bitter envy, brooding revenge, mob pride: all of these struck my eye. It is no longer true that the poor are blessed. The kingdom of heaven, however, is with the cows."

"And why isn't it with the rich?" asked Zarathustra, testing him, while he held back the cows, which were sniffing familiarly at the peaceful man.

"Why are you tempting me?" the other replied. "You know the answer even better than I do. What drove me to the poorest, Zarathustra? Wasn't it my disgust at the richest?

—At the culprits of wealth, with their cold eyes and rank thoughts, who scrape profit out of every kind of rubbish. It was disgust at this rabble whose stench rises to heaven,

—At this gilded, counterfeit mob, whose fathers were pickpockets, or carrion crows, or rag-pickers, with wives who were compliant, lewd, and forgetful. None of them are much different from whores.

Mob above, mob below! What do 'poor' and 'rich' even mean nowadays? I unlearned that distinction. Then I fled farther and ever farther away, until I came to these cows."

So spoke the peaceful man, and he puffed and sweated as he spoke, so that the cows wondered all over again. Zarathustra, however, kept looking into his face with a smile the whole time the man talked so harshly, and silently shook his head.

"You are doing violence to yourself, you Preacher-on-the-Mount, when you use such harsh words. Neither your mouth nor your eyes were made for such harshness.

Nor, I think, was your stomach. All this rage and hatred and frothing over disgusts it. Your stomach wants gentler things. You are no butcher.

You seem to me, rather, a plant-eater and a man of roots. Perhaps you grind grain. In any case, you are certainly averse to the pleasures of the flesh, and you love honey."

"You have guessed me well," answered the voluntary beggar, his heart lighter. "I love honey, and I also grind grain. I have sought out what tastes sweet and makes the breath pure,

—And also what takes a long time: a day's work and a mouth's work for gentle idlers and sluggards.

These cows, to be sure, have carried it furthest. They have invented chewing the cud and lying in the sun. They also abstain from all heavy thoughts, which bloat the heart."

—"Well!" said Zarathustra. "You should also see MY animals, my eagle and my serpent. There are none like them on earth today.

Look, that way leads to my cave. Be its guest tonight. And talk to my animals about the happiness of animals,—

—Until I come home myself. For now a cry of distress calls me hurriedly away from you. And if you find fresh honey at my place, ice-cold, golden honey in the comb, eat it!

But now take leave of your cows at once, you strange one, you lovable one, however hard it may be for you. For they are your warmest friends and teachers!"—

—"Except for one whom I hold dearer still," answered the voluntary beggar. "You yourself are good, Zarathustra, and better even than a cow!"

"Away, away with you, you wicked flatterer!" cried Zarathustra mischievously. "Why do you spoil me with such praise and flattery-honey?

"Away, away from me!" he cried once more, and swung his stick at the affectionate beggar, who nimbly ran off.

## LXIX. THE SHADOW.

Hardly had the voluntary beggar hurried off, leaving Zarathustra alone again, when he heard a new voice behind him calling out: "Stop! Zarathustra! Wait! It's me, Zarathustra, really me, your shadow!"

But Zarathustra did not wait. A sudden irritation came over him at the crowds and crowding in his mountains. "Where has my solitude gone?" he said.

"It is truly becoming too much for me. These mountains are swarming. My kingdom is no longer of THIS world. I need new mountains.

My shadow is calling me? What does my shadow matter! Let it run after me! I will run away from it."

So Zarathustra spoke to his heart, and he ran away. But the one behind him followed, so that suddenly there were three runners, one behind another: first the voluntary beggar, then Zarathustra, and third and last, his shadow.

They had not run this way for long before Zarathustra became aware of his folly. With one jerk he shook off all his irritation and loathing.

"What!" he said. "Haven't the most ridiculous things always happened to us old hermits and saints?

Truly, my folly has grown big in the mountains! Now I hear six old fools' legs clattering one after another!

But does Zarathustra need to be frightened of his own shadow? Besides, I suspect that its legs are longer than mine after all."

So spoke Zarathustra. Laughing with his eyes and his gut, he stopped and turned around quickly, and in doing so he nearly knocked his shadow and follower to the ground. The follower had kept so close at his heels, and was so weak.

When Zarathustra examined him with his gaze, he was startled as if by a sudden ghost, so thin, dark, hollow, and worn out this follower looked.

"Who are you?" Zarathustra demanded. "What are you doing here? And why do you call yourself my shadow? I don't like you."

"Forgive me," answered the shadow, "that it is I. And if you don't like me, well, Zarathustra, I admire you and your good taste for it.

I am a wanderer who has long walked at your heels. I am always on the road, but without a goal and without a home. So truly I fall little short of being the eternal Wandering Jew, except that I am not eternal and not a Jew.

What? Must I always be on the road? Whirled about by every wind, unsettled, driven on? O earth, you have become too round for me!

I have already sat on every surface. Like tired dust I have fallen asleep on mirrors and windowpanes. Everything takes from me, and nothing gives. I grow thin. I am almost like a shadow.

But it was you, Zarathustra, whom I chased and pursued the longest. Though I hid myself from you, I was still your best shadow. Wherever you sat, I sat too.

With you I have wandered through the remotest, coldest worlds, like a ghost that chooses to haunt winter roofs and snows.

With you I have pushed into everything forbidden, everything worst and farthest. And if there is any virtue in me at all, it is that I have not feared any prohibition.

With you I have broken whatever my heart once revered. I have overturned every boundary stone and statue. I have chased the most dangerous wishes. Truly, I once went beyond every crime.

With you I unlearned my faith in words and values and great names. When the devil sheds his skin, doesn't his name fall away too? A name is also skin. Perhaps the devil himself is nothing but skin.

'Nothing is true, everything is permitted': so I told myself. I plunged head and heart into the coldest water. Ah, how often I stood there naked because of it, like a red crab!

Ah, where have all my goodness and all my shame and all my belief in the good gone? Ah, where is that lying innocence I once had, the innocence of the good and of their noble lies?

Too often, truly, I followed close on the heels of truth, and then it kicked me in the face. Sometimes I meant to lie, and then, look! Only then did I hit upon the truth.

Too much has become clear to me, and now none of it concerns me anymore. Nothing that I love is still alive. How then could I still love myself?

'To live as I please, or not to live at all': that is what I want, and the holiest man wants it too. But alas! How do I still have anything that pleases me?

Do I still have a goal? A harbor toward which MY sail is set?

A good wind? Ah, only someone who knows WHERE he is sailing knows which wind is good, and which wind is fair for him.

What do I have left? A heart that is weary and flippant, an unstable will, fluttering wings, a broken backbone.

This searching for MY home: Zarathustra, do you know that this search has been MY homesickness? It is eating me up.

'WHERE is MY home?' I ask for it and look for it, and I have looked, but I have not found it. O eternal everywhere, O eternal nowhere, O eternal in vain!"

So spoke the shadow, and Zarathustra's face lengthened at his words. "You are my shadow!" he said at last, sadly.

"Your danger is not small, you free spirit and wanderer! You have had a bad day. See to it that an even worse evening does not overtake you!

To restless people like you, even a prisoner finally seems blessed. Have you ever seen how captured criminals sleep? They sleep peacefully. They enjoy their new security.

Beware that a narrow faith does not capture you in the end, some hard, rigid delusion! For now everything narrow and fixed seduces and tempts you.

You have lost your goal. Alas, how will you get over that loss and forget it? In losing it, you have also lost your way!

You poor rover and rambler, you tired butterfly! Do you want rest and a home this evening? Then go up to my cave!

That way leads to my cave. And now I will run away from you again quickly. Already something like a shadow lies on me.

I will run alone, so that it may grow bright around me again. For that I must stay cheerfully on my legs a long while yet. But this evening there will be dancing at my place!"—

So spoke Zarathustra.

## LXX. NOONTIDE.

—And Zarathustra ran and ran, but he found no one else. He was alone, and he kept finding himself again. For hours he enjoyed his solitude, drank it in, and thought of good things.

Around noon, however, when the sun stood directly over his head, he passed an old, bent, and gnarled tree. A vine had wrapped it all around in an ardent embrace and hidden it from itself. Yellow grapes hung from it in abundance, right in the wanderer's path.

He felt like quenching a little thirst and breaking off a cluster of grapes for himself. But when he had already stretched out his arm to do so, he felt an even stronger wish for something else: to lie down beside the tree at the hour of perfect noon and sleep.

Zarathustra did this. No sooner had he laid himself on the ground, in the stillness and secrecy of the many-colored grass, than he forgot his little thirst and fell asleep. For as Zarathustra's proverb says: "One thing is more necessary than the other."

Only his eyes stayed open, for they never tired of gazing at and admiring the tree and the vine's love. As he fell asleep, Zarathustra spoke to his heart:

"Hush! Hush! Hasn't the world just become perfect? What has happened to me?

As a delicate wind dances unseen on a sea as smooth as a polished floor, light, feather-light, so sleep dances on me.

It closes none of my eyes. It leaves my soul awake. It is light, truly, feather-light.

It persuades me, I don't know how. It touches me inwardly with a caressing hand. It compels me. Yes, it compels me, so that my soul stretches itself out:—

—How long and weary it grows, my strange soul! Has the evening of a seventh day come to it right at noon? Has it already wandered too long, blissfully, among good and ripe things?

It stretches itself out, long, longer! It lies still, my strange soul. It has already tasted too many good things. This golden sadness weighs on it and twists its mouth.

—Like a ship that puts into the calmest cove, it now draws up to land, weary of long voyages and uncertain seas. Isn't the land more faithful?

When such a ship lies against the shore, nestled close to it, then a spider's thread spun from ship to land is enough. No stronger ropes are needed there.

Like such a weary ship in the calmest cove, I too now rest close to the earth: faithful, trusting, waiting, bound to it by the lightest threads.

O happiness! O happiness! Will you sing, perhaps, my soul? You lie in the grass. But this is the secret, solemn hour, when no shepherd plays his pipe.

Careful! Hot noon is sleeping on the fields. Don't sing! Hush! The world is perfect.

Don't sing, you prairie bird, my soul! Don't even whisper! Look, hush! Old noon is sleeping. It moves its mouth. Isn't it drinking a drop of happiness just now?

—An old brown drop of golden happiness, of golden wine? Something flits across it, and its happiness laughs. That is how a god laughs. Hush!

—'How little it takes to be happy!' I said that once and thought myself wise. But it was blasphemy: THAT I have now learned. Wise fools speak better.

Precisely the least thing, the gentlest thing, the lightest thing, a lizard's rustling, a breath, a flit, a glance: it is LITTLE that makes up the BEST happiness. Hush!

—What has happened to me? Listen! Has time flown away? Am I not falling? Haven't I fallen, listen! into the well of eternity?

—What is happening to me? Hush! Something stings me, alas, in the heart? In the heart! Oh, break, break, my heart, after such happiness, after such a sting!

—What? Hasn't the world just become perfect? Round and ripe? Oh, the golden round ring, where is it flying? Let me run after it! Quick!

Hush—" (and here Zarathustra stretched himself, and felt that he was asleep.)

"Up!" he said to himself. "You sleeper! You noonday sleeper! Come on, up, you old legs! It is time, and more than time. Many a good stretch of road still awaits you.

Now you have slept your fill. For how long? Half an eternity! Come on, up now, my old heart! After such a sleep, how long can you stay awake?"

(But then he fell asleep again, and his soul argued against him, defended itself, and lay down again.) "Leave me alone! Hush! Hasn't the world just become perfect? Oh, the golden round ball!—

"Get up," said Zarathustra, "you little thief, you lazybones! What! Still stretching, yawning, sighing, falling into deep wells?

Who are you, then, my soul!" (and here he grew frightened, for a sunbeam shot down from the sky onto his face.)

"O sky above me," he said with a sigh, and sat up, "are you gazing at me? Are you listening to my strange soul?

"When will you drink this drop of dew that has fallen on all earthly things? When will you drink this strange soul—

—when, you well of eternity, you joyful, terrifying abyss of noon? When will you drink my soul back into yourself?"

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he rose from his resting place beside the tree as if waking from a strange drunkenness. And look! The sun still stood directly above his head. From this one could fairly conclude that Zarathustra had not slept long.

## LXXI. THE GREETING.

It was not until late in the afternoon that Zarathustra, after much useless searching and wandering, came home to his cave again. But when he stood facing it, no more than twenty paces away, the thing he now least expected happened: he heard the great CRY OF DISTRESS once more.

And remarkably, this time the cry came from his own cave. It was a long, many-sided, peculiar cry. Zarathustra could clearly tell that it was made of many voices, although from a distance it might have sounded like the cry of a single mouth.

Then Zarathustra rushed toward his cave, and look! What a sight awaited him after that concert! There sat together all those he had passed during the day: the king on the right and the king on the left, the old magician, the pope, the voluntary beggar, the shadow, the intellectually conscientious one, the sorrowful soothsayer, and the ass.

The ugliest man, however, had put a crown on his head and wrapped two purple sashes around himself. Like all ugly people, he liked to disguise himself and play the handsome one.

In the middle of that sorrowful company stood Zarathustra's eagle, ruffled and uneasy. It had been asked to answer too many things for which its pride had no answer. The wise serpent, however, hung around its neck.

Zarathustra watched all this with great astonishment. Then he examined each guest with courteous curiosity, read their souls, and wondered again. Meanwhile the assembled company had risen from their seats and waited respectfully for Zarathustra to speak. Zarathustra spoke as follows:

"You despairing ones! You strange ones! So it was YOUR cry of distress I heard? And now I also know where to look for the one I searched for in vain today: THE HIGHER MAN—

—he sits in my own cave, the higher man! But why am I surprised? Didn't I myself lure him here with offerings of honey and the cunning calls of my happiness?

Yet it seems to me that you are poorly suited to one another's company. You who cry for help, don't you make each other's hearts irritable when you sit here together? Someone else must come first,

—someone who will make you laugh again: a good, cheerful clown, a dancer, a wind, a wild romp, some old fool. What do you think?

Forgive me, you despairing ones, for speaking such trivial words in front of you. They are truly unworthy of such guests! But you do not guess WHAT makes my heart so playful—

—it is you yourselves, and the sight of you. Forgive me for it! For anyone who sees a despairing person grows courageous. Everyone thinks himself strong enough to encourage someone in despair.

You have given this power to me. It is a good gift, my honored guests! An excellent guest's present! Well then, do not scold me if I offer you something of mine in return.

This is my empire and my domain. But what is mine shall be yours this evening and tonight. My animals shall serve you. Let my cave be your resting place!

In my house and home no one shall despair. Within my grounds I protect everyone from his wild beasts. That is the first thing I offer you: security!

The second thing is my little finger. And once you have THAT, take the whole hand too, yes, and the heart along with it! Welcome here, welcome, my guests!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra, laughing with love and mischief. After this greeting his guests bowed again and kept a respectful silence. The king on the right, however, answered him on their behalf.

"O Zarathustra, from the way you have given us your hand and your greeting, we recognize you as Zarathustra. You have humbled yourself before us. You have almost wounded our reverence—

—but who else could have humbled himself as you have, with such pride? THAT lifts us up. It refreshes our eyes and hearts.

Just to see this, we would gladly climb mountains higher than this one. We came as eager spectators. We wanted to see what makes dim eyes bright.

And look! Now our cries of distress are over. Our minds and hearts are open and enraptured. It would take little for our spirits to grow playful.

Nothing grows more pleasingly on earth, O Zarathustra, than a lofty, strong will. It is the finest growth. A whole landscape refreshes itself at one such tree.

I compare such a person, O Zarathustra, to the pine that grows up as you do: tall, silent, hardy, solitary, made of the best and most supple wood, stately—

—and in the end reaching out for ITS domain with strong green branches, putting weighty questions to the wind, the storm, and whatever else is at home in high places;

—and answering more weightily still, a commander, a victor! Oh, who would not climb high mountains to see such growths?

At your tree, O Zarathustra, even the gloomy and the badly made refresh themselves. At your gaze even the wavering grow steady and heal their hearts.

And truly, many eyes turn toward your mountain and your tree today. A great longing has arisen, and many have learned to ask: 'Who is Zarathustra?'

And all those into whose ears you ever dripped your song and your honey have said to their hearts at the same time. All the hidden ones, those who live alone and those who live in pairs, have said:

'Is Zarathustra still alive? Life is no longer worth living; everything is indifferent, everything is useless. Or else we must live with Zarathustra!'

'Why doesn't he come, after announcing himself for so long?' many people ask. 'Has solitude swallowed him up? Or should we perhaps go to him?'

Now it is happening that solitude itself grows fragile and breaks open, like a grave that bursts and can no longer hold its dead. Everywhere one sees people risen from the dead.

Now the waves rise and rise around your mountain, O Zarathustra. However high your height may be, many of them must rise up to you. Your boat will not rest much longer on dry ground.

We despairing ones have now come into your cave and already no longer despair. That is only an omen and a sign that better ones are on their way to you—

—for they themselves are on their way to you: the last remnant of God among men, that is, all the men of great longing, of great loathing, of great surfeit,

—all who do not want to live unless they learn to HOPE again, unless they learn from you, O Zarathustra, the GREAT hope!"

Thus spoke the king on the right, and he took Zarathustra's hand to kiss it. But Zarathustra stopped this show of veneration and stepped back in alarm, silently and suddenly, as if fleeing into the far distance.

After a little while, however, he was back at home with his guests. He looked at them with clear, searching eyes and said:

"My guests, you higher men, I will speak plain language and speak plainly with you. It is not for YOU that I have waited here in these mountains."

("'Plain language and plainly?' Good God!" the king on the left said to himself at this point. "You can see he doesn't know the good people of the West, this sage from the East!

But he means 'blunt language and bluntly.' Well! That is not the worst taste these days!")

"You may all truly be higher men," Zarathustra went on, "but for me you are neither high enough nor strong enough.

For me, that is, for the relentless thing that is silent in me now but will not always be silent. And even if you belong to me, it is not as my right arm.

For anyone who, like you, stands on sickly and tender legs wants above all to be TREATED GENTLY, whether he knows it or hides it from himself.

But I do not treat my arms and legs gently. I DO NOT TREAT MY WARRIORS GENTLY. How then could you be fit for MY warfare?

With you I would spoil all my victories. And many of you would topple over if you merely heard the loud beating of my drums.

Moreover, you are not beautiful enough or well-born enough for me. I need pure, smooth mirrors for my teachings. On your surface even my own image is distorted.

Many a burden and many a memory press on your shoulders. Many a malicious dwarf crouches in your corners. There is a hidden rabble in you too.

And though you are high and of a higher type, much in you is crooked and misshapen. There is no smith in the world who could hammer you right and straight for me.

You are only bridges: may higher ones cross over on you! You stand for steps, so do not scold the one who climbs beyond you into HIS height!

From your seed a true son and perfect heir may one day arise for me, but that time is distant. You yourselves are not the ones to whom my inheritance and my name belong.

It is not for you that I wait here in these mountains. It is not with you that I may go down for the last time. You have come to me only as an omen that higher ones are on their way to me—

—NOT the men of great longing, of great loathing, of great surfeit, and what you call the remnant of God.

—No! No! Three times No! I wait here in these mountains for OTHERS, and I will not lift my foot from here without them:

—for higher ones, stronger ones, more triumphant ones, merrier ones, for those who are built square in body and soul. LAUGHING LIONS must come!

O my guests, you strange ones, have you heard nothing yet of my children? Nothing of their being on their way to me?

Speak to me of my gardens, of my Happy Isles, of my new and beautiful race. Why do you not speak to me of them?

This is the guest's present I ask of your love: that you speak to me of my children. For them I am rich, for them I became poor. What have I not given up,

—what would I not give up to have one thing: THESE children, THIS living plantation, THESE trees of life grown from my will and my highest hope!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he broke off suddenly in the middle of his speech. His longing had come over him, and he closed his eyes and his mouth because of the turmoil in his heart.

All his guests fell silent too, and stood still and bewildered, except that the old soothsayer made signs with his hands and gestures.

## LXXII. THE SUPPER.

At this point the soothsayer interrupted the greeting between Zarathustra and his guests. He pushed forward like someone with no time to lose, seized Zarathustra's hand, and cried: "But Zarathustra!

One thing is more necessary than another; you say so yourself. Well, one thing is now more necessary TO ME than all the others.

A word at the right moment: didn't you invite me to a MEAL? And here are many who have made long journeys. Surely you don't mean to feed us on speeches alone?

Besides, all of you have thought too much about freezing, drowning, suffocating, and other bodily dangers. But none of you has thought about MY danger, which is dying of hunger—"

(So spoke the soothsayer. But when Zarathustra's animals heard these words, they ran off in terror. They saw that everything they had brought home during the day would not be enough to fill the soothsayer alone.)

"And dying of thirst, too," the soothsayer went on. "I hear water splashing here like words of wisdom, that is, abundantly and tirelessly. But I want WINE!

Not everyone is a born water-drinker like Zarathustra. Nor does water suit the weary and withered. WE deserve wine. It ALONE gives instant vigor and improvised health!"

On this occasion, as the soothsayer was longing for wine, the king on the left, the silent one, also found his voice for once.

"WE have seen to the wine," he said, "I together with my brother, the king on the right. We have plenty of wine, a whole ass-load of it. So nothing is missing but bread."

"Bread?" replied Zarathustra, laughing as he spoke. "Bread is exactly what hermits do not have. But man does not live by bread alone. He lives also by the meat of good lambs, and I have two of them.

THESE we shall slaughter quickly and cook spicily with sage. That is how I like them. And there is no shortage of roots and fruits, good enough even for the fussy and the dainty, nor of nuts and other riddles to crack.

So we shall have a good meal before long. But whoever wishes to eat with us must also lend a hand with the work, even the kings. For at Zarathustra's, even a king may be a cook."

This proposal pleased all of them, except that the voluntary beggar objected to the meat, the wine, and the spices.

"Just listen to this glutton Zarathustra!" he said jokingly. "Does anyone go into caves and up high mountains to hold such feasts?

Now I really understand what he once taught us: 'Blessed be moderate poverty!' And I understand why he wants to do away with beggars."

"Be of good cheer," replied Zarathustra, "as I am. Keep to your customs, you excellent one. Grind your grain, drink your water, praise your cooking, as long as it makes you glad!

I am a law only for my own; I am not a law for everyone. But whoever belongs to me must be strong of bone and light of foot,

—joyful in fighting and in feasting, no sulker, no idle daydreamer, as ready for the hardest task as for the feast, healthy and sound.

The best belongs to my own and to me. If it is not given to us, we take it: the best food, the purest sky, the strongest thoughts, the most beautiful women!”

So spoke Zarathustra. The king on the right answered: “How strange! Has anyone ever heard such sensible things from the mouth of a wise man?

And truly, the strangest thing in a wise man is when, on top of everything else, he is still sensible and not an ass.”

So spoke the king on the right, wondering. But the ass grudgingly said YE-A to his remark. This was the beginning of the long meal that the history books call “The Supper.” Throughout it, nothing was spoken of except THE HIGHER MAN.

## LXXIII. THE HIGHER MAN.

1.

When I first came to people, I committed the hermit’s folly, the great folly: I appeared in the marketplace.

And when I spoke to everyone, I spoke to no one. In the evening my companions were tightrope walkers and corpses, and I myself was almost a corpse.

But with the new morning a new truth came to me. Then I learned to say: “What do the marketplace and the mob matter to me, and the mob’s noise, and the mob’s long ears!”

You higher men, learn THIS from me: in the marketplace nobody believes in higher men. If you want to speak there, very well! But the mob blinks and says: “We are all equal.”

“You higher men,” so the mob blinks, “there are no higher men. We are all equal; a man is a man; before God we are all equal!”

Before God! But now this God has died. And before the mob we will not be equal. You higher men, leave the marketplace!

2.

Before God! But now this God has died! You higher men, this God was your greatest danger.

Only since he was laid in the grave have you risen again. Only now comes the great noon. Only now does the higher man become master!

Have you understood this saying, my brothers? You are frightened: are your hearts growing dizzy? Does the abyss gape open before you here? Does the hound of hell bark at you here?

Well then! Take heart, you higher men! Only now is the mountain of humanity’s future in labor. God has died: now WE want the Superman to live.

3.

The most cautious people ask today: “How is man to be preserved?” But Zarathustra asks, as the first and only one to do so: “How is man to be SURPASSED?”

The Superman is what I have at heart. THAT is my first and only concern, and NOT man: not the neighbor, not the poorest, not the most wretched, not the best.

My brothers, what I can love in man is that he is a crossing-over and a going-under. And in you, too, there is much that makes me love and hope.

That you have despised, you higher men, gives me hope. For the great despisers are the great reverers.

That you have despaired deserves much honor. For you have not learned to submit, and you have not learned petty shrewdness.

Today the petty people have become master. They all preach submission and humility and shrewdness and diligence and consideration, and the long et cetera of petty virtues.

Whatever is effeminate, whatever comes from the servile kind, and above all the mob’s mishmash: THAT now wants to be master of all human destiny. Oh disgust! Disgust! Disgust!

THAT asks and asks and never tires: “How can man preserve himself best, longest, most pleasantly?” This is what makes them the masters of today.

Surpass these masters of today, my brothers, these petty people. THEY are the Superman’s greatest danger!

You higher men, surpass the petty virtues, the petty shrewdness, the grain-of-sand considerateness, the anthill rubbish, the pitiful comfort, the “happiness of the greatest number”!

Despair rather than submit. Truly, I love you because you do not know how to live today, you higher men! For that is how YOU live best!

4.

Do you have courage, my brothers? Are you stout-hearted? I do NOT mean courage in front of witnesses. I mean the courage of hermits and eagles, which not even a God watches anymore.

Cold souls, mules, the blind, and the drunk I do not call stout-hearted. He has heart who knows fear but CONQUERS it; who sees the abyss, but with PRIDE.

He who sees the abyss, but with an eagle’s eyes; he who GRASPS the abyss with an eagle’s talons: he has courage.

5.

“Man is evil”: all the wisest told me this as a consolation. Ah, if only it is still true today! For evil is man’s best strength.

“Man must become better and more evil”: that is what I teach. The most evil is necessary for the Superman’s best.

It may have been good for that preacher of the petty people to suffer and bear the burden of men’s sin. But I rejoice in great sin as my great CONSOLATION.

Such things, however, are not said for long ears. Not every word suits every mouth. These are fine, faraway things, and sheep’s hooves shall not grasp at them!

6.

You higher men, do you think I am here to set right what you have done wrong?

Or that from now on I want to make softer beds for you sufferers? Or show you restless ones, who wander and climb astray, new and easier paths?

No! No! Three times no! More and more, and ever better ones of your kind shall perish, for you shall have it ever worse and harder. Only in this way,

—only in this way does man grow up to the height where lightning strikes and shatters him: high enough for the lightning!

My soul and my seeking go out toward the few, the long, the remote. What do your many small, short miseries matter to me!

You do not yet suffer enough for me! You suffer from yourselves; you have not yet suffered FROM MAN. You would be lying if you said otherwise! None of you suffers from what I have suffered.

7.

It is not enough for me that the lightning no longer does harm. I do not want to conduct it away. It shall learn to work for ME.

My wisdom has gathered for a long time like a cloud, and it is growing stiller and darker. So does all wisdom that is one day to bring forth LIGHTNING.

To these people of today I will not be LIGHT, nor be called light. THEM I will blind. Lightning of my wisdom, put out their eyes!

8.

Do not will anything beyond your strength. There is a nasty falseness in those who will beyond their strength.

This is especially true when they will great things! For these subtle counterfeiters and play-actors awaken distrust of great things,

—until at last they are false even toward themselves: squint-eyed, whitewashed sores, glossed over with strong words, with showy virtues and glittering false deeds.

Be careful here, you higher men! For nothing is more precious to me, or rarer, than honesty.

Does today not belong to the mob? But the mob does not know what is great and what is small, what is straight and what is honest. It is innocently crooked; it always lies.

9.

Today, you higher men, keep a healthy distrust, you brave ones, you open-hearted ones! And keep your reasons secret! For today belongs to the mob.

What the mob once learned to believe without reasons, who could refute for them with reasons?

In the marketplace, people are convinced by gestures. Reasons only make the mob distrustful.

And when truth has once triumphed there, ask yourselves with healthy distrust: “What strong error fought on its side?”

Beware of the scholars too! They hate you because they are unproductive. They have cold, withered eyes, and before them every bird lies stripped of its feathers.

Such people boast that they do not lie. But being unable to lie is still far from loving truth. Be on your guard!

Being free of fever is still far from being knowledge! I do not believe in chilled spirits. He who cannot lie does not know what truth is.

10.

If you want to climb high, use your own legs! Do not let yourselves be CARRIED up; do not sit on other people’s backs and heads!

But you have mounted a horse? You now ride briskly up to your goal? Well, my friend! But your lame foot is on horseback with you too!

When you reach your goal and climb down from your horse, precisely at your HEIGHT, you higher man, you will stumble!

11.

You creators, you higher men! One is pregnant only with one’s own child.

Do not let yourselves be talked into things or taken advantage of! Who, after all, is YOUR neighbor? Even if you act “for your neighbor,” you still do not create for him!

Unlearn this “for,” I beg you, you creators. Your very virtue wants you to have nothing to do with “for” and “on account of” and “because.” Stop your ears against these false little words.

“For one’s neighbor” is the virtue only of petty people. There they say “like for like” and “one hand washes the other.” They have neither the right nor the power for YOUR self-seeking!

In your self-seeking, you creators, lies the foresight and forethought of the pregnant! The fruit, which no eye has yet seen, is what shelters, saves, and nourishes your whole love.

Where your whole love is, with your child, there is also your whole virtue! Your work, your will is YOUR “neighbor.” Do not let false values impose on you!

12.

You creators, you higher men! Whoever must give birth is sick. But whoever has given birth is unclean.

Ask women: one does not give birth because it gives pleasure. Pain makes hens and poets cackle.

You creators, there is much uncleanliness in you. That is because you have had to be mothers.

A new child: oh, how much new filth has come into the world with it! Go aside! Whoever has given birth should wash his soul!

13.

Do not be virtuous beyond your strength! And demand nothing of yourselves that goes against probability!

Walk in the footsteps where your fathers’ virtue has already walked! How could you climb high if your fathers’ will did not climb with you?

But whoever wants to be the first of his line should take care not to become the last of it as well! And where your fathers’ vices are, you should not set yourselves up as saints!

Suppose a man’s fathers were fond of women, strong wine, and wild boar. What would it be if he demanded chastity of himself?

It would be folly! Truly, it seems to me a great deal for such a man if he is the husband of one, or two, or three women.

And if he founded monasteries and wrote over their gates, “The way to holiness,” I would still say: What good is it! It is a new folly!

He has founded a house of penance and refuge for himself: much good may it do him! But I do not believe in it.

In solitude, whatever a person brings into it grows, including the brute in his nature. That is why solitude is unwise for many.

Has there ever been anything filthier on earth than the saints of the wilderness? AROUND THEM not only the devil ran loose, but the swine too.

14.

Shy, ashamed, awkward, like a tiger whose leap has failed: that is how I have often seen you slink aside, you higher men. A THROW of the dice you had made had failed.

But what does it matter, you dice-players! You had not learned to play and mock as one must play and mock! Do we not always sit at a great table of mockery and play?

And if great things have failed for you, does that mean you yourselves have failed? And if you yourselves have failed, has man therefore failed? But if man has failed: well then! Never mind!

15.

The higher its kind, the more rarely a thing succeeds. You higher men here, have you not all been failures?

Be of good cheer. What does it matter? How much is still possible! Learn to laugh at yourselves as you ought to laugh!

What wonder that you have failed and only half-succeeded, you half-shattered ones! Is it not man’s FUTURE that strives and struggles in you?

Man’s farthest, deepest, star-highest concerns, his immense powers: do they not all foam against one another in your vessel?

What wonder that many a vessel shatters! Learn to laugh at yourselves as you ought to laugh! You higher men, oh, how much is still possible!

And truly, how much has already succeeded! How rich this earth is in small, good, perfect things, in things that have turned out well!

Surround yourselves with small, good, perfect things, you higher men. Their golden ripeness heals the heart. What is perfect teaches us to hope.

16.

What has been the greatest sin on earth so far? Was it not the word of the one who said: “Woe unto you that laugh now!”?

Did he himself find no reason to laugh on earth? Then he searched badly. Even a child finds reason for it.

He did not love enough; otherwise he would also have loved us, the laughing ones! But he hated and jeered at us, and he promised us wailing and gnashing of teeth.

Must one curse at once whenever one does not love? That seems to me bad taste. Yet that is what he did, this absolute one. He came from the mob.

And he himself simply did not love enough; otherwise he would have raged less because people did not love him. All great love does not SEEK love: it seeks more.

Get out of the way of all such absolute ones! They are a poor, sickly kind, a mob kind. They look at this life with ill will; they have an evil eye for this earth.

Get out of the way of all such absolute ones! They have heavy feet and sultry hearts, and they do not know how to dance. How could the earth be light for such as these!

17.

All good things approach their goal by winding paths. Like cats, they arch their backs and purr inwardly at the happiness drawing near. All good things laugh.

A person's step shows whether he is already walking on *his own* path. Just watch me walk! But whoever comes near his goal dances.

And truly, I have not become a statue. I do not yet stand there stiff, dull, and stony, like a pillar. I love to run fast.

And though there are swamps and thick miseries on earth, whoever has light feet runs even across the mud, and dances on it as if on swept ice.

Lift up your hearts, my brothers, high, higher! And do not forget your legs! Lift up your legs as well, you good dancers, and better still, stand on your heads!

18.

This crown of laughter, this crown of rose garlands: I have put this crown on myself. I myself have declared my laughter holy. I found no one else today strong enough to do it.

Zarathustra the dancer, Zarathustra the light one, who beckons with his wings, ready for flight, beckoning to all the birds, ready and prepared, blissfully light in spirit:—

Zarathustra the soothsayer, Zarathustra the sooth-laugher, not impatient, not absolute, one who loves leaps and sideways leaps: I myself have put on this crown!

19.

Lift up your hearts, my brothers, high, higher! And do not forget your legs! Lift up your legs as well, you good dancers, and better still, stand on your heads!

There are also heavy animals in a state of happiness, and some are club-footed from birth. They strain themselves in a curious way, like an elephant trying to stand on its head.

But it is better to be foolish with happiness than foolish with misfortune, better to dance clumsily than to walk lamely. So learn my wisdom, I beg you, you higher men: even the worst thing has two good reverse sides,—

—even the worst thing has good legs for dancing. So learn, I beg you, you higher men, to stand on your proper legs!

So unlearn, I beg you, all the sorrowful sighing and all the sadness of the rabble! Oh, how sad the clowns of the rabble seem to me today! But this today belongs to the rabble.

20.

Be like the wind when it rushes out of its mountain caves. It wants to dance to its own piping, and the seas tremble and leap under its footsteps.

That which gives wings to asses and milks lionesses: praise be to that good, unruly spirit that comes like a hurricane to everything of today and to all the rabble,—

—which is hostile to thistle-heads and puzzle-heads, and to all withered leaves and weeds. Praise be to this wild, good, free spirit of the storm, which dances on swamps and miseries as if on meadows!

It hates the consumptive rabble-dogs and the whole misbegotten, sullen brood. Praise be to this spirit of all free spirits, the laughing storm, which blows dust into the eyes of all the dark-sighted and the melancholy!

You higher men, the worst thing about you is that none of you has learned to dance as you ought to dance: to dance beyond yourselves! What does it matter that you have failed!

How many things are still possible! So *learn* to laugh beyond yourselves! Lift up your hearts, you good dancers, high! higher! And do not forget good laughter!

This crown of laughter, this crown of rose garlands: to you, my brothers, I throw this crown! I have declared laughter holy. You higher men, *learn*, I beg you, to laugh!

## LXXIV. THE SONG OF MELANCHOLY.

1.

As Zarathustra spoke these sayings, he was standing near the entrance of his cave. With the last words, however, he slipped away from his guests and fled for a little while into the open air.

"O pure scents around me," he cried, "O blessed stillness around me! But where are my animals? Come here, come here, my eagle and my serpent!

"Tell me, my animals: these higher men, all of them, do they perhaps not *smell* good? O pure scents around me! Only now do I know and feel how much I love you, my animals."

And Zarathustra said once more: "I love you, my animals!" As he spoke these words, the eagle and the serpent pressed close to him and looked up at him. In this pose all three were silent together, sniffing and sipping the good air with one another. For the air out here was better than among the higher men.

2.

But hardly had Zarathustra left the cave when the old magician got up, looked slyly around, and said: "He is gone!

"And already, you higher men (let me tickle you with this complimentary, flattering name, as he himself does), already my evil spirit of deceit and magic is attacking me, my melancholy devil,

"—which is an enemy of this Zarathustra from the bottom of its heart. Forgive it for that! Now it wants to work its magic before you. This is just *its* hour, and I struggle in vain against this evil spirit.

"To all of you, whatever honorable names you like to give yourselves, whether you call yourselves 'the free spirits' or 'the conscientious,' or 'the penitents of the spirit,' or 'the unfettered,' or 'the great longers,'

"—to all of you who, like me, suffer *from the great loathing*, for whom the old God has died while no new God yet lies in cradles and swaddling clothes: to all of you, my evil spirit and magic-devil is well disposed.

"I know you, you higher men. I know him, too. I also know this fiend whom I love in spite of myself, this Zarathustra. He himself often seems to me like the beautiful mask of a saint,

"—like a strange new masquerade that my evil spirit, the melancholy devil, delights in. It often seems to me that I love Zarathustra for the sake of my evil spirit.

"But already *it* is attacking and compelling me, this spirit of melancholy, this devil of the evening twilight. And truly, you higher men, it has a longing—

"—open your eyes!—it longs to come *naked*. Whether as man or as woman, I do not yet know. But it is coming, it compels me, alas! Open your minds!

"The day is dying. Evening now comes to all things, even to the best things. Now hear and see, you higher men, what devil, man or woman, this spirit of evening melancholy is!"

Thus spoke the old magician. He looked slyly around, and then he seized his harp.

3.

In evening's clear air, When the dew's comfort Pours down onto the earth, Unseen and unheard— For the comforting dew wears Soft shoes, like everything gently kind—: Do you remember then, do you remember, burning heart, How once you thirsted For heaven's kindly tears and the dew's soft dripping, Scorched and weary, how you thirsted, While on yellow grassy paths Wicked glances of the western sun Played around you through dark trees, Blinding glances of the glowing sun, delighting in the hurt they caused?

"The suitor of *truth*? You?"—so they mocked— "No! Only a poet! A cunning beast, preying and creeping, That must lie, That must lie knowingly and willingly: Lusting for prey, Masked in motley, Hidden from himself, shrouded, Himself his own prey— *He*, the suitor of truth? No! Only a fool! Only a poet! Speaking only in motley, Shouting confusedly from behind a fool's mask, Clambering around on made-up word-bridges, On many-colored rainbows, Between false heavens And false earths, Roving around us, hovering around us,— *Only a fool! Only a poet!*

*He*, the suitor of truth? Not still, stiff, smooth and cold, Turned into an image, A godlike statue, Set up in front of temples, As a god's own doorkeeper: No! hostile to all such statues of truthfulness, More at home in any desert than at temples, Full of a cat's wantonness, Leaping through every window Quickly into every chance, Sniffing at every wild forest, Sniffing greedily, longingly,

So that you, in wild forests, Among the brightly spotted beasts of prey, Might roam, sinfully healthy and beautifully colored, Smacking your lips with longing, Blissfully mocking, blissfully hellish, blissfully bloodthirsty, Robbing, creeping, lying, roaming:—

Or like eagles that stare fixedly, Long, down into the abyss, Down into *their* abyss:— Oh, how they circle downward now, Down below, down within, Whirling into ever deeper depths!— Then, Suddenly, With straight aim, With quivering flight, Swooping on *lambs*, Headlong down, ravenous, Craving lambs, Fierce against all lamb-souls, Furiously fierce against all that look Sheepish, lamb-eyed, or curly-woolly, —Gray, with the kindliness of lambs and sheep!

Just so, Like eagles, like panthers, Are the poet's desires, Are *your own* desires beneath a thousand masks, You fool! You poet! You who saw humankind As God as much as sheep—: To *tear apart* the God in humankind, As much as the sheep in humankind, And *laughing* while tearing— *That, that* is your own bliss! The bliss of a panther and an eagle! The bliss of a poet and a fool!—

In evening's clear air, When the moon's sickle, Green, between the purple glows, And jealous, creeps out: —Enemy of the day, With every step in secret The rosy hammocks of garlands Cutting down with its sickle, until they sink, Sink toward night, faded, sunk down:—

So I myself once sank From my madness for truth, From my burning longings for the day, Weary of the day, sick of the light, —Sank downward, toward evening, toward shadow: By one single truth Scorched and thirsty: —Do you still remember, do you remember, burning heart, How you thirsted then?— *That I should be banished from all truth!* *Only a fool! Only a poet!*

## LXXV. SCIENCE.

Thus sang the magician, and everyone present walked like unwary birds into the net of his cunning, melancholy sensuality. Only the spiritually conscientious one was not caught. He immediately snatched the harp from the magician and called out: "Air! Let in good air! Let in Zarathustra! You are making this cave stuffy and poisonous, you wicked old magician!

"You false one, you subtle one, you seduce people toward unknown desires and wildernesses. And alas, that someone like you should talk and make a fuss about the *truth*!

"Alas for all free spirits who are not on guard against *such* magicians! Their freedom is finished. You teach and tempt them back into prisons,—

"—you old melancholy devil, a lure sounds out of your lament. You resemble those who praise chastity and thereby secretly invite people to lust!"

Thus spoke the conscientious one. The old magician, however, looked around, enjoying his triumph, and for that reason he put up with the annoyance the conscientious one caused him. "Be quiet!" he said in a modest voice. "Good songs want to echo well. After good songs, one should stay silent for a long time.

"That is what all those present are doing, the higher men. But you have perhaps understood little of my song? There is little of the magic spirit in you."

"You praise me," replied the conscientious one, "by separating me from yourself. Very well! But you others, what do I see? You are all still sitting there with lustful eyes—:

"You free spirits, where has your freedom gone! You almost seem to me like people who have spent a long time watching wicked girls dance naked: your very souls are dancing!

"In you, you higher men, there must be more of what the magician calls his evil spirit of magic and deceit. We must indeed be different.

"And truly, we talked and thought together long enough before Zarathustra came home to his cave for me to know that we *are* different.

"We *seek* different things even up here, you and I. For I seek more *security*, and that is why I came to Zarathustra. For he is still the firmest tower and will—

"—today, when everything is tottering and the whole earth is quaking. But when I see the eyes you are making, it almost seems to me that you seek *more insecurity*,

"—more horror, more danger, more earthquake. You long (so it almost seems to me; forgive my presumption, you higher men)—

"—you long for the worst and most dangerous life, the one that frightens *me* most: the life of wild beasts, of forests, caves, steep mountains, and labyrinthine gorges.

"And it is not those who lead you *out of* danger who please you best, but those who lead you away from all paths: the misleaders. But even if such a longing in you is *real*, it still seems to me *impossible*.

"For fear is humanity's original and fundamental feeling. Everything can be explained through fear, original sin and original virtue alike. Through fear *my* virtue grew too, namely science.

"For the fear of wild animals has been cultivated in human beings the longest, including the animal that a person hides and fears within himself. Zarathustra calls it 'the beast within.'

"This long, ancient fear, finally grown subtle, spiritual, and intellectual: today, I think, it is called *science*."

Thus spoke the conscientious one. But Zarathustra had just come back into his cave and had heard and guessed the meaning of this last speech. He threw a handful of roses at the conscientious one and laughed at his "truths." "What!" he exclaimed. "What did I just hear? Truly, it seems to me that you are a fool, or else I am one myself. And I will quietly and quickly turn your 'truth' upside down.

"For *fear* is the exception with us. But courage and adventure and delight in the uncertain, in the untried: *courage* seems to me the whole prehistory of humankind.

"Man envied the wildest and most courageous animals and robbed them of all their virtues. Only in this way did he become human.

"*This* courage, finally grown subtle, spiritual, and intellectual, this human courage with eagle's wings and serpent's wisdom: *this*, it seems to me, is today called—"

"*Zarathustra!*" cried everyone gathered there, as if with one voice, and at the same moment they burst into loud laughter. But something like a heavy cloud rose from them. Even the magician laughed, and said wisely: "Well! It is gone, my evil spirit!

"And did I not warn you about it myself when I said it was a deceiver, a lying and deceiving spirit?

"Especially when it shows itself naked. But what can I do about its tricks! Did I create it and the world?

"Well! Let us be good again, and cheerful! And although Zarathustra is looking at me with an evil eye (just look at him! he dislikes me)—:

"—before night comes he will learn again to love and praise me. He cannot live long without committing such follies.

"*He* loves his enemies. He knows this art better than anyone I have ever seen. But he takes his revenge for it on his friends!"

Thus spoke the old magician, and the higher men applauded him. Zarathustra then went around and shook hands with his friends, mischievously and lovingly, like someone who has to make amends and apologize to everyone for something. But when he had come in this way to the door of his cave, he again longed for the good air outside and for his animals, and he wanted to slip out.

## LXXVI. AMONG DAUGHTERS OF THE DESERT.

1.

"Don't go away!" said the wanderer who called himself Zarathustra's shadow. "Stay with us, or else the old gloomy affliction might fall on us again.

That old magician has just given us the worst he has, for our own good. And look! The good, pious pope there has tears in his eyes and has set sail once more on the sea of melancholy.

Those kings may still put on a good face in front of us, for that is what THEY have learned best of us all at present! But if no one were watching them, I bet the bad game would start up again with them too,

—the bad game of drifting clouds, of damp melancholy, of curtained skies, of stolen suns, of howling autumn winds,

—the bad game of our howling and crying for help! Stay with us, O Zarathustra! Here there is much hidden misery that wants to speak, much evening, much cloud, much damp air!

You have fed us strong food for men and powerful sayings. Do not let the weak, womanish spirits attack us again over dessert!

You alone make the air around you strong and clear! Have I ever found air anywhere on earth as good as here with you in your cave?

I have seen many lands, and my nose has learned to test and judge many kinds of air. But with you my nostrils taste their greatest delight!

Unless it be... unless it be... forgive an old memory! Forgive me an old after-dinner song that I once composed among daughters of the desert.

For among them the air was just as good, clear, Oriental air. There I was furthest from cloudy, damp, melancholy Old Europe!

Back then I loved such Oriental maidens and other blue kingdoms of heaven, over which hang no clouds and no thoughts.

You would not believe how charmingly they sat there when they were not dancing: deep, but without thoughts, like little secrets, like riddles tied up in ribbons, like nuts served at dessert.

Colorful and foreign, certainly! But without clouds: riddles that can be guessed. To please such maidens I composed an after-dinner psalm back then."

Thus spoke the wanderer who called himself Zarathustra's shadow. Before anyone could answer him, he had seized the old magician's harp, crossed his legs, and looked around him calmly and wisely.

With his nostrils, however, he breathed in the air slowly and questioningly, like someone tasting new, foreign air in new countries. Then he began to sing with a kind of roar.

2. THE DESERTS GROW: WOE TO HIM WHO HIDES THEM!

—Ha! Solemnly! Truly solemnly! A worthy beginning! Solemn in the African manner! Worthy of a lion, Or perhaps of a virtuous howler monkey— —But that means nothing to you, You friendly, dearly loved young women, At whose feet I, A European under palm trees, Am granted a seat For the first time. Selah.

Wonderful, truly! Here I sit now, Near the desert, and yet I am Still so far from the desert, Not yet deserted in any way: That is, I am swallowed down By this smallest of oases—: —It just opened up, yawning, Its loveliest mouth agape, The sweetest-smelling of all little mouths: Then I fell right in, Right down, right through—in among you, You friendly, dearly loved young women! Selah.

Hail, hail to that whale, that fish, If it made things so pleasant For its guest!—(You surely know My learned allusion?) Hail to its belly, If it ever had So lovely an oasis-belly As this one: though I doubt it, —For I come from Old Europe, Which doubts more eagerly than any Elderly married woman. May the Lord improve it! Amen!

Here I sit now, In this smallest of oases, Like a date indeed, Brown, sweet through and through, oozing gold, Longing for a girl's round mouth, But even more for girlish, Ice-cold, snow-white, biting Front teeth: for these, surely, The hearts of all hot dates are pining. Selah.

Like the southern fruits just named, Similar, all too similar, I lie here, sniffed around And played around By little flying insects, And also by still smaller, More foolish, more sinful Wishes and fancies— Surrounded by you, You silent, foreboding Maiden-kittens, Dudu and Suleika, —ROUNDSPHINXED, if I may cram much feeling into one word: (Forgive me, O God, All such sins of speech!)

—I sit here sniffing the best of air, Truly the air of paradise, Bright, light air, flecked with gold, As good an air as ever Fell down from the moon— Did it come by chance, Or did it happen out of arrogance? As the ancient poets tell it. But I, a doubter, now call it Into question: for I come, after all, From Europe, Which doubts more eagerly than any Elderly married woman. May the Lord improve it! Amen.

Drinking this finest air, With nostrils swelled out like goblets, Without future, without memories, So I sit here, you Friendly, dearly loved young women, And watch the palm tree there, How, like a dancing girl, It bows and bends and sways its hips, —One does it too, if one watches it long enough!— Like a dancing girl who, it seems to me, Has stood too long, dangerously long, Always, always on just a SINGLE leg?

—Has she then forgotten, it seems to me, The OTHER leg? For I at least searched in vain For the missing Twin jewel —Namely, the other leg— In the sacred neighborhood Of her dearest, most delicate, Flapping, fluttering, flickering skirt. Yes, if you would, you lovely, friendly ones, Fully believe me: She has, alas, LOST it!

Hoo! Hoo! Hoo! Hoo! Hoo! It is gone! Gone forever! The other leg! Oh, what a pity for that loveliest other leg! Where might it be lingering now, forsaken and weeping? That loneliest leg? Afraid, perhaps, of some Furious, yellow, blond and curly Lion-monster? Or perhaps even Gnawed away, badly nibbled— How wretched, woeful, woeful! badly nibbled! Selah.

Oh, do not weep, Gentle spirits! Do not weep, you Date-fruit spirits! Milk-bosoms! You licorice-hearted Little purses! Weep no more, Pale Dudu! Be a man, Suleika! Courage! Courage! —Or perhaps something strengthening, Something heart-strengthening, Would be most fitting here? Some inspiring text? Some solemn exhortation?—

Ha! Rise up, honor! Moral honor! European honor! Blow again, keep going, Bellows of virtue! Ha! Roar once more, Your moral roar! Roar like a virtuous lion Before the daughters of the desert! —For virtue's howling, You dearest maidens, Is more than all European fervor, European ravenous hunger! And here I stand now, As a European, I can do no other, So help me God! Amen!

THE DESERTS GROW: WOE TO HIM WHO HIDES THEM!

## LXXVII. THE AWAKENING.

1.

After the song of the wanderer and shadow, the cave suddenly filled with noise and laughter. All the assembled guests were talking at once, and even the ass, encouraged by this, no longer kept quiet.

Zarathustra felt a little aversion and scorn for his visitors, though he was glad of their happiness, for it seemed to him a sign of recovery. So he slipped out into the open air and spoke to his animals.

"Where has their distress gone now?" he said, and he already felt relieved of his petty disgust. "With me, it seems, they have unlearned their cries of distress!

—though not yet, alas, their crying." And Zarathustra covered his ears, for just then the YE-A of the ass mixed strangely with the noisy rejoicing of those higher men.

"They are merry," he began again, "and who knows? Perhaps at their host's expense. And if they have learned to laugh from me, it is still not MY laughter they have learned.

But what does that matter! They are old people. They recover in their own way and laugh in their own way. My ears have already put up with worse without becoming sour.

This day is a victory. He is already giving way, he is fleeing: THE SPIRIT OF GRAVITY, my old archenemy! How well this day is about to end, which began so badly and gloomily!

And it is ABOUT TO end. Evening is already coming. It rides here over the sea, the good rider! How it bobs along, the blessed one, the one returning home, in its purple saddles!

The sky gazes brightly at it, and the world lies deep. Oh, all you strange ones who have come to me, it is already worthwhile to have lived with me!"
