Thus spoke Zarathustra. And again the cries and laughter of the higher men came out of the cave. Then he began again:

"They are biting, my bait is working. Their enemy, the spirit of gravity, is leaving them too. Now they are learning to laugh at themselves: am I hearing right?

My manly food is taking effect, my strong and savory sayings. And truly, I did not feed them vegetables that cause wind! I fed them warrior's food, conqueror's food. I awakened new desires.

New hopes are in their arms and legs, and their hearts are expanding. They are finding new words, and soon their spirits will breathe mischief.

Such food may certainly not be suitable for children, nor even for longing girls, old and young. Their insides must be persuaded in other ways. I am not their physician and teacher.

The DISGUST is leaving these higher men. Good! That is my victory. In my domain they grow confident, all foolish shame flees, and they unburden themselves.

They unburden their hearts. Good times return to them; they celebrate and chew things over. They become THANKFUL.

THAT I take as the best sign: they are becoming thankful. It will not be long before they devise festivals and set up memorials to their old joys.

They are CONVALESCENTS!" Thus spoke Zarathustra joyfully to his heart, and he gazed outward. His animals, however, pressed close to him and honored his happiness and his silence.

2.

Suddenly, however, Zarathustra's ear was startled. The cave, which until then had been full of noise and laughter, all at once became as still as death. And his nose smelled a sweet-scented smoke and incense, as if from burning pine cones.

"What is happening? What are they doing?" he asked himself, and he crept up to the entrance so that he could watch his guests without being seen. But wonder upon wonder! What was he then forced to see with his own eyes!

"They have all become PIOUS again, they are PRAYING, they are mad!" he said, and he was astonished beyond measure.

And indeed, all these higher men were kneeling like children and credulous old women, worshipping the ass: the two kings, the retired pope, the evil magician, the voluntary beggar, the wanderer and shadow, the old soothsayer, the spiritually conscientious one, and the ugliest man.

Just then the ugliest man began to gurgle and snort, as if something unspeakable in him were trying to find expression. When he had actually found words, behold! It was a pious, strange litany in praise of the adored and incensed ass. And the litany went like this:

Amen! And glory and honor and wisdom and thanks and praise and strength be to our God, forever and ever!

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

He carries our burdens, he has taken on the form of a servant, he is patient of heart and never says No; and whoever loves his God chastises him.

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

He does not speak, except that he always says Yes to the world he created: that is how he praises his world. It is his cunning that keeps him from speaking, and so he is rarely found to be wrong.

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

He goes through the world unlovely. Gray is the favorite color in which he wraps his virtue. If he has any spirit, he hides it; but everyone believes in his long ears.

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

What hidden wisdom it is to wear long ears and only ever say Yes and never No! Has he not created the world in his own image, namely, as stupid as possible?

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

You walk straight paths and crooked ones; it matters little to you what seems straight or crooked to us humans. Your domain is beyond good and evil. It is your innocence not to know what innocence is.

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

Look how you turn no one away, neither beggars nor kings. You let the little children come to you, and when the bad boys try to lure you, you simply say YE-A.

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

You love she-asses and fresh figs; you are no despiser of food. A thistle tickles your heart when you happen to be hungry. There is the wisdom of a god in that.

—But here the ass brayed YE-A.

## LXXVIII. THE ASS-FESTIVAL.

1.

At this point in the litany, however, Zarathustra could no longer control himself. He himself cried out YE-A, even louder than the ass, and leaped into the middle of his crazed guests.

"Whatever are you doing, you grown-up children?" he exclaimed, pulling the praying ones up from the ground. "Alas, if anyone other than Zarathustra had seen you:

Everyone would think you the worst blasphemers, or the most foolish of old women, with your new faith!

And you yourself, old pope, how does it square with who you are to worship an ass like this, as if it were God?"

"O Zarathustra," answered the pope, "forgive me, but in divine matters I am even more enlightened than you. And it is right that it should be so.

Better to worship God like this, in this form, than in no form at all! Think about this saying, my exalted friend: you will readily guess that there is wisdom in such a saying.

Whoever said 'God is a spirit' took the greatest stride and slide toward unbelief that has ever been made on earth. A saying like that is not easily undone on earth again!

My old heart leaps and bounds because there is still something on earth to worship. Forgive this, Zarathustra, in an old and pious pontiff's heart!"

"And you," said Zarathustra to the wanderer and shadow, "you call yourself a free spirit, and think you are one? And here you practice this kind of idol-worship and priestly devotion?

Truly, you are behaving worse here than you did with your wicked brown girls, you wicked new believer!"

"It is bad enough," answered the wanderer and shadow. "You are right. But how can I help it? The old God lives again, Zarathustra, whatever you may say.

The ugliest man is to blame for all of it. He has woken God up again. And if he says that he once killed him, well, with gods, *death* is always just a prejudice."

"And you," said Zarathustra, "you wicked old magician, what have you done? In this free age, who should believe in you any longer, when *you* believe in such divine donkeyism?

That was a stupid thing you did. How could a shrewd man like you do something so stupid?"

"Zarathustra," answered the shrewd magician, "you are right. It was a stupid thing. It also disgusted me."

"And even you," said Zarathustra to the spiritually conscientious one, "think it over, and put your finger to your nose! Does nothing here go against your conscience? Is your mind not too clean for this praying and the fumes of these worshippers?"

"There is something in it," said the spiritually conscientious one, and put his finger to his nose. "There is something in this spectacle that even does my conscience good.

Perhaps I dare not believe in God. But one thing is certain: God seems to me most worthy of belief in this form.

According to the most pious witnesses, God is eternal. Whoever has so much time takes his time. As slow and as stupid as possible: and *in that way* such a being can still go very far.

And whoever has too much intellect might well fall in love with stupidity and folly. Think of yourself, Zarathustra!

You yourself, truly! Even you could become an ass through an excess of wisdom.

Does the true sage not gladly walk the most crooked paths? The evidence teaches it, Zarathustra: *your own* evidence!"

"And you, finally," said Zarathustra, turning toward the ugliest man, who still lay on the ground, stretching his arm up to the ass (for he was giving it wine to drink). "Tell me, you unspeakable one, what have you been doing?

You seem transformed to me. Your eyes glow, and the cloak of the sublime covers your ugliness. *What* did you do?

Is it true, then, what they say, that you have woken him up again? And why? Was he not killed and done away with for good reasons?

You yourself seem awakened to me. What did you do? Why did *you* turn around? Why did *you* get converted? Speak, you unspeakable one!"

"Zarathustra," answered the ugliest man, "you are a rogue!

Whether *he* still lives, or lives again, or is thoroughly dead, which of us two knows that best? I am asking you.

But one thing I do know, and I learned it from you once, Zarathustra: whoever wants to kill most thoroughly *laughs*.

'Not by anger but by laughter does one kill.' So you once said, Zarathustra, you hidden one, you destroyer without anger, you dangerous saint. You are a rogue!"

2.

Then Zarathustra, astonished at such purely roguish answers, sprang back to the door of his cave. Turning toward all his guests, he cried out in a strong voice:

"You jokers, all of you, you clowns! Why do you pretend and disguise yourselves in front of me?

How all your hearts quivered with delight and mischief because at last you had become like little children again, that is, pious!

And because at last you did again what children do: you prayed, folded your hands, and said 'dear God'!

But now, I beg you, leave *this* nursery, my own cave, where all today's childishness has been going on. Out here, cool down your hot childish high spirits and the commotion in your hearts!

Certainly: unless you become as little children, you shall not enter into *that* kingdom of heaven." (And Zarathustra pointed upward with his hands.)

"But we have no wish at all to enter the kingdom of heaven. We have become men, *so we want the kingdom of earth*."

3.

And once more Zarathustra began to speak. "My new friends," he said, "you strange ones, you higher men, how well you please me now,

now that you have become joyful again! Truly, you have all blossomed. It seems to me that flowers such as you need *new festivals*.

You need a little brave nonsense, some divine service and ass-festival, some old, joyful Zarathustra-fool, some blustering wind to blow your souls clear.

Do not forget this night and this ass-festival, you higher men! *That* is something you invented while you were with me, and I take it as a good sign. Only those who are recovering invent such things.

And if you celebrate it again, this ass-festival, do it out of love for yourselves, and do it also out of love for me! And in remembrance of me!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

## LXXIX. THE DRUNKEN SONG.

1.

Meanwhile, one after another, they had gone out into the open air and the cool, thoughtful night. Zarathustra himself led the ugliest man by the hand, to show him his night-world, the great round moon, and the silvery waterfalls near his cave.

At last they all stood still beside one another. They were all old people, but their hearts were comforted and brave, and they were amazed at themselves that things were so good for them on earth. Meanwhile the mystery of the night came nearer and nearer to their hearts.

And again Zarathustra thought to himself: "Oh, how well these higher men please me now!" But he did not say it aloud, for he respected their happiness and their silence.

Then came the most astonishing thing in that long and astonishing day. The ugliest man began, once more and for the last time, to gurgle and snort. When at last he found words, a question sprang out of his mouth, round and plain: a good, deep, clear question that moved the hearts of all who heard it.

"My friends, all of you," said the ugliest man, "what do you think? For the sake of this day, I am content for the first time to have lived my whole life.

And even that I bear witness to this much is not enough for me. Living on earth is worthwhile. One day, one festival with Zarathustra, has taught me to love the earth.

'Was *that* life?' I will say to death. 'Well, then! Once more!'

My friends, what do you think? Will you not say to death, as I do: 'Was *that* life? For Zarathustra's sake, well, then! Once more!'"

Thus spoke the ugliest man. It was not far from midnight. And what do you think happened then?

As soon as the higher men heard his question, they suddenly became aware of their own transformation and recovery, and of the one who had caused it. They rushed up to Zarathustra, thanking him, honoring him, caressing him, and kissing his hands, each in his own particular way, so that some laughed and some wept.

The old soothsayer danced with delight. Some storytellers suppose that he was full of sweet wine at the time. But he was certainly still fuller of sweet life, and he had given up all weariness.

There are even those who tell that the ass danced then, for the ugliest man had not given it wine to drink for nothing. That may be so, or it may be otherwise.

And if the ass truly did not dance that evening, greater and rarer wonders happened then than the dancing of an ass would have been. In short, as Zarathustra's proverb says: "What does it matter!"

2.

While this was happening with the ugliest man, Zarathustra stood there like someone drunk. His gaze grew dim, his tongue faltered, and his feet staggered. And who could guess what thoughts passed through Zarathustra's soul then?

Apparently his spirit drew back and fled ahead, and was in far distances, "wandering on high mountain ridges," as it is written, "between two seas,

wandering between the past and the future like a heavy cloud."

Gradually, however, while the higher men held him in their arms, he came back to himself a little. He pushed away with his hands the crowd of those honoring him and caring for him, but he did not speak.

Suddenly he turned his head quickly, for he seemed to hear something. Then he laid his finger on his lips and said: "*Come!*"

At once everything around grew still and mysterious, and from the depths there slowly rose the sound of a clock-bell. Zarathustra listened to it, as did the higher men. Then he laid his finger on his lips a second time and said again, "*Come! Come! It is getting near midnight!*" And his voice had changed. But still he had not moved from the spot.

Then it became stiller still and more mysterious, and everything listened: even the ass, and Zarathustra's noble animals, the eagle and the serpent, and likewise Zarathustra's cave, and the great cool moon, and the night itself. Then Zarathustra laid his hand on his mouth for the third time and said:

COME! COME! COME! LET US WANDER NOW! IT IS THE HOUR: LET US WANDER INTO THE NIGHT!

3.

You higher men, it is getting near midnight. So I will say something into your ears, as that old clock-bell says it into mine,

as secretly, as terribly, and as warmly as that midnight clock-bell speaks it to me, the bell that has lived through more than any single human being,

the bell that has already counted the aching heartbeats of your fathers. Ah! Ah! How it sighs! How it laughs in its dream, the old, deep, deep midnight!

Hush! Hush! Many things are heard now that may not be heard by day. But now, in the cool air, when even all the commotion of your hearts has grown still,

now it speaks, now it is heard, now it creeps into over-wakeful souls of the night. Ah! Ah! How the midnight sighs! How it laughs in its dream!

Do you not hear how secretly, terribly, and warmly it speaks to *you*, the old, deep, deep midnight?

O MAN, TAKE HEED!

4.

Woe is me! Where has time gone? Have I not sunk into deep wells? The world is asleep.

Ah! Ah! The dog howls, the moon shines. I would rather die, rather die, than tell you what my midnight heart is thinking now.

I have already died. It is all over. Spider, why are you spinning around me? Do you want blood? Ah! Ah! The dew is falling, the hour is coming,

the hour in which I shiver and freeze, the hour that asks and asks and asks: "Who has courage enough for it?

Who is to be master of the world? Who will say: *This* is how you shall flow, you great and small streams!"

The hour is approaching. O man, you higher man, take heed! This speech is for fine ears, for your ears. WHAT DOES DEEP MIDNIGHT'S VOICE SAY?

5.

It carries me away, my soul is dancing. Day's work! Day's work! Who is to be master of the world?

The moon is cool, the wind is still. Ah! Ah! Have you flown high enough yet? You have danced, but a leg is still not a wing.

You good dancers, now all delight is over. The wine has turned to dregs, every cup has become brittle, the graves are muttering.

You have not flown high enough. Now the graves mutter: "Free the dead! Why is the night so long? Does the moon not make us drunk?"

You higher men, free the graves, wake the corpses! Ah, why does the worm still burrow? The hour is approaching, the hour is approaching.

The clock-bell booms, the heart still throbs, the woodworm still burrows, the heart-worm. Ah! Ah! THE WORLD IS DEEP!

6.

Sweet lyre! Sweet lyre! I love your sound, your drunken, croaking, frog-like sound! From how long ago, from how far away your sound has come to me, from the distance, from the ponds of love!

You old clock-bell, you sweet lyre! Every pain has torn your heart, father-pain, fathers' pain, forefathers' pain. Your speech has grown ripe,

ripe like golden autumn and the afternoon, like my hermit's heart. Now you say: the world itself has grown ripe, the grape is turning brown,

and now it wants to die, to die of happiness. You higher men, do you not feel it? A scent is rising mysteriously,

a perfume and scent of eternity, a rosy-blissful, brown, golden-wine scent of old happiness,

of drunken midnight-death happiness, which sings: the world is deep, AND DEEPER THAN THE DAY COULD READ!

7.

Leave me alone! Leave me alone! I am too pure for you. Do not touch me! Has my world not just now become perfect?

My skin is too pure for your hands. Leave me alone, you dull, doltish, stupid day! Is the midnight not brighter?

The purest are to be masters of the world: the least known, the strongest, the midnight souls, who are brighter and deeper than any day.

O day, are you groping for me? Are you feeling for my happiness? Do you think me rich, solitary, a treasure pit, a chamber of gold?

O world, do you want *me*? Am I worldly to you? Am I spiritual to you? Am I divine to you? But day and world, you are too coarse.

Have cleverer hands, reach for deeper happiness, for deeper unhappiness, reach for some God. Do not reach for me.

My unhappiness and my happiness are deep, you strange day, but still I am no God, no God's hell: DEEP IS ITS WOE.

8.

God's woe is deeper, you strange world! Reach for God's woe, not for me! What am I? A drunken sweet lyre,

a midnight lyre, a bell-frog that no one understands, but that *must* speak before the deaf, you higher men! For you do not understand me!

Gone! Gone! O youth! O noon! O afternoon! Now evening and night and midnight have come. The dog howls, the wind:

—Isn't the wind a dog? It whines, it barks, it howls. Ah! Ah! How she sighs! How she laughs, how she wheezes and pants, the midnight!

How soberly she speaks just now, this drunken poetess! Has she perhaps drunk past her drunkenness? Has she become overawake? Is she chewing things over?

—It is her woe that she chews over in a dream, the old, deep midnight, and still more her joy. For joy, although woe is deep, JOY IS DEEPER STILL THAN GRIEF CAN BE.

9.

You grapevine! Why do you praise me? Didn't I cut you? I am cruel, and you bleed—: what does your praise of my drunken cruelty mean?

"Whatever has become perfect, everything ripe, wants to die!" So you say. Blessed, blessed be the vintner's knife! But everything unripe wants to live: alas!

Woe says: "Away! Go! Away with you, woe!" But everything that suffers wants to live, so that it may become ripe and lively and longing,

—Longing for what is further, higher, brighter. "I want heirs," says everything that suffers. "I want children. I do not want *myself*."

Joy, however, does not want heirs, and it does not want children. Joy wants itself. It wants eternity, it wants recurrence, it wants everything to stay eternally the same as itself.

Woe says: "Break, bleed, heart! Wander, leg! Wing, fly! Onward! Upward! you pain!" Well then! Take heart, O my old heart: WOE SAYS: "AWAY! GO!"

10.

You higher men, what do you think? Am I a soothsayer? Or a dreamer? Or a drunkard? Or an interpreter of dreams? Or a midnight bell?

Or a drop of dew? Or a mist and fragrance of eternity? Don't you hear it? Don't you smell it? Just now my world has become perfect. Midnight is also noon,

Pain is also a joy, a curse is also a blessing, night is also a sun. Go away! Or you will learn that a sage is also a fool.

Did you ever say Yes to a single joy? O my friends, then you also said Yes to ALL woe. All things are chained together, entwined, in love with one another.

—If you ever wanted one moment to come a second time, if you ever said, "You please me, happiness! Instant! Moment!", then you wanted EVERYTHING to come back!

—Everything anew, everything eternal, everything chained together, entwined, in love. Oh, then you LOVED the world.

—You eternal ones, you love it eternally and for all time. And to woe, too, you say: Away! Go! But come back! FOR ALL JOYS WANT ETERNITY!

11.

All joy wants the eternity of all things. It wants honey, it wants dregs, it wants drunken midnight, it wants graves, it wants the consolation of tears shed at graves, it wants the gilded glow of evening.

—*What* does joy not want! It is thirstier, warmer, hungrier, more terrible, more mysterious than all woe. It wants *itself*, it bites into *itself*, and the will of the ring writhes within it.

—It wants love, it wants hate. It is overrich, it gives, it throws away, it begs someone to take from it, it thanks the one who takes, it would gladly be hated.

—So rich is joy that it thirsts for woe, for hell, for hate, for shame, for the lame, for the WORLD, for this world. Oh, you know it well!

You higher men, it longs for you, this joy, this irrepressible, blessed joy. It longs for your woe, you failures! All eternal joy longs for failures.

For all joys want themselves, and therefore they also want grief! O happiness, O pain! Oh break, my heart! You higher men, learn this: joys want eternity.

—Joys want the eternity of ALL things. They WANT DEEP, PROFOUND ETERNITY!

12.

Have you learned my song now? Have you guessed what it means to say? Well then! Take heart! You higher men, sing my roundelay now!

Now sing the song yourselves, the song whose name is "Once more" and whose meaning is "For all eternity!" Sing, you higher men, Zarathustra's roundelay!

O man! Take heed!  
What does deep midnight's voice say?  
"I slept my sleep—  
"From deepest dream I've woken, and I plead:—  
"The world is deep,  
"And deeper than the day could read.  
"Deep is its woe—  
"Joy—deeper still than grief can be:  
"Woe says: Away! Go!  
"But all joys want eternity—  
"—Want deep, profound eternity!"

## LXXX. THE SIGN.

In the morning after this night, Zarathustra leapt up from his bed and girded his loins. He came out of his cave glowing and strong, like a morning sun rising out of dark mountains.

"You great star," he said, as he had said once before, "you deep eye of happiness, what would all your happiness be if you did not have *those* for whom you shine!

And if they stayed in their rooms while you are already awake, coming and giving and handing out your light, how your proud modesty would resent it!

Well! They are still asleep, these higher men, while I am awake. *They* are not my true companions! It is not for them that I wait here in my mountains.

I want to be at my work, at my day. But they do not understand the signs of my morning. My step is not the call that wakes them.

They are still asleep in my cave. Their dreams are still drinking from my drunken songs. The ear that listens for *me*, the *obedient* ear, is still lacking in their limbs."

—Zarathustra had spoken this to his heart as the sun rose. Then he looked upward inquiringly, for he heard the sharp call of his eagle above him. "Well!" he called up. "This is how it pleases me, and how it should be. My animals are awake, because I am awake.

My eagle is awake, and like me it honors the sun. With eagle's talons it grasps at the new light. You are my true animals. I love you.

But I still lack my true men!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra. Then, all of a sudden, he became aware that something was flocking and fluttering around him, as if countless birds surrounded him. The whirring of so many wings and the crowding around his head were so great that he shut his eyes.

And truly, something like a cloud came down upon him, like a cloud of arrows pouring down upon a new enemy. But look: here it was a cloud of love, showered upon a new friend.

"What is happening to me?" Zarathustra thought in his astonished heart, and he slowly sat down on the big stone that lay close to the mouth of his cave. He groped about with his hands, around him, above him, and below him, pushing away the tender birds.

Then something still stranger happened to him. Without realizing it, he reached into a mass of thick, warm, shaggy hair. At the same moment a roar sounded in front of him, a long, soft lion's roar.

"THE SIGN IS COMING," said Zarathustra, and a change came over his heart. And indeed, when his sight cleared, a yellow, powerful animal lay at his feet, resting its head on his knee. Out of love it would not leave him, and it behaved like a dog that has found its old master again.

The doves, however, were no less eager in their love than the lion. Whenever a dove brushed past its nose, the lion shook its head, wondered, and laughed.

While all this was going on, Zarathustra said only one thing: "MY CHILDREN ARE NEAR, MY CHILDREN." Then he fell completely silent. But his heart was released, and tears dropped from his eyes and fell upon his hands.

He paid no more attention to anything, but sat there motionless, no longer pushing the animals away. The doves flew back and forth, perched on his shoulder, stroked his white hair, and never tired of their tenderness and joy. The strong lion kept licking the tears that fell on Zarathustra's hands, roaring and growling shyly. This is what these animals did.

All this went on for a long time, or a short time. Strictly speaking, there is NO time on earth for such things. Meanwhile the higher men had woken up in Zarathustra's cave. When they awoke, they found that he was no longer with them, so they lined up in a procession to go out, meet him, and give him their morning greeting.

But when they reached the door of the cave, the sound of their footsteps carried ahead of them, and the lion started violently. All at once it turned away from Zarathustra and, roaring wildly, sprang toward the cave. When the higher men heard the lion roar, they all cried out as with one voice, fled back inside, and vanished in an instant.

Zarathustra himself, stunned and bewildered, rose from his seat. He looked around, stood there astonished, questioned his heart, collected his thoughts, and found himself alone. "What did I hear?" he said at last, slowly. "What just happened to me?"

But soon his memory returned, and he took in at a glance everything that had happened between yesterday and today. "Here indeed is the stone," he said, stroking his beard. "On *it* I sat yesterday morning. Here the soothsayer came to me, and here I first heard the cry I heard just now, the great cry of distress.

O you higher men, it was *your* distress that the old soothsayer foretold to me yesterday morning.

It was toward your distress that he wanted to lure and tempt me. 'O Zarathustra,' he said to me, 'I have come to tempt you into your last sin.'

Into my last sin?" cried Zarathustra, and laughed angrily at his own words. "*What* has been saved up for me as my last sin?"

And once more Zarathustra became absorbed in himself. He sat down again on the big stone and reflected. Suddenly he sprang up.

"FELLOW-SUFFERING! FELLOW-SUFFERING WITH THE HIGHER MEN!" he cried out, and his face hardened like brass. "Well! *That* has had its time!

My suffering and my fellow-suffering, what do they matter! Am I striving for *happiness*? I am striving for my *work*!

Well! The lion has come, my children are near, Zarathustra has grown ripe, my hour has come.

This is *my* morning, *my* day is beginning: RISE NOW, RISE, YOU GREAT NOON!"

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he left his cave, glowing and strong, like a morning sun rising out of dark mountains.
